=== net-01 | Layers: the Internet model and encapsulation
--- teach
You finished DSA I and II and Discrete Mathematics. You can find shortest paths in a graph, and you know modular arithmetic. This course is about the biggest graph people have built: the Internet, billions of computers joined by copper, glass fibre and radio. It asks one question over and over: how does a message get from a program on one computer to a program on another, when the wires between them lose, flip, delay and reorder bits?

The answer is not one clever program. It is a stack of simple ones, each solving one part of the problem and trusting the one below it for the rest. This first lesson is about that stack: what each layer promises, and how a message is wrapped on the way down and unwrapped on the way up.

### Letters inside envelopes

Picture a letter going abroad. You write it and put it in an envelope addressed to your friend. The post office puts many envelopes in a sack labelled with the destination city. The sack goes in a container on a ship. At the other end, the container is opened, the sack is opened, and the envelope is handed to your friend, who opens it and reads your letter.

Two things make this work. Each worker reads **only their own label**: the ship's crew never opens the sack, and the sorting office never reads your letter. And the wrappings come off in the **reverse order** they went on: the last one put on is the first one taken off.

### Protocols

A **protocol** is a set of rules two parties follow to talk: the exact format of every message, and what each side does when a message arrives. "Say hello, wait for hello back, then speak" is a protocol. So is "every message starts with a 2-byte length".

The two parties who follow a protocol are called **peers**. Your letter's protocol is between you and your friend. The sack's protocol is between two post offices. Each pair of peers only needs to agree with each other.

### Layers, and what each one promises

A **layer** is a group of protocols that offers one kind of service to the layer above it, using only the service of the layer below it. The Internet is usually described with five layers, from the top:

| layer | what it moves | what it promises | examples |
|---|---|---|---|
| application | messages | whatever the program needs: a web page, a name lookup | HTTP, DNS |
| transport | segments | delivery from one **program** to another; TCP adds "reliable, in order" | TCP, UDP |
| network | packets | a best try at delivery from one **computer** to another, across many links | IP |
| link | frames | delivery to a **neighbour** on the same wire or radio channel, with errors detected | Ethernet, Wi-Fi |
| physical | bits | bits on a medium: voltages, light pulses, radio waves | copper, fibre, radio |

Read the promises carefully, because this course lives on them:

- The network layer promises very little. IP is **[[best effort|best-effort]]**: a packet may be lost, duplicated, delayed or delivered out of order, and IP will not tell you.
- The transport layer is where reliability can be built on top of that, and lessons 7, 8 and 8b build it.
- The link layer only reaches one hop: one wire, or one Wi-Fi channel. Getting across the world is the network layer's job.

The model is sometimes drawn with seven layers instead, the **[[OSI model|osi]]**. The five-layer version is the one the Internet is really built on.

### Encapsulation

A layer cannot send anything by itself. It hands its data down to the layer below. That layer puts its own **header** in front (a few bytes of labels, like the address on an envelope), and hands the whole thing down again. Some layers also add a **trailer** at the end.

Putting the upper layer's data inside your own header is called **encapsulation**. The data you carry is your **payload**. You never look inside it: to you it is only bytes.

```text
application:                            [ message ]
transport:                    [ T hdr ][ message ]
network:             [ N hdr ][ T hdr ][ message ]
link:       [ L hdr ][ N hdr ][ T hdr ][ message ][ L trailer ]
physical:   0110100101011100101010010101 ... (the frame, as bits)
```

On the receiving computer the frame goes back **up**. Each layer reads its own header, removes it, and hands the payload to the layer above. That is **decapsulation**. Headers come off in the reverse order they went on, like plates from a stack.

Between the two ends, the boxes in the middle open only as many wrappings as they need:

- a **switch** reads only the link header (lesson 3);
- a **router** removes the link header, reads the network header to choose the next link, and wraps the packet in a new link header for that link (lessons 4 and 5).

### Which layer comes next?

When a receiver removes a header, how does it know what kind of data is inside? A computer runs many protocols at each layer: IP and IPv6, TCP and UDP. So every header carries a small field that names the protocol of its payload. Ethernet calls it the EtherType, and IP calls it the protocol number. For example, an Ethernet header with EtherType 0x0800 carries IPv4, and an IPv4 header with protocol number 17 carries UDP.

Handing each payload to the right protocol above, using that field, is called **demultiplexing**: many protocols share one layer below, and the field splits them apart again.

### Bytes in Python

Headers are made of bytes, so this course works with Python's `bytes` type: an unchangeable sequence of whole numbers, each from 0 to 255. A literal is written `b"..."`. A character that is not a plain letter is written `\x` followed by two [[hexadecimal digits|hex]].

```python
packet = b"\x05\x00\x03abc"
print(len(packet))        # 6: one byte per item
print(packet[0])          # 5: one item is an int
print(packet[3:])         # b'abc': a slice is bytes again
print(list(packet))       # [5, 0, 3, 97, 98, 99]
```

`bytes([...])` builds bytes from a list of numbers, and `+` joins two `bytes` values:

```python
tag = bytes([200, 1])
print(list(tag + b"ok"))  # [200, 1, 111, 107]
```

### Numbers that take more than one byte

A length of 300 does not fit in one byte, whose largest value is 255. It takes two: 300 = 1·256 + 44. The question is which byte goes first. The Internet puts the most significant byte first, called **big-endian** or **[[network byte order|byte-order]]**.

`n.to_bytes(k, "big")` writes the number `n` as `k` bytes, most significant first, and `int.from_bytes(b, "big")` reads it back:

```python
print(list((300).to_bytes(2, "big")))     # [1, 44]
print(list((300).to_bytes(2, "little")))  # [44, 1]: the other order
print(int.from_bytes(bytes([1, 44]), "big"))     # 300
print(int.from_bytes(bytes([1, 44]), "little"))  # 11265: wrong order, wrong number
```

Two bytes hold the numbers 0 to 65,535, which is 2 to the power 16, minus 1. `to_bytes` raises `OverflowError` for a number that does not fit.

### A header with a length

Here is the tiny header this lesson uses. It is 3 bytes long:

```text
byte 0      next: the protocol code of the payload (0 means "application data")
bytes 1-2   length: how many payload bytes follow, big-endian
```

Why a length? Because a receiver has to know where the payload **ends**. The bytes after it might belong to something else. For example, Ethernet pads short frames with zero bytes up to 46 bytes of payload, and the only way to tell padding from data is the length field of the header inside.

Reading one header, step by step, on a 7-byte buffer:

```python
buf = bytes([6, 0, 2, 104, 105, 0, 0])
code = buf[0]                              # 6
size = int.from_bytes(buf[1:3], "big")     # 2
body = buf[3:3 + size]                     # b'hi'
extra = buf[3 + size:]                     # b'\x00\x00': padding, not payload
print(code, size, body, extra)
```

A header that says exactly where its payload stops is called **self-delimiting**. A fixed-size header with a length field is self-delimiting.

### Why unwrapping always gives back what was wrapped

Call wrapping with protocol code c `wrap_c`, and reading one header `unwrap`. The claim: for every payload p that fits (at most 65,535 bytes), unwrapping `wrap_c(p)` gives back exactly c and p.

1. `wrap_c(p)` is 3 header bytes, then the bytes of p. The header holds c in byte 0 and len(p) in bytes 1 and 2.
2. `unwrap` reads byte 0, which is c. It reads bytes 1 and 2 big-endian, which is len(p), because `from_bytes` undoes `to_bytes` for any number that fits.
3. It then takes the next len(p) bytes. Those are exactly the bytes of p, since the header is always 3 bytes long.

Now the whole stack. Claim: if a message is wrapped by k layers and nothing changes on the way, the receiver unwraps it k times and gets back the message and every code, in reverse order. By **induction** on k:

- **Base, k = 0:** nothing was wrapped, so there is nothing to unwrap.
- **Step:** the outermost wrap was the last one applied. By the one-layer claim, one unwrap returns its code and exactly the data that layer was given, which is the message wrapped by k − 1 layers. By the induction hypothesis, the rest of the unwrapping returns the message and the other codes.

This is the **layering principle** in its exact form: layer n on the receiver gets exactly the bytes that layer n on the sender handed down. Each layer can be designed as if it talked straight to its peer.

It also stops. Every unwrap removes at least 3 bytes, and a buffer cannot shrink by 3 forever.

### What it costs

Headers are not free. Every packet pays for them, however little it carries. Real header sizes:

| header | bytes |
|---|---|
| Ethernet header and trailer | 14 + 4 |
| IPv4 | 20 (without options) |
| TCP | 20 (without options) |
| UDP | 8 |

A full Ethernet frame carries at most 1,500 bytes of IP packet. With 20 bytes of IP and 20 of TCP, that leaves 1,460 bytes of your data in a frame of 1,518 bytes, so 1,460 / 1,518 ≈ 96% of the bytes are yours. Send one byte at a time and it is terrible: the frame is padded to its minimum of 64 bytes, and 1 byte in 64 is under 2%.

The number of packets for a message of n bytes, when each packet carries at most c bytes of it, is n / c rounded up. Wrapping and unwrapping one packet costs time proportional to its length, because the bytes are copied.

### Where it is used

- **Every packet on the Internet** is built this way. Tools like [[Wireshark|wireshark]] show a captured packet as its stack of headers, outermost first.
- **Spacecraft** do the same. A telemetry value goes in a [[CCSDS space packet|ccsds]], space packets go in transfer frames, and frames go over the radio link.
- **Tunnels and VPNs** put a whole packet, headers and all, inside the payload of another packet.

**Watch out:** three classic bugs with headers.

- Reading a multi-byte field in the wrong byte order. A length of 300 read little-endian becomes 11,265. It works for every length under 256, so small tests pass.
- Trusting the length field without checking it. A header that says 1,000 bytes when only 10 arrived must be rejected, not read past the end.
- Treating padding as data. Always use the length, never "everything that is left".

::: context best-effort Why the network layer promises so little
Best effort means the network tries to deliver each packet but guarantees nothing: no delivery, no order, no timing. That sounds weak, and it is on purpose. A router that keeps no promises keeps no memory of connections, so it can be simple and fast, and when it crashes nothing is lost that the ends cannot resend. The designers put the hard work at the edges, in the computers. This idea is called the end-to-end argument, from a 1984 paper by Saltzer, Reed and Clark: a check that only the ends can do properly should be done at the ends.
:::

::: context osi The seven-layer model
The OSI model (Open Systems Interconnection) was set out by the International Organization for Standardization around 1984. It has seven layers: physical, data link, network, transport, session, presentation and application. The Internet does not use separate session and presentation layers; programs handle those jobs themselves. Engineers still use the OSI numbers as names, which is why a switch is called a "layer 2" device and a router a "layer 3" device, and why you will hear "layer 7" for the application.
:::

::: context hex Two hexadecimal digits make one byte
Hexadecimal is counting in sixteens, with digits 0 to 9 and then a to f for 10 to 15. One hex digit is exactly 4 bits, so two hex digits are exactly one byte: `\x00` is 0, `\x2c` is 2·16 + 12 = 44, and `\xff` is 255. That is why network engineers read packets in hex. Python prints a byte as a letter when it is a printable character, so `b"\x68\x69"` is shown as `b'hi'`; `list(...)` shows the numbers instead.
:::

::: context byte-order Big-endian and little-endian
A two-byte number can be stored with its big end (most significant byte) first or its little end first. The names come from Gulliver's Travels, where two nations go to war over which end of an egg to crack. Most processors today are little-endian, but Internet headers are big-endian, so network code converts at the boundary. In C that is `htons` ("host to network, short"); in Python you name the order in `to_bytes` and `from_bytes`.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="180" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">300 = 1 × 256 + 44</text>
  <text x="70" y="52" font-size="12" text-anchor="end" fill="#1f2a44">big-endian</text>
  <rect x="80" y="36" width="50" height="24" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="130" y="36" width="50" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="105" y="53" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1</text>
  <text x="155" y="53" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">44</text>
  <text x="70" y="92" font-size="12" text-anchor="end" fill="#1f2a44">little-endian</text>
  <rect x="80" y="76" width="50" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="130" y="76" width="50" height="24" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="105" y="93" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">44</text>
  <text x="155" y="93" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1</text>
  <text x="200" y="53" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">the Internet's order</text>
  <text x="200" y="93" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">most processors' order</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context wireshark Seeing the layers for real
Wireshark and tcpdump are free programs that capture the packets passing through your computer's network card. Wireshark shows each packet as a tree: Ethernet at the top, then IP inside it, then TCP or UDP, then the application's data, with every header field named. Capturing a few seconds of your own traffic is the fastest way to believe that encapsulation is real. On your own machine it needs administrator rights, because it can see everything.
:::

::: context ccsds How spacecraft wrap their data
CCSDS, the Consultative Committee for Space Data Systems, writes the standards that most space agencies use for data links. A space packet has a 6-byte header holding an application process identifier (which instrument or program the data belongs to), a sequence count, and a length. Packets ride inside transfer frames, which carry a frame header and a CRC trailer, and frames go over the radio. It is the same idea as Ethernet and IP, designed for links with long delays and no chance to ask twice.
:::
--- task
Use the 3-byte header from the lesson: byte 0 is `next`, the protocol code of the payload (0 to 255, where 0 means application data), and bytes 1 and 2 are the payload's length, big-endian.

Write four functions.

- `wrap(next_code, payload)` returns the header followed by `payload`, as `bytes`. Raise `ValueError` if `next_code` is not from 0 to 255, or if `payload` is longer than 65,535 bytes.
- `unwrap(data)` reads one header from the front of `data` and returns a tuple `(next_code, payload, rest)`, where `rest` is everything after the payload. Raise `ValueError` if `data` has fewer than 3 bytes, or fewer payload bytes than the length says.
- `encapsulate(message, protocols)`: `protocols` is a list of protocol codes from the **top** of the stack down, so `protocols[0]` is the layer just below the application and `protocols[-1]` is the outermost. Wrap `message` in one header per protocol. The header added by `protocols[0]` has `next` 0. The header added by each later protocol names the protocol inside it. The outermost protocol's own code is not written anywhere. With an empty list, return `message` unchanged.
- `decapsulate(frame, outer)`: `outer` is the code of the outermost protocol. Unwrap one header at a time until a header's `next` is 0. Return a tuple `(codes, message)`: `codes` lists every protocol whose header you removed, outermost first, starting with `outer`. Bytes after a payload are padding: ignore them.

For example, `encapsulate(b"hi", [17, 4, 1])` is `bytes([4, 0, 8, 17, 0, 5, 0, 0, 2, 104, 105])`, and `decapsulate` of that with `outer` 1 is `([1, 4, 17], b"hi")`.
--- starter
def wrap(next_code, payload):
    return payload


def unwrap(data):
    raise NotImplementedError


def encapsulate(message, protocols):
    return message


def decapsulate(frame, outer):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def wrap(next_code, payload):
    if not 0 <= next_code <= 255:
        raise ValueError("next code must fit in one byte")
    if len(payload) > 65535:
        raise ValueError("payload too long for a 2-byte length")
    return bytes([next_code]) + len(payload).to_bytes(2, "big") + payload


def unwrap(data):
    if len(data) < 3:
        raise ValueError("too short for a header")
    next_code = data[0]
    size = int.from_bytes(data[1:3], "big")
    if len(data) - 3 < size:
        raise ValueError("payload cut short")
    return next_code, data[3:3 + size], data[3 + size:]


def encapsulate(message, protocols):
    data = message
    inner = 0
    for code in protocols:
        data = wrap(inner, data)
        inner = code
    return data


def decapsulate(frame, outer):
    codes = [outer]
    data = frame
    while True:
        next_code, data, _ = unwrap(data)
        if next_code == 0:
            return codes, data
        codes.append(next_code)
--- hint
`wrap` joins three pieces with `+`: `bytes([next_code])`, the length as two big-endian bytes (`to_bytes(2, "big")`), and the payload. `unwrap` reads the same three fields back, and checks that enough bytes are there before slicing.
--- hint
In `encapsulate`, keep a variable for "the code of what is inside right now". It starts at 0 (application data). For each protocol from the top down, wrap with that variable, then set it to this protocol's code.
--- hint
In `decapsulate`, start the list with `outer`. Loop: unwrap, and if `next` is 0 you are holding the message; otherwise append `next` and unwrap the payload again. Throw away `rest` each time: it is padding.
--- check case | wrap puts next and a big-endian length in front
wrap(7, b"abc")
=> bytes([7, 0, 3, 97, 98, 99])
--- check case | wrap an empty payload, and a 300-byte one
(wrap(0, b""), wrap(9, bytes(300))[:3])
=> (bytes([0, 0, 0]), bytes([9, 1, 44]))
--- check test | wrap rejects a code over 255, a negative code, and a payload over 65,535 bytes
raises(ValueError, lambda: wrap(256, b"x")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: wrap(-1, b"x")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: wrap(1, bytes(65536))) and len(wrap(1, bytes(65535))) == 65538
--- check case | unwrap splits header, payload and rest
unwrap(bytes([6, 0, 2, 104, 105, 0, 0, 9]))
=> (6, b"hi", bytes([0, 0, 9]))
--- check case | unwrap reads a length of 258 big-endian
(lambda r: (r[0], len(r[1]), r[2]))(unwrap(bytes([3, 1, 2]) + bytes(258) + b"z"))
=> (3, 258, b"z")
?? Bytes 1 and 2 are [1, 2], which is 1·256 + 2 = 258 in big-endian order.
--- check test | unwrap rejects short buffers and cut-short payloads
raises(ValueError, lambda: unwrap(b"")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: unwrap(bytes([1, 0]))) and raises(ValueError, lambda: unwrap(bytes([1, 0, 5, 1, 2, 3, 4]))) and unwrap(bytes([1, 0, 0])) == (1, b"", b"")
--- check case | The example from the task
encapsulate(b"hi", [17, 4, 1])
=> bytes([4, 0, 8, 17, 0, 5, 0, 0, 2, 104, 105])
--- check case | No protocols: the message unchanged
(encapsulate(b"raw", []), encapsulate(b"", [5]))
=> (b"raw", bytes([0, 0, 0]))
--- check case | decapsulate the example
decapsulate(bytes([4, 0, 8, 17, 0, 5, 0, 0, 2, 104, 105]), 1)
=> ([1, 4, 17], b"hi")
--- check case | decapsulate ignores padding at every level
decapsulate(bytes([8, 0, 5, 0, 0, 1, 33, 0, 0, 0, 0]), 2)
=> ([2, 8], b"!")
--- check test | decapsulate undoes encapsulate for 200 random stacks and messages
(lambda R: all((lambda p, m: decapsulate(encapsulate(m, p), p[-1]) == (p[::-1], m))([R.randint(1, 255) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 6))], bytes(R.randint(0, 255) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 400)))) for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(2024))
--- check test | Every layer adds exactly 3 bytes
all(len(encapsulate(bytes(50), [1] * k)) == 50 + 3 * k for k in range(10))
--- check test | decapsulate rejects a truncated frame
raises(ValueError, lambda: decapsulate(encapsulate(b"hello", [6, 4])[:-1], 4))

+++ practice | Bytes on the wire
--- task
A message of `message_len` bytes is split into packets. Every packet carries all the headers in `header_sizes` (a list of header sizes in bytes) plus as many message bytes as fit, and a whole packet may be at most `mtu` bytes long.

Write `wire_bytes(message_len, mtu, header_sizes)` that returns a tuple `(packets, total)`: how many packets are sent, and how many bytes they add up to, headers included. Every packet but the last is full. A message of 0 bytes sends no packets: `(0, 0)`. Raise `ValueError` if the headers leave no room for even one message byte.

For example, `wire_bytes(3000, 1500, [20, 20])` is `(3, 3120)`: each packet carries 1,460 message bytes, so 3,000 bytes need 3 packets, and 3 × 40 = 120 header bytes are added.
--- starter
def wire_bytes(message_len, mtu, header_sizes):
    return (0, 0)
--- solution
def wire_bytes(message_len, mtu, header_sizes):
    overhead = sum(header_sizes)
    room = mtu - overhead
    if room <= 0:
        raise ValueError("headers leave no room for data")
    packets = (message_len + room - 1) // room
    return packets, message_len + packets * overhead
--- hint
Each packet has room for `mtu - sum(header_sizes)` message bytes. The number of packets is the message length divided by that room, rounded **up**.
--- hint
To round up without floats, use `(n + room - 1) // room`. The total is the message itself plus one copy of all the headers per packet.
--- check case | The example from the task
wire_bytes(3000, 1500, [20, 20])
=> (3, 3120)
--- check case | Exactly full packets, and one byte over
(wire_bytes(2920, 1500, [20, 20]), wire_bytes(2921, 1500, [20, 20]))
=> ((2, 3000), (3, 3041))
--- check case | An empty message sends nothing
wire_bytes(0, 1500, [8, 20])
=> (0, 0)
--- check case | No headers at all
wire_bytes(10, 4, [])
=> (3, 10)
--- check test | Headers that fill the packet are an error
raises(ValueError, lambda: wire_bytes(10, 40, [20, 20])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: wire_bytes(10, 30, [20, 20]))
--- check case | One byte of room
wire_bytes(5, 41, [20, 20])
=> (5, 205)

+++ practice | Records back to back
--- task
A buffer holds several records one after another, each in the lesson's format: 1 byte `next`, 2 bytes of big-endian length, then that many payload bytes. There is no padding between records.

Write `split_records(data)` that returns a list of `(next_code, payload)` tuples, one per record, in order. An empty buffer gives `[]`. If the last record is cut short (fewer than 3 header bytes, or fewer payload bytes than its length says), raise `ValueError`.

For example, `split_records(bytes([1, 0, 1, 65, 2, 0, 0]))` is `[(1, b"A"), (2, b"")]`.
--- starter
def split_records(data):
    return []
--- solution
def split_records(data):
    records = []
    pos = 0
    while pos < len(data):
        if len(data) - pos < 3:
            raise ValueError("header cut short")
        code = data[pos]
        size = int.from_bytes(data[pos + 1:pos + 3], "big")
        start = pos + 3
        if len(data) - start < size:
            raise ValueError("payload cut short")
        records.append((code, data[start:start + size]))
        pos = start + size
    return records
--- hint
Keep a position `pos` that starts at 0. While it is before the end, read a header at `pos`, take the payload, and move `pos` to just after it.
--- hint
Check the lengths before you slice: a slice past the end quietly returns fewer bytes instead of failing, so a cut-short record would slip through.
--- check case | The example from the task
split_records(bytes([1, 0, 1, 65, 2, 0, 0]))
=> [(1, b"A"), (2, b"")]
--- check case | An empty buffer
split_records(b"")
=> []
--- check case | A record longer than 255 bytes
(lambda r: [(c, len(p)) for c, p in r])(split_records(bytes([4, 1, 0]) + bytes(256) + bytes([5, 0, 2, 7, 7])))
=> [(4, 256), (5, 2)]
--- check test | A cut-short header or payload is an error
raises(ValueError, lambda: split_records(bytes([1, 0, 1, 65, 2, 0]))) and raises(ValueError, lambda: split_records(bytes([1, 0, 4, 65, 66])))
--- check case | Payload bytes that look like headers are still payload
split_records(bytes([9, 0, 3, 1, 0, 0]))
=> [(9, bytes([1, 0, 0]))]

+++ practice | Counting what the frames carry
--- task
A capture is a list of frames, all using the lesson's 3-byte headers, with outermost protocol code `outer`. Some frames were damaged and cannot be decapsulated.

Write `count_innermost(frames, outer)` that returns a dict counting, for each protocol code, how many frames have it as the **innermost** protocol (the last code before the application data). Count frames that cannot be decapsulated under the key `"damaged"`, and leave that key out when there are none. Write your own `unwrap` and `decapsulate` helpers, as in the lesson's task.

For example, if two frames carry `1 → 4 → 17` and one carries `1 → 4 → 6`, the answer is `{17: 2, 6: 1}`. A frame whose first header already has `next` 0 has `outer` as its innermost protocol.
--- starter
def count_innermost(frames, outer):
    return {}
--- solution
def unwrap(data):
    if len(data) < 3:
        raise ValueError("too short for a header")
    size = int.from_bytes(data[1:3], "big")
    if len(data) - 3 < size:
        raise ValueError("payload cut short")
    return data[0], data[3:3 + size], data[3 + size:]


def decapsulate(frame, outer):
    codes = [outer]
    data = frame
    while True:
        next_code, data, _ = unwrap(data)
        if next_code == 0:
            return codes, data
        codes.append(next_code)


def count_innermost(frames, outer):
    counts = {}
    for frame in frames:
        try:
            codes, _ = decapsulate(frame, outer)
            key = codes[-1]
        except ValueError:
            key = "damaged"
        counts[key] = counts.get(key, 0) + 1
    return counts
--- hint
Decapsulate each frame. The innermost protocol is the last code in the list it returns.
--- hint
Wrap the decapsulation in `try` / `except ValueError` and count a failure under `"damaged"`. `counts.get(key, 0) + 1` counts without a missing-key error.
--- check case | Two kinds of frame
count_innermost([bytes([4, 0, 8, 17, 0, 5, 0, 0, 2, 104, 105]), bytes([4, 0, 6, 17, 0, 3, 0, 0, 0]), bytes([4, 0, 6, 6, 0, 3, 0, 0, 0])], 1)
=> {17: 2, 6: 1}
--- check case | A damaged frame
count_innermost([bytes([4, 0, 9, 17, 0, 0]), bytes([0, 0, 1, 7])], 1)
=> {"damaged": 1, 1: 1}
--- check case | No frames
count_innermost([], 1)
=> {}
--- check case | An empty frame is damaged
count_innermost([b"", bytes([0, 0, 0])], 5)
=> {"damaged": 1, 5: 1}

+++ practice | Padding and the length field
--- task
A link that needs frames of at least `minimum` bytes pads short packets with zero bytes at the end. The packet inside uses the lesson's 3-byte header.

Write two functions.

- `pad(packet, minimum)` returns `packet` followed by enough zero bytes to make it `minimum` bytes long. A packet already that long or longer comes back unchanged.
- `recover(padded)` returns the original packet, header included, with the padding removed. Use the length in the header. Raise `ValueError` if there are fewer than 3 bytes, or fewer payload bytes than the length says.

Careful: a payload may itself end in zero bytes, and those must be kept. For example, `recover(pad(bytes([0, 0, 2, 5, 0]), 8))` is `bytes([0, 0, 2, 5, 0])`.
--- starter
def pad(packet, minimum):
    return packet


def recover(padded):
    return padded.rstrip(b"\x00")
--- solution
def pad(packet, minimum):
    missing = max(0, minimum - len(packet))
    return packet + bytes(missing)


def recover(padded):
    if len(padded) < 3:
        raise ValueError("too short for a header")
    size = int.from_bytes(padded[1:3], "big")
    if len(padded) - 3 < size:
        raise ValueError("payload cut short")
    return padded[:3 + size]
--- hint
`bytes(n)` is n zero bytes, and `bytes(0)` is empty. How many are missing? Never a negative number.
--- hint
Stripping zeros from the end cannot tell padding from a payload that ends in zeros. The header knows: the packet is 3 bytes plus the length it holds.
--- check case | A payload that ends in a zero keeps it
recover(pad(bytes([0, 0, 2, 5, 0]), 8))
=> bytes([0, 0, 2, 5, 0])
--- check case | pad adds zeros up to the minimum, and leaves long packets alone
(pad(bytes([1, 0, 1, 9]), 6), pad(bytes([1, 0, 1, 9]), 4), pad(bytes([1, 0, 1, 9]), 2))
=> (bytes([1, 0, 1, 9, 0, 0]), bytes([1, 0, 1, 9]), bytes([1, 0, 1, 9]))
--- check case | An empty payload padded to 46 bytes
recover(pad(bytes([0, 0, 0]), 46))
=> bytes([0, 0, 0])
--- check case | An all-zero header with an all-zero payload
recover(bytes([0, 0, 3, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0]))
=> bytes([0, 0, 3, 0, 0, 0])
--- check test | Short or cut-short input is an error
raises(ValueError, lambda: recover(bytes([0, 0]))) and raises(ValueError, lambda: recover(bytes([0, 0, 5, 1])))

+++ practice | Debug: lengths over 255
--- task
`unwrap(data)` should read the lesson's 3-byte header (1 byte `next`, 2 bytes of big-endian length) and return `(next_code, payload, rest)`. It passes every test with short payloads, but a packet with a 300-byte payload comes out wrong, or raises an error that says the payload was cut short. Find the bug and fix it. It is one line.
--- starter
def unwrap(data):
    if len(data) < 3:
        raise ValueError("too short for a header")
    next_code = data[0]
    size = int.from_bytes(data[1:3], "little")
    if len(data) - 3 < size:
        raise ValueError("payload cut short")
    return next_code, data[3:3 + size], data[3 + size:]
--- solution
def unwrap(data):
    if len(data) < 3:
        raise ValueError("too short for a header")
    next_code = data[0]
    size = int.from_bytes(data[1:3], "big")
    if len(data) - 3 < size:
        raise ValueError("payload cut short")
    return next_code, data[3:3 + size], data[3 + size:]
--- hint
Lengths under 256 have a first length byte of 0. Why does that make the bug invisible?
--- hint
The Internet sends the most significant byte first. Which order does the code read the two length bytes in?
--- check case | A 300-byte payload
(lambda r: (r[0], len(r[1]), r[2]))(unwrap(bytes([2, 1, 44]) + bytes(300) + b"end"))
=> (2, 300, b"end")
--- check case | A short payload still works
unwrap(bytes([5, 0, 2, 104, 105]))
=> (5, b"hi", b"")
--- check case | A length of 256
(lambda r: len(r[1]))(unwrap(bytes([1, 1, 0]) + bytes(256)))
=> 256
--- check test | A cut-short payload is still an error
raises(ValueError, lambda: unwrap(bytes([1, 1, 0]) + bytes(255)))

+++ practice | Space packets from a telemetry stream
--- task
A spacecraft sends CCSDS space packets back to back. Each has a 6-byte primary header, read as three big-endian 16-bit numbers `w0`, `w1` and `w2`:

- `w0`: the top 3 bits are the version, the next bit the type, the next bit the secondary-header flag, and the **lowest 11 bits are the APID** (which instrument the packet is from).
- `w1`: the top 2 bits are the sequence flags, and the **lowest 14 bits are the sequence count**.
- `w2`: the number of data bytes after the header, **minus 1**. So a packet always has at least 1 data byte.

Write `space_packets(stream)` that returns a list of dicts `{"apid": ..., "seq": ..., "data": ...}`, one per packet, in order. `data` is the packet's data bytes. An empty stream gives `[]`. Raise `ValueError` if a packet is cut short.

For example, `bytes([0x08, 0x2A, 0xC0, 0x05, 0x00, 0x01, 0xAB, 0xCD])` is one packet: `w0` is 0x082A, whose lowest 11 bits are 42, `w1` is 0xC005, whose lowest 14 bits are 5, and `w2` is 1, so 2 data bytes follow: `{"apid": 42, "seq": 5, "data": bytes([0xAB, 0xCD])}`.
--- starter
def space_packets(stream):
    return []
--- solution
def space_packets(stream):
    packets = []
    pos = 0
    while pos < len(stream):
        if len(stream) - pos < 6:
            raise ValueError("header cut short")
        w0 = int.from_bytes(stream[pos:pos + 2], "big")
        w1 = int.from_bytes(stream[pos + 2:pos + 4], "big")
        w2 = int.from_bytes(stream[pos + 4:pos + 6], "big")
        size = w2 + 1
        start = pos + 6
        if len(stream) - start < size:
            raise ValueError("data cut short")
        packets.append({
            "apid": w0 & 0x7FF,
            "seq": w1 & 0x3FFF,
            "data": stream[start:start + size],
        })
        pos = start + size
    return packets
--- hint
Read the three 16-bit words with `int.from_bytes(..., "big")` on two bytes each. The lowest 11 bits of a number are `w & 0x7FF` (eleven 1s), and the lowest 14 bits are `w & 0x3FFF`.
--- hint
Walk the stream with a position, as for back-to-back records. The data length is `w2 + 1`, not `w2`. Check that the whole header and all the data are there before slicing.
--- check case | The example from the task
space_packets(bytes([0x08, 0x2A, 0xC0, 0x05, 0x00, 0x01, 0xAB, 0xCD]))
=> [{"apid": 42, "seq": 5, "data": bytes([0xAB, 0xCD])}]
--- check case | Two packets, the second with 1 data byte and the highest APID and count
space_packets(bytes([0x08, 0x2A, 0xC0, 0x05, 0x00, 0x01, 0xAB, 0xCD, 0x1F, 0xFF, 0xFF, 0xFF, 0x00, 0x00, 0x07]))
=> [{"apid": 42, "seq": 5, "data": bytes([0xAB, 0xCD])}, {"apid": 2047, "seq": 16383, "data": bytes([7])}]
--- check case | An empty stream
space_packets(b"")
=> []
--- check test | A packet with 300 data bytes
(lambda p: len(p) == 1 and p[0]["apid"] == 1 and len(p[0]["data"]) == 300)(space_packets(bytes([0x00, 0x01, 0x00, 0x00, 0x01, 0x2B]) + bytes(300)))
--- check test | Cut-short header or data is an error
raises(ValueError, lambda: space_packets(bytes([0, 1, 0, 0, 0]))) and raises(ValueError, lambda: space_packets(bytes([0, 1, 0, 0, 0, 1, 9])))

+++ question | What a router opens
--- ask
A router receives an Ethernet frame holding an IP packet, which holds a TCP segment, which holds part of a web page. Which headers does the router read to decide where the packet goes next?
--- choice
All of them, down to the web page, so it can pick the best route for that kind of data.
--- choice correct
The link header, which it removes, and the network (IP) header, which holds the destination address. It puts the packet in a new link header for the next link.
--- choice
Only the Ethernet header, since that is the outermost one.
--- choice
Only the TCP header, since that is where the port numbers are.
--- why
A router works at the network layer. It strips the link header (which only described the last hop), reads the IP destination to choose the next link, and wraps the packet in a fresh link header. The transport header and the data are payload to it. Only the two ends read those.

+++ question | The step that needs the length field
--- ask
The proof that unwrapping gives back what was wrapped has a step: "it then takes the next len(p) bytes, and those are exactly the bytes of p". Suppose the header had no length field, and `unwrap` returned everything after the header as the payload. For which kind of input does the claim then fail?
--- choice
For payloads longer than 255 bytes.
--- choice correct
When other bytes follow the payload, such as link-layer padding or the next record: they would be returned as part of the payload.
--- choice
It never fails; the length field is only there for speed.
--- choice
When the payload is empty.
--- why
Without a length, the header is not self-delimiting: it cannot say where its payload stops. That works only if nothing ever follows the payload. Padding, trailers and back-to-back records all break it, which is why real headers carry a length, or are followed by a fixed-size structure whose length is known.

+++ question | Header overhead
--- ask
Each packet carries 40 bytes of headers and at most 1,000 bytes of data. About what fraction of the bytes sent are headers when a program sends a 10-byte message, compared with a 1,000,000-byte message?
--- choice
About 4% in both cases.
--- choice correct
80% for the 10-byte message (40 of 50), and under 4% for the big one (40 per 1,040).
--- choice
40% for the small message and 0% for the big one.
--- why
Headers are paid per packet, not per byte. A 10-byte message is one packet of 50 bytes, so 40/50 = 80% is overhead. The big message is 1,000 full packets of 1,040 bytes, so 40/1,040 ≈ 3.8%. That is why protocols try to fill packets, and why tiny messages are expensive.

=== net-02 | Framing: finding where frames start and end
--- teach
Last lesson a receiver read a header at the front of a buffer and used its length to find the end of the payload. That quietly assumed something big: that the receiver knows where the buffer **starts**. On a real wire it does not. The link hands it a stream of bits that never pauses, frame after frame, and some of those bits arrive flipped. This lesson is about **framing**: marking where each frame begins and ends, so a receiver can find them in the stream and recover after an error.

### Bits on a wire

The physical layer sends bits as a signal: a voltage on copper, a pulse of light in a fibre, a change in a radio wave. Two numbers describe a link:

- the **bit rate** R, in bits per second: how fast bits are put on the wire. A 1 Gb/s Ethernet link sends 10⁹ bits every second.
- the **propagation delay**: how long one bit takes to travel the length of the link, at about two-thirds of the speed of light in copper or fibre.

Sending a frame of L bits takes L / R seconds just to push the bits out. That is the **transmission time**. A 1,500-byte frame is 12,000 bits: 1.2 ms at 10 Mb/s, and 12 microseconds at 1 Gb/s.

Noise, interference and weak signals flip bits now and then. The fraction flipped is the **bit error rate**. A good fibre link may flip one bit in 10¹² or fewer; a radio link to a spacecraft far more. The receiver cannot see which bits are wrong. The next lesson is about detecting that. This one is about staying in step.

### Counting is fragile

The simplest framing puts a length in front of every frame, as last lesson's header did. The receiver reads the length, counts that many bytes, and expects the next length right after.

This works until one bit of a length is flipped. Then the receiver counts the wrong number of bytes, reads the next "length" from the middle of some payload, and gets every frame after that wrong too. It has no way back into step, because nothing in the stream says "a frame starts here". A length alone is not enough on a raw link.

### A flag byte

The fix is to mark the boundaries with a special byte that cannot appear anywhere else. A **flag** byte is sent between frames, so a frame is everything between two flags. PPP, the protocol that used to carry dial-up Internet and still runs many serial links, uses the flag **0x7E** (126).

When a frame is damaged, the receiver throws it away and waits for the next flag. It is back in step within one frame. A stream that can find its place again by itself like this is called **[[self-synchronising|self-sync]]**.

### Byte stuffing

There is a catch: the payload is arbitrary bytes, and some payloads contain 0x7E. The receiver would mistake it for the end of the frame.

The fix is to rewrite the payload so the flag never appears in it. PPP uses a second special byte, the **escape** byte **0x7D** (125), and replaces two byte values:

| payload byte | is sent as |
|---|---|
| 0x7E (the flag) | 0x7D, 0x5E |
| 0x7D (the escape itself) | 0x7D, 0x5D |
| any other byte b | b, unchanged |

0x5E and 0x5D are 0x7E and 0x7D with one bit flipped: each is the original byte XOR 0x20. Rewriting the payload this way is called **byte stuffing**, and undoing it is **unstuffing**.

By hand, on the payload `41 7E 42 7D` (in hex):

```text
payload:        41   7E       42   7D
stuffed:        41   7D 5E    42   7D 5D
on the wire:  7E 41 7D 5E 42 7D 5D 7E
              ^flag                  ^flag
```

The receiver reads bytes between flags. When it meets 0x7D, it drops it and XORs the **next** byte with 0x20 to get the real byte back.

### Why stuffing works

Two claims, with the reasons.

**Claim 1: a stuffed payload never contains 0x7E.** Look at every byte the stuffer can write. An unchanged byte is not 0x7E, because 0x7E is always replaced. An escape byte is 0x7D, not 0x7E. The byte after an escape is 0x5E or 0x5D, neither of which is 0x7E. There is no other kind of byte. So the only 0x7E bytes on the wire are real flags.

**Claim 2: unstuffing gives back exactly the payload.** Walk the stuffed bytes from the left. A byte other than 0x7D was copied unchanged, since the stuffer only writes 0x7D as an escape (a real 0x7D in the payload became 0x7D 0x5D). An 0x7D is always the first byte of a pair, followed by 0x5E or 0x5D, and XOR with 0x20 turns those back into 0x7E and 0x7D. Each piece decodes to exactly the byte it came from, in order, so the whole payload comes back. Put another way: no byte's encoding is the start of another encoding in a way that could be misread, so there is only one way to split the stuffed bytes into pieces.

The same reasoning tells you what an **invalid** stuffed frame looks like: an escape as the very last byte, or an escape followed by anything other than 0x5E or 0x5D. A stuffer never writes those, so they can only come from a damaged frame.

### Growing bytes one at a time

A stuffer builds its output byte by byte. `bytes` cannot change, so `out = out + ...` in a loop makes a whole new copy every time, which is Θ(n²) for n bytes. A **bytearray** is the changeable version of `bytes`, like a list of small numbers:

```python
buf = bytearray()
buf.append(65)            # one byte, given as a number
buf.extend(b"xy")         # several bytes at once
print(buf)                # bytearray(b'Axy')
print(bytes(buf))         # b'Axy': freeze it when done
```

Appending to a bytearray is amortised O(1), like the dynamic array you built in DSA I.

Splitting a stream at a byte value is also built in. `split` on `bytes` returns the pieces between the separators, including empty ones:

```python
print(b"red;green;;blue".split(b";"))   # [b'red', b'green', b'', b'blue']
```

### What it costs

- **Time:** stuffing and unstuffing look at each byte once: Θ(n).
- **Space, worst case:** a payload made only of 0x7E and 0x7D bytes doubles in size, plus the two flags.
- **Space, typical case:** if the bytes are random, each one is special with chance 2/256, so the expected growth is 2n/256, under 1%.

That worst case matters for a system that must reserve buffer space for the largest frame. A method with a fixed worst case, **[[COBS|cobs]]**, adds at most one byte in 254.

### Bit stuffing

Some links frame at the level of bits instead of bytes. HDLC, the ancestor of PPP, uses the flag pattern `01111110`: six 1s between two 0s. To keep six 1s from appearing inside a frame, the sender inserts a 0 after every run of five 1s in the data, whatever comes next. The receiver removes a 0 that follows five 1s. Six 1s in a row can then only be a flag.

```text
data:      0 1 1 1 1 1 1 1 0
stuffed:   0 1 1 1 1 1 0 1 1 0      (a 0 inserted after the fifth 1)
```

The worst case adds one bit for every five, 20%. Bit stuffing also helps the receiver's clock: long runs of identical bits make it hard to tell where one bit ends and the next begins, and [[no data ever has more than five 1s in a row|clock-recovery]].

### Where it is used

- **PPP and HDLC** framing, exactly as above, on serial links, some DSL lines and many industrial radios.
- **SLIP**, an older serial framing, uses a different end byte (0xC0) and escape byte (0xDB), with the same idea.
- **[[Spacecraft links|asm]]** use fixed-length frames that each start with a known 32-bit pattern, and search the bit stream for it.
- Ethernet does its framing at the physical layer, with a preamble before each frame and a silent gap after it.

**Watch out:** the classic byte-stuffing bug is escaping the flag but not the escape byte itself. Then a payload holding 0x7D followed by 0x5E is sent unchanged, and the receiver turns those two bytes into a single 0x7E. The data is silently wrong, and only payloads with that exact pair show it. Always escape both special bytes.

::: context self-sync Getting back in step
A self-synchronising code lets a receiver that has lost its place, because it switched on mid-stream or because noise damaged a frame, find the next boundary by itself. Flags do this: whatever garbage came before, the next 0x7E is a boundary. Length fields alone do not: one bad length sends the reader off into the wrong bytes for good. Real protocols often use both: a flag or sync pattern to find the start, and a length inside to find the end.
:::

::: context cobs A fixed worst case
Consistent Overhead Byte Stuffing, published by Stuart Cheshire and Mary Baker in 1997, removes every zero byte from a packet, so a single zero can mark the frame boundary. Instead of escaping each special byte, it breaks the data at its zeros and puts a count in front of each piece saying how far away the next zero is. The result grows by at most one byte for every 254, whatever the data. Engineers like it on small embedded links because the buffer size can be fixed in advance.
:::

::: context clock-recovery Why long runs of 1s are a problem
The receiver has no separate wire telling it when each bit starts. It works that out from the changes in the signal, and keeps its own clock in step with them. During a long run of identical bits the signal does not change, so the receiver's clock slowly drifts, and it may count seven bits where six were sent. Many links add changes on purpose: Manchester coding, used by the first Ethernet, puts a transition in the middle of every bit, and 8b/10b coding turns every 8 data bits into 10 wire bits with enough changes.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 110" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="10" y="20" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">data 1 0 0 0 0 0 0 1: no changes for six bits</text>
  <polyline points="10,40 50,40 50,70 290,70 290,40 330,40" fill="none" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="50" y1="80" x2="290" y2="80" stroke="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="170" y="96" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">how many bit times is this? the clock must guess</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context asm How spacecraft find frame boundaries
CCSDS telemetry links send frames of a fixed length, each preceded by a 32-bit attached sync marker, 0x1ACFFC1D. The ground receiver slides along the bit stream looking for that pattern; once it has seen it a few times, exactly one frame length apart, it is locked on. The marker is chosen so that it looks very little like itself shifted by a few bits, which makes false locks rare. The payload is not stuffed: a fake marker inside the data is tolerated, because the receiver checks that markers repeat at the right spacing.
:::
--- task
Use PPP's byte stuffing: the flag is `0x7E`, the escape is `0x7D`, a payload byte `0x7E` is sent as `0x7D 0x5E`, a payload byte `0x7D` is sent as `0x7D 0x5D`, and every other byte is sent unchanged.

Write three functions.

- `stuff(payload)` returns the stuffed payload as `bytes` (no flags).
- `frame(payload)` returns a whole frame: a flag, the stuffed payload, and another flag.
- `deframe(stream)` finds every complete frame in `stream` and returns the list of their payloads, unstuffed, in order.
  - A frame is the bytes strictly between two flags. Bytes before the first flag and after the last flag are not complete frames: ignore them.
  - Two flags in a row hold an empty frame: skip it.
  - A frame that is not valid stuffing (an escape as its last byte, or an escape followed by anything other than `0x5E` or `0x5D`) is damaged: drop it and carry on with the next frame.

For example, `stuff(bytes([0x41, 0x7E, 0x42, 0x7D]))` is `bytes([0x41, 0x7D, 0x5E, 0x42, 0x7D, 0x5D])`, and `deframe(bytes([0x99, 0x7E, 0x01, 0x7E, 0x7E, 0x7D, 0x5E, 0x7E, 0x05]))` is `[b"\x01", b"\x7e"]`.
--- starter
FLAG = 0x7E
ESC = 0x7D


def stuff(payload):
    return payload


def frame(payload):
    return payload


def deframe(stream):
    return []
--- solution
FLAG = 0x7E
ESC = 0x7D


def stuff(payload):
    out = bytearray()
    for b in payload:
        if b == FLAG or b == ESC:
            out.append(ESC)
            out.append(b ^ 0x20)
        else:
            out.append(b)
    return bytes(out)


def unstuff(body):
    out = bytearray()
    i = 0
    while i < len(body):
        b = body[i]
        if b == ESC:
            if i + 1 == len(body) or body[i + 1] not in (0x5E, 0x5D):
                return None
            out.append(body[i + 1] ^ 0x20)
            i += 2
        else:
            out.append(b)
            i += 1
    return bytes(out)


def frame(payload):
    return bytes([FLAG]) + stuff(payload) + bytes([FLAG])


def deframe(stream):
    pieces = stream.split(bytes([FLAG]))
    payloads = []
    for body in pieces[1:-1]:
        if not body:
            continue
        data = unstuff(body)
        if data is not None:
            payloads.append(data)
    return payloads
--- hint
`stuff` walks the payload once and appends to a `bytearray`: one byte for an ordinary byte, two (`0x7D`, then the byte XOR `0x20`) for a special one.
--- hint
For `deframe`, `stream.split(bytes([FLAG]))` gives the pieces between flags. The first piece is before the first flag and the last piece is after the last flag, so the complete frames are `pieces[1:-1]`.
--- hint
Write a helper that unstuffs one frame with an index `i`. On an escape, check that another byte follows and that it is `0x5E` or `0x5D`, append it XOR `0x20`, and move `i` on by 2. Return `None` for a damaged frame, and skip those in `deframe`.
--- check case | The stuffing example from the task
stuff(bytes([0x41, 0x7E, 0x42, 0x7D]))
=> bytes([0x41, 0x7D, 0x5E, 0x42, 0x7D, 0x5D])
--- check case | Nothing to stuff, and an empty payload
(stuff(b"hello"), stuff(b""))
=> (b"hello", b"")
--- check case | A payload of only special bytes doubles
stuff(bytes([0x7D, 0x7D, 0x7E]))
=> bytes([0x7D, 0x5D, 0x7D, 0x5D, 0x7D, 0x5E])
--- check case | frame adds a flag at each end
frame(bytes([0x7E, 0x00]))
=> bytes([0x7E, 0x7D, 0x5E, 0x00, 0x7E])
--- check case | The deframing example from the task
deframe(bytes([0x99, 0x7E, 0x01, 0x7E, 0x7E, 0x7D, 0x5E, 0x7E, 0x05]))
=> [b"\x01", b"\x7e"]
--- check case | Empty frames between flags are skipped
deframe(bytes([0x7E, 0x7E, 0x7E, 0x33, 0x7E, 0x7E]))
=> [b"\x33"]
--- check case | Damaged frames are dropped, the rest survive
deframe(bytes([0x7E, 0x01, 0x7D, 0x7E, 0x02, 0x7E, 0x7D, 0x11, 0x7E, 0x03, 0x7E]))
=> [b"\x02", b"\x03"]
?? An escape at the end of a frame, or followed by 0x11, cannot come from stuff: drop that frame only.
--- check case | No complete frame: nothing, one flag, a frame still arriving
(deframe(b""), deframe(bytes([0x7E])), deframe(bytes([0x7E, 0x01, 0x02])))
=> ([], [], [])
--- check test | A stuffed payload never contains the flag, for 300 random payloads
(lambda R: all(0x7E not in stuff(bytes(R.choice([0x7E, 0x7D, 0x5E, 0x5D, 0, 255]) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 60)))) for _ in range(300)))(__import__("random").Random(7))
--- check test | deframe undoes frame for many payloads sent back to back
(lambda R: (lambda ps: deframe(b"".join(frame(p) for p in ps)) == ps)([bytes(R.choice([0x7E, 0x7D, 0x5E, 0x5D, 1, 2, 200]) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 40))) for _ in range(200)]))(__import__("random").Random(11))
--- check test | 200,000 bytes stuff and deframe quickly
(lambda p: deframe(frame(p)) == [p])(bytes(i % 256 for i in range(200000)))
?? Build the output in a bytearray: adding to a bytes value copies it every time.

+++ practice | How long a frame takes to send
--- task
A link sends `bit_rate` bits per second. A payload is sent as a PPP frame: a flag, the payload with every `0x7E` and `0x7D` byte stuffed into two bytes, and another flag.

Write `frame_time(payload, bit_rate)` that returns how many seconds it takes to put the whole frame on the wire: its length in bytes, times 8 bits, divided by the bit rate. You only need the frame's **length**, not its bytes.

For example, `frame_time(bytes([0x7E, 1, 2]), 8000)` is `0.006`: the frame is 1 + 4 + 1 = 6 bytes, 48 bits, and 48 / 8000 = 0.006.
--- starter
def frame_time(payload, bit_rate):
    return len(payload) * 8 / bit_rate
--- solution
def frame_time(payload, bit_rate):
    special = sum(1 for b in payload if b == 0x7E or b == 0x7D)
    length = 2 + len(payload) + special
    return length * 8 / bit_rate
--- hint
Each special byte adds one byte, and the two flags add two more. Count the special bytes with one pass over the payload.
--- hint
The length is `2 + len(payload) + special`, and the time is that times 8, divided by the bit rate.
--- check case | The example from the task
frame_time(bytes([0x7E, 1, 2]), 8000)
=> 0.006
--- check case | An empty payload is still two flags
frame_time(b"", 1600)
=> 0.01
--- check case | Only special bytes
frame_time(bytes([0x7D] * 99), 8000)
=> 0.2
--- check test | 1,500 ordinary bytes at 1 Gb/s
abs(frame_time(bytes(1500), 10**9) - 1502 * 8 / 10**9) < 1e-15

+++ practice | Bit stuffing
--- task
HDLC frames bits with the flag `01111110`. Inside a frame, the sender puts a `0` after every run of five `1`s in a row, whatever the next bit is. The receiver removes a `0` that comes right after five `1`s.

Bits here are strings of the characters `"0"` and `"1"`. Write two functions.

- `bit_stuff(bits)` returns the stuffed bits.
- `bit_unstuff(bits)` returns the original bits. After five `1`s in a row the next bit must be a `0`, which is removed. If it is a `1` instead, raise `ValueError`. If the string ends right after five `1`s, that is fine: there is nothing to remove.

For example, `bit_stuff("0111111")` is `"01111101"`, and `bit_stuff("11111")` is `"111110"`.
--- starter
def bit_stuff(bits):
    return bits


def bit_unstuff(bits):
    return bits
--- solution
def bit_stuff(bits):
    out = []
    run = 0
    for bit in bits:
        out.append(bit)
        if bit == "1":
            run += 1
            if run == 5:
                out.append("0")
                run = 0
        else:
            run = 0
    return "".join(out)


def bit_unstuff(bits):
    out = []
    run = 0
    i = 0
    while i < len(bits):
        bit = bits[i]
        out.append(bit)
        i += 1
        if bit == "1":
            run += 1
            if run == 5:
                if i < len(bits):
                    if bits[i] == "1":
                        raise ValueError("six 1s in a row inside a frame")
                    i += 1
                run = 0
        else:
            run = 0
    return "".join(out)
--- hint
Keep a count of how many `1`s you have seen in a row. A `0` resets it. When it reaches 5, the stuffer writes an extra `0` and the count starts again at 0, because the inserted `0` breaks the run.
--- hint
The unstuffer counts the same way. When the count reaches 5, look at the next bit, if there is one: a `0` is skipped, and a `1` is an error.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(bit_stuff("0111111"), bit_stuff("11111"))
=> ("01111101", "111110")
--- check case | Ten 1s get two zeros
bit_stuff("1111111111")
=> "111110111110"
--- check case | Four 1s need nothing, empty stays empty
(bit_stuff("011110"), bit_stuff(""))
=> ("011110", "")
--- check case | Unstuffing
(bit_unstuff("01111101"), bit_unstuff("111110111110"), bit_unstuff("11111"))
=> ("0111111", "1111111111", "11111")
--- check test | Six 1s in a row is an error
raises(ValueError, lambda: bit_unstuff("0111111"))
--- check test | Round trip and no six 1s, for 300 random bit strings
(lambda R: all((lambda b: bit_unstuff(bit_stuff(b)) == b and "111111" not in bit_stuff(b))("".join(R.choice("1110") for _ in range(R.randint(0, 50)))) for _ in range(300)))(__import__("random").Random(5))

+++ practice | A deframer that is fed in pieces
--- task
A serial port does not hand you whole frames. Each read returns whatever bytes have arrived: part of a frame, several frames, or a frame split across two reads.

Write a class `Deframer` using PPP framing (flag `0x7E`, escape `0x7D`, and an escaped byte is the next byte XOR `0x20`).

- `Deframer()` starts with nothing buffered, and has not yet seen a flag.
- `feed(chunk)` takes the next `bytes` from the port and returns the list of payloads of the frames that became complete, unstuffed, in order.

Follow the same rules as the lesson's `deframe`: bytes before the very first flag ever seen are ignored, empty frames are skipped, and a damaged frame (an escape at its end, or followed by a byte other than `0x5E` or `0x5D`) is dropped. The bytes of a frame that has not ended yet are kept for the next call.

For example, after `d = Deframer()`, `d.feed(bytes([0x7E, 0x01, 0x7D]))` returns `[]`, and then `d.feed(bytes([0x5E, 0x7E]))` returns `[bytes([0x01, 0x7E])]`.
--- starter
class Deframer:
    def feed(self, chunk):
        return []
--- solution
class Deframer:
    def __init__(self):
        self.synced = False
        self.body = bytearray()

    def feed(self, chunk):
        done = []
        for b in chunk:
            if b == 0x7E:
                if self.synced and self.body:
                    data = unstuff(bytes(self.body))
                    if data is not None:
                        done.append(data)
                self.synced = True
                self.body = bytearray()
            elif self.synced:
                self.body.append(b)
        return done


def unstuff(body):
    out = bytearray()
    i = 0
    while i < len(body):
        if body[i] == 0x7D:
            if i + 1 == len(body) or body[i + 1] not in (0x5E, 0x5D):
                return None
            out.append(body[i + 1] ^ 0x20)
            i += 2
        else:
            out.append(body[i])
            i += 1
    return bytes(out)
--- hint
Keep two things between calls: whether a flag has been seen yet, and a `bytearray` holding the bytes of the frame in progress.
--- hint
Go byte by byte. A flag ends the frame in progress (unstuff it if it is not empty, and keep it if it is valid) and starts a new, empty one. Any other byte is added to the frame in progress, but only once a flag has been seen.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda d: (d.feed(bytes([0x7E, 0x01, 0x7D])), d.feed(bytes([0x5E, 0x7E]))))(Deframer())
=> ([], [bytes([0x01, 0x7E])])
--- check case | Several frames in one chunk, garbage first
(lambda d: d.feed(bytes([0x31, 0x32, 0x7E, 0x0A, 0x7E, 0x0B, 0x0C, 0x7E])))(Deframer())
=> [b"\x0a", b"\x0b\x0c"]
--- check case | One byte at a time
(lambda d: [p for b in bytes([0x7E, 0x61, 0x7D, 0x5D, 0x62, 0x7E, 0x7E, 0x63, 0x7E]) for p in d.feed(bytes([b]))])(Deframer())
=> [b"a}b", b"c"]
--- check case | A damaged frame is dropped, empty chunks return nothing
(lambda d: (d.feed(b""), d.feed(bytes([0x7E, 0x7D, 0x01, 0x7E, 0x44, 0x7E]))))(Deframer())
=> ([], [b"D"])
--- check test | Random chunking gives the same frames as whole
(lambda R: all((lambda ps, d: (lambda s: (lambda cuts: [p for a, b in zip([0] + cuts, cuts + [len(s)]) for p in d.feed(s[a:b])] == ps)(sorted(R.sample(range(1, len(s)), 5))))(b"\x7e" + b"\x7e".join(bytes(sum(([0x7D, x ^ 0x20] if x in (0x7E, 0x7D) else [x] for x in p), [])) for p in ps) + b"\x7e"))([bytes(R.choice([0x7E, 0x7D, 1, 2]) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 8))) for _ in range(6)], Deframer()) for _ in range(100)))(__import__("random").Random(3))

+++ practice | SLIP framing
--- task
SLIP is an older serial framing with different special bytes. `END` is `0xC0` and ends every packet. `ESC` is `0xDB`. Inside a packet, a data byte `0xC0` is sent as `0xDB 0xDC`, and a data byte `0xDB` is sent as `0xDB 0xDD`. Every other byte is sent unchanged.

Write two functions.

- `slip_encode(packet)` returns the encoded packet followed by one `END` byte.
- `slip_decode(data)` takes one encoded packet, which must end with its `END` byte, and returns the original packet. Raise `ValueError` if `data` does not end with `END`, if an `END` appears before the last byte, if an `ESC` is the last byte before `END`, or if an `ESC` is followed by anything other than `0xDC` or `0xDD`.

For example, `slip_encode(bytes([1, 0xC0, 0xDB]))` is `bytes([1, 0xDB, 0xDC, 0xDB, 0xDD, 0xC0])`.
--- starter
def slip_encode(packet):
    return packet + bytes([0xC0])


def slip_decode(data):
    return data[:-1]
--- solution
END = 0xC0
ESC = 0xDB
ESC_END = 0xDC
ESC_ESC = 0xDD


def slip_encode(packet):
    out = bytearray()
    for b in packet:
        if b == END:
            out.extend([ESC, ESC_END])
        elif b == ESC:
            out.extend([ESC, ESC_ESC])
        else:
            out.append(b)
    out.append(END)
    return bytes(out)


def slip_decode(data):
    if not data or data[-1] != END:
        raise ValueError("missing END")
    body = data[:-1]
    out = bytearray()
    i = 0
    while i < len(body):
        b = body[i]
        if b == END:
            raise ValueError("END inside a packet")
        if b == ESC:
            if i + 1 == len(body):
                raise ValueError("ESC at the end")
            nxt = body[i + 1]
            if nxt == ESC_END:
                out.append(END)
            elif nxt == ESC_ESC:
                out.append(ESC)
            else:
                raise ValueError("bad escape")
            i += 2
        else:
            out.append(b)
            i += 1
    return bytes(out)
--- hint
The encoder is the same shape as PPP stuffing, but the byte after the escape comes from a small table instead of an XOR: `0xC0` becomes `0xDB 0xDC`, and `0xDB` becomes `0xDB 0xDD`.
--- hint
The decoder first checks the last byte, then walks the rest with an index. Each rule in the task is one `raise`.
--- check case | The example from the task
slip_encode(bytes([1, 0xC0, 0xDB]))
=> bytes([1, 0xDB, 0xDC, 0xDB, 0xDD, 0xC0])
--- check case | An empty packet is one END
(slip_encode(b""), slip_decode(bytes([0xC0])))
=> (bytes([0xC0]), b"")
--- check case | Decoding undoes encoding for special bytes
slip_decode(slip_encode(bytes([0xDB, 0xDC, 0xC0, 0xDD, 0xDB])))
=> bytes([0xDB, 0xDC, 0xC0, 0xDD, 0xDB])
--- check test | Every malformed packet is rejected
all(raises(ValueError, lambda d=d: slip_decode(d)) for d in [b"", bytes([1, 2]), bytes([1, 0xC0, 2, 0xC0]), bytes([1, 0xDB, 0xC0]), bytes([0xDB, 0x01, 0xC0])])
--- check test | 256 byte values round trip
slip_decode(slip_encode(bytes(range(256)))) == bytes(range(256))

+++ practice | Debug: the escape that was not escaped
--- task
`stuff(payload)` should apply PPP byte stuffing: `0x7E` becomes `0x7D 0x5E`, `0x7D` becomes `0x7D 0x5D`, and other bytes stay. `unstuff` reverses it. Most payloads survive a round trip, but a payload holding the two bytes `0x7D 0x5E` comes back as the single byte `0x7E`. Find the bug and fix it. It is one line.
--- starter
def stuff(payload):
    out = bytearray()
    for b in payload:
        if b == 0x7E:
            out.append(0x7D)
            out.append(b ^ 0x20)
        else:
            out.append(b)
    return bytes(out)


def unstuff(body):
    out = bytearray()
    i = 0
    while i < len(body):
        if body[i] == 0x7D:
            out.append(body[i + 1] ^ 0x20)
            i += 2
        else:
            out.append(body[i])
            i += 1
    return bytes(out)
--- solution
def stuff(payload):
    out = bytearray()
    for b in payload:
        if b == 0x7E or b == 0x7D:
            out.append(0x7D)
            out.append(b ^ 0x20)
        else:
            out.append(b)
    return bytes(out)


def unstuff(body):
    out = bytearray()
    i = 0
    while i < len(body):
        if body[i] == 0x7D:
            out.append(body[i + 1] ^ 0x20)
            i += 2
        else:
            out.append(body[i])
            i += 1
    return bytes(out)
--- hint
Stuff `bytes([0x7D, 0x5E])` by hand with this code. What does the receiver make of the result?
--- hint
The receiver treats every `0x7D` as an escape. So a real `0x7D` in the payload must be escaped too.
--- check case | The payload from the task survives
unstuff(stuff(bytes([0x7D, 0x5E])))
=> bytes([0x7D, 0x5E])
--- check case | An escape byte is stuffed
stuff(bytes([0x7D]))
=> bytes([0x7D, 0x5D])
--- check case | The flag is still stuffed
stuff(bytes([1, 0x7E]))
=> bytes([1, 0x7D, 0x5E])
--- check test | Every pair of byte values round trips
all(unstuff(stuff(bytes([a, b]))) == bytes([a, b]) for a in range(256) for b in (0x5E, 0x5D, 0x7E, 0x7D, 0))

+++ practice | COBS: stuffing with a fixed worst case
--- task
Consistent Overhead Byte Stuffing (COBS) rewrites a packet so it contains no zero bytes; a single `0` can then mark the end of each frame. Encoding works in blocks:

- Split the packet at its zero bytes. Encode each piece as one **code byte** equal to (piece length + 1), followed by the piece's bytes. The zero that ended the piece is not written: the code byte stands for it.
- A piece longer than 254 bytes is cut after 254 bytes, and that block's code byte is 255, which means "254 data bytes and **no** zero after them".
- After the last zero there is always one more piece, possibly empty, so an empty packet encodes as `bytes([1])`, and a packet that is one zero encodes as `bytes([1, 1])`. The one exception: when the packet ends exactly after a 255 block, nothing more is written.

Decoding reverses it: read a code byte c, copy the next c − 1 bytes, and then output a zero, unless c is 255 or this was the last block.

Write `cobs_encode(packet)` and `cobs_decode(data)`. The encoded data never contains a zero byte, and it does not include the frame's final `0`. `cobs_decode` raises `ValueError` if `data` contains a zero byte anywhere (encoded data never does), or if a block runs past the end.

For example, `cobs_encode(bytes([0x11, 0x22, 0x00, 0x33]))` is `bytes([0x03, 0x11, 0x22, 0x02, 0x33])`.
--- starter
def cobs_encode(packet):
    return packet


def cobs_decode(data):
    return data
--- solution
def cobs_encode(packet):
    out = bytearray()
    block = bytearray()
    just_full = False
    for b in packet:
        if b == 0:
            out.append(len(block) + 1)
            out.extend(block)
            block = bytearray()
            just_full = False
        else:
            block.append(b)
            if len(block) == 254:
                out.append(255)
                out.extend(block)
                block = bytearray()
                just_full = True
    if block or not just_full:
        out.append(len(block) + 1)
        out.extend(block)
    return bytes(out)


def cobs_decode(data):
    if 0 in data:
        raise ValueError("encoded data never holds a zero")
    out = bytearray()
    i = 0
    while i < len(data):
        code = data[i]
        end = i + code
        if end > len(data):
            raise ValueError("block runs past the end")
        out.extend(data[i + 1:end])
        i = end
        if code != 255 and i < len(data):
            out.append(0)
    return bytes(out)
--- hint
Encode with a `bytearray` for the current block. On a zero, write the block's code byte (its length + 1) and its bytes, and start a new block. When a block reaches 254 bytes, write it with code 255 and start a new one. At the end, write the last block, even if it is empty, unless the packet ended exactly after a 255 block.
--- hint
Decode block by block: the code byte says how many bytes the block holds, including itself. After copying its data, add a zero if the code was not 255 and more data follows.
--- check case | The example from the task
cobs_encode(bytes([0x11, 0x22, 0x00, 0x33]))
=> bytes([0x03, 0x11, 0x22, 0x02, 0x33])
--- check case | Empty, one zero, two zeros
(cobs_encode(b""), cobs_encode(bytes([0])), cobs_encode(bytes([0, 0])))
=> (bytes([1]), bytes([1, 1]), bytes([1, 1, 1]))
--- check case | A zero at the end
cobs_encode(bytes([0x11, 0x00]))
=> bytes([0x02, 0x11, 0x01])
--- check test | 254 and 255 non-zero bytes
cobs_encode(bytes(range(1, 255))) == bytes([255]) + bytes(range(1, 255)) and cobs_encode(bytes(range(1, 255)) + bytes([0])) == bytes([255]) + bytes(range(1, 255)) + bytes([1, 1]) and cobs_encode(bytes(range(1, 256))) == bytes([255]) + bytes(range(1, 255)) + bytes([2, 255])
--- check test | Round trip, no zeros, and at most one extra byte per 254, for 300 random packets
(lambda R: all((lambda p: (lambda e: cobs_decode(e) == p and 0 not in e and len(e) <= len(p) + 1 + len(p) // 254)(cobs_encode(p)))(bytes(R.choice([0, 0, 1, 7, 255]) if R.random() < 0.5 else R.randint(1, 255) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 800)))) for _ in range(300)))(__import__("random").Random(9))
--- check test | Bad input is rejected
raises(ValueError, lambda: cobs_decode(bytes([3, 1]))) and raises(ValueError, lambda: cobs_decode(bytes([2, 0, 1])))

+++ question | The step behind "the flag never appears"
--- ask
The proof that a stuffed payload never contains 0x7E looks at every byte the stuffer can write. Which fact about the escape pair is the one that step needs?
--- choice
0x5E and 0x5D are both smaller than 0x7E.
--- choice correct
The byte written after an escape is 0x5E or 0x5D, and neither of those is 0x7E; the escape itself is 0x7D, also not 0x7E.
--- choice
The receiver checks a CRC, so a stray 0x7E would be caught anyway.
--- choice
XOR with 0x20 is its own inverse.
--- why
Claim 1 is a case check on the kinds of byte the stuffer writes: an unchanged byte (never 0x7E, since 0x7E is always replaced), the escape 0x7D, and the byte after an escape (0x5E or 0x5D). None is 0x7E. XOR being its own inverse is what Claim 2, the round trip, uses; it does not show the flag is absent.

+++ question | One flipped bit in a length
--- ask
Two links carry the same stream of frames. Link A frames them only with a 2-byte length in front of each frame. Link B uses PPP flags and byte stuffing. One bit of one frame's first byte is flipped by noise. What happens next?
--- choice
Both links lose exactly that one frame.
--- choice correct
Link A may lose every frame after it, because it reads later "lengths" from the wrong places; link B loses at most that frame and finds the next flag.
--- choice
Link B loses every later frame, because stuffing changes the lengths.
--- choice
Neither loses anything, because the length and the flags both detect errors.
--- why
A length-only link has no way back into step: once one length is wrong, every later boundary is computed from a wrong position. A flag is self-synchronising: the receiver drops the bad frame and the next 0x7E is a real boundary. Neither method detects that bits changed inside a frame; that is the next lesson's job.

+++ question | Worst-case growth
--- ask
A PPP frame's payload has n bytes. What is the largest the whole frame can be, flags included, and which payload reaches it?
--- choice
n + 2 bytes, for any payload.
--- choice correct
2n + 2 bytes, for a payload made only of 0x7E and 0x7D bytes.
--- choice
n + n/256 + 2 bytes, for random bytes.
--- choice
4n bytes, for a payload of zeros.
--- why
Each special byte becomes two bytes and each other byte stays one, so the stuffed payload is at most 2n, reached when every byte is special. The flags add 2. For random data the expected size is about n + 2n/256 + 2, but a buffer must be sized for the worst case.

=== net-02b | Error detection: parity, checksums and CRC
--- teach
Last lesson you found where frames start and end, even after noise damaged one. But noise does more than break boundaries: it flips bits in the middle of a frame, and the frame still arrives looking perfectly normal. This lesson is about **error detection**: adding a few extra bits to every frame so the receiver can tell, with very high probability, whether any bit changed on the way. You will build the method that Ethernet, Wi-Fi, USB, zip files and spacecraft links all use: the **cyclic redundancy check**, or CRC.

### The idea: a summary that travels with the data

Picture a shipping clerk who writes the number of boxes on the delivery note. The receiver counts the boxes and compares. If a box fell off the truck, the counts differ. The count is a tiny **summary** of the delivery, sent along with it.

Error detection works the same way. The sender computes a few **check bits** from the data, with an agreed function, and sends them with it. The receiver recomputes the function on the data it got and compares. If they differ, some bit changed, and the frame is thrown away. Getting it again is a job for a higher layer, as you will see in lessons 7, 8 and 8b.

No summary catches everything. If the data changes in a way that happens to give the same check bits, the error is **undetected**. So the question for every method is: which errors does it always catch, and how likely is it to miss the rest?

### Parity: one check bit

The simplest summary is one bit. **Even parity** chooses the check bit so that the total number of 1 bits, data and check bit together, is even.

```python
reading = 0b1101001
ones = bin(reading).count("1")
print(ones, ones % 2)      # 4 0: already even, so the parity bit is 0
```

**Why it catches every odd number of flipped bits:** each flip changes the count of 1s by exactly one, up or down, so it changes whether the count is even. An odd number of flips changes it an odd number of times, so an even count becomes odd, and the receiver sees it. An even number of flips changes it back again, so parity misses **every** two-bit error. Noise often comes in bursts that hit several neighbouring bits, so parity alone is weak.

### The Internet checksum

IP, UDP and TCP use a 16-bit **checksum**. Split the data into 16-bit big-endian words, add a zero byte at the end if the length is odd, and add up the words with **[[ones' complement addition|ones-complement]]**: whenever a sum goes past 16 bits, the carry is added back in at the bottom. The checksum is that sum with every bit flipped.

Here is the end-around carry on its own, for two words:

```python
a, b = 0xF00D, 0x2345
total = a + b
print(hex(total))                           # 0x11352: more than 16 bits
total = (total & 0xFFFF) + (total >> 16)    # add the carry back in
print(hex(total))                           # 0x1353
```

**Why the receiver's check works:** the receiver adds all the words **including** the checksum. If the data sum is s, the checksum is 0xFFFF − s (flipping all 16 bits is subtracting from 0xFFFF), and s + (0xFFFF − s) = 0xFFFF. So an undamaged packet always sums to 0xFFFF.

The checksum is cheap to compute in software, which is why the Internet's designers picked it. But it is weak in the same way any sum is: addition does not care about order, so two swapped words go unnoticed, and so do two errors that happen to cancel out.

### Bits as polynomials

A CRC treats a string of bits as a **polynomial**: the bits are its coefficients, highest power first. The bits `1011` are 1·x³ + 0·x² + 1·x + 1, written x³ + x + 1.

Arithmetic on these polynomials is done with each coefficient taken **mod 2**, as in Discrete Mathematics: 1 + 1 = 0, and there are no carries. So adding two polynomials is XOR of their bit strings, and subtracting is the same as adding, because −1 and 1 are the same mod 2.

```python
print(bin(0b1011 ^ 0b0110))   # 0b1101: (x³+x+1) + (x²+x) = x³+x²+1
```

Multiplying by x shifts every bit one place left. Multiplying by x to the power r shifts left by r, which is the same as appending r zero bits.

### The CRC

Sender and receiver agree on a **generator** polynomial G of degree r, whose top bit is always 1. The r-bit CRC of a message M is the **remainder** when M·x^r (the message with r zero bits appended) is divided by G. The sender transmits the message followed by the remainder.

Division works like the long division you know, with XOR instead of subtraction: wherever the leading bit is 1, XOR the generator in underneath it. Here is message `101101` with generator `1011` (x³ + x + 1, so r = 3):

```text
101101000      the message with 3 zeros appended
1011           leading bit is 1: XOR the generator
---------
000001000
     1011      next 1 is at position 5: XOR again
---------
000000011      the last 3 bits are the remainder: 011
```

The sender transmits `101101` followed by `011`: `101101011`.

### Why an undamaged frame divides exactly

Write the division out as an equation. Dividing M·x^r by G gives some quotient Q and the remainder R:

M·x^r = Q·G + R.

The transmitted bits are T = M·x^r + R. Substitute:

T = Q·G + R + R = Q·G,

because R + R = 0 when coefficients are taken mod 2. So T is a multiple of G, and the receiver, dividing everything it received by G, gets remainder 0 on an undamaged frame.

Now suppose the channel flips some bits. The flipped positions form an **error polynomial** E, and the receiver gets T + E. Its remainder is the remainder of E alone, since T contributes nothing. So:

**An error goes undetected exactly when G divides E.**

That one sentence gives every guarantee a CRC makes:

1. **Every single-bit error is caught,** when G has at least two terms. A single flip is E = x^i. Its only divisors are powers of x, and G = x^r + … + 1 is not a power of x.
2. **Every burst of length r or less is caught,** when G ends in + 1. A burst of length b is E = x^i·B(x), where B has degree b − 1 and ends in 1. G has no factor x, so G would have to divide B. But B has a smaller degree than G and is not zero, so it cannot.
3. **Every odd number of flips is caught,** when G is a multiple of x + 1. Put x = 1: E(1) is the number of flipped bits mod 2, which is 1. If G divided E, then x + 1 would divide E, and E(1) would be 0.

For longer, random damage, about one error pattern in 2^r slips through: one in 65,536 for a 16-bit CRC, and one in 4 billion for a 32-bit one.

### From long division to a register

Long division only ever uses the r bits just below the leading bit. So a program keeps them in an integer `reg` of r bits. One step of the division is: shift `reg` left one place, and if the bit that falls off the top was a 1, XOR in the generator's lower r bits. Those lower bits are written `poly`: the generator with its top term left out, since the top term is always there.

One such step, on a 4-bit register:

```python
width = 4
poly = 0b0011                        # x⁴ + x + 1, top term left out
mask = (1 << width) - 1              # 0b1111: keeps the register 4 bits wide
reg = 0b1010
fell_off = reg & (1 << (width - 1))  # the top bit, about to be shifted out
reg = (reg << 1) & mask
if fell_off:
    reg ^= poly
print(bin(reg))                      # 0b111
```
