+++ practice | One router's update
--- task
Write `update(me, links, vectors)`: the Bellman-Ford update for one router.

- `me` is this router's name.
- `links` is a dict from each neighbour's name to the cost of the link to it.
- `vectors` is a dict from each neighbour's name to the distance vector it just sent: a dict from destination to cost.

Return this router's new vector as a dict from destination to `(cost, next_hop)`. It holds `me` at `(0, me)`, and every destination any neighbour offers (other than `me`), with the smallest `link cost + offered cost`; on a tie, the neighbour whose name sorts first. A neighbour in `links` that sent no vector offers nothing.

For example, `update("Q", {"P": 2, "R": 1}, {"P": {"P": 0, "S": 9}, "R": {"R": 0, "S": 4}})` is `{"Q": (0, "Q"), "P": (2, "P"), "R": (1, "R"), "S": (5, "R")}`.
--- starter
def update(me, links, vectors):
    return {me: (0, me)}
--- solution
def update(me, links, vectors):
    best = {me: (0, me)}
    for v, cost in links.items():
        for dest, d in vectors.get(v, {}).items():
            if dest == me:
                continue
            offer = (cost + d, v)
            if dest not in best or offer < best[dest]:
                best[dest] = offer
    return best
--- hint
For each neighbour and each destination in its vector, the offer is `(link cost + its cost, neighbour)`. Use `vectors.get(v, {})` for a neighbour that sent nothing.
--- hint
Keep the smallest offer per destination. Comparing the tuples compares cost first, then the name, which is the tie rule. Skip offers for `me`.
--- check case | The example from the task
update("Q", {"P": 2, "R": 1}, {"P": {"P": 0, "S": 9}, "R": {"R": 0, "S": 4}})
=> {"Q": (0, "Q"), "P": (2, "P"), "R": (1, "R"), "S": (5, "R")}
--- check case | A neighbour's route back to me is ignored
update("A", {"B": 1}, {"B": {"B": 0, "A": 1, "C": 4}})
=> {"A": (0, "A"), "B": (1, "B"), "C": (5, "B")}
--- check case | A tie goes to the name that sorts first
update("S", {"N": 3, "M": 1}, {"N": {"T": 1}, "M": {"T": 3}})
=> {"S": (0, "S"), "T": (4, "M")}
--- check case | No neighbours, or a silent neighbour
(update("Z", {}, {}), update("Z", {"Y": 1}, {}))
=> ({"Z": (0, "Z")}, {"Z": (0, "Z")})
--- check case | A direct link can lose to a detour
update("P", {"Q": 2, "S": 9}, {"Q": {"Q": 0, "S": 5}, "S": {"S": 0}})
=> {"P": (0, "P"), "Q": (2, "Q"), "S": (7, "Q")}

+++ practice | Following the next hops
--- task
Once the tables have converged, a packet travels by next hops: each router sends it to its next hop for the destination, until it arrives.

Write `route(tables, src, dst, ttl)`. `tables` maps each router to its vector (destination → `(cost, next_hop)`). Starting at `src` with the packet's TTL, repeat: if the current router is `dst`, stop; if it has no entry for `dst`, stop; if the TTL is 0, stop; otherwise subtract 1 from the TTL and move to the next hop.

Return a tuple `(status, path)`. `path` lists every router the packet visited, starting with `src`. `status` is `"delivered"` if it reached `dst`, `"no route"` if a router had no entry for `dst`, and `"ttl expired"` if the TTL ran out first.

For example, with the converged tables of the line A–B–C, `route(tables, "A", "C", 64)` is `("delivered", ["A", "B", "C"])`.
--- starter
def route(tables, src, dst, ttl):
    return ("delivered", [src, dst])
--- solution
def route(tables, src, dst, ttl):
    path = [src]
    here = src
    while here != dst:
        if dst not in tables[here]:
            return ("no route", path)
        if ttl == 0:
            return ("ttl expired", path)
        ttl -= 1
        here = tables[here][dst][1]
        path.append(here)
    return ("delivered", path)
--- hint
Loop while the current router is not `dst`. Check the two ways to stop (no entry, TTL 0) before moving.
--- hint
Moving is: subtract 1 from the TTL, set the current router to `tables[here][dst][1]`, and append it to the path.
--- check case | The example from the task
route({"A": {"A": (0, "A"), "B": (1, "B"), "C": (2, "B")}, "B": {"B": (0, "B"), "A": (1, "A"), "C": (1, "C")}, "C": {"C": (0, "C"), "B": (1, "B"), "A": (2, "B")}}, "A", "C", 64)
=> ("delivered", ["A", "B", "C"])
--- check case | Source and destination are the same router
route({"A": {"A": (0, "A")}}, "A", "A", 0)
=> ("delivered", ["A"])
--- check case | No route, found partway
route({"A": {"A": (0, "A"), "D": (2, "B")}, "B": {"B": (0, "B")}}, "A", "D", 9)
=> ("no route", ["A", "B"])
--- check case | A loop left by count to infinity: the TTL ends it
route({"P": {"P": (0, "P"), "R": (4, "Q")}, "Q": {"Q": (0, "Q"), "R": (3, "P")}}, "P", "R", 5)
=> ("ttl expired", ["P", "Q", "P", "Q", "P", "Q"])
--- check case | TTL exactly enough, and one short
(route({"A": {"C": (2, "B")}, "B": {"C": (1, "C")}, "C": {}}, "A", "C", 2)[0], route({"A": {"C": (2, "B")}, "B": {"C": (1, "C")}, "C": {}}, "A", "C", 1))
=> ("delivered", ("ttl expired", ["A", "B"]))

+++ practice | Counting to infinity
--- task
Watch count to infinity happen. Write `after_failure(nodes, links, broken, infinity)`:

1. Converge the network on `links`, starting from each router knowing only itself (as in the lesson's task).
2. Remove every link between the two routers named in the pair `broken`.
3. Run synchronous rounds on the remaining links until a round changes nothing, where an offer whose cost is **at least** `infinity` counts as unreachable and is not kept.

Return a tuple `(tables, rounds)`: the final tables, and how many rounds after the failure changed something. Use the same rules as the lesson's `dv_round` (fresh vector each round, `(cost, next_hop)` entries, smallest tuple wins).

For example, on the line A–B–C with both costs 1, breaking B–C with infinity 16 takes 15 rounds, and ends with A and B each knowing only each other, and C knowing only itself.
--- starter
def after_failure(nodes, links, broken, infinity):
    return {}, 0
--- solution
def neighbours(links):
    adj = {}
    for a, b, cost in links:
        for x, y in ((a, b), (b, a)):
            adj.setdefault(x, {})
            if y not in adj[x] or cost < adj[x][y]:
                adj[x][y] = cost
    return adj


def one_round(tables, links, infinity):
    adj = neighbours(links)
    new = {}
    for x in tables:
        vector = {x: (0, x)}
        for v, cost in adj.get(x, {}).items():
            for y, (d, _) in tables[v].items():
                offer = (cost + d, v)
                if y == x or offer[0] >= infinity:
                    continue
                if y not in vector or offer < vector[y]:
                    vector[y] = offer
        new[x] = vector
    return new


def settle(tables, links, infinity):
    rounds = 0
    while True:
        new = one_round(tables, links, infinity)
        if new == tables:
            return tables, rounds
        tables = new
        rounds += 1


def after_failure(nodes, links, broken, infinity):
    tables, _ = settle({x: {x: (0, x)} for x in nodes}, links, infinity)
    rest = [link for link in links if {link[0], link[1]} != set(broken)]
    return settle(tables, rest, infinity)
--- hint
Reuse the round from the lesson's task, with one more rule: skip an offer whose cost is at least `infinity`. Write a helper that runs rounds until nothing changes and counts the rounds that changed something.
--- hint
Converge first (with the same helper), then filter the links with `{a, b} != set(broken)`, and run the helper again from the converged tables.
--- check case | The line from the task
after_failure(["A", "B", "C"], [("A", "B", 1), ("B", "C", 1)], ("B", "C"), 16)
=> ({"A": {"A": (0, "A"), "B": (1, "B")}, "B": {"B": (0, "B"), "A": (1, "A")}, "C": {"C": (0, "C")}}, 15)
--- check case | A smaller infinity stops the counting sooner
after_failure(["A", "B", "C"], [("A", "B", 1), ("B", "C", 1)], ("B", "C"), 6)[1]
=> 5
--- check case | With a backup path, the count stops at the backup's cost
after_failure(["A", "B", "C"], [("A", "B", 1), ("B", "C", 1), ("A", "C", 10)], ("B", "C"), 16)
=> ({"A": {"A": (0, "A"), "B": (1, "B"), "C": (10, "C")}, "B": {"B": (0, "B"), "A": (1, "A"), "C": (11, "A")}, "C": {"C": (0, "C"), "A": (10, "A"), "B": (11, "A")}}, 10)
--- check case | Breaking a link nobody used changes nothing
after_failure(["A", "B", "C"], [("A", "B", 1), ("B", "C", 1), ("A", "C", 10)], ("A", "C"), 16)[1]
=> 0
--- check case | The broken pair's parallel links all go
after_failure(["A", "B"], [("A", "B", 1), ("B", "A", 2)], ("A", "B"), 16)
=> ({"A": {"A": (0, "A")}, "B": {"B": (0, "B")}}, 1)

+++ practice | Debug: costs that never go up
--- task
`dv_round(tables, links)` should compute every router's new vector from scratch, from its neighbours' old vectors (links are `(a, b, cost)` tuples; vectors map destination to `(cost, next_hop)`). This version passes every test that builds routes up from nothing, but after a link's cost is raised from 1 to 50, routers keep using the old cheap cost forever. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def dv_round(tables, links):
    adj = {}
    for a, b, cost in links:
        adj.setdefault(a, {})[b] = cost
        adj.setdefault(b, {})[a] = cost
    new = {}
    for x in tables:
        vector = dict(tables[x])
        vector[x] = (0, x)
        for v, cost in adj.get(x, {}).items():
            for y, (d, _) in tables[v].items():
                if y == x:
                    continue
                offer = (cost + d, v)
                if y not in vector or offer < vector[y]:
                    vector[y] = offer
        new[x] = vector
    return new
--- solution
def dv_round(tables, links):
    adj = {}
    for a, b, cost in links:
        adj.setdefault(a, {})[b] = cost
        adj.setdefault(b, {})[a] = cost
    new = {}
    for x in tables:
        vector = {}
        vector[x] = (0, x)
        for v, cost in adj.get(x, {}).items():
            for y, (d, _) in tables[v].items():
                if y == x:
                    continue
                offer = (cost + d, v)
                if y not in vector or offer < vector[y]:
                    vector[y] = offer
        new[x] = vector
    return new
--- hint
Which vector does each router's new vector start from? What happens to an entry whose route just got more expensive?
--- hint
Starting from the old vector means an old entry survives unless something beats it, so a cost can only go down. Start each new vector empty, apart from the router itself.

+++ practice | Poisoned reverse
--- task
Add **split horizon with poisoned reverse** to the synchronous simulation: when router v sends its vector to neighbour x, it leaves out (reports as unreachable) every destination whose next hop in v's vector is x. Everything else is as in the lesson's `dv_round`: fresh vectors each round, `(cost, next_hop)` entries, the smallest tuple wins, and offers of cost at least `infinity` are not kept.

Write `after_failure_poisoned(nodes, links, broken, infinity)`, which does the same three steps as before (converge from scratch, remove every link between the pair `broken`, run rounds until nothing changes), but with poisoned reverse in **every** round, including while converging. Return `(tables, rounds)`, counting only the rounds after the failure that changed something.

For example, on the line A–B–C with both costs 1, breaking B–C with infinity 16 now takes only 2 rounds, instead of 15.
--- starter
def after_failure_poisoned(nodes, links, broken, infinity):
    return {}, 0
--- solution
def neighbours(links):
    adj = {}
    for a, b, cost in links:
        for x, y in ((a, b), (b, a)):
            adj.setdefault(x, {})
            if y not in adj[x] or cost < adj[x][y]:
                adj[x][y] = cost
    return adj


def one_round(tables, links, infinity):
    adj = neighbours(links)
    new = {}
    for x in tables:
        vector = {x: (0, x)}
        for v, cost in adj.get(x, {}).items():
            for y, (d, hop) in tables[v].items():
                if y == x or hop == x:
                    continue
                offer = (cost + d, v)
                if offer[0] >= infinity:
                    continue
                if y not in vector or offer < vector[y]:
                    vector[y] = offer
        new[x] = vector
    return new


def settle(tables, links, infinity):
    rounds = 0
    while True:
        new = one_round(tables, links, infinity)
        if new == tables:
            return tables, rounds
        tables = new
        rounds += 1


def after_failure_poisoned(nodes, links, broken, infinity):
    tables, _ = settle({x: {x: (0, x)} for x in nodes}, links, infinity)
    rest = [link for link in links if {link[0], link[1]} != set(broken)]
    return settle(tables, rest, infinity)
--- hint
Poisoning happens on the receiving side of the simulation just as well: when router x reads neighbour v's old vector, skip every entry whose next hop is x itself.
--- hint
That is one extra condition in the inner loop: `if y == x or hop == x: continue`. The rest is the same as the count-to-infinity problem.
--- check case | The line from the task
after_failure_poisoned(["A", "B", "C"], [("A", "B", 1), ("B", "C", 1)], ("B", "C"), 16)
=> ({"A": {"A": (0, "A"), "B": (1, "B")}, "B": {"B": (0, "B"), "A": (1, "A")}, "C": {"C": (0, "C")}}, 2)
--- check case | A longer line: the bad news walks down it one router per round
after_failure_poisoned(["A", "B", "C", "D", "E"], [("A", "B", 1), ("B", "C", 1), ("C", "D", 1), ("D", "E", 1)], ("D", "E"), 16)[1]
=> 4
--- check case | With a backup path, the switch happens quickly
after_failure_poisoned(["A", "B", "C"], [("A", "B", 1), ("B", "C", 1), ("A", "C", 10)], ("B", "C"), 16)
=> ({"A": {"A": (0, "A"), "B": (1, "B"), "C": (10, "C")}, "B": {"B": (0, "B"), "A": (1, "A"), "C": (11, "A")}, "C": {"C": (0, "C"), "A": (10, "A"), "B": (11, "A")}}, 3)

(lambda R, reduce: all((lambda nodes: (lambda links: (lambda t, d: all({y: c for y, (c, _) in t[x].items()} == {y: c for y, c in d[x].items() if c < 10**9} for x in nodes))(after_failure_poisoned(nodes, links, ("nobody1", "nobody2"), 10**6)[0], reduce(lambda d, k: {i: {j: min(d[i][j], d[i][k] + d[k][j]) for j in nodes} for i in nodes}, nodes, {i: {j: 0 if i == j else min([c for a, b, c in links if {a, b} == {i, j}] or [10**9]) for j in nodes} for i in nodes})))([(nodes[i], nodes[R.randrange(i)], R.randint(1, 9)) for i in range(1, len(nodes))] + [tuple(R.sample(nodes, 2)) + (R.randint(1, 9),) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 6))]))([chr(65 + i) for i in range(R.randint(2, 8))]) for _ in range(20)))(__import__("random").Random(21), __import__("functools").reduce)

+++ practice | How long until the network agrees
--- task
A ground-station network wants to know how many synchronous rounds its distance-vector routing needs after a restart, when every router starts knowing only itself, **without** running the simulation.

Three facts about the lesson's rules give the answer.

- By the lesson's invariant, router v's **cost** to y is final after h(v, y) rounds, where h(v, y) is the fewest links on any cheapest path from v to y (and h(y, y) = 0).
- The final next hop from x to y is, among the neighbours v with `cost(x, v) + dist(v, y) == dist(x, y)`, the one whose name sorts first.
- x's entry for y becomes final exactly one round after that next hop's **cost** to y becomes final.

So the number of rounds that change something is the largest value of 1 + h(v, y), where v is x's final next hop for y, over all pairs x ≠ y that can reach each other. It is 0 if no router can reach another.

Write `rounds_needed(nodes, links)` that computes it. `links` are undirected `(a, b, cost)` tuples with positive costs; for parallel links only the cheapest counts. Run Dijkstra's algorithm from every router with the pair `(total cost, number of links)` as the distance, so it finds both dist and h at once.

For example, with links `[("W", "X", 1), ("X", "Y", 1), ("Y", "Z", 1), ("W", "Z", 10)]`, W's final next hop for Z is X, and h(X, Z) is 2, so that pair needs 3 rounds. No pair needs more, so the answer is `3`.
--- starter
def rounds_needed(nodes, links):
    return len(nodes) - 1
--- solution
import heapq


def rounds_needed(nodes, links):
    adj = {x: {} for x in nodes}
    for a, b, cost in links:
        for x, y in ((a, b), (b, a)):
            if y not in adj[x] or cost < adj[x][y]:
                adj[x][y] = cost
    best = {}
    for src in nodes:
        found = {src: (0, 0)}
        heap = [(0, 0, src)]
        while heap:
            d, hops, u = heapq.heappop(heap)
            if (d, hops) > found[u]:
                continue
            for v, cost in adj[u].items():
                cand = (d + cost, hops + 1)
                if v not in found or cand < found[v]:
                    found[v] = cand
                    heapq.heappush(heap, (cand[0], cand[1], v))
        best[src] = found
    worst = 0
    for x in nodes:
        for y, (dist, _) in best[x].items():
            if y == x:
                continue
            hop = min(v for v in adj[x] if y in best[v] and adj[x][v] + best[v][y][0] == dist)
            worst = max(worst, 1 + best[hop][y][1])
    return worst
--- hint
Dijkstra works for any distance that adds up along a path and compares consistently. With `(cost, links)` pairs, it settles on the cheapest cost and, among the cheapest paths, the fewest links: that is dist and h together.
--- hint
For each pair, the next hop is `min(v for v in adj[x] if ...)` over the neighbours on a cheapest path; `min` on names picks the one that sorts first. The pair needs `1 + h(next hop, y)` rounds.
--- check case | The example from the task
rounds_needed(["W", "X", "Y", "Z"], [("W", "X", 1), ("X", "Y", 1), ("Y", "Z", 1), ("W", "Z", 10)])
=> 3
--- check case | Equal costs: the name decides, even when it means more links
(rounds_needed(["A", "B", "C"], [("A", "B", 1), ("B", "C", 1), ("A", "C", 2)]), rounds_needed(["A", "B", "Z"], [("A", "Z", 1), ("Z", "B", 1), ("A", "B", 2)]))
=> (2, 1)
--- check case | No links, and one link
(rounds_needed(["A", "B"], []), rounds_needed(["A", "B"], [("A", "B", 4)]))
=> (0, 1)

(lambda R: all((lambda nodes: (lambda links: rounds_needed(nodes, links) == (lambda sim: sim(sim, {x: {x: (0, x)} for x in nodes}, 0))(lambda self, t, r: (lambda n: r if n == t else self(self, n, r + 1))({x: (lambda vec: vec)(dict([(x, (0, x))] + sorted(((y, (c + d, v)) for a, b, c in links for (u, v) in ((a, b), (b, a)) if u == x for y, (d, _) in t[v].items() if y != x), key=lambda kv: kv[1], reverse=True))) for x in nodes})))([(nodes[i], nodes[R.randrange(i)], R.randint(1, 5)) for i in range(1, len(nodes))] + [tuple(R.sample(nodes, 2)) + (R.randint(1, 5),) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 6))]))([chr(65 + i) for i in range(R.randint(2, 8))]) for _ in range(25)))(__import__("random").Random(13))

+++ question | The induction step
--- ask
The proof that "after k rounds, D_x(y) is the cheapest cost using at most k links" has an induction step. It takes a cheapest (at most k + 1)-link path from x to y, with first hop v. What does it use about the rest of the path?
--- choice
The rest has at most k + 1 links, so after k + 1 rounds v knows its cost.
--- choice correct
The rest goes from v to y with at most k links, so by the assumption for round k, v's estimate D_v(y) is at most its cost, and some path of that estimate's cost exists.
--- choice
The rest is the same for every router, so all routers agree on it.
--- choice
The rest has no loops, so its cost is positive.
--- why
Round k + 1 computes min over v of c(x, v) + D_v(y) using the vectors from round k. The induction hypothesis says those vectors are exactly the cheapest (at most k)-link costs. Splitting the path into its first link plus a k-link rest is what connects round k to round k + 1, just as in the Bellman-Ford proof.

+++ question | Why bad news travels slowly
--- ask
On the line P–Q–R, the link Q–R breaks, and Q then picks a route to R through P at cost 3. Why does Q believe P can still reach R?
--- choice
P's link to R is still working.
--- choice correct
P's vector only says "R: 2", not that its route goes through Q, so Q cannot tell that P's route depends on the link that just broke.
--- choice
Q forgot to include itself at cost 0.
--- choice
The costs are too small to notice the change.
--- why
A distance vector carries costs, not paths. P's "2" was built on Q's old "1", but nothing in the message says so, and Q happily builds on it, creating a loop that counts upward. Path-vector protocols such as BGP fix this by sending the path, and poisoned reverse fixes the two-router case by having P report infinity to Q.

+++ question | Choosing infinity
--- ask
RIP uses hop counts as costs and treats 16 as infinity. What does that choice trade off?
--- choice
A bigger infinity would make count to infinity end sooner.
--- choice correct
A small infinity ends count to infinity quickly, but no path may be longer than 15 hops, so RIP only suits small networks.
--- choice
16 is the largest number that fits in the packet header.
--- choice
Infinity must be a power of two for the Bellman-Ford equation to work.
--- why
After a failure, the loop counts up until it reaches infinity, so the number of rounds grows with infinity. But any real path costing 16 or more would also be called unreachable. RIP picked 16 to bound the counting at a few minutes of 30-second updates, and accepted the limit on network size.

=== net-05b | Routing II: link-state routing
--- teach
Last lesson each router learned only distances, from its neighbours, and you saw how that can go wrong: a router cannot tell that a neighbour's route runs back through itself, so bad news counts slowly up to infinity. This lesson takes the opposite approach. Every router learns the **whole map** of the network, and then computes its own routes with Dijkstra's algorithm from DSA II. This is **link-state routing**, and it is what runs inside most large networks today.

### Everyone draws the same map

Picture a group of hikers spread across a mountain, each able to see only the trails right next to them. Each one writes a card, "I am at the hut; there is a 2 km trail to the lake and a 5 km trail to the pass", and passes copies to everyone they can reach, who pass them on. Soon everyone holds every card, lays them out, and has the same complete map. Each hiker then finds their own shortest route anywhere, with no more talking.

That is link-state routing in two steps: **spread the map**, then **compute locally**.

### Link-state advertisements

Each router describes only what it knows first-hand: its own links. That description is a **link-state advertisement** (LSA):

- the **origin**: the router it describes;
- a **sequence number**, which the origin increases every time it sends a new version;
- the origin's **links**: each neighbour and the cost of the link to it.

Every router keeps the newest LSA from every origin in its **link-state database** (LSDB). Together, those LSAs are the graph of the whole network.

Here is a small LSDB, written as a dict from each origin to its links:

```python
lsdb = {
    "K": {"L": 2, "M": 5},
    "L": {"K": 2, "M": 2, "N": 6},
    "M": {"K": 5, "L": 2, "N": 1},
    "N": {"L": 6, "M": 1},
}
print(sorted(lsdb["M"]))     # ['K', 'L', 'N']: M's own view of its neighbours
```

### Flooding

To get every LSA to every router, routers **flood** it. When a router receives an LSA:

1. If its sequence number is **higher** than the one stored for that origin (or none is stored), it stores the LSA and sends a copy to every neighbour **except** the one it came from.
2. Otherwise it is old news, a copy already seen: discard it.

The origin starts the flood by storing its own new LSA and sending it to all its neighbours.

**Why flooding stops.** Each router forwards a given LSA at most once, the first time it sees that sequence number; after that, every copy is discarded. So each link carries the LSA at most once in each direction: at most 2E messages for E links.

**Why it reaches everyone.** Take any router R connected to the origin by a path. By induction along the path: the origin stores the LSA. If a router on the path stores it, it sends it to all neighbours except the sender, and the next router on the path either is the sender (so it already has it) or receives it and stores it (or already had it). Either way the next router ends up with it, and so does R.

The [[sequence number|sequence-numbers]] is what makes both arguments work. Without it, a router could not tell a new LSA from a copy of one it has already forwarded, and flooding in a network with loops would never stop, like the broadcast storm in the switching lesson.

### The two-way check

An LSA is one router's claim. A link is only used if **both** ends report it: u's LSA lists v, **and** v's LSA lists u. That stops a router from routing over a link that works in only one direction, or that the other end has already declared dead. The cost of travelling from u to v is the cost in u's LSA.

### Computing routes: Dijkstra with first hops

With the full graph, each router runs Dijkstra's algorithm with itself as the source. What it needs from the result is not the path, only the **first hop** toward each destination: that goes in the forwarding table.

Track the first hop along with the distance:

- a neighbour v reached straight from the source has first hop v;
- any other router reached from u **inherits** u's first hop.

Here is the run from K on the LSDB above. Each row is one router popped from the heap, with the labels that change:

| popped | its label | relaxes to |
|---|---|---|
| K | 0 | L: 2 via L, M: 5 via M |
| L | 2 via L | M: 2 + 2 = 4 via L (better than 5), N: 2 + 6 = 8 via L |
| M | 4 via L | N: 4 + 1 = 5 via L (better than 8) |
| N | 5 via L | nothing better |

So K's forwarding table sends everything to L: M costs 4 and N costs 5, both via L. The direct link to M (cost 5) is not used.

When two paths cost the same, the protocol needs a fixed rule so that the result does not depend on the order things were added. In this lesson the rule is: the first hop whose name sorts first wins. Dijkstra handles this if its labels are the pairs `(cost, first hop)`, compared as tuples. That still works because adding a link's positive cost to two pairs keeps their order.

### Why the routes never loop

Suppose every router has the **same** LSDB and uses the same rule. Router x sends a packet for y to its first hop n, which lies on a cheapest path from x to y. So

dist(n, y) = dist(x, y) − cost(x, n) < dist(x, y),

because link costs are positive. Router n does the same with its own first hop. The distance to y goes down strictly at every hop, so no router can be visited twice, and since there are finitely many routers, the packet reaches y.

The condition "the same LSDB" matters. While a new LSA is still being flooded, some routers have the new map and some the old one, and for a moment two of them can point at each other. Those short-lived **microloops** last only until flooding finishes, usually a fraction of a second; the TTL from the IP lesson cleans up any packet caught in one.

### What it costs

- **Flooding:** one LSA crosses each link at most twice, so a full refresh, every router sending one LSA, costs O(N·E) messages for N routers and E links.
- **Memory:** the whole LSDB, O(N + E).
- **Computing:** Dijkstra with a heap, O(E log N) on every router, every time the map changes.

Compared with distance vector:

| | distance vector | link state |
|---|---|---|
| each router knows | distances from its neighbours | the whole graph |
| after a failure | can count to infinity | re-floods one LSA, recomputes |
| work per router | small | a Dijkstra per change |
| a router with bad data | its wrong distances spread | only its own links are wrong |

To keep the LSDB and the Dijkstra runs small in a very large network, it is split into **[[areas|areas]]**, each with its own map.

### Where it is used

- **OSPF** (Open Shortest Path First) runs inside most enterprise and campus networks. Its default cost for a link is 100 Mb/s divided by the link's speed, so faster links look cheaper.
- **IS-IS** does the same job inside most large ISPs and many data centres.
- The same idea, a shared map and local computation, is used for [[satellite constellations|constellations]], where every satellite can predict the map from orbital data.

**Watch out:** in the Dijkstra, the first hop is the router right after the **source**, not the router right before the destination. Code that records the parent `u` as the first hop of `v` is right for neighbours of the source and for two-link paths, and wrong for everything further away. Carry the first hop forward from `u` instead.

::: context sequence-numbers What happens when the counter runs out
A sequence number is stored in a fixed number of bits, 32 in OSPF, so it cannot grow forever. OSPF starts at 0x80000001, and when a router reaches the top, it first flushes its old LSA from every database by flooding it with the maximum age, then starts again from the bottom. Every LSA also carries an age that counts up, and an LSA that is not refreshed within an hour is deleted, so a router that dies silently eventually disappears from everyone's map. The reliable transport lessons return to sequence numbers that wrap around.
:::

::: context areas Keeping the map small
OSPF divides a big network into areas. Routers inside an area flood full LSAs and know that area's map in detail; the border routers between areas pass on only summaries, "these prefixes are reachable through me at this cost", which is distance-vector style. Every area connects to a central backbone, area 0, so summaries cannot loop. This keeps each router's LSDB and each Dijkstra run small, at the price of slightly less precise routes between areas.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <ellipse cx="180" cy="40" rx="70" ry="25" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="180" y="44" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">area 0 (backbone)</text>
  <ellipse cx="70" cy="100" rx="55" ry="22" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="70" y="104" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">area 1</text>
  <ellipse cx="290" cy="100" rx="55" ry="22" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="290" y="104" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">area 2</text>
  <circle cx="118" cy="62" r="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="242" cy="62" r="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="122" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">orange: border routers pass on summaries only</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context constellations Routing among moving satellites
In a constellation of low Earth orbit satellites joined by laser links, the network's shape changes every few minutes as satellites move and ground stations come into and out of view. But the motion is predictable: from the orbits, every satellite can compute the future link map ahead of time. So instead of flooding LSAs to discover changes, routes can be planned in advance for each moment, and flooding is kept for surprises, such as a failed laser terminal. NASA's delay-tolerant networking work uses a similar idea, called contact graph routing, for deep-space links that exist only at scheduled times.
:::
--- task
Write `forwarding_table(lsdb, me)`, the route computation of one router in a link-state protocol.

- `lsdb` maps every router that has sent an LSA to its links: a dict from neighbour to cost (positive).
- A link between u and v is used only if `lsdb[u]` lists v **and** `lsdb[v]` lists u. Travelling from u to v costs `lsdb[u][v]`. A router with no LSA in `lsdb` cannot be used.
- Run Dijkstra's algorithm from `me` with a heap. Return a dict mapping every router reachable from `me` to a tuple `(cost, first_hop)`, including `me` itself as `(0, me)`. The first hop is the neighbour of `me` that the path starts with.
- When two paths to a router cost the same, keep the one whose first hop's name sorts first. Compare `(cost, first_hop)` tuples.

It must be fast: a check runs it on a network of 20,000 routers.

For example, with the lesson's LSDB (`"K": {"L": 2, "M": 5}`, `"L": {"K": 2, "M": 2, "N": 6}`, `"M": {"K": 5, "L": 2, "N": 1}`, `"N": {"L": 6, "M": 1}`), `forwarding_table(lsdb, "K")` is `{"K": (0, "K"), "L": (2, "L"), "M": (4, "L"), "N": (5, "L")}`.
--- starter
def forwarding_table(lsdb, me):
    return {me: (0, me)}
--- solution
import heapq


def forwarding_table(lsdb, me):
    best = {me: (0, me)}
    heap = [(0, me, me)]
    done = set()
    while heap:
        cost, first, u = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if u in done:
            continue
        done.add(u)
        for v, c in lsdb.get(u, {}).items():
            if v not in lsdb or u not in lsdb[v]:
                continue
            label = (cost + c, v if u == me else first)
            if v not in best or label < best[v]:
                best[v] = label
                heapq.heappush(heap, (label[0], label[1], v))
    return best
--- hint
Use a heap of `(cost, first_hop, router)` entries, starting with `(0, me, me)`. Pop the smallest, skip it if that router is already done, and relax its links.
--- hint
Skip a link u → v unless `v` has an LSA that lists `u`. The new label for `v` is `(cost + c, v)` when `u` is `me`, and `(cost + c, first)` otherwise: the first hop is inherited.
--- hint
Replace `best[v]` when the new label tuple is smaller, and push it on the heap. Tuples compare the cost first, then the first hop's name, which is the tie rule.
--- check case | The example from the task

=> {"K": (0, "K"), "L": (2, "L"), "M": (4, "L"), "N": (5, "L")}

=> (2, "X")

+++ practice | The graph behind the database
--- task
Write `two_way_graph(lsdb)`. `lsdb` maps each router to its LSA's links (a dict from neighbour to cost). Return the graph the routers will actually use: a dict mapping **every** router in `lsdb` to a dict of its usable links, where a link from u to v is usable only if `lsdb[u]` lists v and `v` has an LSA that lists u. Keep u's own cost for the direction from u. Do not change `lsdb`.

For example, `two_way_graph({"A": {"B": 1, "C": 2}, "B": {"A": 3}, "C": {}})` is `{"A": {"B": 1}, "B": {"A": 3}, "C": {}}`: C does not list A, so the link A–C is not used.
--- starter
def two_way_graph(lsdb):
    return lsdb
--- solution
def two_way_graph(lsdb):
    return {u: {v: c for v, c in links.items() if v in lsdb and u in lsdb[v]} for u, links in lsdb.items()}
--- hint
Build a new dict of dicts. For each router `u` and each `(v, c)` in its links, keep the link only if `v in lsdb and u in lsdb[v]`.
--- hint
A dict comprehension inside a dict comprehension does it in one expression, and it never touches the original.
--- check case | The example from the task
two_way_graph({"A": {"B": 1, "C": 2}, "B": {"A": 3}, "C": {}})
=> {"A": {"B": 1}, "B": {"A": 3}, "C": {}}
--- check case | A neighbour with no LSA at all

=> {"A": {"B": 1}, "B": {"A": 1}}
--- check case | Empty database
two_way_graph({})
=> {}
--- check test | The input is not changed

+++ practice | Flooding one LSA
--- task
Simulate the flooding of one new LSA. Write `flood(links, origin, seq, stored)`.

- `links` is a list of `(a, b)` pairs: undirected links between routers.
- `stored` maps a router to the highest sequence number it has stored for this origin; a router not in `stored` has none. Do not change it.
- The origin first stores sequence number `seq` itself, if `seq` is higher than what it has, and then sends a copy to every neighbour. If it already has `seq` or higher, nothing happens.
- Copies are handled in the order they were sent (first in, first out). A router receiving a copy stores it and sends a copy to every neighbour **except** the sender, if `seq` is higher than what it has stored; otherwise it discards the copy.
- Neighbours are sent to in sorted order of their names.

Return a tuple `(messages, installed)`: the total number of copies sent, and the sorted list of routers that stored the new LSA (the origin included).

For example, on the triangle `[("A", "B"), ("B", "C"), ("C", "A")]`, `flood(links, "A", 5, {})` is `(4, ["A", "B", "C"])`: A sends to B and C, B sends to C, C sends to B, and the last two copies are discarded.
--- starter
def flood(links, origin, seq, stored):
    return (0, [origin])
--- solution
from collections import deque


def flood(links, origin, seq, stored):
    neighbours = {}
    for a, b in links:
        neighbours.setdefault(a, set()).add(b)
        neighbours.setdefault(b, set()).add(a)
    have = dict(stored)
    if have.get(origin, -1) >= seq:
        return (0, [])
    have[origin] = seq
    installed = [origin]
    queue = deque((origin, n) for n in sorted(neighbours.get(origin, ())))
    messages = len(queue)
    while queue:
        sender, router = queue.popleft()
        if have.get(router, -1) >= seq:
            continue
        have[router] = seq
        installed.append(router)
        for n in sorted(neighbours[router]):
            if n != sender:
                queue.append((router, n))
                messages += 1
    return (messages, sorted(installed))
--- hint
Build a dict from each router to the set of its neighbours, and copy `stored` so you can change the copy. A queue of `(sender, receiver)` pairs (a `collections.deque`) holds the copies in flight.
--- hint
Count a message every time you put a copy in the queue. When a copy arrives, compare `seq` with `have.get(router, -1)`: store and pass it on only if it is newer.
--- check case | The example from the task
flood([("A", "B"), ("B", "C"), ("C", "A")], "A", 5, {})
=> (4, ["A", "B", "C"])
--- check case | A line: one copy per link, and each end stops it
flood([("A", "B"), ("B", "C"), ("C", "D")], "B", 1, {})
=> (3, ["A", "B", "C", "D"])
--- check case | Routers that already have this or a newer LSA stop the flood
flood([("A", "B"), ("B", "C"), ("C", "D")], "A", 7, {"C": 7})
=> (2, ["A", "B"])
--- check case | The origin already has it: nothing is sent
flood([("A", "B")], "A", 3, {"A": 3})
=> (0, [])
--- check case | A router with no links
flood([("B", "C")], "A", 1, {})
=> (0, ["A"])
--- check test | On 50 random networks: at most 2 copies per link, and the whole connected group installs it
(lambda R: all((lambda names: (lambda links: (lambda m, inst: m <= 2 * len(links) and inst == sorted((lambda comp: comp(comp, {names[0]}))(lambda self, seen: (lambda more: seen if not more else self(self, seen | more))({b for a, b in links + [(y, x) for x, y in links] if a in seen and b not in seen}))))(*flood(links, names[0], 9, {})))(list({tuple(sorted(R.sample(names, 2))) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 12))}) if len(names) > 1 else []))([chr(65 + i) for i in range(R.randint(1, 8))]) for _ in range(50)))(__import__("random").Random(17))

+++ practice | From the map to a prefix table
--- task
Real LSAs also list the prefixes each router can deliver to directly, and the forwarding table maps prefixes to next hops. Write `prefix_table(lsdb, prefixes, me)`.

- `lsdb` is as in the lesson: router → {neighbour: cost}, with the two-way check.
- `prefixes` maps a router to the list of prefixes (strings like `"10.1.0.0/16"`) it advertises.
- Return a dict mapping each prefix to the next hop router `me` should use for it: `"local"` if `me` advertises it itself; otherwise the first hop toward the cheapest router that advertises it (on a tie in cost, the first hop whose name sorts first). Leave out prefixes whose advertisers cannot be reached.

Use your own Dijkstra with first hops, as in the lesson's task. For example, with the lesson's LSDB and `{"N": ["10.9.0.0/16"], "K": ["10.1.0.0/16"]}`, `prefix_table(lsdb, prefixes, "K")` is `{"10.9.0.0/16": "L", "10.1.0.0/16": "local"}`.
--- starter
def prefix_table(lsdb, prefixes, me):
    return {}
--- solution
import heapq


def forwarding_table(lsdb, me):
    best = {me: (0, me)}
    heap = [(0, me, me)]
    done = set()
    while heap:
        cost, first, u = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if u in done:
            continue
        done.add(u)
        for v, c in lsdb.get(u, {}).items():
            if v not in lsdb or u not in lsdb[v]:
                continue
            label = (cost + c, v if u == me else first)
            if v not in best or label < best[v]:
                best[v] = label
                heapq.heappush(heap, (label[0], label[1], v))
    return best


def prefix_table(lsdb, prefixes, me):
    routes = forwarding_table(lsdb, me)
    choice = {}
    for router, owned in prefixes.items():
        if router not in routes:
            continue
        cost, first = routes[router]
        hop = "local" if router == me else first
        key = (cost, "" if router == me else first)
        for prefix in owned:
            if prefix not in choice or key < choice[prefix][0]:
                choice[prefix] = (key, hop)
    return {prefix: hop for prefix, (key, hop) in choice.items()}
--- hint
Run the Dijkstra from the lesson's task once, from `me`. Then every advertising router you reached offers its prefixes at `(its cost, its first hop)`.
--- hint
For each prefix keep the smallest offer. `me`'s own prefixes cost 0, so they always win, and map to `"local"`.
--- check case | The example from the task
prefix_table({"K": {"L": 2, "M": 5}, "L": {"K": 2, "M": 2, "N": 6}, "M": {"K": 5, "L": 2, "N": 1}, "N": {"L": 6, "M": 1}}, {"N": ["10.9.0.0/16"], "K": ["10.1.0.0/16"]}, "K")
=> {"10.9.0.0/16": "L", "10.1.0.0/16": "local"}
--- check case | Two routers advertise the same prefix: the cheaper one wins
prefix_table({"A": {"B": 1, "C": 3}, "B": {"A": 1}, "C": {"A": 3}}, {"B": ["0.0.0.0/0"], "C": ["0.0.0.0/0", "10.0.0.0/8"]}, "A")
=> {"0.0.0.0/0": "B", "10.0.0.0/8": "C"}
--- check case | Equal cost: the first hop that sorts first
prefix_table({"A": {"Y": 1, "X": 1}, "X": {"A": 1}, "Y": {"A": 1}}, {"X": ["1.0.0.0/8"], "Y": ["1.0.0.0/8"]}, "A")
=> {"1.0.0.0/8": "X"}
--- check case | Unreachable advertisers are left out
prefix_table({"A": {"B": 1}, "B": {"A": 1}, "C": {}}, {"C": ["9.0.0.0/8"], "B": ["8.0.0.0/8"]}, "A")
=> {"8.0.0.0/8": "B"}
--- check case | Local beats every other advertiser
prefix_table({"A": {"B": 1}, "B": {"A": 1}}, {"B": ["5.0.0.0/8"], "A": ["5.0.0.0/8"]}, "A")
=> {"5.0.0.0/8": "local"}

+++ practice | Edge cases in the route computation
--- task
Write `route_costs(lsdb, me)` that returns a dict mapping every router reachable from `me` (not including `me`) to its cheapest cost, using the lesson's rules (the two-way check; each direction's own cost). Your code must handle all of these:

- `me` has no LSA in `lsdb`, or no usable links: return `{}`;
- a neighbour listed in an LSA that has no LSA of its own;
- a router's LSA that lists `me`, while `me`'s LSA does not list it;
- links whose two directions have different costs;
- parts of the network that `me` cannot reach.

For example, `route_costs({"A": {"B": 2}, "B": {"A": 5, "C": 1}, "C": {"B": 1}}, "A")` is `{"B": 2, "C": 3}`.
--- starter
def route_costs(lsdb, me):
    return {v: c for v, c in lsdb[me].items()}
--- solution
import heapq


def route_costs(lsdb, me):
    if me not in lsdb:
        return {}
    dist = {me: 0}
    heap = [(0, me)]
    while heap:
        d, u = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if d > dist[u]:
            continue
        for v, c in lsdb[u].items():
            if v not in lsdb or u not in lsdb[v]:
                continue
            if v not in dist or d + c < dist[v]:
                dist[v] = d + c
                heapq.heappush(heap, (d + c, v))
    del dist[me]
    return dist
--- hint
Start with a guard for `me not in lsdb`. Then run Dijkstra, skipping every link that fails the two-way check.
--- hint
Only routers with an LSA ever get relaxed from, since a usable link needs an LSA at both ends. Remove `me` from the result at the end.
--- check case | The example from the task
route_costs({"A": {"B": 2}, "B": {"A": 5, "C": 1}, "C": {"B": 1}}, "A")
=> {"B": 2, "C": 3}
--- check case | me has no LSA, or no usable links
(route_costs({"B": {"A": 1}}, "A"), route_costs({"A": {"B": 1}}, "A"), route_costs({"A": {}, "B": {"A": 1}}, "A"))
=> ({}, {}, {})
--- check case | A one-sided link from the other end is ignored
route_costs({"A": {"C": 4}, "B": {"A": 1}, "C": {"A": 4, "B": 1}}, "A")
=> {"C": 4}
--- check case | Different costs in the two directions
(route_costs({"A": {"B": 1}, "B": {"A": 9}}, "A"), route_costs({"A": {"B": 1}, "B": {"A": 9}}, "B"))
=> ({"B": 1}, {"A": 9})
--- check case | A separate part of the network
route_costs({"A": {"B": 1}, "B": {"A": 1}, "C": {"D": 1}, "D": {"C": 1}}, "A")
=> {"B": 1}

+++ practice | Debug: the wrong first hop
--- task
`first_hops(lsdb, me)` should return a dict mapping every router reachable from `me` (not `me` itself) to the first hop of a cheapest path from `me`. On a tie in cost, the first hop whose name sorts first wins. Every link here is reported by both ends with the same cost. It is right for the neighbours of `me` and for routers two links away, but wrong for routers further away. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
import heapq


def first_hops(lsdb, me):
    best = {me: (0, me)}
    heap = [(0, me, me)]
    done = set()
    while heap:
        cost, first, u = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if u in done:
            continue
        done.add(u)
        for v, c in lsdb[u].items():
            label = (cost + c, v if u == me else u)
            if v not in best or label < best[v]:
                best[v] = label
                heapq.heappush(heap, (label[0], label[1], v))
    return {v: hop for v, (cost, hop) in best.items() if v != me}
--- solution
import heapq


def first_hops(lsdb, me):
    best = {me: (0, me)}
    heap = [(0, me, me)]
    done = set()
    while heap:
        cost, first, u = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if u in done:
            continue
        done.add(u)
        for v, c in lsdb[u].items():
            label = (cost + c, v if u == me else first)
            if v not in best or label < best[v]:
                best[v] = label
                heapq.heappush(heap, (label[0], label[1], v))
    return {v: hop for v, (cost, hop) in best.items() if v != me}
--- hint
Follow a line A–B–C–D from A. What does the code record as the first hop of D?
--- hint
It records the router just before D. The first hop must be carried along from `u`'s own label.
--- check case | A line of four
first_hops({"A": {"B": 1}, "B": {"A": 1, "C": 1}, "C": {"B": 1, "D": 1}, "D": {"C": 1}}, "A")
=> {"B": "B", "C": "B", "D": "B"}
--- check case | Two branches
first_hops({"S": {"P": 1, "Q": 1}, "P": {"S": 1, "P2": 1}, "P2": {"P": 1, "P3": 1}, "P3": {"P2": 1}, "Q": {"S": 1, "Q2": 1}, "Q2": {"Q": 1, "Q3": 1}, "Q3": {"Q2": 1}}, "S")
=> {"P": "P", "Q": "Q", "P2": "P", "Q2": "Q", "P3": "P", "Q3": "Q"}
--- check case | Neighbours only
first_hops({"A": {"B": 3, "C": 1}, "B": {"A": 3}, "C": {"A": 1}}, "A")
=> {"B": "B", "C": "C"}

+++ practice | Microloops while the map changes
--- task
During flooding, routers can have different versions of the map. Find out where packets go.

Write `trace_all(views, dst)`. `views` maps each router to **its own** LSDB (router → {neighbour: cost}), which may be out of date. Each router computes its next hop toward `dst` from its own view, by the lesson's rules: two-way check, each direction's own cost, cheapest path, and on a tie the first hop whose name sorts first.

A packet from a router follows next hops. Return a dict mapping every router in `views` other than `dst` to:

- `"delivered"` if the packet reaches `dst`;
- `"dropped"` if it reaches a router that has no route to `dst` in its own view (or that router is not in `views`);
- `"loop"` if it comes back to a router it has already visited.

For example, if the link B–D has just failed and B has the new map but A still has the old one, A may send packets for D to B while B sends them back to A: both are `"loop"`.
--- starter
def trace_all(views, dst):
    return {r: "delivered" for r in views if r != dst}
--- solution
import heapq


def next_hop(lsdb, me, dst):
    best = {me: (0, me)}
    heap = [(0, me, me)]
    done = set()
    while heap:
        cost, first, u = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if u in done:
            continue
        done.add(u)
        for v, c in lsdb.get(u, {}).items():
            if v not in lsdb or u not in lsdb[v]:
                continue
            label = (cost + c, v if u == me else first)
            if v not in best or label < best[v]:
                best[v] = label
                heapq.heappush(heap, (label[0], label[1], v))
    return best[dst][1] if dst in best else None


def trace_all(views, dst):
    hops = {r: next_hop(view, r, dst) for r, view in views.items() if r != dst}
    result = {}
    for start in hops:
        seen = set()
        here = start
        while True:
            if here == dst:
                result[start] = "delivered"
                break
            if here in seen:
                result[start] = "loop"
                break
            if here not in hops or hops[here] is None:
                result[start] = "dropped"
                break
            seen.add(here)
            here = hops[here]
    return result
--- hint
First compute each router's next hop toward `dst` from its own view, with the Dijkstra from the lesson's task (return `None` if `dst` is not reachable in that view).
--- hint
Then walk from each router along next hops with a set of visited routers. Check "arrived" first, then "seen before", then "no route".
--- check case | All views agree: everyone delivers
(lambda g: trace_all({r: g for r in g}, "D"))({"A": {"B": 1, "C": 3}, "B": {"A": 1, "D": 1}, "C": {"A": 3, "D": 1}, "D": {"B": 1, "C": 1}})
=> {"A": "delivered", "B": "delivered", "C": "delivered"}
--- check case | B knows the link B-D failed, A does not: a microloop
(lambda old, new: trace_all({"A": old, "B": new, "C": new, "D": new}, "D"))({"A": {"B": 1, "C": 3}, "B": {"A": 1, "D": 1}, "C": {"A": 3, "D": 1}, "D": {"B": 1, "C": 1}}, {"A": {"B": 1, "C": 3}, "B": {"A": 1}, "C": {"A": 3, "D": 1}, "D": {"C": 1}})
=> {"A": "loop", "B": "loop", "C": "delivered"}
--- check case | Once A learns too, the loop is gone
(lambda new: trace_all({"A": new, "B": new, "C": new, "D": new}, "D"))({"A": {"B": 1, "C": 3}, "B": {"A": 1}, "C": {"A": 3, "D": 1}, "D": {"C": 1}})
=> {"A": "delivered", "B": "delivered", "C": "delivered"}
--- check case | A router with no route drops
trace_all({"A": {"A": {"B": 1}, "B": {"A": 1, "C": 1}, "C": {"B": 1}}, "B": {"B": {"A": 1}, "A": {"B": 1}}}, "C")
=> {"A": "dropped", "B": "dropped"}
--- check case | A next hop that has no view of its own drops the packet
trace_all({"A": {"A": {"X": 1}, "X": {"A": 1, "D": 1}, "D": {"X": 1}}}, "D")
=> {"A": "dropped"}

+++ question | What stops a flood
--- ask
Flooding sends each new LSA to every neighbour except the one it came from. In a network with loops, why does the flood end?
--- choice
Each copy carries a hop count that runs out.
--- choice correct
A router forwards an LSA only the first time it sees that sequence number from that origin, so each link carries it at most once in each direction.
--- choice
The origin stops the flood when every router has acknowledged it.
--- choice
Routers flood only along the shortest-path tree.
--- why
The sequence number turns "have I seen this?" into a comparison with a stored number. After a router's first copy, every later copy is discarded, so the total number of copies is at most 2E. Without it, flooding in a loop behaves like an Ethernet broadcast storm. (Real protocols also acknowledge LSAs, but for reliability, not to stop the flood.)

+++ question | Why same maps give loop-free routes
--- ask
All routers have the same LSDB and the same tie rule. The proof that packets never loop says that at every hop the distance to the destination goes down. What makes it go down strictly?
--- choice
Every router is closer to the destination than the router before it in the list of names.
--- choice correct
The first hop n lies on a cheapest path, so dist(n, y) = dist(x, y) − cost(x, n), and every link cost is positive.
--- choice
Dijkstra visits routers in order of their distance.
--- choice
The TTL goes down at every hop.
--- why
If costs could be 0, two routers at the same distance could point at each other forever. With positive costs and consistent maps, the distance to y is a quantity that strictly decreases along the path, so the path cannot repeat a router. The TTL is a safety net for inconsistent moments, not part of this argument.

+++ question | Distance vector or link state after a failure
--- ask
In a line P–Q–R, the link Q–R breaks. What happens under link-state routing?
--- choice
P and Q count to infinity, as in distance vector.
--- choice correct
Q floods a new LSA without the link (and so does R); every router recomputes Dijkstra on the new map and sees R is unreachable, with no counting.
--- choice
Nothing changes until R's old LSA is deleted an hour later.
--- choice
P keeps sending packets for R to Q forever.
--- why
Link state spreads facts about links, not distances built on other routers' claims. Q's new LSA no longer lists R, and R's no longer lists Q; with the two-way check, even one of them is enough to remove the link from everyone's map. Each router then computes from the true map. The cost is a Dijkstra per change and a full map in every router.

=== net-06 | UDP and ports: datagrams between programs
--- teach
The last lessons got a packet from one computer to another, across switches and routers, with IP addresses and routing. But a packet is not for a computer. It is for a **program**: your browser, a DNS lookup, a telemetry display. A computer runs hundreds of programs at once, all sharing one IP address. This lesson starts the transport layer, whose first job is to get data to the right program. You will meet **ports**, **sockets**, and the simplest transport protocol, **UDP**, and learn exactly what it means when a protocol is called "unreliable".

### Flats in a building

A letter addressed to a block of flats needs more than the street address: it also needs the flat number. The postman delivers to the building, and the building's mailroom sorts by flat number.

The IP address is the building. The **port** is the flat number: a 16-bit number, from 0 to 65,535, that names one program's mailbox on that computer. The transport layer on each computer is the mailroom.

### Ports

- **Well-known ports**, below 1,024, belong to standard services: DNS listens on 53, HTTP on 80, HTTPS on 443, NTP (time) on 123. A client knows where to find a server because the port is agreed in advance.
- **Ephemeral ports** are [[handed out by the operating system|ephemeral]] to programs that just need *some* port to send from, usually a client. The official range is 49,152 to 65,535.

The pair (IP address, port) names one endpoint of a conversation, anywhere on the Internet. A packet carries two of them: the source and the destination.

### Sockets

A **socket** is what a program opens to send and receive: a mailbox with an address. Giving a socket its local address is called **binding** it. For UDP, a socket is identified by the (local IP, local port) it is bound to.

When a datagram arrives, the operating system looks at its destination (IP, port) and puts it in the queue of the socket bound to that pair. This is the **demultiplexing** from lesson 1, now keyed by the port. If no socket is bound there, the datagram is thrown away.

This works because the OS allows only **one** socket per (IP, port): a second `bind` to the same pair fails with "address already in use". So the binding table is a function from address to socket, and every datagram has at most one place to go.

### The real socket API

On a real computer, Python's `socket` module talks to the operating system. A UDP server looks like this (this code needs a real network, so it does not run here):

```python fragment
import socket

sock = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)   # IPv4, datagrams: UDP
sock.bind(("0.0.0.0", 9000))            # listen on port 9000, on every local address
data, addr = sock.recvfrom(2048)        # wait for one datagram; addr is (ip, port) of the sender
sock.sendto(b"ack " + data, addr)       # reply to whoever sent it
```

And a client:

```python fragment
sock = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
sock.sendto(b"ping", ("10.0.0.7", 9000))    # the OS binds an ephemeral port first
reply, addr = sock.recvfrom(2048)
```

- `AF_INET` means IPv4 addresses, and `SOCK_DGRAM` means datagrams, which is UDP.
- `bind` with IP `"0.0.0.0"` is the **wildcard**: accept datagrams sent to any of this computer's addresses.
- `sendto` on a socket that was never bound makes the OS pick a free ephemeral port first.
- `recvfrom(n)` returns one whole datagram (at most n bytes of it) and the sender's address.

In this course, links and hosts are simulated, so you will build a small in-memory version of exactly this interface.

### The UDP header

UDP adds only 8 bytes in front of the program's data:

```text
| source port 2 | destination port 2 | length 2 | checksum 2 | data ... |
```

- **length** counts the header and the data, so it is at least 8.
- **checksum** is the Internet checksum from lesson 2b, computed over the header, the data, and a **pseudo-header** of the source and destination IP addresses, the protocol number 17, and the UDP length. Including the IP addresses means a datagram delivered to the wrong computer fails the check. In IPv4 a checksum of 0 means "not computed", so a computed 0 is sent as 0xFFFF.

That is everything. UDP adds ports and a checksum to IP, and nothing else.

### What "unreliable" means, exactly

"Unreliable" is a precise promise, or rather a precise lack of one. A datagram sent with UDP may be:

- **lost**: a router's queue was full, or a bit error made the checksum fail;
- **duplicated**: a lower layer resent it and both copies arrived;
- **reordered**: two datagrams took different paths, or were queued differently;
- **delayed** by any amount.

UDP does not retransmit, does not number datagrams, does not slow down when the receiver is busy, and sets up no connection. Here is what it **does** promise:

- **Boundaries are kept.** One `sendto` is one datagram, and one `recvfrom` returns exactly one whole datagram, never half of one or two glued together.
- **Damage is detected.** A datagram that fails its checksum is dropped, not delivered wrong (with the checksum's usual chance of missing an error).

If a program needs more than that, it must build it: numbering its messages, noticing gaps, asking again. That is what lessons 7, 8 and 8b do, and what TCP does for you.

### Numbers to know

A request and its reply each cross the network once. If each crossing is lost with probability p, independently, both arrive with probability (1 − p)². With p = 0.1 that is 0.81, so about one request in five needs a second try.

The biggest UDP payload over IPv4 is 65,535 − 20 − 8 = 65,507 bytes. But a datagram larger than the path's MTU (1,500 bytes on Ethernet, so 1,472 bytes of UDP data) is cut into IP **[[fragments|fragments]]**, and losing any one fragment loses the whole datagram. So real UDP programs keep their datagrams small.

### Why programs choose UDP anyway

- **No setup:** the first datagram carries data. TCP needs a round trip of handshake first.
- **No waiting for old data:** a video call or a game would rather skip a late frame than stall until it is resent.
- **Small and simple:** DNS asks one question in one datagram and gets one answer back.

### Where it is used

- **DNS** (lesson 11) and **NTP** use UDP for their short question-and-answer exchanges.
- **Voice, video and games** send media in UDP datagrams and hide the losses.
- **[[QUIC|quic]]**, the transport under HTTP/3, builds reliable streams on top of UDP.
- **Telemetry**: many ground systems stream spacecraft packets over UDP inside the ground network, and flag gaps in the sequence counts.

**Watch out:** the classic UDP mistake is writing a program that works perfectly on your own computer and fails in the field. On one machine nothing is ever lost, duplicated or reordered, so the code never learns to cope. Every program that uses UDP must decide what it does when a datagram does not arrive, arrives twice, or arrives after a later one.

::: context ephemeral How the OS picks a port
When a program sends without binding, the operating system picks a free port from its ephemeral range. Linux uses 32,768 to 60,999 by default, Windows the IANA range 49,152 to 65,535. Modern systems pick the port at random within the range rather than counting up, so that an attacker cannot easily guess which port a DNS reply must be sent to. That was the main fix for the 2008 Kaminsky attack on DNS caches: with a random port as well as a random 16-bit query ID, a forged reply has to guess about 30 bits instead of 16.
:::

::: context fragments Why big datagrams are fragile
When an IP packet is bigger than the next link's MTU, IPv4 can split it into fragments, each with its own IP header and an offset saying where it belongs. Only the destination puts them back together. If one fragment is lost, the others are useless and are eventually thrown away, and UDP will not ask for them again. With 45 fragments for a 64 KB datagram, even a 1% loss rate loses about 36% of whole datagrams. IPv6 routers never fragment at all; senders must find the path's MTU and stay under it.
:::

::: context quic Reliable streams built on UDP
QUIC was designed at Google around 2012 and standardised by the IETF in 2021. It runs over UDP because middleboxes on the Internet (firewalls and NAT boxes) pass UDP through but often block new protocols. On top of UDP, QUIC adds sequence numbers, acknowledgements, retransmission, congestion control and encryption, all the machinery of the next lessons, inside the application. HTTP/3 runs over QUIC, and a large share of web traffic now does.
:::
--- task
Build an in-memory UDP network. Write two classes.

`Network()` holds every socket, and has an attribute `dropped`, starting at 0.

- `net.socket(ip)` returns a new, unbound `UdpSocket` on the host with address `ip`.

`UdpSocket` has these methods.

- `bind(port)` binds the socket to `(its ip, port)` and returns the port. If `port` is 0, use the lowest port from 49152 to 65535 that is free on that IP. Raise `ValueError` if the socket is already bound, if `port` is outside 0 to 65535, if that port is already bound on that IP, or if no ephemeral port is free.
- `sendto(data, addr)` sends the bytes `data` to `addr`, an `(ip, port)` pair, and returns `len(data)`. If the socket is not bound, it first binds to port 0. Raise `ValueError` if `data` is longer than 65,507 bytes. Delivery is instant and never lost here: if a socket is bound to `addr`, add `(data, (sender ip, sender port))` to the end of its queue; otherwise add 1 to `net.dropped`.
- `recvfrom()` removes and returns the oldest `(data, addr)` in the queue, or `None` if it is empty.
- `close()` frees the socket's port, so another socket can bind it. Datagrams sent to that address afterwards are dropped.

Each socket also has an attribute `addr`: its `(ip, port)` once it is bound, and `None` before. For example: `server = net.socket("10.0.0.7")`, `server.bind(9000)`, `client = net.socket("10.0.0.2")`, `client.sendto(b"ping", ("10.0.0.7", 9000))`, and then `server.recvfrom()` is `(b"ping", ("10.0.0.2", 49152))`.
--- starter
class Network:
    def __init__(self):
        self.dropped = 0

    def socket(self, ip):
        return UdpSocket(self, ip)


class UdpSocket:
    def __init__(self, net, ip):
        self.net = net
        self.ip = ip
        self.addr = None

    def bind(self, port):
        return port

    def sendto(self, data, addr):
        return len(data)

    def recvfrom(self):
        return None

    def close(self):
        pass
--- solution
from collections import deque


class Network:
    def __init__(self):
        self.dropped = 0
        self.bound = {}

    def socket(self, ip):
        return UdpSocket(self, ip)


class UdpSocket:
    def __init__(self, net, ip):
        self.net = net
        self.ip = ip
        self.addr = None
        self.queue = deque()

    def bind(self, port):
        if self.addr is not None:
            raise ValueError("already bound")
        if not 0 <= port <= 65535:
            raise ValueError("no such port")
        if port == 0:
            port = next((p for p in range(49152, 65536) if (self.ip, p) not in self.net.bound), None)
            if port is None:
                raise ValueError("no ephemeral port free")
        elif (self.ip, port) in self.net.bound:
            raise ValueError("address already in use")
        self.addr = (self.ip, port)
        self.net.bound[self.addr] = self
        return port

    def sendto(self, data, addr):
        if len(data) > 65507:
            raise ValueError("too big for one datagram")
        if self.addr is None:
            self.bind(0)
        target = self.net.bound.get(tuple(addr))
        if target is None:
            self.net.dropped += 1
        else:
            target.queue.append((data, self.addr))
        return len(data)

    def recvfrom(self):
        if not self.queue:
            return None
        return self.queue.popleft()

    def close(self):
        if self.addr is not None:
            del self.net.bound[self.addr]
            self.addr = None
        self.queue.clear()
--- hint
Give the `Network` a dict from `(ip, port)` to the socket bound there. `bind` checks the rules in order, then stores `self` under its new address. The lowest free ephemeral port is the first `p` in `range(49152, 65536)` whose `(ip, p)` is not in the dict.
--- hint
Each socket keeps its own queue, a `collections.deque`. `sendto` binds first if needed, looks up `addr` in the network's dict, and either appends `(data, self.addr)` to that socket's queue or counts a drop.
--- hint
`recvfrom` pops from the left of the queue, or returns `None`. `close` removes the socket's entry from the dict and sets `addr` back to `None`.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda net: (lambda server, client: (server.bind(9000), client.sendto(b"ping", ("10.0.0.7", 9000)), server.recvfrom(), server.recvfrom()))(net.socket("10.0.0.7"), net.socket("10.0.0.2")))(Network())
=> (9000, 4, (b"ping", ("10.0.0.2", 49152)), None)
--- check case | A reply goes back to the sender's ephemeral port
(lambda net: (lambda s, c: (s.bind(53), c.sendto(b"q", ("10.0.0.1", 53)), (lambda d, a: s.sendto(b"answer", a))(*s.recvfrom()), c.recvfrom(), c.addr))(net.socket("10.0.0.1"), net.socket("10.0.0.9")))(Network())
=> (53, 1, 6, (b"answer", ("10.0.0.1", 53)), ("10.0.0.9", 49152))
--- check case | Datagrams stay separate and in order, and each is received once
(lambda net: (lambda s, c: (s.bind(7), c.sendto(b"one", ("h", 7)), c.sendto(b"", ("h", 7)), c.sendto(b"three", ("h", 7)), [s.recvfrom()[0] for _ in range(3)], s.recvfrom())[4:])(net.socket("h"), net.socket("g")))(Network())
=> ([b"one", b"", b"three"], None)
--- check case | The same port on two hosts is two different sockets
(lambda net: (lambda a, b, c: (a.bind(53), b.bind(53), c.sendto(b"to-b", ("10.0.0.2", 53)), a.recvfrom(), b.recvfrom()[0])[3:])(net.socket("10.0.0.1"), net.socket("10.0.0.2"), net.socket("10.0.0.3")))(Network())

--- check case | Nobody bound there: dropped and counted
