=> (1, 1, 2)
--- check test | Binding errors
(lambda net: (lambda a, b, c: a.bind(80) == 80 and raises(ValueError, lambda: b.bind(80)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: a.bind(81)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: c.bind(65536)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: c.bind(-1)) and c.bind(65535) == 65535)(net.socket("h"), net.socket("h"), net.socket("h")))(Network())
--- check case | Ephemeral ports: lowest free first, per host
(lambda net: (lambda a, b, c, d: (a.bind(49152), b.bind(0), c.bind(0), d.bind(0)))(net.socket("h"), net.socket("h"), net.socket("h"), net.socket("other")))(Network())
=> (49152, 49153, 49154, 49152)
--- check test | close frees the port; later datagrams to it are dropped
(lambda net: (lambda a, b, c: a.bind(5000) == 5000 and c.sendto(b"1", ("h", 5000)) == 1 and a.close() is None and a.addr is None and c.sendto(b"2", ("h", 5000)) == 1 and net.dropped == 1 and b.bind(5000) == 5000 and c.sendto(b"3", ("h", 5000)) == 1 and b.recvfrom()[0] == b"3")(net.socket("h"), net.socket("h"), net.socket("g")))(Network())
--- check test | 65,507 bytes is allowed, 65,508 is not
(lambda net: (lambda s, c: s.bind(9) == 9 and c.sendto(bytes(65507), ("h", 9)) == 65507 and raises(ValueError, lambda: c.sendto(bytes(65508), ("h", 9))))(net.socket("h"), net.socket("g")))(Network())
--- check test | The last ephemeral port can be used, and then none is left
(lambda net: all(net.socket("h").bind(p) == p for p in range(49152, 65535)) and net.socket("h").bind(0) == 65535 and raises(ValueError, lambda: net.socket("h").bind(0)) and net.socket("g").bind(0) == 49152)(Network())

+++ practice | The UDP header
--- task
Write two functions for the 8-byte UDP header.

- `udp_pack(src_port, dst_port, payload)` returns the header followed by `payload`: source port, destination port, length (8 plus the payload's length) and checksum, each as 2 big-endian bytes. Put 0 in the checksum ("not computed"). Raise `ValueError` if a port is outside 0 to 65535 or the payload is longer than 65,527 bytes.
- `udp_unpack(datagram)` returns `(src_port, dst_port, payload)`. Raise `ValueError` if the datagram is shorter than 8 bytes, or its length field is less than 8 or different from the datagram's real length.

For example, `udp_pack(5000, 53, b"hi")` is `bytes([0x13, 0x88, 0x00, 0x35, 0x00, 0x0A, 0x00, 0x00, 0x68, 0x69])`.
--- starter
def udp_pack(src_port, dst_port, payload):
    return payload


def udp_unpack(datagram):
    return (0, 0, datagram)
--- solution
def udp_pack(src_port, dst_port, payload):
    for port in (src_port, dst_port):
        if not 0 <= port <= 65535:
            raise ValueError("a port is 0 to 65535")
    if len(payload) > 65527:
        raise ValueError("payload too long")
    length = 8 + len(payload)
    header = src_port.to_bytes(2, "big") + dst_port.to_bytes(2, "big") + length.to_bytes(2, "big") + bytes(2)
    return header + payload


def udp_unpack(datagram):
    if len(datagram) < 8:
        raise ValueError("shorter than a UDP header")
    length = int.from_bytes(datagram[4:6], "big")
    if length < 8 or length != len(datagram):
        raise ValueError("bad length field")
    return int.from_bytes(datagram[0:2], "big"), int.from_bytes(datagram[2:4], "big"), datagram[8:]
--- hint
Each field is `value.to_bytes(2, "big")`, and the checksum is `bytes(2)`, two zero bytes. The length counts the 8 header bytes too.
--- hint
Unpacking reads bytes 0–1, 2–3 and 4–5 with `int.from_bytes(..., "big")`, checks the length against `len(datagram)`, and returns everything from byte 8 on as the payload.
--- check case | The example from the task
udp_pack(5000, 53, b"hi")
=> bytes([0x13, 0x88, 0x00, 0x35, 0x00, 0x0A, 0x00, 0x00, 0x68, 0x69])
--- check case | Round trip, including an empty payload
(udp_unpack(udp_pack(65535, 0, b"data")), udp_unpack(udp_pack(1, 2, b"")))
=> ((65535, 0, b"data"), (1, 2, b""))
--- check test | Bad ports and oversized payloads are rejected
raises(ValueError, lambda: udp_pack(65536, 1, b"")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: udp_pack(1, -1, b"")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: udp_pack(1, 1, bytes(65528))) and len(udp_pack(1, 1, bytes(65527))) == 65535
--- check test | Short datagrams and wrong length fields are rejected
raises(ValueError, lambda: udp_unpack(bytes(7))) and raises(ValueError, lambda: udp_unpack(bytes([0, 1, 0, 2, 0, 9, 0, 0, 65]) + b"B")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: udp_unpack(bytes([0, 1, 0, 2, 0, 5, 0, 0]))) and udp_unpack(bytes([0, 1, 0, 2, 0, 8, 0, 0])) == (1, 2, b"")

+++ practice | The UDP checksum
--- task
Write `udp_checksum(src_ip, dst_ip, src_port, dst_port, payload)`. The IPs are dotted-decimal strings. Return the checksum a sender puts in the UDP header:

1. Build the **pseudo-header**: the 4 bytes of the source IP, the 4 bytes of the destination IP, a zero byte, the byte 17, and the UDP length (8 + the payload's length) as 2 big-endian bytes.
2. Build the UDP header with its checksum field set to 0 (as in the header problem).
3. Compute the Internet checksum of pseudo-header + header + payload: 16-bit big-endian words, a zero byte added at the end if the length is odd, ones'-complement sum with end-around carry, then flip all 16 bits.
4. If the result is 0, return `0xFFFF` instead, because 0 means "no checksum".

For example, `udp_checksum("192.168.0.31", "192.168.0.30", 20, 10, b"Hi")` is `0x35C5`.
--- starter
def udp_checksum(src_ip, dst_ip, src_port, dst_port, payload):
    return 0
--- solution
def ones_complement_checksum(data):
    if len(data) % 2:
        data = data + b"\x00"
    total = 0
    for i in range(0, len(data), 2):
        total += int.from_bytes(data[i:i + 2], "big")
        total = (total & 0xFFFF) + (total >> 16)
    return ~total & 0xFFFF


def ip_bytes(text):
    return bytes(int(part) for part in text.split("."))


def udp_checksum(src_ip, dst_ip, src_port, dst_port, payload):
    length = 8 + len(payload)
    pseudo = ip_bytes(src_ip) + ip_bytes(dst_ip) + bytes([0, 17]) + length.to_bytes(2, "big")
    header = src_port.to_bytes(2, "big") + dst_port.to_bytes(2, "big") + length.to_bytes(2, "big") + bytes(2)
    result = ones_complement_checksum(pseudo + header + payload)
    return result if result != 0 else 0xFFFF
--- hint
The pseudo-header is 12 bytes: `ip_bytes(src) + ip_bytes(dst) + bytes([0, 17]) + length.to_bytes(2, "big")`. It is only used for the sum, never sent.
--- hint
Reuse the Internet checksum from lesson 2b on the joined bytes. Only the final step is new: turn a result of 0 into `0xFFFF`.
--- check case | The example from the task
udp_checksum("192.168.0.31", "192.168.0.30", 20, 10, b"Hi")
=> 0x35C5
--- check case | An empty payload
udp_checksum("10.0.0.1", "10.0.0.2", 53000, 53, b"")
=> 0x1C9E
--- check case | An odd-length payload
udp_checksum("127.0.0.1", "127.0.0.1", 1234, 5678, b"hello, orbit")
=> 0xA67F
--- check case | A computed 0 is sent as 0xFFFF
udp_checksum("10.0.0.1", "10.0.0.2", 35392, 53, b"ab")
=> 0xFFFF
--- check test | Delivering to the wrong IP changes the checksum
udp_checksum("10.0.0.1", "10.0.0.2", 7, 7, b"x") != udp_checksum("10.0.0.1", "10.0.0.3", 7, 7, b"x")

+++ practice | Spotting loss, duplicates and reordering
--- task
A ground station receives telemetry over UDP. The spacecraft numbers its datagrams 0, 1, 2, … in the first 4 bytes, big-endian. The network may lose, duplicate or reorder them.

Write `analyse(datagrams, sent)`. `datagrams` is the list of datagrams in the order they arrived, and `sent` is how many the spacecraft sent (numbered 0 to `sent - 1`). Return a dict:

- `"lost"`: the sorted list of numbers that never arrived;
- `"duplicates"`: how many arrivals were a copy of a number that had already arrived;
- `"late"`: how many arrivals (counting first copies only) had a number smaller than some number that arrived before them.

For example, arrivals numbered 0, 2, 1, 2, 4 with `sent = 5` give `{"lost": [3], "duplicates": 1, "late": 1}`: the second 2 is a duplicate, and 1 arrived after 2.
--- starter
def analyse(datagrams, sent):
    return {"lost": [], "duplicates": 0, "late": 0}
--- solution
def analyse(datagrams, sent):
    seen = set()
    duplicates = 0
    late = 0
    highest = -1
    for d in datagrams:
        n = int.from_bytes(d[:4], "big")
        if n in seen:
            duplicates += 1
            continue
        seen.add(n)
        if n < highest:
            late += 1
        highest = max(highest, n)
    lost = [n for n in range(sent) if n not in seen]
    return {"lost": lost, "duplicates": duplicates, "late": late}
--- hint
Read each number with `int.from_bytes(d[:4], "big")`. Keep a set of numbers seen and the highest number seen so far.
--- hint
A number already in the set is a duplicate (skip the rest for it). A first copy smaller than the highest so far is late. The lost numbers are those in `range(sent)` never seen.
--- check case | The example from the task
analyse([(0).to_bytes(4, "big") + b"a", (2).to_bytes(4, "big"), (1).to_bytes(4, "big"), (2).to_bytes(4, "big"), (4).to_bytes(4, "big") + b"zz"], 5)
=> {"lost": [3], "duplicates": 1, "late": 1}
--- check case | Nothing arrived
analyse([], 3)
=> {"lost": [0, 1, 2], "duplicates": 0, "late": 0}
--- check case | Everything in order
analyse([(i).to_bytes(4, "big") for i in range(4)], 4)
=> {"lost": [], "duplicates": 0, "late": 0}
--- check case | Fully reversed, with a copy of the first
analyse([(i).to_bytes(4, "big") for i in [3, 2, 1, 0, 3]], 4)
=> {"lost": [], "duplicates": 1, "late": 3}
--- check case | A late duplicate counts once, as a duplicate
analyse([(i).to_bytes(4, "big") for i in [5, 0, 5, 0]], 6)
=> {"lost": [1, 2, 3, 4], "duplicates": 2, "late": 1}
--- check case | Large numbers
analyse([(70000).to_bytes(4, "big"), (69999).to_bytes(4, "big")], 70001)["late"]
=> 1

+++ practice | Wildcards and the most specific socket
--- task
Real systems let a socket bind to the wildcard IP `"0.0.0.0"`, meaning "any of this computer's addresses". Write a class `BindTable` for one computer.

- `bind(ip, port)` records a binding. Raise `ValueError` if `port` is outside 1 to 65535, or if it conflicts with an existing binding on the same port: the same IP, or either of the two IPs is the wildcard. (So a wildcard binding blocks every specific binding on its port, and the other way round.)
- `unbind(ip, port)` removes a binding; raise `KeyError` if there is none.
- `deliver(dst_ip, dst_port)` returns the binding `(ip, port)` that a datagram to that address goes to: an exact match on IP and port first, otherwise a wildcard binding on that port, otherwise `None`.

For example, after `bind("10.0.0.1", 53)`, a datagram to `("10.0.0.1", 53)` goes to `("10.0.0.1", 53)`, and binding `("0.0.0.0", 53)` raises `ValueError`.
--- starter
class BindTable:
    def bind(self, ip, port):
        pass

    def unbind(self, ip, port):
        pass

    def deliver(self, dst_ip, dst_port):
        return None
--- solution
class BindTable:
    WILDCARD = "0.0.0.0"

    def __init__(self):
        self.bound = set()

    def bind(self, ip, port):
        if not 1 <= port <= 65535:
            raise ValueError("no such port")
        for other_ip, other_port in self.bound:
            if other_port == port and (other_ip == ip or self.WILDCARD in (ip, other_ip)):
                raise ValueError("address already in use")
        self.bound.add((ip, port))

    def unbind(self, ip, port):
        if (ip, port) not in self.bound:
            raise KeyError((ip, port))
        self.bound.remove((ip, port))

    def deliver(self, dst_ip, dst_port):
        if (dst_ip, dst_port) in self.bound:
            return (dst_ip, dst_port)
        if (self.WILDCARD, dst_port) in self.bound:
            return (self.WILDCARD, dst_port)
        return None
--- hint
Keep a set of `(ip, port)` pairs. Two bindings on the same port conflict when their IPs are equal, or when either IP is `"0.0.0.0"`.
--- hint
`deliver` tries the exact pair, then `("0.0.0.0", port)`. It is longest-prefix match in miniature: the more specific binding wins.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda t: (t.bind("10.0.0.1", 53), t.deliver("10.0.0.1", 53), raises(ValueError, lambda: t.bind("0.0.0.0", 53)))[1:])(BindTable())
=> (("10.0.0.1", 53), True)
--- check case | A wildcard catches every local address on its port
(lambda t: (t.bind("0.0.0.0", 80), t.deliver("10.0.0.1", 80), t.deliver("192.168.1.9", 80), t.deliver("10.0.0.1", 81))[1:])(BindTable())
=> (("0.0.0.0", 80), ("0.0.0.0", 80), None)
--- check test | Conflicts in every direction
(lambda t: t.bind("0.0.0.0", 9) is None and raises(ValueError, lambda: t.bind("10.0.0.1", 9)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: t.bind("0.0.0.0", 9)) and t.bind("10.0.0.1", 10) is None and t.bind("10.0.0.2", 10) is None and raises(ValueError, lambda: t.bind("10.0.0.2", 10)))(BindTable())
--- check test | Port limits
(lambda t: raises(ValueError, lambda: t.bind("h", 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: t.bind("h", 65536)) and t.bind("h", 65535) is None and t.bind("h", 1) is None)(BindTable())
--- check test | unbind frees the port, and a missing binding raises KeyError
(lambda t: t.bind("10.0.0.1", 5) is None and raises(KeyError, lambda: t.unbind("10.0.0.2", 5)) and t.unbind("10.0.0.1", 5) is None and t.deliver("10.0.0.1", 5) is None and t.bind("0.0.0.0", 5) is None and t.deliver("10.0.0.1", 5) == ("0.0.0.0", 5))(BindTable())

+++ practice | Debug: the datagram that went to the wrong host
--- task
`Hub` is a tiny simulated network: `bind(ip, port, name)` registers a program called `name` at `(ip, port)`, and `send(dst_ip, dst_port, data)` should put `data` in the inbox of the program bound to exactly that address, returning its name, or return `None` if nobody is bound there. Each host may run a DNS server on port 53. With two hosts both on port 53, every query goes to whichever server bound last, even queries for the other host. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
class Hub:
    def __init__(self):
        self.programs = {}
        self.inbox = {}

    def bind(self, ip, port, name):
        self.programs[port] = name
        self.inbox[name] = []

    def send(self, dst_ip, dst_port, data):
        name = self.programs.get(dst_port)
        if name is None:
            return None
        self.inbox[name].append(data)
        return name
--- solution
class Hub:
    def __init__(self):
        self.programs = {}
        self.inbox = {}

    def bind(self, ip, port, name):
        self.programs[(ip, port)] = name
        self.inbox[name] = []

    def send(self, dst_ip, dst_port, data):
        name = self.programs.get((dst_ip, dst_port))
        if name is None:
            return None
        self.inbox[name].append(data)
        return name
--- hint
What is the key of `self.programs`? Two servers on port 53 of different hosts: what happens to the first one's entry?
--- hint
A socket's address is the pair (IP, port). Key the dict by that pair, in both `bind` and `send`.
--- check case | Two DNS servers on two hosts
(lambda h: (h.bind("10.0.0.1", 53, "dns-a"), h.bind("10.0.0.2", 53, "dns-b"), h.send("10.0.0.1", 53, b"q1"), h.send("10.0.0.2", 53, b"q2"), h.inbox)[2:])(Hub())
=> ("dns-a", "dns-b", {"dns-a": [b"q1"], "dns-b": [b"q2"]})
--- check case | The right port on a host with nothing bound
(lambda h: (h.bind("10.0.0.1", 53, "dns-a"), h.send("10.0.0.9", 53, b"q"))[1])(Hub())
=> None
--- check case | Two ports on one host
(lambda h: (h.bind("h", 1, "one"), h.bind("h", 2, "two"), h.send("h", 2, b"x"), h.send("h", 3, b"y"), h.inbox["two"])[2:])(Hub())
=> ("two", None, [b"x"])

+++ practice | Asking again, safely
--- task
A ground station sends commands to a spacecraft over a lossy UDP link. A command must be executed **at most once**, even when it is sent several times. Simulate the exchange.

Write `run_commands(commands, lost, max_tries)`.

- `commands` is a list of command strings, sent one after another. Command i has request id i.
- Every datagram sent, in either direction, gets the next number from a counter starting at 0: the first datagram sent is number 0, the next number 1, and so on. `lost` is a set of those numbers: a datagram whose number is in `lost` disappears.
- For each command, the station sends a request `(id, command)` and waits for the reply. If the request or the reply is lost, it sends the same request again, up to `max_tries` sends in all. After that it gives up on this command and moves to the next.
- The spacecraft keeps a dict from request id to its reply. On a request whose id it has not seen, it executes the command, which means appending it to a list of executed commands, and stores the reply `"done " + command`. On an id it has seen, it does not execute anything; it sends the stored reply again. Every request that arrives gets a reply.

Return a tuple `(replies, executed, sent)`: the list of replies the station received, one per command, with `None` for a command it gave up on; the spacecraft's list of executed commands; and the total number of datagrams sent.

For example, `run_commands(["heater on"], {1}, 3)` is `(["done heater on"], ["heater on"], 4)`: request 0 arrives and the command runs, reply 1 is lost, request 2 is a retry that is answered from the stored reply, and reply 3 arrives.
--- starter
def run_commands(commands, lost, max_tries):
    return ([None] * len(commands), [], 0)
--- solution
def run_commands(commands, lost, max_tries):
    counter = 0
    stored = {}
    executed = []
    replies = []
    for req_id, command in enumerate(commands):
        reply = None
        for _ in range(max_tries):
            request_number = counter
            counter += 1
            if request_number in lost:
                continue
            if req_id not in stored:
                executed.append(command)
                stored[req_id] = "done " + command
            reply_number = counter
            counter += 1
            if reply_number in lost:
                continue
            reply = stored[req_id]
            break
        replies.append(reply)
    return (replies, executed, counter)
--- hint
Keep one counter for all datagrams. Each try sends a request (takes a number). Only if the request arrives does the spacecraft reply, and the reply takes the next number.
--- hint
The spacecraft looks up the id in its dict before executing: a retry of a command that already ran gets the stored reply and runs nothing. Stop trying when a reply arrives.
--- check case | The example from the task
run_commands(["heater on"], {1}, 3)
=> (["done heater on"], ["heater on"], 4)
--- check case | Nothing lost
run_commands(["a", "b"], set(), 3)
=> (["done a", "done b"], ["a", "b"], 4)
--- check case | Lost requests are never executed
run_commands(["a"], {0, 1}, 3)
=> (["done a"], ["a"], 4)
--- check case | Giving up: the command may still have run
run_commands(["a", "b"], {1, 3, 5}, 3)
=> ([None, "done b"], ["a", "b"], 8)
--- check case | Giving up when every request is lost
run_commands(["a"], {0, 1, 2}, 3)
=> ([None], [], 3)
--- check case | No commands
run_commands([], {0}, 5)
=> ([], [], 0)

+++ question | What UDP keeps and what it does not
--- ask
A program sends three datagrams with `sendto`: 100 bytes, then 200 bytes, then 300 bytes. Which of these can the receiver's `recvfrom` calls return?
--- choice
One 600-byte message, since UDP may join datagrams together.
--- choice
A 150-byte message and then a 450-byte message.
--- choice correct
The 300-byte datagram, then the 100-byte one, and never the 200-byte one.
--- choice
Only the three datagrams, always in the order they were sent.
--- why
UDP keeps message boundaries: each `recvfrom` returns exactly one whole datagram. It does not keep order and does not guarantee delivery, so reordering and loss are both possible. Joining or splitting messages is what a byte stream like TCP does, not UDP.

+++ question | Why one socket per address
--- ask
The operating system refuses a second `bind` to an (IP, port) that is already bound. Which property of demultiplexing does that rule guarantee?
--- choice
That datagrams arrive in order.
--- choice correct
That every incoming datagram has at most one socket to go to, so delivery is well-defined.
--- choice
That no datagram is ever lost.
--- choice
That the checksum is always correct.
--- why
The binding table must be a function from address to socket. If two sockets could hold the same (IP, port), the OS would have no rule for choosing between them. (Some systems have an option to share a port on purpose, and then they spread datagrams over the sockets by an explicit rule.)

+++ question | The pseudo-header
--- ask
Why does the UDP checksum cover a pseudo-header with the source and destination IP addresses, even though those are not part of the UDP header?
--- choice
So that routers can check the UDP data on the way.
--- choice correct
So that a datagram whose IP addresses were damaged, and which reached the wrong computer or port, fails the check there instead of being delivered.
--- choice
To encrypt the datagram with the addresses as a key.
--- choice
Because UDP has no header of its own.
--- why
A datagram is only correct if it reached the right endpoint. The IP header checksum protects the addresses hop by hop, but only the pseudo-header lets the destination's transport layer check, end to end, that the datagram was meant for this address. It is the end-to-end argument from lesson 1 in miniature.

=== net-07 | Reliable transport I: stop-and-wait
--- teach
Last lesson you met UDP, and its honest promise: a datagram may be lost, duplicated, delayed or damaged, and nobody will tell you. Yet when you download a file, every byte arrives, once, in order. Something turns an unreliable channel into a reliable one. This lesson builds the simplest protocol that does it, **stop-and-wait**, and proves that it works. The next lesson makes it fast.

### Sending a letter by a careless courier

Picture sending pages of a manuscript to an editor, one at a time, by a courier who sometimes loses things. You send page 1 and wait. When the editor gets it, she sends back a note: "got it". Only then do you send page 2. If no note arrives after a while, you assume the page was lost and send it again.

But what if the page arrived and only the **note** was lost? You send page 1 again, and the editor receives it twice. She must be able to tell "a second copy of page 1" from "page 2". So you number the pages. That is the whole protocol: **acknowledgements**, **timeouts**, and **sequence numbers**. Each one fixes a specific failure, and the rest of this lesson shows why together they are enough.

### Building it up, one failure at a time

Protocols like this are designed by adding one mechanism per failure the channel can cause.

1. **Bits can be flipped.** Put a checksum or CRC on every packet (lesson 2b). The receiver drops damaged packets, which turns "damaged" into "lost".
2. **The receiver must say what it got.** It answers each good data packet with an **acknowledgement** (ACK): a short packet saying "I received it".
3. **Packets and ACKs can be lost.** The sender starts a **timer** when it sends. If no ACK arrives before the timer runs out, a **timeout**, it sends the same packet again: a **retransmission**.
4. **Retransmissions make duplicates.** If an ACK was lost, the receiver gets the same data twice. So each data packet carries a **sequence number**, and each ACK says which number it acknowledges. The receiver delivers a packet to the program only the first time it sees it, and ACKs every copy, so the sender can stop resending.

With one packet outstanding at a time, a single bit of sequence number is enough: 0, 1, 0, 1, … That is why this protocol is also called the **alternating-bit protocol**. Why one bit suffices is the heart of the proof below.

### The two sides, exactly

**The sender** keeps the index of the current message and its sequence bit s.

- To send: transmit DATA(s, message) and start the timer.
- On ACK(s), undamaged: stop the timer. Move to the next message, flip s, and send it. When there are no more messages, the sender is done.
- On a damaged packet, or ACK(1 − s): ignore it. It says nothing about the current message.
- On timeout: send DATA(s, message) again and restart the timer.

**The receiver** keeps the bit e it expects next, starting at 0.

- On DATA(e, data), undamaged: deliver the data to the program, flip e, and send ACK with the sequence number it just received.
- On DATA(1 − e), undamaged: this is a copy of something already delivered. Do not deliver it again, but send its ACK again, because the sender is clearly still waiting for one.
- On a damaged packet: drop it.

### A run with losses

Here is a trace with a one-way delay of 10 ms and a timeout of 30 ms, sending two messages, where packet 1 (the first ACK) is lost:

| time | event |
|---|---|
| 0 | sender sends DATA(0, "a") [packet 0], timer set for 30 |
| 10 | receiver gets DATA(0): delivers "a", e becomes 1, sends ACK(0) [packet 1, lost] |
| 30 | timeout: sender sends DATA(0, "a") again [packet 2] |
| 40 | receiver gets DATA(0), but expects 1: a duplicate. Sends ACK(0) [packet 3], delivers nothing |
| 50 | sender gets ACK(0): sends DATA(1, "b") [packet 4] |
| 60 | receiver gets DATA(1): delivers "b", sends ACK(1) [packet 5] |
| 70 | sender gets ACK(1): done |

"a" was delivered once even though it arrived twice, and the loss cost one timeout.

### The simulator

Real sockets cannot run here, so the course gives you a small **discrete-event simulator**. It keeps a queue of things that will happen, each with a time, and runs them in time order, jumping the clock straight to the next one. Time is in milliseconds.

The pieces you use:

- `sim.now` is the current time.
- `sim.transmit(raw, deliver)` puts the bytes `raw` on the link. After the link's delay, it calls `deliver(raw)` on the other side, unless the link loses the packet or flips a bit in it. Every packet put on the link, in either direction, gets the next number, starting at 0; the simulator is told which numbers to lose and which to damage.
- `Timer(sim, callback)` is a timer: `start(duration)` arranges for `callback()` to be called that many milliseconds from now (restarting it if it was already running), and `stop()` cancels it.
- `make_packet(kind, seq, data)` builds a packet with a CRC-32 at the end, and `parse_packet(raw)` returns `(kind, seq, data)`, or `None` if the CRC shows it was damaged.

Here is a timer on its own, with a fragment that assumes the simulator code is above it:

```python fragment
sim = Sim(delay=10)
alarm = Timer(sim, lambda: print("rang at", sim.now))
alarm.start(25)
sim.run()                  # rang at 25
```

And a packet that was damaged on the way:

```python fragment
raw = make_packet(DATA, 1, b"temp=21")
print(parse_packet(raw))                          # (0, 1, b'temp=21')
print(parse_packet(bytes([raw[0] ^ 4]) + raw[1:]))   # None: the CRC caught it
```

### Why stop-and-wait is correct

The channel here can lose and damage packets, and delay them, but it is **FIFO**: first in, first out. Packets in one direction arrive in the order they were sent. Under that assumption, here is why every message is delivered exactly once, in order.

Call the sender's bit s and the receiver's bit e. First, two facts about what can still be on its way. Both come from FIFO.

- **(A) Every DATA on its way to the receiver is a copy of the current message or of the previous one.** The sender moved past the previous message only after an ACK for it, and the receiver only ACKs what it has received, so some copy of the previous message has arrived. Every copy of an older message was sent before that copy, so FIFO has already delivered it.
- **(B) Every ACK on its way to the sender is for the current message or the previous one.** ACKs for an older message were sent in answer to its DATA copies, which all reached the receiver before any copy of the previous message did (FIFO again). So they were all sent before the first ACK for the previous message, and that ACK has arrived, since the sender moved on. FIFO delivered them first.

Now the **invariant**: e = s exactly when the current message has **not** been delivered yet. At the start, s = e = 0 and nothing has been delivered, so it holds. By (A) and (B), there are only four kinds of event to check:

1. **The receiver gets DATA of the current message** (number s). If e = s, the invariant says it has not been delivered: it is delivered now, once, and e flips, so e ≠ s and the message is delivered. If e ≠ s, it was already delivered: the receiver only re-ACKs. Correct either way.
2. **The receiver gets DATA of the previous message** (number 1 − s). It was already delivered. Could e be 1 − s right now, so that the receiver delivers it again? That would mean, by the invariant, that the current message has been delivered, so a copy of the current message has arrived. But every copy of the previous message was sent before any copy of the current one, and FIFO delivers them first. So e = s, the number does not match, and the receiver only re-ACKs.
3. **The sender gets the ACK of the current message** (number s). The receiver sent it only after receiving the current message, so it has been delivered and moving on is safe. The sender flips s, which makes s equal to e again, and the new current message has not been delivered: the invariant holds.
4. **The sender gets the ACK of the previous message** (number 1 − s). It says nothing new, and the sender ignores it.

So each message is delivered exactly once, and in order, since the receiver only ever delivers the current message.

Every step uses FIFO. That is exactly why one bit is enough here, and why it is **not** enough on a channel that can reorder packets, like IP across the Internet. TCP uses 32-bit sequence numbers for that reason.

It also always finishes, as long as the link does not lose every copy forever: each timeout sends the current message again, and one copy and its ACK eventually get through.

### What it costs

Stop-and-wait is correct, and slow. Say a packet has L bits, the link sends R bits per second, and the round-trip time (there and back) is RTT. The sender is busy for L/R seconds, then waits for the ACK. The fraction of time it is sending, its **utilisation**, is:

U = (L/R) / (RTT + L/R).

For a 1,500-byte packet (12,000 bits) on a 1 Gb/s link across a continent, with RTT = 30 ms: L/R = 12 microseconds, so U = 0.000012 / 0.030012 ≈ 0.0004. The link is idle 99.96% of the time, and the throughput is about 12,000 bits every 30 ms, 400 kb/s, on a link that could carry 1,000,000 kb/s.

Losses add retransmissions. If each packet is lost with probability p, independently, one attempt succeeds when both the data and its ACK get through, with probability (1 − p)². The number of attempts until the first success is **[[geometric|geometric]]**, so its expected value is 1 / (1 − p)². With p = 0.1, that is about 1.23 sends per message.

The timeout must be longer than the RTT. Shorter, and the sender retransmits packets that were never lost, a **premature timeout**: still correct, as the proof shows, but wasteful. Much longer, and every loss costs a long wait. TCP measures the RTT as it goes to choose its timeout.

### Where it is used

- **[[TFTP|tftp]]**, the trivial file transfer protocol used to boot network devices, is stop-and-wait over UDP.
- **Bluetooth, USB and many radio links** use stop-and-wait at the link layer, where the RTT is tiny and the idle time small.
- **Spacecraft commanding** has used stop-and-wait style acknowledgement for decades: with a [[round trip to the Moon of about 2.6 seconds|moon-rtt]], a ground station sending one command at a time spends almost all of its time waiting. The next lesson fixes that.

**Watch out:** the sender must check that an ACK is for the **current** sequence number. A sender that treats every ACK as "the current packet got through" works perfectly until a premature timeout, when a duplicate ACK for the old packet arrives and makes it move on before the new packet was received. Messages are then silently skipped.

::: context geometric Counting tries until the first success
If each try succeeds with probability q, independently, the chance that the first success is on try k is (1 − q) to the power k − 1, times q. The expected number of tries is 1/q: a sum you met in the probability lessons of Discrete Mathematics. For stop-and-wait, q = (1 − p)² because both the packet and its ACK must survive. Notice how fast it grows on bad links: with p = 0.5, q = 0.25, so a message needs 4 sends on average.
:::

::: context tftp The protocol that boots your router
TFTP, from 1981, sends a file in 512-byte blocks over UDP. Each block carries a 16-bit block number, and the receiver acknowledges each one before the next is sent: stop-and-wait with a bigger counter. It is simple enough to fit in the tiny boot code of a network card, which is why devices still use it to load their first program over the network. Its original version had a famous bug, the "sorcerer's apprentice", where duplicate ACKs caused every block to be sent twice from then on.
:::

::: context moon-rtt How long light takes
The Moon is on average about 384,400 km away, and radio travels at the speed of light, about 300,000 km per second. One way takes about 1.28 seconds, so a command and its acknowledgement take about 2.56 seconds, before any processing time. Mars is between about 3 and 22 light-minutes away depending on where the planets are, so a round trip takes between about 6 and 44 minutes. Nothing about protocols can shorten that; protocols can only avoid wasting it.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="60" y1="20" x2="60" y2="110" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="300" y1="20" x2="300" y2="110" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="60" y="14" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Earth</text>
  <text x="300" y="14" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Moon</text>
  <line x1="60" y1="25" x2="300" y2="60" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="300" y1="60" x2="60" y2="95" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="180" y="36" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">command, 1.28 s</text>
  <text x="180" y="92" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">ACK, 1.28 s</text>
  <text x="30" y="62" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">2.56 s</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
The starter holds the simulator, the packet helpers and a `run` function. Do not change them. Write the two classes of the alternating-bit protocol.

`Sender(sim, messages, timeout)`, where `messages` is a list of `bytes`:

- It has attributes `peer` (set by `run` to the receiver's `on_packet`), `done` (starts `False`) and `done_at` (starts `None`).
- `start()`: if there are no messages, set `done` to `True` and `done_at` to `sim.now`. Otherwise send message 0 with sequence number 0.
- To send: `sim.transmit(make_packet(DATA, seq, message), self.peer)`, then start a `Timer` for `timeout` ms.
- `on_packet(raw)`: ignore it if it is damaged (`parse_packet` returns `None`), if it is not an ACK, if its sequence number is not the current one, or if the sender is done. Otherwise stop the timer, move to the next message, and flip the sequence number (0 becomes 1, 1 becomes 0). If that was the last message, set `done` to `True` and `done_at` to `sim.now`; otherwise send the next one.
- On timeout, send the current message again (and start the timer again).

`Receiver(sim)`:

- It has attributes `peer` (set by `run`) and `delivered`, a list starting empty. It expects sequence number 0 first.
- `on_packet(raw)`: ignore it if it is damaged or not DATA. If its sequence number is the expected one, append its data to `delivered` and flip the expected number. In both cases (new or duplicate), send `make_packet(ACK, seq)` back, with the packet's own sequence number, using `sim.transmit(..., self.peer)`.

`run(messages, delay, timeout, lose, corrupt)` returns `(delivered, packets_sent, done_at)`. For example, `run([b"a", b"b"], 10, 30, lose={1})` is `([b"a", b"b"], 6, 70)`, as in the lesson's trace.
--- starter
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None

    def start(self):
        pass

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        pass


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim):
        self.sim = sim
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        pass


def run(messages, delay, timeout, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- solution
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.peer = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.index = 0
        self.seq = 0
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def send_current(self):
        packet = make_packet(DATA, self.seq, self.messages[self.index])
        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, seq, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK or seq != self.seq:
            return
        self.timer.stop()
        self.index += 1
        self.seq = 1 - self.seq
        if self.index == len(self.messages):
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.send_current()


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim):
        self.sim = sim
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        if seq == self.expected:
            self.delivered.append(data)
            self.expected = 1 - self.expected
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)


def run(messages, delay, timeout, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- hint
The sender needs four more attributes: a `Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)`, the index of the current message, the current sequence bit, and a helper method that sends the current message and starts the timer. `on_timeout` just calls that helper.
--- hint
In `Sender.on_packet`, parse first and return early on every case to ignore: `None`, `kind != ACK`, `seq != self.seq`, or `self.done`. Then stop the timer, add 1 to the index, set `self.seq = 1 - self.seq`, and either finish or send the next message.
--- hint
The receiver keeps `expected`, starting at 0. For undamaged DATA: if `seq == expected`, deliver and flip `expected`. Then, always, transmit `make_packet(ACK, seq)` to `self.peer`.
--- check case | The trace from the lesson
run([b"a", b"b"], 10, 30, lose={1})
=> ([b"a", b"b"], 6, 70)
--- check case | No losses: 2 packets and one round trip per message
run([b"one", b"two", b"three"], 10, 30)
=> ([b"one", b"two", b"three"], 6, 60)
--- check case | A lost data packet costs one timeout
run([b"x"], 10, 30, lose={0})
=> ([b"x"], 3, 50)
--- check case | A lost ACK: the copy is not delivered twice
run([b"x"], 10, 30, lose={1})
=> ([b"x"], 4, 50)
--- check case | Damaged packets are treated as lost
run([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 10, 30, corrupt={0, 3})
=> ([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 8, 120)
--- check case | No messages: done at once, nothing sent
run([], 10, 30)
=> ([], 0, 0)
--- check case | A premature timeout makes duplicates but stays correct
run([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 10, 15)
=> ([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 12, 60)
--- check case | Timeout exactly one round trip: the timer fires first, then the ACK arrives
run([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 10, 20)
=> ([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 12, 60)
--- check test | Many losses in a row
run([b"p", b"q"], 5, 12, lose=set(range(0, 20, 2)) | {1, 3})[0] == [b"p", b"q"]
--- check test | 500 random runs: always exactly the messages, in order
(lambda R: all((lambda msgs: run(msgs, 10, R.choice([5, 15, 19, 20, 21, 40]), {i for i in range(80) if R.random() < 0.3}, {i for i in range(80) if R.random() < 0.1})[0] == msgs)([bytes([R.randint(0, 255)]) * R.randint(1, 3) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 8))]) for _ in range(500)))(__import__("random").Random(1))
?? Check the sequence number of every ACK, and re-ACK duplicates without delivering them.

+++ practice | How slow is stop-and-wait
--- task
Write two functions.

- `stop_and_wait(packet_bits, rate, rtt)` returns a tuple `(utilisation, throughput)` for a stop-and-wait sender with no losses: `packet_bits` is the packet size L in bits, `rate` the link's rate R in bits per second, and `rtt` the round-trip time in seconds. Utilisation is (L/R) / (RTT + L/R), and throughput is L / (RTT + L/R) in bits per second.
- `expected_sends(p)` returns the expected number of times one message is sent when every packet (data and ACK) is lost with probability `p`, independently: 1 / (1 − p)². Raise `ValueError` if `p` is not at least 0 and less than 1.

For example, `stop_and_wait(8000, 1000000, 0.024)` is `(0.25, 250000.0)`: sending takes 8 ms, and 8 of every 32 ms are spent sending.
--- starter
def stop_and_wait(packet_bits, rate, rtt):
    return (1.0, rate)


def expected_sends(p):
    return 1.0
--- solution
def stop_and_wait(packet_bits, rate, rtt):
    send_time = packet_bits / rate
    cycle = rtt + send_time
    return send_time / cycle, packet_bits / cycle


def expected_sends(p):
    if not 0 <= p < 1:
        raise ValueError("p must be at least 0 and less than 1")
    return 1 / (1 - p) ** 2
--- hint
Compute the transmission time L/R first. One cycle of stop-and-wait is that plus the RTT.
--- hint
One send succeeds with probability (1 − p)², since the data and its ACK must both get through. The expected number of tries until the first success is 1 over that.
--- check case | The example from the task
stop_and_wait(8000, 1000000, 0.024)
=> (0.25, 250000.0)
--- check test | A gigabit link across a continent
(lambda r: abs(r[0] - 0.000399840064) < 1e-9 and abs(r[1] - 399840.064) < 1e-3)(stop_and_wait(12000, 10**9, 0.030))
--- check case | RTT of zero: always busy
stop_and_wait(1000, 1000, 0)
=> (1.0, 1000.0)
--- check case | expected_sends
(expected_sends(0), expected_sends(0.5))
=> (1.0, 4.0)
--- check test | expected_sends rejects impossible probabilities
raises(ValueError, lambda: expected_sends(1)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: expected_sends(-0.1)) and abs(expected_sends(0.1) - 1 / 0.81) < 1e-12

+++ practice | The receiver on its own
--- task
Write the alternating-bit receiver as a plain function. `receive(arrivals)` takes the list of data packets that reach the receiver, in order, each a tuple `(seq, data, ok)`: `seq` is 0 or 1, and `ok` is `False` if the packet was damaged.

The receiver expects sequence number 0 first. A damaged packet is dropped with no answer. An undamaged packet with the expected number is delivered and flips the expected number. Every undamaged packet, new or duplicate, is answered with an ACK carrying its own sequence number.

Return a tuple `(delivered, acks)`: the list of delivered data, and the list of ACK numbers sent, in order.

For example, `receive([(0, b"a", True), (0, b"a", True), (1, b"b", False), (1, b"b", True)])` is `([b"a", b"b"], [0, 0, 1])`.
--- starter
def receive(arrivals):
    return ([data for seq, data, ok in arrivals], [])
--- solution
def receive(arrivals):
    expected = 0
    delivered = []
    acks = []
    for seq, data, ok in arrivals:
        if not ok:
            continue
        if seq == expected:
            delivered.append(data)
            expected = 1 - expected
        acks.append(seq)
    return delivered, acks
--- hint
Keep `expected`, starting at 0. Skip damaged packets entirely.
--- hint
Deliver only when `seq == expected`, then flip it. Append `seq` to the ACK list for every undamaged packet.
--- check case | The example from the task
receive([(0, b"a", True), (0, b"a", True), (1, b"b", False), (1, b"b", True)])
=> ([b"a", b"b"], [0, 0, 1])
--- check case | Nothing arrived
receive([])
=> ([], [])
--- check case | Only damaged packets
receive([(0, b"a", False), (0, b"a", False)])
=> ([], [])
--- check case | A stray packet with number 1 before anything: re-ACKed, not delivered
receive([(1, b"old", True), (0, b"a", True)])
=> ([b"a"], [1, 0])
--- check case | Equal data in two different messages is delivered twice
receive([(0, b"same", True), (1, b"same", True), (1, b"same", True), (0, b"x", True)])
=> ([b"same", b"same", b"x"], [0, 1, 1, 0])

+++ practice | Sending a file in chunks
--- task
Send a whole file with stop-and-wait. The starter holds the lesson's simulator. Write `Sender`, `Receiver` and `send_file(data, chunk_size, delay, timeout, lose)`.

- `send_file` cuts `data` into chunks of `chunk_size` bytes (the last one may be shorter). The first message sent is a 4-byte big-endian number: the total length of `data`. Then come the chunks, in order. It runs the simulation with your classes and returns a tuple `(received, packets_sent, done_at)`.
- The receiver reads the length from the first message it delivers, then joins the data of later deliveries. `received` is the joined bytes once all of them have arrived, or `None` if fewer bytes arrived than the length said.
- The protocol is the lesson's: sequence bits, ACKs for every undamaged DATA, duplicates re-ACKed and not delivered, and a timer on the sender.

For example, `send_file(b"hello world", 4, 10, 30, set())` sends 4 messages (the length, then `b"hell"`, `b"o wo"`, `b"rld"`), so it returns `(b"hello world", 8, 80)`. An empty file sends only the length.
--- starter
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


def send_file(data, chunk_size, delay, timeout, lose):
    return (None, 0, 0)
--- solution
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.peer = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.index = 0
        self.seq = 0
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def send_current(self):
        packet = make_packet(DATA, self.seq, self.messages[self.index])
        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, seq, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK or seq != self.seq:
            return
        self.timer.stop()
        self.index += 1
        self.seq = 1 - self.seq
        if self.index == len(self.messages):
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.send_current()


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim):
        self.sim = sim
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        if seq == self.expected:
            self.delivered.append(data)
            self.expected = 1 - self.expected
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)


def send_file(data, chunk_size, delay, timeout, lose):
    messages = [len(data).to_bytes(4, "big")]
    messages += [data[i:i + chunk_size] for i in range(0, len(data), chunk_size)]
    sim = Sim(delay, lose)
    receiver = Receiver(sim)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    if not receiver.delivered:
        return (None, sim.sent, sender.done_at)
    total = int.from_bytes(receiver.delivered[0], "big")
    body = b"".join(receiver.delivered[1:])
    received = body if len(body) == total else None
    return (received, sim.sent, sender.done_at)
--- hint
Your `Sender` and `Receiver` are exactly the ones from the lesson's task. `send_file` builds the list of messages, wires the two together as the lesson's `run` does, and runs the simulator.
--- hint
The chunks are `data[i:i + chunk_size]` for `i` in `range(0, len(data), chunk_size)`. After the run, the first delivered message is the length; join the rest with `b"".join(...)` and compare lengths.
--- check case | The example from the task
send_file(b"hello world", 4, 10, 30, set())
=> (b"hello world", 8, 80)
--- check case | An empty file
send_file(b"", 4, 10, 30, set())
=> (b"", 2, 20)
--- check case | Losses cost time, not data
send_file(b"abcdef", 3, 5, 20, {0, 3, 6})
=> (b"abcdef", 9, 90)
--- check test | Chunks that contain zero bytes and repeated chunks
send_file(bytes(9) + b"xyzxyz", 3, 7, 25, {2, 5})[0] == bytes(9) + b"xyzxyz"
--- check test | 5,000 bytes in chunks of 100, with every tenth packet lost
(lambda d: (lambda r: r[0] == d and r[2] > 51 * 20)(send_file(d, 100, 10, 30, set(range(9, 400, 10)))))(bytes(i % 251 for i in range(5000)))

+++ practice | Giving up after too many tries
--- task
A real sender cannot retry forever. The starter holds the lesson's simulator and a `run` that also reports failure. Write `Sender` and `Receiver` as in the lesson, with one change: the sender sends each message at most `max_tries` times in all (the first send counts). If the timer runs out after the last allowed send, the sender gives up: it sets `failed` to `True`, stops, and sends nothing more.

`Sender(sim, messages, timeout, max_tries)` has attributes `peer`, `done`, `done_at` and `failed` (starts `False`). `done` becomes `True` only if every message was acknowledged.

`run(messages, delay, timeout, max_tries, lose)` returns `(delivered, packets_sent, done, failed)`. Watch the edges: a message whose last allowed send gets through is a success; a message whose last send arrives but whose ACK is lost is a failure for the sender even though the receiver delivered it.

For example, `run([b"a"], 10, 30, 2, {0})` is `([b"a"], 3, True, False)`, and `run([b"a"], 10, 30, 2, {0, 1})` is `([], 2, False, True)`: both allowed sends were lost.
--- starter

--- solution
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, max_tries):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.max_tries = max_tries
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None
        self.failed = False
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.index = 0
        self.seq = 0
        self.tries = 0

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def send_current(self):
        self.tries += 1
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(DATA, self.seq, self.messages[self.index]), self.peer)
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done or self.failed:
            return
        kind, seq, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK or seq != self.seq:
            return
        self.timer.stop()
        self.index += 1
        self.seq = 1 - self.seq
        self.tries = 0
        if self.index == len(self.messages):
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def on_timeout(self):
        if self.tries >= self.max_tries:
            self.failed = True
        else:
            self.send_current()


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim):
        self.sim = sim
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        if seq == self.expected:
            self.delivered.append(data)
            self.expected = 1 - self.expected
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)


def run(messages, delay, timeout, max_tries, lose):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose)
    receiver = Receiver(sim)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout, max_tries)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done, sender.failed
--- hint
Count the sends of the current message in an attribute, add 1 in the method that sends, and reset it to 0 when you move on to the next message.
--- hint
On timeout, compare the count with `max_tries` **before** sending again: if it has already reached the limit, set `failed` and do nothing else. Once failed, ignore late ACKs too.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(run([b"a"], 10, 30, 2, {0}), run([b"a"], 10, 30, 2, {0, 1}))
=> (([b"a"], 3, True, False), ([], 2, False, True))
--- check case | One try only
(run([b"a", b"b"], 10, 30, 1, set()), run([b"a", b"b"], 10, 30, 1, {0}))
=> (([b"a", b"b"], 4, True, False), ([], 1, False, True))
--- check case | The last allowed try gets through
run([b"a"], 10, 30, 3, {0, 1})
=> ([b"a"], 4, True, False)
--- check case | Delivered, but the sender never learns it
run([b"a", b"b"], 10, 30, 2, {1, 3})
=> ([b"a"], 4, False, True)
--- check case | The count starts again for each message
run([b"a", b"b"], 10, 30, 2, {0, 3})
=> ([b"a", b"b"], 6, True, False)
--- check case | No messages
run([], 10, 30, 1, set())
=> ([], 0, True, False)

+++ practice | Debug: the sender that skips messages
--- task
This stop-and-wait sender works on every test with a sensible timeout. With a timeout shorter than the round trip, some messages are never delivered: `run([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 10, 15)` delivers only some of them, although nothing was lost. The receiver is correct. Find the bug in the sender and fix it.
--- starter

        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.index = 0
        self.seq = 0

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def send_current(self):
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(DATA, self.seq, self.messages[self.index]), self.peer)
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, seq, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK:
            return
        self.timer.stop()
        self.index += 1
        self.seq = 1 - self.seq
        if self.index == len(self.messages):
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.send_current()


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim):
        self.sim = sim
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        if seq == self.expected:
            self.delivered.append(data)
            self.expected = 1 - self.expected
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)


def run(messages, delay, timeout, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- solution
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.peer = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.index = 0
        self.seq = 0
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def send_current(self):
        packet = make_packet(DATA, self.seq, self.messages[self.index])
        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, seq, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK or seq != self.seq:
            return
        self.timer.stop()
        self.index += 1
        self.seq = 1 - self.seq
        if self.index == len(self.messages):
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.send_current()


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim):
        self.sim = sim
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        if seq == self.expected:
            self.delivered.append(data)
            self.expected = 1 - self.expected
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)


def run(messages, delay, timeout, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- hint
Follow the premature timeout: the first DATA is sent twice, so two ACK(0)s come back. What does the sender do with the second one?
--- hint
The sender must ignore an ACK whose sequence number is not the current one. Add that test next to the check for the packet kind.
--- check case | Premature timeouts no longer skip messages
run([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 10, 15)
=> ([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 12, 60)
--- check case | A normal timeout still works
run([b"a", b"b"], 10, 30, lose={1})
=> ([b"a", b"b"], 6, 70)
--- check test | Very short timeouts and losses
all(run([b"m%d" % i for i in range(6)], 10, t, lose={3, 8})[0] == [b"m%d" % i for i in range(6)] for t in (3, 7, 12, 19))

+++ practice | A counterexample: when one bit is not enough
--- task
The lesson's proof used the fact that the link is FIFO. Show that the assumption is needed, by finding a counterexample.

The starter holds the alternating-bit protocol, correct as in the lesson, over a simulator whose link can **reorder**: packet number n (counting every packet on the link, in both directions) takes `delays[n]` ms to arrive, and packets beyond the end of the list take 1 ms. Nothing is lost. `run_reordering(delays)` sends `[b"A", b"B", b"C"]` with a timeout of 10 ms and returns what the receiver delivered.

Write `find_bad_delays()`. It must return a list of at most 8 delays, each a whole number from 1 to 30, for which `run_reordering` does **not** deliver exactly `[b"A", b"B", b"C"]`. Search for one: try delay lists built from a few values, such as 1 and 15, and return the first that breaks the protocol.
--- starter
import heapq
import itertools
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class ReorderingSim:
    def __init__(self, delays):
        self.now = 0
        self.delays = delays
        self.sent = 0
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        delay = self.delays[n] if n < len(self.delays) else 1
        self.at(self.now + delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=20000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.peer = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.index = 0
        self.seq = 0
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def send_current(self):
        packet = make_packet(DATA, self.seq, self.messages[self.index])
        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, seq, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK or seq != self.seq:
            return
        self.timer.stop()
        self.index += 1
        self.seq = 1 - self.seq
        if self.index == len(self.messages):
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.send_current()


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim):
        self.sim = sim
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        if seq == self.expected:
            self.delivered.append(data)
            self.expected = 1 - self.expected
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)


def run_reordering(delays):
    sim = ReorderingSim(delays)
    receiver = Receiver(sim)
    sender = Sender(sim, [b"A", b"B", b"C"], 10)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered


def find_bad_delays():
    return [1] * 8

    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")

    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]

    def __init__(self, delays):
        self.now = 0
        self.delays = delays
        self.sent = 0
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        delay = self.delays[n] if n < len(self.delays) else 1
        self.at(self.now + delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=20000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1

        self.entry = None
        self.callback()

        self.index = 0

        self.done_at = None

            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.send_current()

    def send_current(self):

        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)

    def on_packet(self, raw):

        self.sim = sim
        self.peer = None
