def run_reordering(delays):
    sim = ReorderingSim(delays)
    receiver = Receiver(sim)
    sender = Sender(sim, [b"A", b"B", b"C"], 10)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered


def find_bad_delays():
    for k in range(1, 9):
        for delays in itertools.product([1, 15], repeat=k):
            if run_reordering(list(delays)) != [b"A", b"B", b"C"]:
                return list(delays)
    return None
--- hint
`itertools.product([1, 15], repeat=k)` gives every list of k delays made of 1s and 15s. Try k from 1 to 8, run each, and return the first list (as a `list`) whose delivery is wrong.
--- hint
The idea to look for: an early copy of DATA(0, "A") is slow. The timer resends it, the fast copy gets through, and the protocol moves on to "B" (seq 1) and then "C" (seq 0 again). When the slow old copy finally arrives, it carries seq 0, exactly what the receiver now expects.
--- check test | The delays break the protocol, and follow the rules
(lambda d: isinstance(d, list) and 1 <= len(d) <= 8 and all(isinstance(x, int) and 1 <= x <= 30 for x in d) and run_reordering(d) != [b"A", b"B", b"C"])(find_bad_delays())
?? Look for a slow copy of the first packet that arrives after the sender has come back round to sequence number 0.
--- check case | With no reordering the protocol is right
run_reordering([])
=> [b"A", b"B", b"C"]
--- check test | Equal delays for every packet (a FIFO link) never break it
all(run_reordering([d] * 1000) == [b"A", b"B", b"C"] for d in range(1, 31))

+++ question | Where the proof needs FIFO
--- ask
Case 2 of the proof says: a copy of the **previous** message cannot reach the receiver after it has delivered the current message. What is that step based on?
--- choice
The CRC, which catches any old packet.
--- choice correct
The link being FIFO: every copy of the previous message was sent before any copy of the current one, so all of them arrive first.
--- choice
The timeout being longer than the round-trip time.
--- choice
The sender ignoring ACKs with the wrong number.
--- why
An old copy is not damaged, so the CRC does not help, and the proof holds for any timeout, even a premature one. With one bit, an old copy of the previous message and the current message's successor carry the same number; only FIFO order stops a late old copy from being taken for new data. The counterexample problem shows exactly that failure when packets can overtake each other.

+++ question | Utilisation
--- ask
A stop-and-wait sender uses 1,000-byte packets on a 100 Mb/s link with a round-trip time of 10 ms. About what fraction of the time is it sending?
--- choice
About 50%.
--- choice
About 8%.
--- choice correct
About 0.8%.
--- choice
About 0.08%.
--- why
L/R = 8,000 bits / 100,000,000 bits per second = 0.08 ms. U = 0.08 / (10 + 0.08) ≈ 0.0079, under 1%. The link waits for the ACK almost all the time, which is why the next lesson sends many packets before waiting.

+++ question | Premature timeouts
--- ask
The timeout is set shorter than the round-trip time, so the sender retransmits every packet once even when nothing is lost. What happens?
--- choice
Messages are delivered twice.
--- choice
The protocol deadlocks.
--- choice correct
Every message is still delivered exactly once, in order, but twice as many data packets and ACKs are sent.
--- choice
Messages are skipped.
--- why
The receiver recognises the second copy by its sequence number and only re-ACKs it; the sender ignores the ACK for the old number. Correctness does not depend on the timeout at all, only efficiency does. (A sender that did not check the ACK's number would skip messages here: that is the debug problem.)

=== net-08 | Reliable transport II: sliding windows and Go-Back-N
--- teach
Last lesson you built stop-and-wait and proved it correct, and then measured it: on a fast link across a continent it sends for 12 microseconds and waits for 30 milliseconds, leaving the link idle more than 99.9% of the time. This lesson fixes that by keeping **many** packets in flight at once. You will build **Go-Back-N**, the first **sliding-window** protocol, and prove exactly how big the window may be for a given number of sequence-number bits.

### Filling the pipe

Picture a bucket brigade passing water along a line of people. Stop-and-wait is one bucket: you hand it over, wait for it to reach the fire and for the empty bucket to come back, and only then send the next. Nobody would fight a fire like that. You keep handing buckets as fast as you can, so the whole line is full.

A network link is the same. While the first packet travels, the sender can send the second, the third, and so on. The number of packets that fit "in the pipe" during one round trip is what the sender needs in flight to keep the link busy.

### The sliding window

The sender allows at most W packets that have been sent but not yet acknowledged. W is the **window size**. Picture the messages in a row, and a frame W wide laid over them:

```text
 acked       | sent, not acked  | may send | not yet allowed
 m0 m1 m2    | m3 m4 m5         | m6 m7    | m8 m9 ...
             ^ base             ^ next     ^ base + W
```

- **base** is the index of the oldest unacknowledged message;
- **next** is the index of the next message to send;
- the sender may send while next < base + W.

When the ACK for message `base` arrives, base moves right, and so does the whole window: that is why it is called a **sliding window**. New messages become sendable at its right edge.

### Numbers that wrap around

A packet carries its sequence number in a fixed number of bits k, so there are only M = 2^k different numbers. Message i carries i mod M, the remainder from Discrete Mathematics. With k = 2, messages 0 to 7 carry 0, 1, 2, 3, 0, 1, 2, 3. The sender and receiver keep **indexes** that grow forever; only the packets carry the wrapped number.

```python
M = 2 ** 2
print([i % M for i in range(8)])      # [0, 1, 2, 3, 0, 1, 2, 3]
print((1 - 3) % M)                    # 2: how far 1 is ahead of 3, going round
```

Python's `%` always gives an answer from 0 to M − 1, even for a negative left side, so `(a - b) % M` is "how many steps forward from b to a, going round the circle".

### Go-Back-N

In Go-Back-N the receiver is as simple as possible: it accepts packets **only in order**.

**The receiver** keeps e, the sequence number it expects next, starting at 0.

- On DATA(e), undamaged: deliver it, and set e to (e + 1) mod M.
- On any other undamaged DATA: throw it away. It is either a duplicate or it came after a gap.
- After every undamaged DATA, send ACK((e − 1) mod M): the number of the **last message delivered in order**. This is a **[[cumulative ACK|cumulative]]**: "I have everything up to and including this one."

**The sender** keeps base and next.

- Send while next < base + W and messages remain. Start the timer when sending into an empty window (when base equals next).
- On an undamaged ACK(a): the message it acknowledges is the one whose index j is in the window (base ≤ j < next) with j mod M = a. Its offset from base is `(a - base) % M`. If that offset is less than next − base, set base to j + 1: everything up to j is acknowledged at once. Otherwise the ACK is old: ignore it. If the window is now empty, stop the timer; otherwise restart it. Then send whatever the window now allows.
- On timeout: restart the timer and resend **every** message from base to next − 1. That is the "go back N" in the name.

There is one timer, for the oldest unacknowledged message.

### A run with one loss

Window W = 3, sequence numbers of k = 2 bits (M = 4), one-way delay 10 ms, timeout 30 ms, five messages, and packet 1 (the first send of m1) is lost:

| time | event |
|---|---|
| 0 | send m0, m1, m2 (seq 0, 1, 2); m1 is lost |
| 10 | receiver: m0 delivered, ACK 0. m2 is out of order: thrown away, ACK 0 again |
| 20 | sender: first ACK 0 moves base to 1, sends m3 (seq 3). Second ACK 0: offset (0 − 1) mod 4 = 3, not less than 3, ignored |
| 30 | receiver: m3 out of order, thrown away, ACK 0 |
| 50 | timeout: resend m1, m2, m3 |
| 60 | receiver: delivers m1, m2, m3; ACK 1, ACK 2, ACK 3 |
| 70 | sender: base moves to 2, 3, 4; sends m4 with seq 4 mod 4 = 0 |
| 80 | receiver: seq 0 is what it expects after 3: delivers m4, ACK 0 |
| 90 | sender: done |

One lost packet cost the resending of m2 and m3 too, which had arrived fine. That is Go-Back-N's weakness, and the reason for the next lesson's selective repeat.

### How big may the window be?

With k bits there are M = 2^k sequence numbers. Why not use a window of M?

Here is what goes wrong with k = 2 and W = 4. The sender sends m0 to m3, with seq 0, 1, 2, 3. All four arrive and are delivered, and the receiver now expects seq 0 (for m4). All four ACKs are lost. The sender times out and resends m0 to m3. The resent m0 carries seq 0, exactly what the receiver expects, so it delivers m0 **again**, as if it were m4. The receiver cannot tell "a new window" from "the old window again", because both use the same numbers.

**Claim: over a FIFO link, Go-Back-N is correct whenever W ≤ M − 1.** Let r be the index the receiver expects next.

1. **r is inside the window's reach:** base ≤ r ≤ next. The receiver has only received messages the sender sent, so r ≤ next; and base only moves past an index j when an ACK says j was delivered, so base ≤ r.
2. **Every DATA in flight has an index i with r − W ≤ i < r + W.** Upper end: i was sent while i < base + W, and base never moves back, so i < base + W ≤ r + W. Lower end: the copy of message r − 1 that the receiver accepted, and everything sent before it, has already arrived (FIFO). Any copy still in flight was sent after it, when next was already at least r, so base was at least r − W, and i ≥ base ≥ r − W.
3. **Among those indexes, only r itself has the sequence number r mod M.** Two indexes with the same number differ by a multiple of M. Every other index in the range differs from r by at least 1 and at most W, and W ≤ M − 1 < M. So a packet carrying the expected number really is message r.

A similar argument shows the sender never mistakes an old ACK for a new one: old ACKs in flight can only be for index base − 1, whose offset works out to M − 1, which is at least W, so it is ignored.

The counterexample above has W = M. At W = M − 1 = 3, the resent m0 would carry seq 0 while the receiver expects seq 3 (for m3), and it would be thrown away correctly.

### What it costs

- **Throughput with no losses:** the sender can have W packets in flight per round trip, so utilisation is U = min(1, W·(L/R) / (RTT + L/R)). To keep the link fully busy, W must be at least (RTT + L/R) / (L/R): the round trip measured in packet times. For the gigabit link from last lesson, that is 30.012 / 0.012, about 2,500 packets.
- **Cost of a loss:** one lost packet makes the sender resend up to W packets. With a big window and a lossy link, most of the traffic can be needless copies.
- **State:** the sender keeps up to W unacknowledged messages for resending; the receiver keeps only one number.

The number of packets needed to fill the pipe is the link's **[[bandwidth-delay product|bdp]]** divided by the packet size, and lesson 15 comes back to it.

### Where it is used

- **Sliding windows** are how TCP keeps a long, fast link busy; TCP's cumulative ACKs are Go-Back-N's idea with byte numbers instead of packet numbers.
- **[[CCSDS COP-1|cop1]]**, the protocol that sends telecommands to many spacecraft, is a Go-Back-N protocol with 8-bit sequence numbers.
- **HDLC and its descendants** on serial and radio links offer Go-Back-N as one of their modes, with 3-bit or 7-bit sequence numbers.

**Watch out:** the receiver must acknowledge the last message it delivered **in order**, (e − 1) mod M, not the sequence number of the packet that just arrived. If it ACKs an out-of-order packet's own number, the sender reads that cumulative ACK as "everything up to here arrived", slides past the lost packet, and it is never sent again.

::: context cumulative Why cumulative ACKs forgive lost ACKs
A cumulative ACK says "everything up to here", not "this one packet". So if the ACKs for messages 5 and 6 are lost but the ACK for 7 arrives, the sender learns all three were delivered and moves on without resending anything. Losing ACKs costs nothing as long as a later one gets through before the timer runs out. TCP's acknowledgement numbers work the same way, counting bytes: an ACK number of 5,001 means "I have every byte before 5,001, send me that one next".
:::

::: context bdp How much fits in the pipe
The bandwidth-delay product is the link's rate times its round-trip time: the number of bits that have been sent but whose acknowledgement has not come back yet, when the link is fully busy. A 1 Gb/s link with a 30 ms round trip holds 30 million bits, about 3.75 megabytes, in flight. A sender whose window is smaller than that cannot use the whole link, however fast the link is. That is why TCP's window can be scaled up to about a gigabyte.
:::

::: context cop1 Commanding a spacecraft with Go-Back-N
The CCSDS Communications Operation Procedure, COP-1, has two halves. On the ground, FOP-1 sends telecommand frames with 8-bit sequence numbers, keeps up to a window of them outstanding, and on a timeout or a report of a gap resends from the oldest unacknowledged frame. On board, FARM-1 accepts frames only in order and reports the next number it expects in a status word sent back in the telemetry, which acts as a cumulative acknowledgement. The window is limited to well under 256, for exactly the reason proved in this lesson.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 110" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">

  <text x="295" y="61" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">FARM-1 (craft)</text>
  <line x1="110" y1="48" x2="250" y2="48" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="180" y="40" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">commands 7, 8, 9</text>
  <line x1="250" y1="68" x2="110" y2="68" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
```

:::
--- task
The starter holds the simulator from the last lesson and a `run` function. Do not change them. Write Go-Back-N.

`Sender(sim, messages, timeout, window, bits)`:

- Raise `ValueError` if `window` is not from 1 to `2 ** bits - 1`.
- Attributes: `peer` (set by `run`), `done` (starts `False`), `done_at` (starts `None`).
- Message i is sent as `make_packet(DATA, i % 2 ** bits, messages[i])` with `sim.transmit(..., self.peer)`.
- `start()`: if there are no messages, set `done` and `done_at` to `sim.now`; otherwise send as the window allows.
- Keep `base` and `next`. Send while `next < base + window` and messages remain; start the timer (for `timeout` ms) when sending into an empty window (when `base == next`).
- `on_packet(raw)`: ignore damaged packets, non-ACKs, and anything once done. For ACK `a`, the offset is `(a - base) % 2 ** bits`. If it is at least `next - base`, ignore the ACK. Otherwise set `base` to `base + offset + 1`. If every message is now acknowledged, stop the timer and set `done` and `done_at`. Otherwise, stop the timer if the window is empty, or restart it if not, and send as the window allows.
- On timeout: restart the timer and resend every message from `base` to `next - 1`, in order.

`Receiver(sim, bits)`:

- Attributes: `peer` and `delivered` (a list). It expects sequence number 0 first.
- `on_packet(raw)`: ignore damaged packets and non-DATA. If the sequence number is the expected one, deliver the data and add 1 to the expected number, modulo `2 ** bits`. Then, for every undamaged DATA, send `make_packet(ACK, (expected - 1) % 2 ** bits)`.

For example, `run([b"m0", b"m1", b"m2", b"m3", b"m4"], 10, 30, 3, 2, lose={1})` is `([b"m0", b"m1", b"m2", b"m3", b"m4"], 15, 90)`, the run from the lesson.
--- starter
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, window, bits):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.window = window
        self.bits = bits
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None

    def start(self):
        pass

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        pass


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, bits):
        self.sim = sim
        self.bits = bits
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        pass


def run(messages, delay, timeout, window, bits, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim, bits)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout, window, bits)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- solution
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, window, bits):
        if not 1 <= window <= 2 ** bits - 1:
            raise ValueError("the window must be from 1 to 2 ** bits - 1")
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.window = window
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.base = 0
        self.next = 0

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.fill()

    def send(self, i):
        packet = make_packet(DATA, i % self.modulus, self.messages[i])
        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)

    def fill(self):
        while self.next < len(self.messages) and self.next < self.base + self.window:
            if self.base == self.next:
                self.timer.start(self.timeout)
            self.send(self.next)
            self.next += 1

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, ack, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK:
            return
        offset = (ack - self.base) % self.modulus
        if offset >= self.next - self.base:
            return
        self.base += offset + 1
        if self.base == len(self.messages):
            self.timer.stop()
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
            return
        if self.base == self.next:
            self.timer.stop()
        else:
            self.timer.start(self.timeout)
        self.fill()

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)
        for i in range(self.base, self.next):
            self.send(i)


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, bits):
        self.sim = sim
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        if seq == self.expected:
            self.delivered.append(data)
            self.expected = (self.expected + 1) % self.modulus
        last = (self.expected - 1) % self.modulus
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, last), self.peer)


def run(messages, delay, timeout, window, bits, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim, bits)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout, window, bits)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- hint
Give the sender a `Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)`, `base = 0`, `next = 0`, and two helpers: one that transmits message i, and one ("fill") that sends while the window allows, starting the timer when `base == next` just before a send.
--- hint
In `on_packet`, compute `offset = (a - self.base) % M` with `M = 2 ** bits`. Only an offset smaller than `self.next - self.base` acknowledges something new; then `self.base += offset + 1`. Finish, or fix the timer (stop if `base == next`, else restart), then call the fill helper.
--- hint
The receiver keeps `expected`. On in-order DATA it delivers and sets `expected = (expected + 1) % M`. Then it always sends `make_packet(ACK, (expected - 1) % M)`.
--- check case | The run from the lesson
run([b"m0", b"m1", b"m2", b"m3", b"m4"], 10, 30, 3, 2, lose={1})
=> ([b"m0", b"m1", b"m2", b"m3", b"m4"], 15, 90)
--- check case | No losses: a window of 4 sends 8 messages in two round trips
run([bytes([65 + i]) for i in range(8)], 10, 50, 4, 3)
=> ([b"A", b"B", b"C", b"D", b"E", b"F", b"G", b"H"], 16, 40)
--- check case | Window 1 is stop-and-wait
run([bytes([65 + i]) for i in range(8)], 10, 50, 1, 1)
=> ([b"A", b"B", b"C", b"D", b"E", b"F", b"G", b"H"], 16, 160)
--- check case | Losing the first packet of a big window resends all of it
run([bytes([65 + i]) for i in range(8)], 10, 50, 7, 3, lose={0})
=> ([b"A", b"B", b"C", b"D", b"E", b"F", b"G", b"H"], 29, 90)
--- check case | A window bigger than the number of messages, and no messages
(run([b"x", b"y", b"z"], 10, 50, 7, 3), run([], 10, 50, 7, 3))
=> (([b"x", b"y", b"z"], 6, 20), ([], 0, 0))
--- check test | The window must fit the sequence numbers

--- check test | 1,000 random runs: always exactly the messages, in order

?? Only in-order data is delivered, the receiver always ACKs the last in-order number, and the sender ignores ACKs whose offset is not inside the window.

+++ practice | How big a window fills the link
--- task
Write two functions for a sliding-window sender with no losses. `packet_bits` is the packet size L in bits, `rate` the link's rate R in bits per second, and `rtt` the round-trip time in seconds.

- `window_needed(packet_bits, rate, rtt)` returns the smallest whole number of packets W that keeps the link always busy: W·(L/R) must be at least RTT + L/R. Use exact arithmetic: `(rtt * rate + packet_bits)` divided by `packet_bits`, rounded up. (Pass `rtt` as a `fractions.Fraction` or an int to avoid rounding surprises; the checks do.)
- `utilisation(window, packet_bits, rate, rtt)` returns min(1, W·(L/R) / (RTT + L/R)).

For example, `window_needed(12000, 10**9, Fraction(3, 100))` is `2501`, and `utilisation(1, 12000, 10**9, Fraction(3, 100))` is the stop-and-wait figure, 1/2501.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def window_needed(packet_bits, rate, rtt):
    return 1


def utilisation(window, packet_bits, rate, rtt):
    return 1
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction
import math


def window_needed(packet_bits, rate, rtt):
    return math.ceil(Fraction(rtt) * rate / packet_bits + 1)


def utilisation(window, packet_bits, rate, rtt):
    send_time = Fraction(packet_bits, rate)
    return min(Fraction(1), window * send_time / (Fraction(rtt) + send_time))
--- hint
The pipe holds RTT + L/R seconds of sending, and one packet takes L/R seconds. Their ratio is (RTT·R + L) / L; round it up with `math.ceil`.
--- hint
Keep everything as `Fraction` so the answers are exact. `min(Fraction(1), ...)` caps the utilisation at 1.
--- check case | The example from the task
(window_needed(12000, 10**9, Fraction(3, 100)), utilisation(1, 12000, 10**9, Fraction(3, 100)))
=> (2501, Fraction(1, 2501))
--- check case | A window that exactly fills the link, and one bigger
(utilisation(2501, 12000, 10**9, Fraction(3, 100)), utilisation(5000, 12000, 10**9, Fraction(3, 100)))
=> (Fraction(1), Fraction(1))
--- check case | No delay at all: one packet at a time is enough
(window_needed(1000, 1000, 0), utilisation(1, 1000, 1000, 0))
=> (1, Fraction(1))
--- check case | A round trip that is not a whole number of packet times rounds up
window_needed(1000, 1000, Fraction(5, 2))
=> 4
--- check case | Half a pipe
utilisation(2, 1000, 1000, 3)
=> Fraction(1, 2)

+++ practice | The Go-Back-N receiver on its own
--- task
Write the Go-Back-N receiver as a plain function. `gbn_receive(arrivals, bits)` takes the list of data packets that reach the receiver, in order, each a tuple `(seq, data, ok)`, where `ok` is `False` for a damaged packet. Sequence numbers are modulo `2 ** bits`.

The receiver expects 0 first. A damaged packet is dropped with no answer. An undamaged packet with the expected number is delivered, and the expected number goes up by 1 (modulo `2 ** bits`). Every undamaged packet, in order or not, is answered with ACK `(expected - 1) % 2 ** bits`.

Return `(delivered, acks)`. For example, `gbn_receive([(0, b"a", True), (2, b"c", True), (1, b"b", True), (2, b"c", True)], 2)` is `([b"a", b"b", b"c"], [0, 0, 1, 2])`.
--- starter
def gbn_receive(arrivals, bits):
    return ([d for s, d, ok in arrivals if ok], [s for s, d, ok in arrivals if ok])
--- solution
def gbn_receive(arrivals, bits):
    modulus = 2 ** bits
    expected = 0
    delivered = []
    acks = []
    for seq, data, ok in arrivals:
        if not ok:
            continue
        if seq == expected:
            delivered.append(data)
            expected = (expected + 1) % modulus
        acks.append((expected - 1) % modulus)
    return delivered, acks
--- hint
Keep `expected`. Deliver only when `seq == expected`, then add 1 modulo `2 ** bits`.
--- hint
The ACK is not the packet's own number: it is always `(expected - 1) % 2 ** bits`, the last number delivered in order.
--- check case | The example from the task
gbn_receive([(0, b"a", True), (2, b"c", True), (1, b"b", True), (2, b"c", True)], 2)
=> ([b"a", b"b", b"c"], [0, 0, 1, 2])
--- check case | Out of order before anything: ACK of the number before 0
gbn_receive([(1, b"b", True)], 3)
=> ([], [7])
--- check case | Wrapping around with 1-bit numbers
gbn_receive([(0, b"a", True), (1, b"b", True), (0, b"c", True), (0, b"c", True), (1, b"d", True)], 1)
=> ([b"a", b"b", b"c", b"d"], [0, 1, 0, 0, 1])
--- check case | Damaged packets get no answer
gbn_receive([(0, b"a", False), (0, b"a", True), (1, b"b", False)], 2)
=> ([b"a"], [0])
--- check case | Nothing
gbn_receive([], 4)
=> ([], [])

+++ practice | Reading a cumulative ACK
--- task
Sender and receiver keep indexes that grow forever, but a packet carries only the index modulo M = 2 ** bits. Turning an ACK's number back into an index is modular arithmetic.

Write `new_base(ack, base, next_index, bits)`. The sender's window holds the indexes `base` to `next_index - 1` (at most M − 1 of them). A cumulative ACK with number `ack` acknowledges the index j in that range with j mod M equal to `ack`, and everything before it. Return the new base, j + 1. If no index in the window has that number (the ACK is old), return `base` unchanged.

For example, with `bits` 2 the window 6, 7, 8 carries the numbers 2, 3, 0. So `new_base(0, 6, 9, 2)` is `9`, and `new_base(1, 6, 9, 2)` is `6`, because no index in the window has the number 1.
--- starter
def new_base(ack, base, next_index, bits):
    return ack + 1
--- solution
def new_base(ack, base, next_index, bits):
    offset = (ack - base) % (2 ** bits)
    if offset < next_index - base:
        return base + offset + 1
    return base
--- hint
`(ack - base) % M` is how many steps forward from `base`'s number to `ack`, going round the circle of numbers.
--- hint
That offset names an index in the window only if it is less than the window's current size, `next_index - base`.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(new_base(1, 6, 9, 2), new_base(0, 6, 9, 2))
=> (6, 9)
--- check case | The first index in the window
new_base(2, 6, 9, 2)
=> 7
--- check case | An empty window: nothing to acknowledge
new_base(5, 12, 12, 3)
=> 12
--- check case | The old ACK for base - 1 is ignored
new_base(3, 12, 19, 3)
=> 12
--- check test | Agrees with a search over the window for many cases
all(new_base(a, b, n, k) == next((j + 1 for j in range(b, n) if j % 2 ** k == a), b) for k in range(1, 5) for b in range(0, 40, 3) for n in range(b, b + 2 ** k) for a in range(2 ** k))

+++ practice | A Go-Back-N window with no simulator
--- task
Write the sender's window as a class with no timer and no simulator, so every rule can be tested on its own. `GbnWindow(count, window, bits)` manages `count` messages with the given window size; raise `ValueError` unless `window` is from 1 to `2 ** bits - 1`.

- `send()`: if the window allows another message (next < base + window, and next < count), return its sequence number (next modulo `2 ** bits`) and add 1 to next; otherwise return `None`.
- `ack(a)`: apply a cumulative ACK with number `a` as Go-Back-N does, and return how many messages it newly acknowledged (0 for an old ACK).
- `timeout()`: return the list of sequence numbers to resend (from base to next − 1).
- `finished()`: `True` when all `count` messages are acknowledged.

For example, with `w = GbnWindow(5, 3, 2)`, three `w.send()` calls return 0, 1 and 2, a fourth returns `None`, `w.ack(0)` returns 1, and then `w.send()` returns 3.
--- starter
class GbnWindow:
    def __init__(self, count, window, bits):
        self.count = count

    def send(self):
        return None

    def ack(self, a):
        return 0

    def timeout(self):
        return []

    def finished(self):
        return True
--- solution
class GbnWindow:
    def __init__(self, count, window, bits):
        if not 1 <= window <= 2 ** bits - 1:
            raise ValueError("window too big for the sequence numbers")
        self.count = count
        self.window = window
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.base = 0
        self.next = 0

    def send(self):
        if self.next >= self.count or self.next >= self.base + self.window:
            return None
        seq = self.next % self.modulus
        self.next += 1
        return seq

    def ack(self, a):
        offset = (a - self.base) % self.modulus
        if offset >= self.next - self.base:
            return 0
        self.base += offset + 1
        return offset + 1

    def timeout(self):
        return [i % self.modulus for i in range(self.base, self.next)]

    def finished(self):
        return self.base == self.count
--- hint
Keep `base` and `next` as ever-growing indexes and only wrap them (`% 2 ** bits`) when you hand out a sequence number.
--- hint
`ack` uses the offset rule from the lesson; the number newly acknowledged is `offset + 1`.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda w: (w.send(), w.send(), w.send(), w.send(), w.ack(0), w.send()))(GbnWindow(5, 3, 2))
=> (0, 1, 2, None, 1, 3)
--- check case | Numbers wrap after 3 with 2 bits
(lambda w: [w.send(), w.send(), w.send(), w.ack(2), w.send(), w.send(), w.send()])(GbnWindow(6, 3, 2))
=> [0, 1, 2, 3, 3, 0, 1]
--- check case | A duplicate or old ACK acknowledges nothing
(lambda w: (w.send(), w.send(), w.ack(0), w.ack(0), w.ack(3), w.timeout()))(GbnWindow(4, 3, 2))
=> (0, 1, 1, 0, 0, [1])
--- check case | finished, and a timeout with nothing outstanding
(lambda w: (w.finished(), w.send(), w.send(), w.send(), w.ack(1), w.finished(), w.timeout()))(GbnWindow(2, 3, 2))
=> (False, 0, 1, None, 2, True, [])
--- check test | Window limits and no messages
raises(ValueError, lambda: GbnWindow(3, 4, 2)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: GbnWindow(3, 0, 1)) and GbnWindow(0, 1, 1).finished() and GbnWindow(0, 1, 1).send() is None
--- check case | A timeout resends the whole window, wrapped
(lambda w: ([w.send() for _ in range(4)], w.ack(0), w.ack(1), w.ack(2), [w.send() for _ in range(3)], w.timeout()))(GbnWindow(8, 3, 2))
=> ([0, 1, 2, None], 1, 1, 1, [3, 0, 1], [3, 0, 1])

+++ practice | Debug: the receiver that ACKs the wrong number
--- task
This Go-Back-N run loses messages: with packet 1 lost, `run([b"m0", b"m1", b"m2", b"m3"], 10, 30, 3, 2, lose={1})` delivers some messages but never `b"m1"`, and the sender still finishes. The sender is correct. Find the bug in the receiver and fix it.
--- starter
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, window, bits):
        if not 1 <= window <= 2 ** bits - 1:
            raise ValueError("the window must be from 1 to 2 ** bits - 1")
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.window = window
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.base = 0
        self.next = 0

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.fill()

    def send(self, i):
        packet = make_packet(DATA, i % self.modulus, self.messages[i])
        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)

    def fill(self):
        while self.next < len(self.messages) and self.next < self.base + self.window:
            if self.base == self.next:
                self.timer.start(self.timeout)
            self.send(self.next)
            self.next += 1

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, ack, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK:
            return
        offset = (ack - self.base) % self.modulus
        if offset >= self.next - self.base:
            return
        self.base += offset + 1
        if self.base == len(self.messages):
            self.timer.stop()
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
            return
        if self.base == self.next:
            self.timer.stop()
        else:
            self.timer.start(self.timeout)
        self.fill()

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)
        for i in range(self.base, self.next):
            self.send(i)


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, bits):
        self.sim = sim
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        if seq == self.expected:
            self.delivered.append(data)
            self.expected = (self.expected + 1) % self.modulus
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)


def run(messages, delay, timeout, window, bits, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim, bits)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout, window, bits)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- solution
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, window, bits):
        if not 1 <= window <= 2 ** bits - 1:
            raise ValueError("the window must be from 1 to 2 ** bits - 1")
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.window = window
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.base = 0
        self.next = 0

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.fill()

    def send(self, i):
        packet = make_packet(DATA, i % self.modulus, self.messages[i])
        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)

    def fill(self):
        while self.next < len(self.messages) and self.next < self.base + self.window:
            if self.base == self.next:
                self.timer.start(self.timeout)
            self.send(self.next)
            self.next += 1

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, ack, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK:
            return
        offset = (ack - self.base) % self.modulus
        if offset >= self.next - self.base:
            return
        self.base += offset + 1
        if self.base == len(self.messages):
            self.timer.stop()
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
            return
        if self.base == self.next:
            self.timer.stop()
        else:
            self.timer.start(self.timeout)
        self.fill()

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)
        for i in range(self.base, self.next):
            self.send(i)


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, bits):
        self.sim = sim
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        if seq == self.expected:
            self.delivered.append(data)
            self.expected = (self.expected + 1) % self.modulus
        last = (self.expected - 1) % self.modulus
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, last), self.peer)


def run(messages, delay, timeout, window, bits, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim, bits)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout, window, bits)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- hint
Follow m2 when m1 was lost. The receiver throws m2 away, and then which ACK does it send? What does a cumulative ACK with that number tell the sender?
--- hint
A cumulative ACK must name the last message delivered in order: `(self.expected - 1) % self.modulus`, not the number of the packet that just arrived.
--- check case | The run from the task delivers everything
run([b"m0", b"m1", b"m2", b"m3"], 10, 30, 3, 2, lose={1})
=> ([b"m0", b"m1", b"m2", b"m3"], 13, 70)
--- check case | No losses
run([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 10, 30, 3, 2)
=> ([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 6, 20)
--- check test | Random losses never lose data
(lambda R: all(run([bytes([i]) for i in range(12)], 10, 35, 3, 2, {i for i in range(200) if R.random() < 0.3})[0] == [bytes([i]) for i in range(12)] for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(5))

+++ practice | Why the window must be smaller than 2 to the power k
--- task
The lesson proved Go-Back-N correct when the window is at most M − 1, where M = 2 ** bits, and showed by hand that a window of M fails. Find such a failure by search.

The starter holds a correct Go-Back-N over the lesson's FIFO simulator, except that its sender **allows** a window of exactly `2 ** bits`. `run_full_window(bits, messages, lose)` runs it with a window of `2 ** bits`, a delay of 10 ms and a timeout of 50 ms, and returns what was delivered.

Write `find_bad_losses(bits)`. It must return a set of packet numbers to lose, all less than 40, such that `run_full_window(bits, [bytes([i]) for i in range(2 * 2 ** bits)], lose)` does **not** deliver exactly those messages. Search: try every set of up to `2 ** bits` numbers below `3 * 2 ** bits`, smallest sets first, and return the first that fails.
--- starter

class FullWindowSender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, bits):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.window = 2 ** bits
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.base = 0
        self.next = 0

    def start(self):
        self.fill()

    def send(self, i):
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(DATA, i % self.modulus, self.messages[i]), self.peer)

    def fill(self):

            if self.base == self.next:
                self.timer.start(self.timeout)
            self.send(self.next)
            self.next += 1

            self.done = True
            return

            self.timer.stop()
        else:

        self.fill()

    def on_timeout(self):

            self.send(i)

import itertools
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):

        self.sent += 1

            return

class FullWindowSender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, bits):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.window = 2 ** bits
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)
        self.base = 0
        self.next = 0

    def start(self):
        self.fill()

    def send(self, i):
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(DATA, i % self.modulus, self.messages[i]), self.peer)

    def fill(self):

            if self.base == self.next:
                self.timer.start(self.timeout)
            self.send(self.next)
            self.next += 1

    def on_packet(self, raw):

        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, ack, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK:
            return
        offset = (ack - self.base) % self.modulus
        if offset >= self.next - self.base:
            return
        self.base += offset + 1
        if self.base == len(self.messages):
            self.timer.stop()
            self.done = True
            return
        if self.base == self.next:
            self.timer.stop()
        else:
            self.timer.start(self.timeout)
        self.fill()

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.timer.start(self.timeout)
        for i in range(self.base, self.next):
            self.send(i)


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, bits):
        self.sim = sim
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        if seq == self.expected:
            self.delivered.append(data)
            self.expected = (self.expected + 1) % self.modulus
        self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, (self.expected - 1) % self.modulus), self.peer)


def run_full_window(bits, messages, lose):
    sim = Sim(10, lose)
    receiver = Receiver(sim, bits)
    sender = FullWindowSender(sim, messages, 50, bits)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run(limit=20000)
    return receiver.delivered


def find_bad_losses(bits):
    m = 2 ** bits
    messages = [bytes([i]) for i in range(2 * m)]
    for size in range(m + 1):
        for lose in itertools.combinations(range(3 * m), size):
            if run_full_window(bits, messages, set(lose)) != messages:
                return set(lose)
    return None
--- hint
`itertools.combinations(range(3 * 2 ** bits), size)` gives every set of a given size. Loop over sizes from 0 up to `2 ** bits`, run each, and return `set(...)` of the first one that goes wrong.
--- hint
The failure to look for: the whole first window arrives and is delivered, but every ACK for it is lost. The sender resends the old window, and its first packet carries the number the receiver now expects for a new message.
--- check test | The losses make the full window fail, for 1, 2 and 3 bits
all((lambda lose: isinstance(lose, set) and all(isinstance(n, int) and 0 <= n < 40 for n in lose) and run_full_window(k, [bytes([i]) for i in range(2 * 2 ** k)], lose) != [bytes([i]) for i in range(2 * 2 ** k)])(find_bad_losses(k)) for k in (1, 2, 3))
?? Lose every ACK of the first window.
--- check case | With no losses even the full window works
(run_full_window(2, [bytes([i]) for i in range(8)], set()), run_full_window(1, [b"a", b"b"], set()))
=> ([bytes([i]) for i in range(8)], [b"a", b"b"])
--- check test | The search tries small sets first: at most 2 ** bits losses
len(find_bad_losses(2)) <= 4 and len(find_bad_losses(1)) <= 2

+++ question | The step that needs W less than M
--- ask
The proof that Go-Back-N is correct shows every DATA in flight has an index i with r − W ≤ i < r + W, where r is the index the receiver expects. Why does that make W ≤ M − 1 enough?
--- choice
Because the receiver has room for at most M − 1 messages.
--- choice correct
Two indexes with the same sequence number differ by a multiple of M, and every other index in that range differs from r by 1 to W, which is less than M; so only message r can carry the expected number.
--- choice
Because the sender never has more than W messages to resend.
--- choice
Because cumulative ACKs cover M − 1 messages each.
--- why
The receiver only sees i mod M. It can mistake message i for message r exactly when i ≡ r (mod M) with i ≠ r, which needs |i − r| ≥ M. The range argument bounds |i − r| by W, so W < M rules it out. With W = M, the old copy at i = r − M fits in the range, which is the counterexample.

+++ question | The cost of one loss
--- ask
A Go-Back-N sender has a window of 100 packets, all in flight, and the first of them is lost. Nothing else is lost. How many packets does it resend at the timeout?
--- choice
1: only the lost one.
--- choice correct
100: every packet from base to next − 1, although 99 of them arrived.
--- choice
50 on average.
--- choice
None: the receiver asks for the missing packet.
--- why
The Go-Back-N receiver throws away everything after a gap, so the 99 good packets were discarded and must be sent again. That waste grows with the window, which is why long, fast, lossy paths need selective repeat, the next lesson.

+++ question | Filling a long link
--- ask
A link runs at 10 Mb/s with a round-trip time of 100 ms, and packets are 1,250 bytes (10,000 bits). About how big must a sliding window be to keep the link busy?
--- choice
About 10 packets.
--- choice correct
About 101 packets.
--- choice
About 1,000 packets.
--- choice
1 packet is enough, since the link is slow.
--- why
One packet takes 10,000 / 10,000,000 = 1 ms to send. The pipe holds RTT + L/R = 101 ms of sending, which is 101 packets. With fewer in flight, the link waits for ACKs; with a window of 1 it is stop-and-wait, busy 1% of the time.

=== net-08b | Reliable transport III: selective repeat and wrap-around
--- teach
Last lesson Go-Back-N kept the pipe full, but one lost packet made it resend the whole window, even the packets that had arrived safely, because its receiver threw away everything after a gap. This lesson builds **selective repeat**: the receiver keeps good packets that arrive early, and the sender resends only the ones that were really lost. The price is a subtler question about sequence numbers, and you will prove the exact answer: with k bits, the window can be at most half of 2^k.

### Keeping the pages that arrive early

Picture the editor from the stop-and-wait lesson, now receiving pages from a fast courier service that loses one now and then. Pages 1, 2, 4 and 5 arrive; page 3 is missing. A Go-Back-N editor would throw away pages 4 and 5 and wait for everything from page 3 again. A sensible editor keeps 4 and 5 on the desk, tells you exactly which pages arrived, and waits only for page 3. When it comes, she files 3, 4 and 5 together, in order.

### Selective repeat

**The receiver** has a window too: W sequence numbers starting at `expected`, the next one it needs to deliver.

- On an undamaged DATA whose number is **inside** its window: ACK that number (an **individual ACK**, meaning only this packet), and store the data in a buffer if it is not there already. Then deliver from the buffer as long as the next expected packet is there, moving `expected` forward each time.
- On an undamaged DATA whose number is in the W numbers **just below** its window: it was already delivered, and its ACK must have been lost. ACK it again, and deliver nothing.
- Anything else: ignore it.

**The sender** has one timer **per packet** in flight.

- Send while next < base + W. Each packet gets its own timer when sent.
- On ACK(a): find the index j in the window with j mod M = a, using the same offset rule as last lesson, `(a - base) % M` less than next − base. Mark j acknowledged and stop its timer. If j was base, slide base forward past every acknowledged message, and send what the window now allows.
- When a packet's timer runs out, resend **that packet only** and restart its timer.

A trace with W = 3, sequence numbers of 3 bits (M = 8), delay 10 ms, timeout 30 ms, five messages, and the first send of m1 lost:

| time | event |
|---|---|
| 0 | send m0, m1, m2; m1 is lost |
| 10 | receiver: m0 delivered, ACK 0. m2 is inside the window: buffered, ACK 2 |
| 20 | sender: ACK 0 slides base to 1, sends m3. ACK 2 marks m2 done; base stays at 1 |
| 30 | m1's timer runs out: resend m1 only. Receiver buffers m3, ACK 3 |
| 40 | receiver: m1 arrives, and m1, m2, m3 are delivered together; ACK 1 |
| 50 | sender: ACK 1 slides base to 4, sends m4 |
| 70 | ACK 4 arrives: done |

That run sent 11 packets. Go-Back-N with the same loss resends m2 and m3 too.

### Numbers on a circle

A sequence number of k bits lives on a circle of M = 2^k positions: after M − 1 comes 0 again. To ask "how far ahead of b is a?", go round the circle: `(a - b) % M`.

The receiver's two regions, drawn on a circle of 8 numbers with W = 3 and `expected` = 6:

```text
already delivered (re-ACK):   3 4 5
receiver's window (accept):   6 7 0
never valid right now:        1 2
```

```python
M, W, expected = 8, 3, 6
for seq in (7, 0, 4, 1):
    offset = (seq - expected) % M
    print(seq, offset, offset < W, offset >= M - W)
# 7 1 True False    inside the window
# 0 2 True False    inside the window, after wrapping round
# 4 6 False True    just below: re-ACK
# 1 3 False False   neither
```

For this to work, the two regions must not overlap. Each has W numbers, so together they need 2W distinct numbers: 2W ≤ M.

### Why the window can be at most half

First, the failure, with M = 4 and W = 3. The sender sends m0, m1, m2 with numbers 0, 1, 2. All arrive and are delivered, so the receiver's window moves to the numbers 3, 0, 1, for messages m3, m4, m5. Every ACK is lost. m0's timer runs out and it is resent, carrying number 0. Number 0 is inside the receiver's window, standing for m4, so the old m0 is buffered as if it were m4. The data is now wrong, and nothing will ever notice.

**Claim: over a FIFO link, selective repeat is correct whenever 2W ≤ M.** Let r be the index the receiver expects next, and b the sender's base.

1. **r − W ≤ b ≤ r.** The sender's base only passes index j when j and everything before it were acknowledged, so were received and delivered: b ≤ r. And the receiver received message r − 1, so the sender had sent it: next ≥ r, and next ≤ b + W, so b ≥ r − W.
2. **Every DATA in flight has an index i with r − W ≤ i < r + W.** Upper end: i was sent while i < (base at the time) + W ≤ b + W ≤ r + W. Lower end: the copy of message r − 1 that arrived, and everything sent before it, has already arrived, because the link is FIFO. A copy still in flight was sent later, when next was already at least r, so its index was at least r − W.
3. **The receiver classifies every such packet correctly.** The indexes from r − W to r + W − 1 are 2W consecutive whole numbers. If 2W ≤ M, no two of them have the same remainder mod M, so each sequence number the receiver sees stands for exactly one of them. Those from r − W to r − 1 land in the "re-ACK" region, and those from r to r + W − 1 land in the window, each under its own number.

With 2W > M the range holds two indexes with the same number, and the counterexample above is one of them.

On the Internet, packets can be reordered, so the FIFO step fails. Real protocols replace it with a **maximum packet lifetime**: IP's TTL, plus the assumption that no packet survives in the network longer than a couple of minutes. A sender then must not reuse a sequence number until every older packet carrying it must have died. That is why TCP's 32-bit numbers can [[run out on very fast links|tcp-wrap]].

### Comparing wrapped numbers

"Is a newer than b?" is not `a > b` once numbers wrap: with 8-bit numbers, 3 comes right after 254. The rule used by TCP and many other protocols, **serial number arithmetic** (RFC 1982), says a is newer than b when `(a - b) % M` is between 1 and M/2 − 1. It works as long as the two numbers are less than half the circle apart, the same condition as the window bound.

### What it costs

| | Go-Back-N | selective repeat |
|---|---|---|
| sender resends after a loss | up to W packets | the lost packet only |
| receiver buffer | none | up to W packets |
| timers | one | one per packet in flight |
| largest window with k bits | 2^k − 1 | 2^(k−1) |
| ACKs | cumulative | individual |

With loss probability p per packet and no other effects, selective repeat sends each message about 1/(1 − p) times, however big the window is. Go-Back-N can send far more on a long, lossy link, since every loss costs a whole window.

### Where it is used

- **TCP with SACK** (selective acknowledgements) tells the sender exactly which byte ranges arrived, so it resends only the gaps: selective repeat inside TCP.
- **[[QUIC|quic-numbers]]** never reuses a packet number, so it avoids the wrap-around problem entirely.
- **[[Deep-space file delivery|cfdp]]** uses selective repeat, because with a round trip of many minutes, resending good data would waste hours of antenna time.

**Watch out:** a selective-repeat receiver must re-ACK a packet it has **already delivered**. If it ignores numbers just below its window, a lost ACK is never replaced: the sender keeps resending that packet, the receiver keeps ignoring it, and the transfer never finishes. That region of the circle exists precisely for these late copies.

::: context tcp-wrap When 32 bits are not enough
TCP numbers bytes, not packets, with 32-bit sequence numbers: 2^32 bytes, about 4.3 GB, before they repeat. At 10 Mb/s that takes almost an hour, longer than any packet survives. At 10 Gb/s it takes about 3.4 seconds, while IP only promises that packets die within about 2 minutes. So a very old, delayed segment could carry a number that has come round again. TCP's timestamps option, with a check called PAWS (Protection Against Wrapped Sequences), adds a second number that tells old segments from new ones.
:::

::: context quic-numbers Numbers that are never reused
QUIC gives every packet a new packet number, counting up from 0 to at most 2^62 − 1, and never sends two packets with the same number, not even a retransmission: resent data goes in a new packet with a new number. On the wire it sends only the lowest 1 to 4 bytes of the number, and the receiver rebuilds the full number from the largest one it has seen, picking the candidate closest to what it expects. The practice problems include that decoding step.
:::

::: context cfdp Sending files across the solar system
The CCSDS File Delivery Protocol, CFDP, moves files between spacecraft and Earth. In its acknowledged mode the receiver keeps every segment that arrives and, when it notices gaps, sends a NAK listing exactly which byte ranges are missing; the sender resends only those. With a Mars round trip of up to 44 minutes, a Go-Back-N style resend of everything after a gap would waste enormous amounts of link time, so selective repeat is the only sensible choice.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 100" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="30" width="40" height="24" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="60" y="30" width="40" height="24" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="100" y="30" width="40" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <rect x="140" y="30" width="40" height="24" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="180" y="30" width="40" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <rect x="220" y="30" width="40" height="24" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="120" y="80" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">missing</text>
  <text x="200" y="80" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">missing</text>
  <text x="300" y="46" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">NAK: only these</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
The starter holds the lesson's simulator and a `run` function. Do not change them. Write selective repeat.

`Sender(sim, messages, timeout, window, bits)`:

- Raise `ValueError` if `window` is not from 1 to `2 ** (bits - 1)`.
- Attributes: `peer`, `done` (starts `False`) and `done_at` (starts `None`).
- Message i is sent as `make_packet(DATA, i % 2 ** bits, messages[i])`. Each message in flight has its **own** `Timer`; sending (or resending) message i starts its timer for `timeout` ms, and when it runs out, message i alone is sent again.
- `start()`: with no messages, set `done` and `done_at`; otherwise send while `next < base + window` and messages remain.
- `on_packet(raw)`: ignore damaged packets, non-ACKs, and anything once done. For ACK `a`, if `(a - base) % 2 ** bits` is at least `next - base`, ignore it. Otherwise it names index `j = base + offset`: if j is already acknowledged, ignore it; else mark it, stop its timer, and move `base` forward past every acknowledged index. If all messages are acknowledged, set `done` and `done_at`; otherwise send what the window now allows.

`Receiver(sim, bits, window)`:

- Attributes: `peer` and `delivered`. It expects sequence number 0 first and keeps a buffer.
- `on_packet(raw)`: ignore damaged packets and non-DATA. Let `offset = (seq - expected) % 2 ** bits`. If `offset < window`: send `make_packet(ACK, seq)`, store the data if that number is not already stored, then deliver from the buffer while the expected number is stored, adding 1 (modulo `2 ** bits`) each time. Else if `offset >= 2 ** bits - window`: send `make_packet(ACK, seq)` only. Otherwise ignore it.

For example, `run([b"m0", b"m1", b"m2", b"m3", b"m4"], 10, 30, 3, 3, lose={1})` is `([b"m0", b"m1", b"m2", b"m3", b"m4"], 11, 70)`, the lesson's trace.
--- starter
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, window, bits):
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.window = window
        self.bits = bits
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None

    def start(self):
        pass

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        pass


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, bits, window):
        self.sim = sim
        self.bits = bits
        self.window = window
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        pass


def run(messages, delay, timeout, window, bits, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim, bits, window)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout, window, bits)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- solution
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, window, bits):
        if not 1 <= window <= 2 ** (bits - 1):
            raise ValueError("the window must be from 1 to 2 ** (bits - 1)")
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.window = window
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None
        self.timers = {}
        self.acked = set()
        self.base = 0
        self.next = 0

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.fill()

    def send(self, i):
        packet = make_packet(DATA, i % self.modulus, self.messages[i])
        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)
        self.timers[i].start(self.timeout)

    def fill(self):
        while self.next < len(self.messages) and self.next < self.base + self.window:
            i = self.next
            self.timers[i] = Timer(self.sim, lambda i=i: self.send(i))
            self.send(i)
            self.next += 1

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, ack, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK:
            return
        offset = (ack - self.base) % self.modulus
        if offset >= self.next - self.base:
            return
        j = self.base + offset
        if j in self.acked:
            return
        self.acked.add(j)
        self.timers.pop(j).stop()
        while self.base in self.acked:
            self.acked.remove(self.base)
            self.base += 1
        if self.base == len(self.messages):
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.fill()


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, bits, window):
        self.sim = sim
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.window = window
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0
        self.buffer = {}

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        offset = (seq - self.expected) % self.modulus
        if offset < self.window:
            self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)
            if seq not in self.buffer:
                self.buffer[seq] = data
            while self.expected in self.buffer:
                self.delivered.append(self.buffer.pop(self.expected))
                self.expected = (self.expected + 1) % self.modulus
        elif offset >= self.modulus - self.window:
            self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)


def run(messages, delay, timeout, window, bits, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim, bits, window)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout, window, bits)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- hint
Give the sender a dict from index to its `Timer`, a set of acknowledged indexes, `base` and `next`. When you first send message i, create `Timer(self.sim, lambda i=i: self.send(i))`: the `i=i` freezes the current value of i inside the lambda.
--- hint
In `on_packet`, after marking j and stopping its timer, slide with `while self.base in self.acked:` (removing each one from the set as you pass it). Then finish, or send what the window allows.
--- hint
The receiver's buffer is a dict keyed by sequence number. After storing, `while self.expected in self.buffer:` deliver `self.buffer.pop(self.expected)` and move `expected` on, modulo `2 ** bits`. Remember the second region: numbers just below the window are ACKed again.

=> ([b"m0", b"m1", b"m2", b"m3", b"m4"], 11, 70)
--- check case | No losses: a window of 4 sends 8 messages in two round trips
run([bytes([65 + i]) for i in range(8)], 10, 50, 4, 3)
=> ([b"A", b"B", b"C", b"D", b"E", b"F", b"G", b"H"], 16, 40)
--- check case | Losing the first packet resends only it
run([bytes([65 + i]) for i in range(8)], 10, 50, 4, 3, lose={0})
=> ([b"A", b"B", b"C", b"D", b"E", b"F", b"G", b"H"], 17, 90)
