=> (([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 6, 60), ([], 0, 0))
--- check test | The window must be at most half the sequence numbers
raises(ValueError, lambda: Sender(Sim(1), [b"a"], 10, 3, 2)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Sender(Sim(1), [b"a"], 10, 0, 3)) and Sender(Sim(1), [b"a"], 10, 4, 3).done is False
--- check test | A lost ACK for an already-delivered packet is replaced
(lambda r: r[0] == [b"a", b"b", b"c", b"d"] and r[2] is not None)(run([b"a", b"b", b"c", b"d"], 10, 30, 2, 2, lose={2, 3}))
?? The receiver must ACK again a packet just below its window, or the sender resends it forever.
--- check test | Sequence numbers wrap: 30 messages with 2-bit numbers and losses
(lambda r: r[0] == [bytes([i]) for i in range(30)] and r[2] is not None)(run([bytes([i]) for i in range(30)], 10, 45, 2, 2, lose={1, 4, 7, 8, 20, 33}))
--- check test | 1,500 random runs: always exactly the messages, in order, and finished
(lambda R: all((lambda bits: (lambda msgs: (lambda r: r[0] == msgs and r[2] is not None)(run(msgs, 10, R.choice([5, 15, 20, 25, 60]), R.randint(1, 2 ** (bits - 1)), bits, {i for i in range(400) if R.random() < 0.25}, {i for i in range(400) if R.random() < 0.05})))([bytes([R.randint(0, 255)]) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 20))]))(R.randint(1, 4)) for _ in range(1500)))(__import__("random").Random(3))

+++ practice | Which sequence number is newer
--- task
Write `seq_diff(a, b, bits)`: how many steps a is ahead of b on the circle of M = 2 ** bits sequence numbers, as a signed number. Return the d with d ≡ a − b (mod M) and −M/2 < d < M/2: positive when a is newer, negative when a is older, 0 when they are equal. When a and b are exactly M/2 apart, which one is newer cannot be decided (RFC 1982 leaves it undefined): raise `ValueError`.

Also write `newer(a, b, bits)`, which returns `True` exactly when `seq_diff(a, b, bits)` is positive.

For example, with 8 bits, `seq_diff(3, 254, 8)` is `5` (3 comes 5 steps after 254), and `seq_diff(254, 3, 8)` is `-5`.
--- starter
def seq_diff(a, b, bits):
    return a - b


def newer(a, b, bits):
    return a > b
--- solution
def seq_diff(a, b, bits):
    m = 2 ** bits
    d = (a - b) % m
    if d * 2 == m:
        raise ValueError("exactly half the circle apart")
    return d if d * 2 < m else d - m


def newer(a, b, bits):
    return seq_diff(a, b, bits) > 0
--- hint
`(a - b) % M` is between 0 and M − 1. If it is less than M/2, that is the answer; if more, subtract M to get the negative distance.
--- hint
Compare `d * 2` with `M` to avoid fractions. Equality is the undecidable case.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(seq_diff(3, 254, 8), seq_diff(254, 3, 8))
=> (5, -5)
--- check case | Equal, next and previous
(seq_diff(7, 7, 3), seq_diff(0, 7, 3), seq_diff(7, 0, 3))
=> (0, 1, -1)
--- check test | Exactly half the circle apart is undefined
raises(ValueError, lambda: seq_diff(0, 4, 3)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: seq_diff(1, 0, 1)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: newer(200, 72, 8))
--- check case | newer
(newer(3, 254, 8), newer(254, 3, 8), newer(5, 5, 8))
=> (True, False, False)
--- check test | 32-bit numbers, as TCP uses
seq_diff(5, 2 ** 32 - 10, 32) == 15 and seq_diff(2 ** 31 - 1, 0, 32) == 2 ** 31 - 1 and seq_diff(0, 2 ** 31 - 1, 32) == -(2 ** 31 - 1)

+++ practice | The selective-repeat receiver on its own
--- task
Write the selective-repeat receiver as a plain function. `sr_receive(arrivals, bits, window)` takes the data packets that reach the receiver, in order, each a tuple `(seq, data)` (all undamaged). The receiver expects 0 first.

For each packet, with `offset = (seq - expected) % 2 ** bits`: if `offset < window`, ACK `seq`, store the data unless that number is already stored, and deliver from the buffer while the expected number is there (moving `expected` on modulo `2 ** bits`). Else if `offset >= 2 ** bits - window`, ACK `seq` and nothing else. Otherwise do nothing.

Return `(delivered, acks, waiting)`: the delivered data, the ACK numbers sent, and the sorted list of sequence numbers still in the buffer at the end.

For example, `sr_receive([(1, b"b"), (0, b"a"), (1, b"b"), (3, b"d")], 2, 2)` is `([b"a", b"b"], [1, 0, 1, 3], [3])`.
--- starter
def sr_receive(arrivals, bits, window):
    return ([d for s, d in arrivals], [s for s, d in arrivals], [])
--- solution
def sr_receive(arrivals, bits, window):
    modulus = 2 ** bits
    expected = 0
    buffer = {}
    delivered = []
    acks = []
    for seq, data in arrivals:
        offset = (seq - expected) % modulus
        if offset < window:
            acks.append(seq)
            if seq not in buffer:
                buffer[seq] = data
            while expected in buffer:
                delivered.append(buffer.pop(expected))
                expected = (expected + 1) % modulus
        elif offset >= modulus - window:
            acks.append(seq)
    return delivered, acks, sorted(buffer)
--- hint
Keep `expected` and a dict `buffer`. The three cases depend only on `offset`.
--- hint
After storing, a `while expected in buffer:` loop delivers every packet that is now in order. What is left in the buffer at the end is waiting for a gap to be filled.
--- check case | The example from the task
sr_receive([(1, b"b"), (0, b"a"), (1, b"b"), (3, b"d")], 2, 2)
=> ([b"a", b"b"], [1, 0, 1, 3], [3])
--- check case | A late copy just below the window is re-ACKed, not delivered
sr_receive([(0, b"a"), (1, b"b"), (0, b"a")], 3, 2)
=> ([b"a", b"b"], [0, 1, 0], [])
--- check case | A number too far ahead is ignored
sr_receive([(5, b"x"), (0, b"a")], 3, 2)
=> ([b"a"], [0], [])
--- check case | Wrapping round a 2-bit circle
sr_receive([(0, b"a"), (1, b"b"), (3, b"d"), (2, b"c"), (0, b"e")], 2, 2)
=> ([b"a", b"b", b"c", b"d", b"e"], [0, 1, 3, 2, 0], [])
--- check case | A second copy of a buffered packet does not replace it
sr_receive([(1, b"first"), (1, b"second"), (0, b"a")], 2, 2)
=> ([b"a", b"first"], [1, 1, 0], [])
--- check case | Nothing
sr_receive([], 3, 4)
=> ([], [], [])

+++ practice | Rebuilding a full packet number
--- task
QUIC numbers packets 0, 1, 2, … up to 2^62 − 1 and never reuses a number, but to save space it sends only the lowest `bits` bits of each. The receiver rebuilds the full number from the largest packet number it has received so far, by picking the full number closest to the one it expects next.

Write `decode_packet_number(largest, truncated, bits)`, following RFC 9000:

1. `expected = largest + 1`, `win = 2 ** bits`, `half = win // 2`, `mask = win - 1`.
2. `candidate = (expected & ~mask) | truncated`: the expected number with its low bits replaced.
3. If `candidate <= expected - half` and `candidate < 2 ** 62 - win`, return `candidate + win`.
4. If `candidate > expected + half` and `candidate >= win`, return `candidate - win`.
5. Otherwise return `candidate`.

For example, `decode_packet_number(0xA82F30EA, 0x9B32, 16)` is `0xA82F9B32` (the example in RFC 9000).
--- starter
def decode_packet_number(largest, truncated, bits):
    return truncated
--- solution
def decode_packet_number(largest, truncated, bits):
    expected = largest + 1
    win = 2 ** bits
    half = win // 2
    mask = win - 1
    candidate = (expected & ~mask) | truncated
    if candidate <= expected - half and candidate < 2 ** 62 - win:
        return candidate + win
    if candidate > expected + half and candidate >= win:
        return candidate - win
    return candidate
--- hint
`expected & ~mask` clears the lowest `bits` bits of `expected`; OR-ing in `truncated` puts the received bits there. That candidate is within one `win` of the right answer.
--- hint
The two tests move the candidate up or down by one `win` when it is more than half a window away from `expected`. The extra conditions stop it going below 0 or above the largest allowed number.
--- check case | The RFC 9000 example
decode_packet_number(0xA82F30EA, 0x9B32, 16)
=> 0xA82F9B32
--- check case | The low bits wrapped past the expected number: go up
decode_packet_number(250, 3, 8)
=> 259
--- check case | A slightly older packet: go down
decode_packet_number(257, 254, 8)
=> 254
--- check case | Near zero it cannot go below 0
(decode_packet_number(0, 200, 8), decode_packet_number(-1, 0, 8))
=> (200, 0)
--- check test | Round trip for every valid number within half a window of the largest seen
all(decode_packet_number(big, (big + d) % 2 ** k, k) == big + d for k in (8, 16) for big in (300, 70000, 2 ** 40 + 7) for d in range(-(2 ** (k - 1)) + 2, 2 ** (k - 1)) if big + d >= 0)

+++ practice | A selective-repeat window with no simulator
--- task
Write the selective-repeat sender's bookkeeping as a class, so every rule can be tested alone. `SrWindow(count, window, bits)` manages `count` messages; raise `ValueError` unless `window` is from 1 to `2 ** (bits - 1)`.

- `send()`: if next < base + window and next < count, return next's sequence number (modulo `2 ** bits`) and add 1 to next; otherwise `None`.
- `ack(a)`: apply an individual ACK with number `a`. If it names an index in the window that is not yet acknowledged, mark it, slide base past every acknowledged index, and return how far base moved (possibly 0). If it names nothing new (outside the window, or already acknowledged), return `None`.
- `outstanding()`: the sorted list of sequence numbers sent but not yet acknowledged (these are the ones whose timers are running).
- `finished()`: `True` when all messages are acknowledged.

For example, with `w = SrWindow(5, 2, 2)`: `w.send()` gives 0 and 1, then `None`; `w.ack(1)` returns 0 (base cannot move past the unacknowledged 0); `w.ack(0)` returns 2; `w.send()` gives 2.
--- starter
class SrWindow:
    def __init__(self, count, window, bits):
        self.count = count

    def send(self):
        return None

    def ack(self, a):
        return None

    def outstanding(self):
        return []

    def finished(self):
        return True
--- solution
class SrWindow:
    def __init__(self, count, window, bits):
        if not 1 <= window <= 2 ** (bits - 1):
            raise ValueError("window too big for selective repeat")
        self.count = count
        self.window = window
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.base = 0
        self.next = 0
        self.acked = set()

    def send(self):
        if self.next >= self.count or self.next >= self.base + self.window:
            return None
        seq = self.next % self.modulus
        self.next += 1
        return seq

    def ack(self, a):
        offset = (a - self.base) % self.modulus
        if offset >= self.next - self.base:
            return None
        j = self.base + offset
        if j in self.acked:
            return None
        self.acked.add(j)
        start = self.base
        while self.base in self.acked:
            self.acked.remove(self.base)
            self.base += 1
        return self.base - start

    def outstanding(self):
        return sorted(i % self.modulus for i in range(self.base, self.next) if i not in self.acked)

    def finished(self):
        return self.base == self.count
--- hint
Keep `base`, `next` and a set of acknowledged indexes, as in the lesson's sender, without timers.
--- hint
`ack` returns `None` for an ACK that changes nothing, and otherwise the distance base slid, which is 0 when the ACK was for a later packet.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda w: (w.send(), w.send(), w.send(), w.ack(1), w.ack(0), w.send()))(SrWindow(5, 2, 2))
=> (0, 1, None, 0, 2, 2)
--- check case | Duplicate and out-of-window ACKs change nothing
(lambda w: (w.send(), w.send(), w.ack(1), w.ack(1), w.ack(3), w.outstanding()))(SrWindow(4, 2, 2))
=> (0, 1, 0, None, None, [0])
--- check case | Wrapping: numbers 3, 0 after 2 with 2-bit numbers
(lambda w: ([w.send(), w.send()], w.ack(0), w.ack(1), [w.send(), w.send()], w.ack(0), w.outstanding(), w.ack(3), w.finished()))(SrWindow(4, 2, 2))
=> ([0, 1], 1, 1, [2, 3], None, [2, 3], 0, False)
--- check test | Window limits, and no messages
raises(ValueError, lambda: SrWindow(3, 3, 2)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: SrWindow(3, 0, 3)) and SrWindow(0, 1, 1).finished() and SrWindow(0, 1, 1).send() is None
--- check case | Finishing
(lambda w: (w.send(), w.send(), w.ack(1), w.finished(), w.ack(0), w.finished(), w.outstanding()))(SrWindow(2, 2, 3))
=> (0, 1, 0, False, 2, True, [])

+++ practice | Debug: the transfer that never finishes
--- task
This selective-repeat run delivers every message, but the sender never finishes when an ACK is lost: `run([b"a", b"b", b"c", b"d"], 10, 30, 2, 2, lose={2, 3})` returns the right data but `done_at` is `None`, because the sender resends the same packets until the simulator's step limit. The sender is correct. Find the bug in the receiver and fix it.
--- starter
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, window, bits):
        if not 1 <= window <= 2 ** (bits - 1):
            raise ValueError("the window must be from 1 to 2 ** (bits - 1)")
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.window = window
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None
        self.timers = {}
        self.acked = set()
        self.base = 0
        self.next = 0

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.fill()

    def send(self, i):
        packet = make_packet(DATA, i % self.modulus, self.messages[i])
        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)
        self.timers[i].start(self.timeout)

    def fill(self):
        while self.next < len(self.messages) and self.next < self.base + self.window:
            i = self.next
            self.timers[i] = Timer(self.sim, lambda i=i: self.send(i))
            self.send(i)
            self.next += 1

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, ack, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK:
            return
        offset = (ack - self.base) % self.modulus
        if offset >= self.next - self.base:
            return
        j = self.base + offset
        if j in self.acked:
            return
        self.acked.add(j)
        self.timers.pop(j).stop()
        while self.base in self.acked:
            self.acked.remove(self.base)
            self.base += 1
        if self.base == len(self.messages):
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.fill()


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, bits, window):
        self.sim = sim
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.window = window
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0
        self.buffer = {}

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        offset = (seq - self.expected) % self.modulus
        if offset < self.window:
            self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)
            if seq not in self.buffer:
                self.buffer[seq] = data
            while self.expected in self.buffer:
                self.delivered.append(self.buffer.pop(self.expected))
                self.expected = (self.expected + 1) % self.modulus


def run(messages, delay, timeout, window, bits, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim, bits, window)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout, window, bits)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- solution
import heapq
import zlib

DATA = 0
ACK = 1


def make_packet(kind, seq, data=b""):
    body = bytes([kind, seq]) + data
    return body + zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big")


def parse_packet(raw):
    body, crc = raw[:-4], raw[-4:]
    if len(raw) < 6 or zlib.crc32(body).to_bytes(4, "big") != crc:
        return None
    return body[0], body[1], body[2:]


class Sim:
    def __init__(self, delay, lose=(), corrupt=()):
        self.now = 0
        self.delay = delay
        self.lose = set(lose)
        self.corrupt = set(corrupt)
        self.sent = 0
        self.log = []
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def transmit(self, raw, deliver):
        n = self.sent
        self.sent += 1
        self.log.append((self.now, n, raw))
        if n in self.lose:
            return
        if n in self.corrupt:
            raw = bytes([raw[0] ^ 1]) + raw[1:]
        self.at(self.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(raw))

    def run(self, limit=200000):
        steps = 0
        while self.queue and steps < limit:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is None:
                continue
            self.now = time
            action()
            steps += 1


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Sender:
    def __init__(self, sim, messages, timeout, window, bits):
        if not 1 <= window <= 2 ** (bits - 1):
            raise ValueError("the window must be from 1 to 2 ** (bits - 1)")
        self.sim = sim
        self.messages = messages
        self.timeout = timeout
        self.window = window
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.peer = None
        self.done = False
        self.done_at = None
        self.timers = {}
        self.acked = set()
        self.base = 0
        self.next = 0

    def start(self):
        if not self.messages:
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.fill()

    def send(self, i):
        packet = make_packet(DATA, i % self.modulus, self.messages[i])
        self.sim.transmit(packet, self.peer)
        self.timers[i].start(self.timeout)

    def fill(self):
        while self.next < len(self.messages) and self.next < self.base + self.window:
            i = self.next
            self.timers[i] = Timer(self.sim, lambda i=i: self.send(i))
            self.send(i)
            self.next += 1

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None or self.done:
            return
        kind, ack, _ = parsed
        if kind != ACK:
            return
        offset = (ack - self.base) % self.modulus
        if offset >= self.next - self.base:
            return
        j = self.base + offset
        if j in self.acked:
            return
        self.acked.add(j)
        self.timers.pop(j).stop()
        while self.base in self.acked:
            self.acked.remove(self.base)
            self.base += 1
        if self.base == len(self.messages):
            self.done = True
            self.done_at = self.sim.now
        else:
            self.fill()


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, bits, window):
        self.sim = sim
        self.modulus = 2 ** bits
        self.window = window
        self.peer = None
        self.delivered = []
        self.expected = 0
        self.buffer = {}

    def on_packet(self, raw):
        parsed = parse_packet(raw)
        if parsed is None:
            return
        kind, seq, data = parsed
        if kind != DATA:
            return
        offset = (seq - self.expected) % self.modulus
        if offset < self.window:
            self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)
            if seq not in self.buffer:
                self.buffer[seq] = data
            while self.expected in self.buffer:
                self.delivered.append(self.buffer.pop(self.expected))
                self.expected = (self.expected + 1) % self.modulus
        elif offset >= self.modulus - self.window:
            self.sim.transmit(make_packet(ACK, seq), self.peer)


def run(messages, delay, timeout, window, bits, lose=(), corrupt=()):
    sim = Sim(delay, lose, corrupt)
    receiver = Receiver(sim, bits, window)
    sender = Sender(sim, messages, timeout, window, bits)
    sender.peer = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.peer = sender.on_packet
    sender.start()
    sim.run()
    return receiver.delivered, sim.sent, sender.done_at
--- hint
When the ACK for a delivered packet is lost, the sender resends that packet. Where does its sequence number fall relative to the receiver's window, and what does this receiver do with it?
--- hint
Numbers in the W positions just below the window are late copies of delivered packets: they must be ACKed again. Add that case.
--- check test | The run from the task finishes
(lambda r: r[0] == [b"a", b"b", b"c", b"d"] and r[2] is not None and r[1] < 30)(run([b"a", b"b", b"c", b"d"], 10, 30, 2, 2, lose={2, 3}))
--- check case | No losses
run([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 10, 30, 2, 2)
=> ([b"a", b"b", b"c"], 6, 40)
--- check test | Random losses always finish
(lambda R: all((lambda r: r[0] == [bytes([i]) for i in range(10)] and r[2] is not None)(run([bytes([i]) for i in range(10)], 10, 35, 4, 3, {i for i in range(200) if R.random() < 0.3})) for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(8))

+++ practice | Reassembling telemetry from two ground stations
--- task
A spacecraft's packets carry a 14-bit sequence count (0 to 16383, then 0 again). Two ground stations receive the downlink, and their streams are merged, so packets arrive out of order and often twice. Put them back in order.

Write `reassemble(arrivals, window)`. `arrivals` is a list of `(count, data)` in arrival order. The receiver starts by expecting the count of the **first** arrival. For each arrival, let `d = (count - expected) % 16384`:

- if `d < window`: if that count is already buffered, it is a duplicate; otherwise buffer it. Then deliver from the buffer while the expected count is there, moving `expected` on modulo 16384;
- else if `d >= 16384 - window`: it was already delivered, a duplicate;
- otherwise it is too far ahead to buffer: drop it.

Return a dict with `"delivered"` (the data in order), `"duplicates"` and `"dropped"` (counts), and `"waiting"` (the sorted list of counts still buffered, behind a gap). An empty list of arrivals delivers nothing.

For example, `reassemble([(16382, b"a"), (0, b"c"), (16383, b"b"), (0, b"c")], 8)` delivers `[b"a", b"b", b"c"]` with 1 duplicate, 0 dropped and nothing waiting.
--- starter
def reassemble(arrivals, window):
    return {"delivered": [d for c, d in sorted(arrivals)], "duplicates": 0, "dropped": 0, "waiting": []}
--- solution
def reassemble(arrivals, window):
    modulus = 16384
    delivered = []
    duplicates = 0
    dropped = 0
    buffer = {}
    expected = arrivals[0][0] if arrivals else 0
    for count, data in arrivals:
        d = (count - expected) % modulus
        if d < window:
            if count in buffer:
                duplicates += 1
                continue
            buffer[count] = data
            while expected in buffer:
                delivered.append(buffer.pop(expected))
                expected = (expected + 1) % modulus
        elif d >= modulus - window:
            duplicates += 1
        else:
            dropped += 1
    return {"delivered": delivered, "duplicates": duplicates, "dropped": dropped, "waiting": sorted(buffer)}
--- hint
This is the selective-repeat receiver with a 14-bit circle, no ACKs, and counters. Start `expected` at the first arrival's count.
--- hint
A copy of a count that is still in the buffer is a duplicate too. Sorting the waiting counts is plain `sorted(buffer)`; they have not wrapped past each other because they all lie within one window.
--- check case | The example from the task
reassemble([(16382, b"a"), (0, b"c"), (16383, b"b"), (0, b"c")], 8)
=> {"delivered": [b"a", b"b", b"c"], "duplicates": 1, "dropped": 0, "waiting": []}
--- check case | A gap leaves packets waiting
reassemble([(10, b"x"), (12, b"z"), (13, b"w")], 4)
=> {"delivered": [b"x"], "duplicates": 0, "dropped": 0, "waiting": [12, 13]}
--- check case | Too far ahead is dropped; already delivered is a duplicate
reassemble([(100, b"a"), (101, b"b"), (200, b"far"), (100, b"a"), (102, b"c")], 16)
=> {"delivered": [b"a", b"b", b"c"], "duplicates": 1, "dropped": 1, "waiting": []}
--- check case | Nothing arrived
reassemble([], 8)
=> {"delivered": [], "duplicates": 0, "dropped": 0, "waiting": []}
--- check test | Two shuffled copies of 3,000 packets across the wrap
(lambda R: (lambda pkts: (lambda r: r["delivered"] == [d for c, d in pkts] and r["duplicates"] == 3000 and r["dropped"] == 0 and r["waiting"] == [])(reassemble([pkts[0]] + [p for _, p in sorted([(i + R.random() * 20, p) for i, p in enumerate(pkts)][1:] + [(i + R.random() * 20, p) for i, p in enumerate(pkts)])], 64)))([((15000 + i) % 16384, i.to_bytes(2, "big")) for i in range(3000)]))(__import__("random").Random(4))

+++ question | Why half the circle
--- ask
The proof that selective repeat is correct shows every DATA the receiver can get has an index between r − W and r + W − 1. Why does that make 2W ≤ M the right condition?
--- choice
Because the sender and the receiver each need a buffer of W packets.
--- choice correct
Those are 2W consecutive indexes. With 2W ≤ M they all have different sequence numbers, so the receiver can tell a late copy (below r) from new data (from r on).
--- choice
Because ACKs use half the sequence numbers and data uses the other half.
--- choice
Because the timer can only track M/2 packets.
--- why
The receiver sees only i mod M. Go-Back-N's receiver accepts a single number, r mod M, so it only needs W < M. Selective repeat's receiver accepts a whole window of numbers and must also recognise the window just below it, so the two windows together, 2W numbers, must fit on the circle without overlapping.

+++ question | Resending after one loss
--- ask
A window of 50 packets is in flight, and only the 10th is lost. What does each protocol resend?
--- choice
Both resend all 50.
--- choice correct
Go-Back-N resends packets 10 to 50, since its receiver threw away 11 to 50; selective repeat resends only packet 10, since its receiver buffered 11 to 50.
--- choice
Go-Back-N resends only packet 10; selective repeat resends 10 to 50.
--- choice
Neither resends anything, because later ACKs cover the loss.
--- why
Go-Back-N's cumulative ACKs stop at 9 and its single timer resends everything from base. Selective repeat ACKs 11 to 50 individually, and only packet 10's own timer runs out. The price is a receive buffer and one timer per packet.

+++ question | Sequence numbers on a fast link
--- ask
TCP numbers bytes with 32 bits. Why can a 10 Gb/s TCP connection need something more than sequence numbers to reject old segments?
--- choice
Because 10 Gb/s links reorder every packet.
--- choice correct
2^32 bytes are sent in about 3.4 seconds, but an old segment may survive in the network for up to about two minutes, so an old number can come round again while a stale segment is still alive.
--- choice
Because 32 bits cannot hold a 10 Gb/s rate.
--- choice
Because the window must be larger than 2^32 bytes.
--- why
The window bound assumes old packets are gone before their numbers are reused. On the Internet that assumption is a maximum packet lifetime, and at high rates the sequence space wraps faster than that lifetime. TCP's timestamps (PAWS) add a second, independent number to tell the generations apart.

=== net-09 | TCP: the handshake, flow control and the sliding window
--- teach
Lessons 7, 8 and 8b built reliable delivery over a lossy link: sequence numbers, acknowledgements, timeouts, and a window of packets in flight, with numbers that wrap around. **TCP**, the Transmission Control Protocol, is those ideas turned into the protocol that carries most of the Internet's traffic: web pages, email, file transfers, remote logins. This lesson covers what TCP adds on top of them: numbering **bytes** instead of packets, a **handshake** that sets up a connection, a way to close it, and **flow control**, which stops a fast sender from drowning a slow receiver.

### A phone call, not a postcard

UDP is like sending postcards: each one goes on its own, and nothing connects one to the next. TCP is like a phone call. First you set it up: "Hello?" "Hi, it's Ada." "Hi Ada, go ahead." Then both people can talk at once, every word arrives in the order it was said, and if a word is lost you say "sorry, what?" and it is said again. At the end, each person says goodbye, and the line is hung up.

### What TCP promises

TCP gives two programs a **connection**: a two-way channel with four promises.

- **Reliable:** every byte sent arrives, or the connection reports an error. Lost segments are resent.
- **In order:** bytes are handed to the receiving program in exactly the order they were sent.
- **A byte stream:** TCP carries bytes, not messages. Two `send` calls of 100 bytes each may arrive as one `recv` of 200 bytes, or as three pieces. The program must find its own message boundaries, with the framing ideas of lesson 2.
- **Full duplex:** both directions work at the same time, each with its own numbers.

A connection is named by four numbers, the **4-tuple**: source IP, source port, destination IP and destination port. One server port (say 443) can hold thousands of connections at once, because each client brings a different IP address or port.

### The segment

A TCP packet is called a **segment**. Its header is 20 bytes without options:

| field | bytes | meaning |
|---|---|---|
| source port, destination port | 2 + 2 | the two ends, as in UDP |
| sequence number | 4 | the number of the first data byte in this segment |
| acknowledgement number | 4 | the next byte this side expects to receive |
| data offset and flags | 2 | header length in 4-byte words; the flags SYN, ACK, FIN, RST, PSH, URG |
| window | 2 | how many more bytes this side can accept |
| checksum, urgent pointer | 2 + 2 | the Internet checksum with a pseudo-header, as in UDP |

The **flags** are single bits: **SYN** asks to start a connection, **ACK** says the acknowledgement number is valid, **FIN** says "I have nothing more to send", and **RST** aborts the connection at once.

### Numbering bytes, not segments

In lessons 7 to 8b each packet had a number. TCP numbers every **byte** instead. If a sender's first data byte is number 5,001 and it sends 300 bytes, the segment carries sequence number 5,001, and its bytes are numbers 5,001 to 5,300.

The receiver's **acknowledgement number** is the number of the next byte it expects: "I have everything **before** this". That is a **cumulative ACK**, like Go-Back-N's. After receiving bytes 5,001 to 5,300 in order, the receiver sends ACK 5,301.

```python
first = 5001
lengths = [300, 300, 120]
for n in lengths:
    print("segment seq", first, "carries bytes", first, "to", first + n - 1)
    first += n
print("final ACK:", first)    # 5721
```

Two control flags also take up one sequence number each, as if they were a byte: **SYN** and **FIN**. That way they are acknowledged and resent exactly like data. So a connection that starts with a SYN numbered 900 has its first data byte at 901.

### Numbers that wrap

The sequence number is 32 bits, so it counts modulo 2³² = 4,294,967,296: after 4,294,967,295 comes 0. You met wrap-around in lesson 8b and in modular arithmetic. That means you can never compare two sequence numbers with a plain `<`.

TCP uses **serial number arithmetic** instead. Define the distance from a to b as (b − a) mod 2³². Then b is **after** a when that distance is between 1 and 2³¹ − 1.

```python
MOD = 2 ** 32

def distance(a, b):
    return (b - a) % MOD

print(distance(100, 250))              # 150: 250 is after 100
print(distance(4294967000, 200))       # 496: 200 is after 4294967000, across the wrap
print(distance(250, 100))              # 4294967146: huge, so 100 is before 250
```

**Why it is correct.** Lesson 8b proved this for small numbers; here is the same argument for 32 bits. Suppose the true (unwrapped) numbers A and B differ by less than 2³¹, so −2³¹ < B − A < 2³¹. The wrapped numbers are a = A mod 2³² and b = B mod 2³², so (b − a) mod 2³² = (B − A) mod 2³². If B − A is between 0 and 2³¹ − 1, the remainder is B − A itself. If B − A is negative, the remainder is B − A + 2³², which is at least 2³¹. So the distance is below 2³¹ exactly when B really comes after A (or equals it). TCP keeps this condition true by never having more than 2³¹ bytes outstanding, and by letting old segments die (the Watch out and the TIME-WAIT part say how).

### The three-way handshake

Before any data flows, both sides must agree on where their numbers start. Each side picks an **initial sequence number** (ISN) for the bytes it will send. The **three-way handshake** exchanges them:

```text
client                                   server  (listening)
  |  SYN, seq = x                           |
  | --------------------------------------> |
  |          SYN + ACK, seq = y, ack = x+1  |
  | <-------------------------------------- |
  |  ACK, seq = x+1, ack = y+1              |
  | --------------------------------------> |
  both sides ESTABLISHED: data may flow
```

1. The client sends a SYN carrying its ISN x.
2. The server answers with a segment that is both a SYN (carrying its own ISN y) and an ACK of the client's SYN: ack = x + 1, because the SYN used up number x.
3. The client acknowledges the server's SYN: ack = y + 1.

**Why three messages, and not two?** Each side must know two facts: its own ISN reached the other side, and it has learned the other side's ISN. After message 2, the client knows both. But the server does not yet know that the client received y. Worse, message 1 might be an **old duplicate** SYN from a connection that ended long ago, delayed in the network. With a two-way handshake the server would open a connection nobody asked for, and accept old data as new. Message 3 settles it: only a client that really saw y can send ack = y + 1. A real client that receives a SYN+ACK for a connection it never started answers with RST, and the server gives up.

Each side picks its ISN **at random**, not starting from 0. Then an old segment from an earlier connection on the same 4-tuple is unlikely to fall inside the new connection's numbers, and an attacker who cannot see the traffic cannot guess the numbers well enough to [[inject fake segments|isn]].

### Closing a connection

Each direction is closed on its own, because each side decides when it has finished sending. A side that is done sends a **FIN**. The other side ACKs it, may keep sending its own data, and later sends its own FIN, which is ACKed in turn. That is four segments, though the middle two are often combined.

The side that sent the first FIN ends in a state called **[[TIME-WAIT|time-wait]]**, and waits there for twice the longest time a segment can live in the network (the **maximum segment lifetime**, MSL, often taken as 30 seconds to 2 minutes). Two reasons. If its last ACK is lost, the other side will resend its FIN, and someone must still be there to ACK it again. And any stray segments from this connection must die out before the same 4-tuple can be used for a new one.

The states a connection moves through have names you will see in tools like `netstat` and `ss`:

| state | meaning |
|---|---|
| LISTEN | a server waiting for SYNs |
| SYN-SENT, SYN-RECEIVED | halfway through the handshake |
| ESTABLISHED | the handshake is done; data flows |
| FIN-WAIT-1, FIN-WAIT-2 | I sent a FIN; waiting for its ACK, then for the other FIN |
| CLOSE-WAIT | the other side sent a FIN; I may still send |
| LAST-ACK | I sent my FIN after theirs; waiting for its ACK |
| TIME-WAIT | both FINs are done; waiting 2·MSL before forgetting |

### The receiver: a buffer and a pointer

A TCP receiver keeps a **receive buffer** of fixed size, say 64 KB, and one important number, called `rcv_nxt` in the standard: the next byte it expects. Arriving bytes land in the buffer:

- A byte numbered `rcv_nxt` is next in order. It joins the in-order data, and `rcv_nxt` moves on by one, then again for every byte already waiting right behind it.
- A byte further ahead arrived **out of order**, because an earlier segment was lost or delayed. It is kept in the buffer, not thrown away, which is what selective repeat did in lesson 8b.
- A byte before `rcv_nxt` is an old duplicate. It is ignored.

Every segment that arrives gets an ACK with the current `rcv_nxt`. So while there is a gap, every new segment produces the **same** ACK number again: a **duplicate ACK**. The sender will use those next lesson.

The program reads in-order bytes out of the buffer with `recv`, whenever it likes. Until it does, they take up space.

### Flow control: the receive window

If a sender goes faster than the receiving program reads, the buffer fills up. TCP stops that with **flow control**: the receiver tells the sender how much room it has left. That number is the **receive window**, rwnd, sent in the window field of every ACK:

rwnd = buffer size − bytes received in order but not yet read.

The receiver accepts only bytes numbered from `rcv_nxt` up to, but not including, `rcv_nxt + rwnd`. The sender's rule is to keep its unacknowledged bytes within the window it was last told:

last byte sent − last byte acknowledged ≤ rwnd.

A tiny example of the window changing, with a 1,000-byte buffer:

```python
size = 1000
unread = 0
for arrived, read in [(400, 0), (500, 100), (200, 0), (0, 700)]:
    unread += arrived - read
    print("unread", unread, "window", size - unread)
```

The last lines show the window closing to 0 and then reopening when the program reads.

**Why the buffer never overflows.** Call R = `rcv_nxt` + rwnd the **right edge** of the window. Every stored byte is either in-order and unread, or numbered between `rcv_nxt` and R. There are (buffer size − rwnd) of the first kind and at most rwnd of the second, so at most (buffer size) in all. The right edge also never moves left: when a byte arrives in order, `rcv_nxt` goes up by one and rwnd goes down by one, and when the program reads, rwnd goes up. So a sender that obeyed an older, larger window never sends past the current edge.

**A window of zero** means "stop". The sender then sends a tiny **window probe** every so often (driven by its **persist timer**), because the ACK that reopens the window could be lost, and without probes both sides would wait forever.

### The sliding window, from the sender's side

The sender sees its byte stream in four parts:

```text
 acknowledged | sent, not yet ACKed | may send now | may not send yet
--------------+---------------------+--------------+-------------------
              ^ last ACK            ^ next to send ^ last ACK + window
```

Each ACK slides the left edge forward. Each window update moves the right edge. This is the sliding window of lesson 8, measured in bytes, with its width set by the receiver.

The sender also needs a **retransmission timeout** (RTO): how long to wait for an ACK before resending. It cannot be fixed, because round-trip times range from under a millisecond in a data centre to over half a second over a satellite. TCP measures the round-trip time (RTT) of its segments and keeps a smoothed average and a measure of how much it varies. A practice problem below builds that estimator.

### What it costs

- **Throughput is capped by the window.** A sender can have at most rwnd bytes unacknowledged, and an ACK takes one round trip to come back. So it sends at most rwnd bytes per RTT: throughput ≤ rwnd / RTT. With the header's 16-bit window (at most 65,535 bytes) and an RTT of 100 ms, that is 655,350 bytes a second, about 5.2 megabits per second, however fast the link is. That is why TCP has a [[window scale option|wscale]].
- **Setup costs one round trip** before the first data byte, and closing costs another exchange. Short connections pay this every time, which is why HTTP keeps connections open (lesson 12).
- **Memory:** one buffer per connection in each direction. A server with 10,000 connections and 64 KB buffers needs 640 MB for receive buffers alone.
- **Per segment**, the receiver does work proportional to the segment's length, plus a lookup in the structure holding out-of-order data.

### Where it is used

- **The web, email, SSH, file transfer and databases** all run over TCP, or over QUIC, which rebuilds the same ideas on UDP.
- **Every operating system** implements TCP in its kernel. Your program only calls `connect`, `send` and `recv` (lesson 13); the handshake, retransmission and windows happen underneath.
- **Not to deep space.** A round trip to Mars takes between about 6 and 44 minutes, so TCP's handshake and timers do not work there. Space agencies use [[other protocols|dtn]] built for long delays.

**Watch out:** the classic TCP bugs.

- Comparing sequence numbers with `<`. It works in every test until the numbers wrap past 2³², and ISNs are random, so in real traffic a connection can start close to the wrap.
- Forgetting that SYN and FIN each use one sequence number. The ACK of a SYN numbered x is x + 1, not x.
- Treating one `recv` as one message. TCP is a byte stream: a message may come in pieces, or two may come glued together.

::: context isn How ISNs are chosen
Early TCP stacks increased the ISN by a fixed amount every few microseconds. In 1985 Robert Morris showed that an attacker could then predict the next ISN, send a SYN with a faked source address, and send the third handshake message without ever seeing the server's reply, so the server believed it was talking to a trusted machine. Kevin Mitnick used this in 1994. Today RFC 6528 says to add a secret hash of the 4-tuple to a clock, so each connection's numbers look random to outsiders.
:::

::: context time-wait Why TIME-WAIT lasts so long
Suppose a connection closes and a new one starts at once on the same 4-tuple. A delayed segment from the old connection could arrive, carry a sequence number that happens to fit the new one, and be accepted as data. Waiting 2·MSL guarantees that every segment of the old connection has either arrived or been discarded, since routers drop packets whose TTL runs out. Busy servers therefore collect thousands of connections in TIME-WAIT, which is normal. It is also why a restarted server needs the `SO_REUSEADDR` option to bind its port again at once.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="120" y="14" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">closes first</text>
  <text x="320" y="14" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">other side</text>
  <line x1="120" y1="20" x2="120" y2="165" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="320" y1="20" x2="320" y2="165" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="120" y1="28" x2="320" y2="44" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="220" y="30" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">FIN</text>
  <line x1="320" y1="52" x2="120" y2="68" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="220" y="54" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">ACK</text>
  <line x1="320" y1="80" x2="120" y2="96" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="220" y="82" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">FIN</text>
  <line x1="120" y1="100" x2="320" y2="116" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="220" y="102" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">ACK</text>
  <rect x="110" y="100" width="20" height="62" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="104" y="135" font-size="11" text-anchor="end" fill="#1f2a44">TIME-WAIT</text>
  <text x="104" y="149" font-size="11" text-anchor="end" fill="#6c7a93">2 · MSL</text>
```
