:::

::: context wscale Windows bigger than 64 KB
The window field has only 16 bits, which was plenty in 1981. The window scale option (RFC 7323), sent in the SYN, says "multiply every window I advertise by 2 to the power s", with s up to 14, allowing windows of up to about 1 GB. Both sides must offer it in the handshake or it is not used. A path with a 1 Gbit/s link and a 100 ms round trip needs about 12.5 MB in flight to stay busy, which is lesson 15's bandwidth-delay product.
:::

::: context dtn Networking to the planets
For links with delays of minutes or hours and contact windows that open and close as planets turn, CCSDS and NASA use Delay-Tolerant Networking: the Bundle Protocol stores data at each hop until the next link is available, instead of expecting an end-to-end path. For reliability over one long hop they use the Licklider Transmission Protocol, which sends a whole block at once and asks only for the missing parts, with no handshake. NASA tested it on the International Space Station and on the EPOXI spacecraft.
:::
--- task
Write a class `TcpReceiver`, the receiving half of one TCP connection. All sequence numbers are modulo 2³².

- `TcpReceiver(capacity)` makes a receiver with a buffer of `capacity` bytes. It has an attribute `closed`, starting `False`.
- `segment(seq, data=b"", syn=False, fin=False)` handles one arriving segment and returns the ACK the receiver sends back, as a tuple `(ack, window)`. `ack` is the next byte number expected, and `window` is `capacity` minus the number of in-order bytes not yet read.
- `read(n)` removes and returns up to `n` in-order bytes from the front of the received data, as `bytes`.

The rules for `segment`:

1. Before the first SYN, a segment without `syn` is ignored and `segment` returns `None`. The first SYN sets the next expected number to `seq + 1`. A SYN carries no data, and a later SYN (a retransmitted one) changes nothing, though it still gets an ACK.
2. Byte `i` of `data` has number `seq + i`. It is **accepted** when its distance from the next expected number, `(number - next) mod 2³²`, is less than the window at the moment the segment arrives. Other bytes are ignored: they are old duplicates, or beyond the window.
3. After storing the accepted bytes, move every byte that is now in order into the received data, advancing the next expected number.
4. A FIN has the number `seq + len(data)`. Remember it if its distance from the next expected number is at most the window. When every byte before it has arrived, the FIN uses up one more number, and `closed` becomes `True`. No test sends data numbered after a FIN.

For example, with `r = TcpReceiver(8)`: `r.segment(99, syn=True)` is `(100, 8)`. Then `r.segment(103, b"efg")` is `(100, 8)`: the three bytes are held out of order. Then `r.segment(100, b"abc")` is `(106, 2)`, because bytes 100 to 105 are now all in order and 6 of the 8 bytes of buffer are full. Then `r.read(4)` is `b"abce"`, and the window grows back to 6.
--- starter
class TcpReceiver:
    def __init__(self, capacity):
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.closed = False

    def segment(self, seq, data=b"", syn=False, fin=False):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def read(self, n):
        return b""
--- solution
MOD = 2 ** 32


class TcpReceiver:
    def __init__(self, capacity):
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.closed = False
        self.next = None
        self.stream = bytearray()
        self.pending = {}
        self.fin = None

    def window(self):
        return self.capacity - len(self.stream)

    def segment(self, seq, data=b"", syn=False, fin=False):
        if self.next is None:
            if not syn:
                return None
            self.next = (seq + 1) % MOD
            return (self.next, self.window())
        if not syn and not self.closed:
            room = self.window()
            for i, byte in enumerate(data):
                number = (seq + i) % MOD
                if (number - self.next) % MOD < room:
                    self.pending[number] = byte
            if fin:
                end = (seq + len(data)) % MOD
                if (end - self.next) % MOD <= room:
                    self.fin = end
            while self.next in self.pending:
                self.stream.append(self.pending.pop(self.next))
                self.next = (self.next + 1) % MOD
            if self.fin == self.next:
                self.next = (self.next + 1) % MOD
                self.closed = True
        return (self.next, self.window())

    def read(self, n):
        out = bytes(self.stream[:n])
        del self.stream[:n]
        return out
--- hint
Keep four things: the next expected number (`None` until the SYN), a `bytearray` of in-order bytes not yet read, a dict from byte number to byte for bytes that arrived early, and the FIN's number once you know it. The window is always `capacity - len(in-order bytes)`.
--- hint
Compute the window once when a segment arrives. For each byte, its distance is `(number - next) % 2**32`; store it in the dict only if the distance is below that window. Then loop: while the dict holds the next expected number, pop it onto the in-order bytes and add one (mod 2³²).
--- hint
For a FIN, the number is `(seq + len(data)) % 2**32`. After moving bytes in order, if the next expected number equals the FIN's number, add one more and set `closed`. Every call that is not ignored returns `(next, window)`, even for a duplicate.
--- check case | Nothing before the SYN; the SYN is ACKed with seq + 1
(lambda r: (r.segment(5, b"x"), r.segment(1000, syn=True), r.segment(1000, syn=True)))(TcpReceiver(64))
=> (None, (1001, 64), (1001, 64))
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda r: (r.segment(99, syn=True), r.segment(103, b"efg"), r.segment(100, b"abc"), r.read(4), r.segment(106, b""), r.read(10)))(TcpReceiver(8))
=> ((100, 8), (100, 8), (106, 2), b"abce", (106, 6), b"fg")
--- check case | In-order segments: cumulative ACKs
(lambda r: (r.segment(0, syn=True), r.segment(1, b"hello"), r.segment(6, b" "), r.segment(7, b"world"), r.read(100)))(TcpReceiver(100))
=> ((1, 100), (6, 95), (7, 94), (12, 89), b"hello world")
--- check case | A gap gives duplicate ACKs, and filling it jumps the ACK
(lambda r: (r.segment(10, syn=True), r.segment(14, b"de"), r.segment(16, b"f"), r.segment(11, b"abc"), r.read(9)))(TcpReceiver(20))
=> ((11, 20), (11, 20), (11, 20), (17, 14), b"abcdef")
--- check case | Old duplicates and a partly old segment
(lambda r: (r.segment(0, syn=True), r.segment(1, b"abcd"), r.segment(1, b"abcd"), r.segment(3, b"cdef"), r.read(20)))(TcpReceiver(50))
=> ((1, 50), (5, 46), (5, 46), (7, 44), b"abcdef")
--- check case | Bytes beyond the window are dropped; a zero window takes nothing
(lambda r: (r.segment(0, syn=True), r.segment(1, b"abcdefg"), r.segment(6, b"xyz"), r.read(2), r.segment(6, b"fgh"), r.read(9)))(TcpReceiver(5))
=> ((1, 5), (6, 0), (6, 0), b"ab", (8, 0), b"cdefg")
?? With 5 bytes of room, only bytes 1 to 5 fit. Reading 2 bytes opens the window to 2, so bytes 6 and 7 fit and byte 8 does not.
--- check case | FIN uses one sequence number and closes
(lambda r: (r.segment(200, syn=True), r.segment(201, b"bye", fin=True), r.closed, r.segment(204, b"", fin=True), r.read(9)))(TcpReceiver(10))
=> ((201, 10), (205, 7), True, (205, 7), b"bye")
--- check case | A FIN that arrives before a gap waits for it
(lambda r: (r.segment(0, syn=True), r.segment(4, b"d", fin=True), r.closed, r.segment(1, b"abc"), r.closed))(TcpReceiver(10))
=> ((1, 10), (1, 10), False, (6, 6), True)
--- check case | A FIN still fits in a zero window
(lambda r: (r.segment(0, syn=True), r.segment(1, b"abcd", fin=True), r.closed))(TcpReceiver(4))
=> ((1, 4), (6, 0), True)
--- check case | Sequence numbers wrap past 2**32
(lambda r: (r.segment(4294967293, syn=True), r.segment(4294967294, b"abcd"), r.segment(2, b"ef", fin=True), r.read(10)))(TcpReceiver(16))
=> ((4294967294, 16), (2, 12), (5, 10), b"abcdef")
--- check test | 100 random transfers, cut up, duplicated and shuffled, arrive intact
(lambda R: all((lambda isn, data: (lambda cuts: (lambda segs: (lambda r: r.segment(isn, syn=True) is not None and all(r.segment(s, d, fin=f) is not None for s, d, f in R.sample(segs, len(segs))) and r.closed and r.read(10 ** 6) == data and r.segment(isn, syn=True) == ((isn + len(data) + 2) % 2 ** 32, len(data)))(TcpReceiver(len(data))))([((isn + 1 + a) % 2 ** 32, data[a:b], b == len(data)) for a, b in zip(cuts, cuts[1:])] * 2))([0] + sorted(R.sample(range(1, len(data)), R.randint(0, min(12, len(data) - 1)))) + [len(data)]))(R.choice([R.randrange(2 ** 32), 2 ** 32 - R.randint(1, 50)]), bytes(R.randrange(256) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 300)))) for _ in range(100)))(__import__("random").Random(9))
--- check test | With a small buffer and no reads, exactly the first capacity bytes are kept, whatever the order
(lambda R: all((lambda isn, data, cap: (lambda segs: (lambda r: (r.segment(isn, syn=True), [r.segment(s, d) for s, d in R.sample(segs, len(segs))], r.segment(isn, syn=True) == ((isn + 1 + cap) % 2 ** 32, 0) and r.read(10 ** 6) == data[:cap])[-1])(TcpReceiver(cap)))([((isn + 1 + a) % 2 ** 32, data[a:a + 7]) for a in range(0, len(data), 7)]))(R.randrange(2 ** 32), bytes(R.randrange(256) for _ in range(200)), R.randint(1, 150)) for _ in range(60)))(__import__("random").Random(31))

+++ practice | Reading a TCP header
--- task
Write `parse_segment(seg)` for a TCP segment given as `bytes`. The header is laid out like this, every number big-endian:

```text
bytes 0-1   source port          bytes 2-3   destination port
bytes 4-7   sequence number      bytes 8-11  acknowledgement number
byte 12     upper 4 bits: data offset, the header length in 4-byte words
byte 13     flags: FIN 0x01, SYN 0x02, RST 0x04, PSH 0x08, ACK 0x10, URG 0x20
bytes 14-15 window               bytes 16-19 checksum and urgent pointer
then options, up to the header length, then the payload
```

Return a tuple `(src, dst, seq, ack, flags, window, payload)`, where `flags` is a list of the names of the flags that are set, in the order FIN, SYN, RST, PSH, ACK, URG, and `payload` is the bytes after the header (skipping any options). Raise `ValueError` if the segment is shorter than 20 bytes, if the data offset is less than 5, or if the header length is longer than the segment.

For example, a segment whose byte 12 is `0x60` has a 24-byte header: 20 bytes, then 4 bytes of options.
--- starter
def parse_segment(seg):
    return None
--- solution
FLAG_NAMES = [("FIN", 0x01), ("SYN", 0x02), ("RST", 0x04), ("PSH", 0x08), ("ACK", 0x10), ("URG", 0x20)]


def parse_segment(seg):
    if len(seg) < 20:
        raise ValueError("too short for a TCP header")
    header_len = (seg[12] >> 4) * 4
    if header_len < 20 or header_len > len(seg):
        raise ValueError("bad data offset")
    src = int.from_bytes(seg[0:2], "big")
    dst = int.from_bytes(seg[2:4], "big")
    seq = int.from_bytes(seg[4:8], "big")
    ack = int.from_bytes(seg[8:12], "big")
    flags = [name for name, bit in FLAG_NAMES if seg[13] & bit]
    window = int.from_bytes(seg[14:16], "big")
    return (src, dst, seq, ack, flags, window, seg[header_len:])
--- hint
The data offset is `seg[12] >> 4` (shift right by 4 to keep the upper 4 bits), and the header length in bytes is that times 4.
--- hint
A flag is set when `seg[13] & bit` is not zero. Build the list by going through the six (name, bit) pairs in order.
--- check case | A SYN from port 50000 to 443
parse_segment(bytes.fromhex("c35001bb000003e8000000005002faf000000000"))
=> (50000, 443, 1000, 0, ["SYN"], 64240, b"")
--- check case | ACK with PSH and a payload
parse_segment(bytes.fromhex("01bbc350ffffffff0000000a5018010000000000") + b"hi")
=> (443, 50000, 4294967295, 10, ["PSH", "ACK"], 256, b"hi")
--- check case | Options are skipped
parse_segment(bytes.fromhex("0016d431000000010000000260110000abcd0000") + bytes([2, 4, 5, 180]) + b"data")
=> (22, 54321, 1, 2, ["FIN", "ACK"], 0, b"data")
--- check case | Every flag at once
parse_segment(bytes(12) + bytes([0x50, 0x3F]) + bytes(6))[4]
=> ["FIN", "SYN", "RST", "PSH", "ACK", "URG"]
--- check test | Too short, offset below 5, or a header longer than the segment
raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_segment(bytes(19))) and raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_segment(bytes(12) + bytes([0x40]) + bytes(7))) and raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_segment(bytes(12) + bytes([0x60]) + bytes(7))) and parse_segment(bytes(12) + bytes([0x60]) + bytes(11))[6] == b""

+++ practice | Trimming a segment to the window
--- task
Real TCP receivers keep only the part of a segment that falls inside the window. Write `trim(next_seq, window, seq, data)`. The window holds the byte numbers from `next_seq` up to, but not including, `next_seq + window`, modulo 2³². The segment's bytes are numbered `seq`, `seq + 1`, and so on, modulo 2³².

Return a tuple `(new_seq, kept)`: the number of the first byte kept and the kept bytes, which are always one unbroken slice of `data`. If no byte falls inside the window, return `None`. Treat a byte as inside the window when its distance from `next_seq`, `(number - next_seq) mod 2³²`, is less than `window`. You may assume `data` is shorter than 2³¹ bytes and the window is at most 2³⁰.

For example, `trim(100, 50, 90, bytes(20))` is `(100, bytes(10))`: bytes 90 to 99 are old and are cut off.
--- starter
def trim(next_seq, window, seq, data):
    return (seq, data)
--- solution
MOD = 2 ** 32


def trim(next_seq, window, seq, data):
    ahead = (seq - next_seq) % MOD
    if ahead < 2 ** 31:
        start = 0
    else:
        start = min(MOD - ahead, len(data))
    first = (seq + start) % MOD
    offset = (first - next_seq) % MOD
    if start == len(data) or offset >= window:
        return None
    end = min(len(data), start + window - offset)
    return (first, data[start:end])
--- hint
First find how many bytes at the front are old. If the segment starts before `next_seq`, the distance `(seq - next_seq) % 2**32` is at least 2³¹, and the number of old bytes is `2**32` minus that distance.
--- hint
After cutting the old bytes, the first kept byte is at some offset inside the window. If that offset is already `window` or more (or nothing is left), return `None`. Otherwise keep at most `window - offset` bytes.
--- check case | The example: old bytes cut from the front
trim(100, 50, 90, bytes(20))
=> (100, bytes(10))
--- check case | Cut at both ends
trim(1000, 5, 998, b"abcdefghij")
=> (1000, b"cdefg")
--- check case | Entirely inside, entirely old, entirely beyond
(trim(10, 100, 20, b"xyz"), trim(10, 100, 5, b"abcde"), trim(10, 100, 110, b"late"))
=> ((20, b"xyz"), None, None)
--- check case | A zero window keeps nothing
trim(50, 0, 50, b"data")
=> None
--- check case | Across the wrap
(trim(4294967294, 4, 4294967290, b"ABCDEFGHIJ"), trim(3, 10, 4294967295, b"abcdefghij"))
=> ((4294967294, b"EFGH"), (3, b"efghij"))
--- check test | Matches a byte-by-byte filter on 500 random cases
(lambda R, M: all((lambda n, w, s, d: (lambda keep: (trim(n, w, s, d) is None) if not keep else trim(n, w, s, d) == (keep[0][0], bytes(b for _, b in keep)))([((s + i) % M, b) for i, b in enumerate(d) if ((s + i - n) % M) < w]))(n, R.randint(0, 40), (n + R.randint(-60, 60)) % M, bytes(R.randrange(256) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 50)))) for n in [R.choice([R.randrange(M), M - R.randint(1, 30)]) for _ in range(500)]))(__import__("random").Random(8), 2 ** 32)

+++ practice | The handshake over a lossy link
--- task
Simulate the three-way handshake when segments can be lost, with the timeouts of lesson 7. Write `handshake(x, y, lost, tries)`, where `x` is the client's ISN and `y` the server's.

Every segment sent, by either side, gets the next number from a counter starting at 0. A segment whose number is in the set `lost` disappears. The exchange goes like this:

1. **Phase 1.** The client sends a SYN. If the SYN is lost, the client times out and sends it again. If it arrives, the server answers with a SYN+ACK (every SYN that arrives gets one). If that is lost, the client times out and sends the SYN again. The client sends at most `tries` SYNs in all; if none gets a SYN+ACK back, it gives up.
2. **Phase 2.** When a SYN+ACK arrives, the client is ESTABLISHED and sends an ACK. If the ACK arrives, the server is ESTABLISHED. If it is lost, the server times out and sends the SYN+ACK again, and the client answers every SYN+ACK that arrives with an ACK. The server sends at most `tries` SYN+ACKs in phase 2, counting only the ones it sends there.

Return a tuple `(trace, client_state, server_state)`. `trace` lists every segment sent, as strings in exactly these forms, with `" lost"` added at the end of a lost one:

```text
C SYN seq=100
S SYN-ACK seq=300 ack=101
C ACK seq=101 ack=301
```

The states are `"ESTABLISHED"` or `"CLOSED"` for the client (closed if it gave up), and `"ESTABLISHED"`, `"SYN-RECEIVED"` (a SYN arrived but no ACK did) or `"LISTEN"` (no SYN ever arrived) for the server.
--- starter
def handshake(x, y, lost, tries):
    return ([], "CLOSED", "LISTEN")
--- solution
def handshake(x, y, lost, tries):
    trace = []
    count = 0

    def send(text):
        nonlocal count
        gone = count in lost
        count += 1
        trace.append(text + (" lost" if gone else ""))
        return not gone

    syn = f"C SYN seq={x}"
    synack = f"S SYN-ACK seq={y} ack={x + 1}"
    ack = f"C ACK seq={x + 1} ack={y + 1}"
    server = "LISTEN"
    connected = False
    for _ in range(tries):
        if send(syn):
            server = "SYN-RECEIVED"
            if send(synack):
                connected = True
                break
    if not connected:
        return (trace, "CLOSED", server)
    if send(ack):
        return (trace, "ESTABLISHED", "ESTABLISHED")
    for _ in range(tries):
        if send(synack) and send(ack):
            return (trace, "ESTABLISHED", "ESTABLISHED")
    return (trace, "ESTABLISHED", server)
--- hint
Write one helper that takes the text of a segment, gives it the next number, records it in the trace (with " lost" if its number is in `lost`), and returns whether it arrived. Everything else is two loops.
--- hint
Phase 1 loops up to `tries` times: send the SYN; if it arrived, the server is SYN-RECEIVED and sends a SYN+ACK; if that arrived, stop. Phase 2 first sends one ACK. If it was lost, loop up to `tries` times: the server resends the SYN+ACK, and if it arrives the client sends another ACK.
--- check case | Nothing lost: three segments
handshake(100, 300, set(), 3)
=> (["C SYN seq=100", "S SYN-ACK seq=300 ack=101", "C ACK seq=101 ack=301"], "ESTABLISHED", "ESTABLISHED")
--- check case | The first SYN is lost
handshake(7, 50, {0}, 3)[0]
=> ["C SYN seq=7 lost", "C SYN seq=7", "S SYN-ACK seq=50 ack=8", "C ACK seq=8 ack=51"]
--- check case | The SYN+ACK is lost: the server answers the retransmitted SYN again
handshake(7, 50, {1}, 3)[0]
=> ["C SYN seq=7", "S SYN-ACK seq=50 ack=8 lost", "C SYN seq=7", "S SYN-ACK seq=50 ack=8", "C ACK seq=8 ack=51"]
--- check case | The last ACK is lost: the server resends its SYN+ACK
handshake(7, 50, {2}, 3)
=> (["C SYN seq=7", "S SYN-ACK seq=50 ack=8", "C ACK seq=8 ack=51 lost", "S SYN-ACK seq=50 ack=8", "C ACK seq=8 ack=51"], "ESTABLISHED", "ESTABLISHED")
--- check case | The client gives up; the server never heard a SYN
handshake(1, 2, {0, 1}, 2)
=> (["C SYN seq=1 lost", "C SYN seq=1 lost"], "CLOSED", "LISTEN")
--- check case | The client gives up, but the server is left half-open
handshake(1, 2, {1, 3}, 2)
=> (["C SYN seq=1", "S SYN-ACK seq=2 ack=2 lost", "C SYN seq=1", "S SYN-ACK seq=2 ack=2 lost"], "CLOSED", "SYN-RECEIVED")
--- check case | The client is established but the server gives up waiting for the ACK
handshake(10, 20, {2, 3, 5}, 2)
=> (["C SYN seq=10", "S SYN-ACK seq=20 ack=11", "C ACK seq=11 ack=21 lost", "S SYN-ACK seq=20 ack=11 lost", "S SYN-ACK seq=20 ack=11", "C ACK seq=11 ack=21 lost"], "ESTABLISHED", "SYN-RECEIVED")

+++ practice | Before and after, across the wrap
--- task
Write two functions on 32-bit sequence numbers, using serial number arithmetic.

- `seq_cmp(a, b)` returns `-1` if `a` comes before `b`, `0` if they are equal, and `1` if `a` comes after `b`. Here `b` comes after `a` when `(b - a) mod 2³²` is from 1 to 2³¹ − 1. When `(b - a) mod 2³²` is exactly 2³¹, neither is after the other: raise `ValueError`. Also raise `ValueError` if `a` or `b` is not a whole number from 0 to 2³² − 1.
- `latest(numbers)` returns the number in the list that comes after all the others (or equals them), or `None` for an empty list. Raise `ValueError` if some two numbers in the list cannot be compared (their distance is exactly 2³¹), or if no single number is latest.

For example, `seq_cmp(4294967295, 3)` is `-1`, and `latest([4294967290, 5, 4294967295])` is `5`.
--- starter
def seq_cmp(a, b):
    if a < b:
        return -1
    return 1 if a > b else 0


def latest(numbers):
    return max(numbers)
--- solution
MOD = 2 ** 32
HALF = 2 ** 31


def seq_cmp(a, b):
    for v in (a, b):
        if not isinstance(v, int) or not 0 <= v < MOD:
            raise ValueError("not a 32-bit sequence number")
    d = (b - a) % MOD
    if d == 0:
        return 0
    if d == HALF:
        raise ValueError("exactly half the space apart")
    return -1 if d < HALF else 1


def latest(numbers):
    if not numbers:
        return None
    best = numbers[0]
    for n in numbers[1:]:
        if seq_cmp(best, n) < 0:
            best = n
    for n in numbers:
        if seq_cmp(best, n) < 0:
            raise ValueError("no single latest number")
    return best
--- hint
Compute `d = (b - a) % 2**32` once. Zero means equal, below 2³¹ means `b` is after `a` (so return -1), exactly 2³¹ is the error, and above means `a` is after `b`.
--- hint
Serial order is not a true order over the whole circle: three numbers spread around it can each be "after" the one before. So after finding a candidate with one pass, make a second pass to check that nothing comes after it.
--- check case | Plain cases
(seq_cmp(5, 9), seq_cmp(9, 5), seq_cmp(7, 7))
=> (-1, 1, 0)
--- check case | Across the wrap
(seq_cmp(4294967295, 3), seq_cmp(3, 4294967295), seq_cmp(0, 4294967295))
=> (-1, 1, 1)
--- check case | Just under half the space, and exactly half
(seq_cmp(0, 2147483647), seq_cmp(2147483647, 0), raises(ValueError, lambda: seq_cmp(0, 2147483648)), raises(ValueError, lambda: seq_cmp(3000000000, 852516352)))
=> (-1, 1, True, True)
--- check test | Out-of-range and non-integer inputs are rejected
raises(ValueError, lambda: seq_cmp(-1, 5)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: seq_cmp(5, 2 ** 32)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: seq_cmp(1.5, 2))
--- check case | latest across the wrap, a single item, and an empty list
(latest([4294967290, 5, 4294967295]), latest([42]), latest([]), latest([9, 9, 3]))
=> (5, 42, None, 9)
--- check test | Three numbers spread around the circle have no latest
raises(ValueError, lambda: latest([0, 1500000000, 3000000000])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: latest([10, 10 + 2 ** 31]))

+++ practice | Debug: the timeout that jumps around
--- task
TCP sets its retransmission timeout (RTO) from measured round-trip times, following RFC 6298. `RtoEstimator` should work like this, with times in milliseconds:

- Before any measurement, `rto` is 1000.
- The first measurement R sets SRTT = R and RTTVAR = R / 2.
- Each later measurement R first updates RTTVAR = 0.75 · RTTVAR + 0.25 · |SRTT − R|, using the **old** SRTT, and only then updates SRTT = 0.875 · SRTT + 0.125 · R.
- After every measurement, `rto` = max(`min_rto`, SRTT + 4 · RTTVAR).
- `timeout()` doubles `rto`, up to at most 60000.

The code below gives the right first RTO, but later ones are wrong: with `min_rto` set to 0, after measurements of 100 and then 300, `rto` should be 475 but it is 450. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
class RtoEstimator:
    def __init__(self, min_rto=1000):
        self.min_rto = min_rto
        self.srtt = None
        self.rttvar = None
        self.rto = 1000

    def sample(self, r):
        if self.srtt is None:
            self.srtt = r
            self.rttvar = r / 2
        else:
            self.srtt = 0.875 * self.srtt + 0.125 * r
            self.rttvar = 0.75 * self.rttvar + 0.25 * abs(self.srtt - r)
        self.rto = max(self.min_rto, self.srtt + 4 * self.rttvar)

    def timeout(self):
        self.rto = min(self.rto * 2, 60000)
--- solution
class RtoEstimator:
    def __init__(self, min_rto=1000):
        self.min_rto = min_rto
        self.srtt = None
        self.rttvar = None
        self.rto = 1000

    def sample(self, r):
        if self.srtt is None:
            self.srtt = r
            self.rttvar = r / 2
        else:
            self.rttvar = 0.75 * self.rttvar + 0.25 * abs(self.srtt - r)
            self.srtt = 0.875 * self.srtt + 0.125 * r
        self.rto = max(self.min_rto, self.srtt + 4 * self.rttvar)

    def timeout(self):
        self.rto = min(self.rto * 2, 60000)
--- hint
Work the example by hand. After the first sample, SRTT is 100 and RTTVAR is 50. Which SRTT should the second RTTVAR use, 100 or the new 125?
--- hint
The two update lines are in the wrong order: RTTVAR must be computed from the SRTT before it moves.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda e: (e.sample(100), e.sample(300), e.srtt, e.rttvar, e.rto)[2:])(RtoEstimator(0))
=> (125.0, 87.5, 475.0)
--- check case | A steady RTT: the variation shrinks
(lambda e: [(e.sample(200), e.rto)[1] for _ in range(4)])(RtoEstimator(0))
=> [600.0, 500.0, 425.0, 368.75]
--- check case | The minimum applies, and timeouts double up to 60 s
(lambda e: (e.rto, e.sample(40), e.rto, e.timeout(), e.rto, [e.timeout() for _ in range(10)], e.rto)[::2])(RtoEstimator())
=> (1000, 1000, 2000, 60000)
--- check case | A sudden jump in RTT
(lambda e: (e.sample(80), e.sample(80), e.sample(400), round(e.srtt, 4), round(e.rttvar, 4)))(RtoEstimator(0))[3:]
=> (120.0, 102.5)

+++ practice | A slow reader
--- task
A telemetry recorder receives a file over TCP, but its disk sometimes stalls, so flow control sets the pace. Simulate it in ticks. Write `slow_reader(total, capacity, reads)`.

The receiver has a buffer of `capacity` bytes and starts by advertising a window of `capacity`. `reads[i]` is how many bytes the receiving program tries to read in tick `i` (counting from 0); 0 means the disk is stalled. After the list runs out, the program reads everything in the buffer each tick. In each tick:

1. If bytes remain to be sent and the last advertised window is 0, the sender sends a **window probe**, which carries no data. Otherwise it sends `min(remaining, window)` bytes, where `window` is the last advertised window.
2. The bytes arrive at once and go into the buffer.
3. The program reads `min(reads[i], bytes in the buffer)` bytes out of it (or all of them, once the list has run out).
4. The receiver advertises its window: `capacity` minus the bytes still in the buffer.

The transfer is finished at the end of the first tick in which the program has read all `total` bytes. Return a tuple `(ticks, sent, probes)`: the number of ticks, the list of bytes sent in each tick (0 for a probe, or when nothing is left to send), and the number of probes. A `total` of 0 takes no ticks: `(0, [], 0)`.

For example, `slow_reader(10, 4, [1, 0, 0, 4])` is `(6, [4, 1, 0, 0, 4, 1], 2)`. After tick 1 the buffer is full and the program is stalled, so the window is 0 and ticks 2 and 3 are probes. In tick 3 the program reads 4 bytes, the window opens again, and the rest follows.
--- starter
def slow_reader(total, capacity, reads):
    return (0, [], 0)
--- solution
def slow_reader(total, capacity, reads):
    remaining = total
    buffered = 0
    read = 0
    window = capacity
    sent = []
    probes = 0
    while read < total:
        if remaining > 0 and window == 0:
            probes += 1
            n = 0
        else:
            n = min(remaining, window)
        remaining -= n
        buffered += n
        tick = len(sent)
        sent.append(n)
        take = min(reads[tick], buffered) if tick < len(reads) else buffered
        buffered -= take
        read += take
        window = capacity - buffered
    return (len(sent), sent, probes)
--- hint
Keep four numbers: bytes not yet sent, bytes in the buffer, bytes read so far, and the last advertised window. Each tick does the four steps in order and appends what was sent.
--- hint
The tick number is how many ticks have run before this one, which is the length of the list of sends so far. Use it to look up `reads`, and read the whole buffer once it is past the end of the list.
--- check case | The example from the task
slow_reader(10, 4, [1, 0, 0, 4])
=> (6, [4, 1, 0, 0, 4, 1], 2)
--- check case | A reader that keeps up never closes the window
slow_reader(10, 4, [4, 4, 4])
=> (3, [4, 4, 2], 0)
--- check case | Partial reads shrink the window without closing it
slow_reader(9, 6, [2, 2, 2])
=> (4, [6, 2, 1, 0], 0)
--- check case | A long stall: probe after probe
slow_reader(3, 2, [0, 0, 0, 0])
=> (6, [2, 0, 0, 0, 0, 1], 4)
?? After tick 0 the buffer holds 2 bytes and nothing is read until the list runs out. Ticks 1 to 4 are probes: in tick 4 the probe goes out before the program empties the buffer.
--- check case | Nothing to send
slow_reader(0, 8, [0])
=> (0, [], 0)
--- check case | The window is exactly used up by the last byte
slow_reader(4, 4, [0, 0])
=> (3, [4, 0, 0], 0)

+++ question | Why the handshake has three messages
--- ask
Why does TCP use three messages to open a connection instead of two (SYN, then SYN+ACK)?
--- choice
Because the checksum needs three segments to be computed.
--- choice correct
Because each side must learn the other's ISN and know its own ISN was received; after two messages the server does not know the client saw its ISN, and an old duplicate SYN would open a connection nobody wanted.
--- choice
Because the third message carries the receive window, which cannot fit in a SYN.
--- choice
Because routers need three segments to learn the route.
--- why
Message 2 proves to the client that its SYN arrived, and message 3 proves the same to the server. Without message 3, a delayed SYN from an old connection would leave the server believing in a connection the client never asked for. The window is sent in every segment, including the SYN.

+++ question | A distance across the wrap
--- ask
With 32-bit sequence numbers, what is the distance from 4294967290 to 5, that is, (5 − 4294967290) mod 2³²? Type the number.
--- answer
11
--- why
4294967290 is 6 below 2³² = 4294967296. Counting on: 6 steps reach 0, and 5 more reach 5, so the distance is 11, which is less than 2³¹. So 5 comes after 4294967290, across the wrap.

+++ question | What TIME-WAIT protects
--- ask
The side that closes a TCP connection first waits in TIME-WAIT for twice the maximum segment lifetime. What goes wrong if it skips the wait and a new connection opens at once on the same 4-tuple?
--- choice
Nothing: the new connection has a new random ISN, so old segments are always rejected.
--- choice correct
A delayed segment from the old connection could land inside the new connection's window and be accepted as data; and if the last ACK was lost, nobody is left to answer the other side's resent FIN.
--- choice
The receive buffer of the old connection would overflow.
--- choice
The routers would still have the old connection in their tables.
--- why
A random ISN makes a collision unlikely, not impossible, and TCP wants it impossible. After 2·MSL every old segment has arrived or been dropped. Routers keep no per-connection state at all, which is the end-to-end design from lesson 1.

=== net-10 | Congestion control: AIMD, slow start and fast retransmit
--- teach
Last lesson's flow control protects the **receiver**: the sender never has more bytes in flight than the receiver has room for. But between them sit routers, and a router's link to the next hop has a fixed speed. If senders push more into it than it can carry, its queue fills and it drops packets. Nothing in flow control prevents that. This lesson is about **congestion control**: how every TCP sender, with no one in charge, finds a fair share of the network and backs off when the network is overloaded.

### A crowded doorway

Picture a hall with one door, and a crowd trying to leave. If everyone walks calmly, people flow out at the door's full rate. If everyone pushes, people jam in the doorway and fewer get out per minute than before. Pushing harder makes it worse. The fix is for each person to slow down the moment they feel the crush, and speed up gently when there is space.

### Congestion collapse

A router that receives packets faster than its outgoing link can send them keeps them in a queue. When the queue is full it drops arriving packets. Senders notice the loss and **retransmit**, which adds even more packets, many of them copies of packets that were only delayed. The links stay busy, but mostly with copies and with packets that will be dropped further on. Useful throughput, called **goodput**, falls towards zero.

This is **congestion collapse**, and it really happened: in October 1986 the link between two Berkeley labs [[fell from 32,000 bits a second to 40|collapse]]. Van Jacobson's fix, published in 1988, is the core of every TCP sender today.

### The congestion window

The sender keeps a second window, the **congestion window** cwnd: its own guess of how many bytes the network can take in flight. It may send only as much as both windows allow:

bytes in flight ≤ min(cwnd, rwnd).

rwnd comes from the receiver (flow control). cwnd is never sent anywhere; the sender works it out alone. Since about one window's worth of data is sent each round-trip time, the sending rate is about cwnd / RTT.

How does a sender know the network is overloaded? Routers do not tell it (though see [[ECN|ecn]]). TCP uses **loss** as the signal: a timeout, or duplicate ACKs, means a packet was probably dropped at a full queue.

The windows are counted in bytes, but the rules are easiest to state in **MSS**, the maximum segment size: the most data one segment carries, typically 1,460 bytes.

### Slow start

A new connection knows nothing about the path. It starts with cwnd = 1 MSS (modern systems use [[10 MSS|iw]]) and adds **1 MSS for every ACK** that acknowledges new data. Each segment sent and ACKed is replaced by two, so cwnd **doubles every round trip**:

```python
cwnd = 1                       # in segments
for rtt in range(1, 6):
    acks = cwnd                # every segment of this round is ACKed
    cwnd += acks               # one more segment per ACK
    print("after RTT", rtt, "cwnd =", cwnd)
```

That prints 2, 4, 8, 16, 32. Despite its name, **slow start** grows exponentially. It is only "slow" compared with blasting a whole window at once. Reaching a window of W segments takes about log₂ W round trips.

Slow start stops when cwnd reaches the **slow start threshold**, ssthresh: TCP's memory of the window size where trouble happened last time. At the start, ssthresh is set very high.

### Congestion avoidance: additive increase

Once cwnd ≥ ssthresh, TCP is in **congestion avoidance**. Now it grows by only about **1 MSS per round trip**. On each ACK:

cwnd = cwnd + MSS · MSS / cwnd.

Why this adds one MSS per round trip: in one round trip about cwnd / MSS segments are ACKed, and each ACK adds MSS² / cwnd, so together they add (cwnd / MSS) · (MSS² / cwnd) = MSS. With whole numbers of bytes, the division rounds down:

```python
mss = 1000
cwnd = 5000
for ack in range(5):
    cwnd += mss * mss // cwnd
    print(cwnd)
```

Five ACKs, one round trip at a window of 5 segments, add 200 + 192 + 185 + 179 + 173 = 929 bytes: a little under one MSS, because cwnd grows along the way.

### Loss: multiplicative decrease

When a loss is detected, TCP halves its idea of a safe window:

ssthresh = max(cwnd / 2, 2 · MSS).

The standard says to halve the amount of data in flight. When the sender always has data to send, that is the same as cwnd, and this lesson uses cwnd. The 2 · MSS floor keeps the threshold useful even after repeated losses. What happens to cwnd depends on how the loss was noticed.

- **A timeout** means nothing is getting through: every segment in flight was lost, or all their ACKs were. TCP sets cwnd = 1 MSS and starts slow start again, up to the new ssthresh.
- **Three duplicate ACKs** mean segments after the lost one are still arriving, since each duplicate ACK was triggered by one. The network is working, just overloaded. TCP reacts more gently, below.

### Fast retransmit

Last lesson's receiver sends a duplicate ACK for every segment that arrives after a gap. So when segment k is lost and segments k + 1, k + 2 and k + 3 arrive, the sender gets three duplicate ACKs, all naming the start of segment k.

**Fast retransmit**: after the **third** duplicate ACK, resend the missing segment at once, without waiting for the timeout, which is often hundreds of milliseconds.

Why three, and not one? Packets are sometimes **reordered** on the way, for example when they take two parallel links. Then one or two duplicate ACKs come even though nothing was lost. Three in a row is rare without a loss.

### Fast recovery

After a fast retransmit, TCP Reno does **fast recovery** instead of slow start:

1. ssthresh = max(cwnd / 2, 2 · MSS), and cwnd = ssthresh + 3 · MSS. The 3 · MSS counts the three segments that the duplicate ACKs prove have left the network.
2. Each **further** duplicate ACK adds 1 MSS to cwnd. Another segment has left the network, so another may go in. This is called **window inflation**.
3. When a **new** ACK arrives (the retransmission filled the gap), set cwnd = ssthresh. This is **deflation**. TCP then continues in congestion avoidance from half the old window.

The whole behaviour, from the sender's side:

| event | ssthresh | cwnd |
|---|---|---|
| new ACK, cwnd < ssthresh | same | + 1 MSS (slow start) |
| new ACK, cwnd ≥ ssthresh | same | + MSS²/cwnd (avoidance) |
| 3rd duplicate ACK | max(cwnd/2, 2·MSS) | ssthresh + 3·MSS, resend the missing segment |
| more duplicate ACKs | same | + 1 MSS each |
| new ACK ending recovery | same | = ssthresh |
| timeout | max(cwnd/2, 2·MSS) | 1 MSS, slow start again |

Over time, cwnd traces a **sawtooth**: it climbs by one MSS per round trip until a loss, drops to half, and climbs again. This pattern is called **AIMD**: **additive increase, multiplicative decrease**.

### Why AIMD is fair

Take two flows sharing a bottleneck link of capacity C, with windows x and y. Assume the simplest model: every round trip both add a (additive increase), and when x + y goes over C, both lose a packet and multiply their windows by b, with 0 < b < 1 (b = 1/2 for TCP).

Look at the **gap** d = x − y between them:

- **Increase:** (x + a) − (y + a) = x − y. The gap does not change.
- **Decrease:** b·x − b·y = b·(x − y). The gap shrinks by the factor b.

The windows cannot grow forever without crossing C, so decreases keep happening. After k of them, the gap is bᵏ times what it was, which goes to 0. So the two flows approach **equal shares**, whatever they started with. At the same time the total keeps climbing to C and falling to b·C, so the link stays mostly used: AIMD is **efficient** as well as fair.

```python
x, y = 50, 4          # two very unequal flows
cap = 60
for step in range(200):
    if x + y > cap:
        x, y = x / 2, y / 2
        print("halve: gap", x - y)
    else:
        x, y = x + 1, y + 1
```

The gap printed at each halving is 23, 11.5, 5.75, and so on. Try the other choices and the argument fails. **Additive decrease** subtracts the same amount from both, so the gap never shrinks. **Multiplicative increase** (both multiply by the same factor) grows the gap as fast as it shrinks.

### What one flow gets: the square-root law

How fast does one Reno flow go when packets are lost at a rate p? Take the ideal sawtooth. The window climbs from W/2 to W, one MSS per round trip, so a cycle lasts about W/2 round trips. The segments sent in one cycle are about the average window times the number of rounds: (3W/4) · (W/2) = 3W²/8. One of them is lost per cycle, so p ≈ 8 / (3W²), which gives W ≈ √(8 / (3p)).

The average window is 3W/4, so the average rate is

rate ≈ (3/4) · √(8 / (3p)) · MSS / RTT = √(3/2) · MSS / (RTT · √p) ≈ 1.22 · MSS / (RTT · √p).

This is the **Mathis formula**. Two things follow from it.

- Throughput falls like **1 / √p**: to go 10 times faster, the loss rate must be 100 times smaller.
- Throughput falls like **1 / RTT**. Two flows with the same loss rate but different round trips do not get equal shares: the one with the shorter RTT adds its MSS more often, and gets more. This is **RTT unfairness**.

```python
from math import sqrt
mss, rtt = 1460, 0.080         # bytes, seconds
for p in [0.01, 0.0001]:
    rate = sqrt(1.5) * mss / (rtt * sqrt(p))
    print(p, round(rate * 8 / 1e6, 2), "Mbit/s")
```

At 1% loss and an 80 ms round trip, one flow gets about 1.79 Mbit/s. At 0.01% loss, ten times as much.

### What it costs

- **Per ACK**, the sender does a constant amount of work: O(1).
- **In time**, slow start needs about log₂ W round trips to reach a window of W segments. After a loss in congestion avoidance, getting back from W/2 to W takes W/2 round trips. On a fast long path this is painfully slow: at 10 Gbit/s with a 100 ms round trip, W is about 85,000 segments, and recovering from one loss takes 42,500 round trips, over an hour. That is why Linux replaced Reno's growth with [[CUBIC|cubic]].
- **Many flows:** N flows through one bottleneck of capacity C each end up with about C / N, adjusted for RTT unfairness. A program that opens many connections at once gets more than its share, which is why browsers limit the connections they open to one server.

### Where it is used

- **Every TCP stack** implements slow start, AIMD in some form, fast retransmit and fast recovery. Linux uses CUBIC by default; Google uses [[BBR|bbr]] for much of its traffic.
- **QUIC** uses the same ideas over UDP; its standard describes a Reno-like controller.
- **Video streaming** adapts its quality to the rate TCP achieves, so congestion control decides what picture you see.
- **Ground stations** that move large science files over shared networks run many flows or tuned controllers, because one Reno flow with a long RTT and a little loss cannot fill a fast link.

**Watch out:** the classic mistakes.

- Confusing the two windows. Flow control (rwnd) is about the receiver's buffer; congestion control (cwnd) is about the network. The sender obeys the smaller of the two.
- Doing the loss steps in the wrong order. ssthresh must be computed from cwnd **before** cwnd is cut: set cwnd to 1 MSS first, and the threshold becomes the floor every time.
- Treating one duplicate ACK as a loss. Reordering causes single duplicates all the time; the threshold is three.
- Mixing units. If cwnd is in bytes, slow start adds MSS bytes per ACK, not 1.

::: context collapse The 1986 collapse
In October 1986 the connection between Lawrence Berkeley Laboratory and UC Berkeley, 400 yards and three routers apart, dropped from 32 kbit/s to 40 bit/s. Van Jacobson and Michael Karels traced it to TCP senders that retransmitted into full queues without ever slowing down. Their 1988 paper "Congestion Avoidance and Control" introduced slow start, congestion avoidance, better RTT estimation and fast retransmit. It is one of the most cited papers in networking, and its ideas have run on nearly every computer on the Internet since.
:::

::: context ecn Routers that warn instead of drop
Explicit Congestion Notification (RFC 3168) lets a router mark a packet instead of dropping it when its queue starts to fill. It sets two bits in the IP header; the receiver echoes the mark back in its TCP ACKs, and the sender reacts as if a packet had been lost, without anything actually being lost. Data centres use it heavily: DCTCP reacts to the fraction of marked packets, keeping queues tiny and delays low.
:::

::: context iw Starting at ten segments
The first TCPs started slow start at one segment. RFC 6928 (2013) allowed an initial window of 10 segments, about 14 KB, after Google measured that most web responses were small and finished while still in slow start. With 10 segments, a typical small page arrives in one or two round trips instead of four or five. Larger initial windows are debated, because they can overload slow links.
:::

::: context cubic CUBIC
CUBIC, the default in Linux since 2006, grows cwnd as a cubic function of the time since the last loss, not by one MSS per round trip. It climbs fast when far below the window where the loss happened, slows down near it, and probes carefully above it. Because its growth depends on time rather than round trips, flows with different RTTs are treated more fairly. Its decrease factor is 0.7 rather than 0.5.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">

  <line x1="30" y1="140" x2="30" y2="30" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="30" y1="40" x2="345" y2="40" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-dasharray="4 4"/>
  <text x="34" y="36" font-size="9" fill="#6c7a93">window at the last loss</text>
  <polyline points="30,90 90,40 90,90 150,40 150,90 210,40 210,90 270,40 270,90 330,40" fill="none" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
```
