```svg
  <text x="38" y="24" font-size="10" fill="#b4232c">CUBIC: fast, then flat near the old window, then probe</text>
  <text x="188" y="150" font-size="10" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">time</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context bbr BBR
BBR (Bottleneck Bandwidth and Round-trip propagation time), published by Google in 2016, does not use loss as its main signal. It measures the delivery rate and the minimum RTT, and sends at the estimated bottleneck rate with about one bandwidth-delay product in flight (lesson 15). That keeps router queues short, and works well on long paths with random loss, where Reno would keep halving for no reason.
:::
--- task
Write a class `RenoSender` that keeps TCP Reno's congestion state, in bytes.

- `RenoSender(mss, ssthresh)` starts with `cwnd = mss`, the given `ssthresh`, `dupacks = 0` and `in_recovery = False`. All four are attributes.
- `on_ack(acked)` is called for a **new** ACK that acknowledges `acked` new bytes (at least 1):
  - if in recovery, set `cwnd = ssthresh` and leave recovery (no other growth on this ACK);
  - otherwise, if `cwnd < ssthresh`, add `min(acked, mss)` (slow start);
  - otherwise add `max(1, mss * mss // cwnd)` (congestion avoidance).
  - In every case, set `dupacks` back to 0.
- `on_dupack()` is called for a duplicate ACK, and returns `True` exactly when the segment should be fast-retransmitted now:
  - add 1 to `dupacks`;
  - if in recovery, add `mss` to `cwnd` and return `False`;
  - if `dupacks` is now 3, set `ssthresh = max(cwnd // 2, 2 * mss)`, then `cwnd = ssthresh + 3 * mss`, enter recovery, and return `True`;
  - otherwise return `False`.
- `on_timeout()` sets `ssthresh = max(cwnd // 2, 2 * mss)`, then `cwnd = mss`, `dupacks = 0`, and leaves recovery.

For example, with `s = RenoSender(1000, 4000)`, four calls of `s.on_ack(1000)` take `cwnd` to 2000, 3000, 4000, and then 4250, since the last one is in congestion avoidance: 1000 · 1000 // 4000 = 250.
--- starter
class RenoSender:
    def __init__(self, mss, ssthresh):
        self.mss = mss
        self.cwnd = mss
        self.ssthresh = ssthresh
        self.dupacks = 0
        self.in_recovery = False

    def on_ack(self, acked):
        pass

    def on_dupack(self):
        return False

    def on_timeout(self):
        pass
--- solution
class RenoSender:
    def __init__(self, mss, ssthresh):
        self.mss = mss
        self.cwnd = mss
        self.ssthresh = ssthresh
        self.dupacks = 0
        self.in_recovery = False

    def on_ack(self, acked):
        if self.in_recovery:
            self.cwnd = self.ssthresh
            self.in_recovery = False
        elif self.cwnd < self.ssthresh:
            self.cwnd += min(acked, self.mss)
        else:
            self.cwnd += max(1, self.mss * self.mss // self.cwnd)
        self.dupacks = 0

    def on_dupack(self):
        self.dupacks += 1
        if self.in_recovery:
            self.cwnd += self.mss
            return False
        if self.dupacks == 3:
            self.ssthresh = max(self.cwnd // 2, 2 * self.mss)
            self.cwnd = self.ssthresh + 3 * self.mss
            self.in_recovery = True
            return True
        return False

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.ssthresh = max(self.cwnd // 2, 2 * self.mss)
        self.cwnd = self.mss
        self.dupacks = 0
        self.in_recovery = False
--- hint
`on_ack` is one `if / elif / else` on two questions, in this order: am I in recovery, and am I below ssthresh? The avoidance step uses whole-number division, `mss * mss // cwnd`, with at least 1.
--- hint
In `on_dupack`, count first. Being in recovery wins over everything: inflate by one MSS and return `False`. Otherwise only the third duplicate does anything.
--- hint
In both loss reactions, compute the new `ssthresh` from the current `cwnd` first, and only then change `cwnd`.
--- check case | The example: slow start, then avoidance
(lambda s: [(s.on_ack(1000), s.cwnd)[1] for _ in range(4)])(RenoSender(1000, 4000))
=> [2000, 3000, 4000, 4250]
--- check case | Slow start adds at most one MSS per ACK, even for a big ACK
(lambda s: (s.on_ack(3000), s.cwnd, s.on_ack(200), s.cwnd)[1::2])(RenoSender(1000, 100000))
=> (2000, 2200)
--- check case | Congestion avoidance: about one MSS per window of ACKs
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 10000), [s.on_ack(1000) for _ in range(10)], s.cwnd)[2])(RenoSender(1000, 5000))
=> 10956
--- check case | Avoidance always grows by at least one byte
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 2000000), s.on_ack(10), s.cwnd)[2])(RenoSender(1000, 1000))
=> 2000001
--- check case | One and two duplicate ACKs do nothing; the third retransmits
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 16000), s.on_dupack(), s.on_dupack(), s.cwnd, s.on_dupack(), s.ssthresh, s.cwnd, s.in_recovery)[1:])(RenoSender(1000, 64000))
=> (False, False, 16000, True, 8000, 11000, True)
--- check case | More duplicates inflate the window, and a new ACK deflates it
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 16000), [s.on_dupack() for _ in range(6)], s.cwnd, s.on_ack(5000), s.cwnd, s.in_recovery, s.dupacks)[1:])(RenoSender(1000, 64000))
=> ([False, False, True, False, False, False], 14000, None, 8000, False, 0)
--- check case | After recovery, growth continues in avoidance
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 16000), [s.on_dupack() for _ in range(3)], s.on_ack(1000), s.on_ack(1000), s.cwnd)[4])(RenoSender(1000, 64000))
=> 8125
--- check case | A new ACK between duplicates starts the count again
(lambda s: (s.on_dupack(), s.on_dupack(), s.on_ack(1000), s.on_dupack(), s.on_dupack(), s.dupacks, s.in_recovery)[3:])(RenoSender(1000, 64000))
=> (False, False, 2, False)
--- check case | A timeout: half the window as threshold, back to one MSS
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 20000), s.on_timeout(), s.ssthresh, s.cwnd, [s.on_ack(1000) for _ in range(10)], s.cwnd)[2:])(RenoSender(1000, 64000))
=> (10000, 1000, [None] * 10, 10100)
?? After the timeout, slow start runs from 1000 up to the new ssthresh of 10000 (nine ACKs), and the tenth ACK is in avoidance: 1000 · 1000 // 10000 = 100.
--- check case | The threshold never falls below two MSS
(lambda s: (s.on_timeout(), s.ssthresh, s.on_timeout(), s.ssthresh, s.cwnd)[1::2])(RenoSender(1460, 50000))
=> (2920, 2920)
--- check case | A timeout during recovery leaves recovery
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 12000), [s.on_dupack() for _ in range(4)], s.cwnd, s.on_timeout(), s.ssthresh, s.cwnd, s.in_recovery, s.dupacks)[2:])(RenoSender(1000, 64000))
=> (10000, None, 5000, 1000, False, 0)
--- check test | 2,000 random events: cwnd never below one MSS, ssthresh never below two
(lambda R: (lambda s: all((s.on_ack(R.randint(1, 3000)) if (e := R.random()) < 0.8 else s.on_dupack() if e < 0.97 else s.on_timeout()) in (None, True, False) and s.cwnd >= 1000 and s.ssthresh >= 2000 and s.dupacks >= 0 for _ in range(2000)))(RenoSender(1000, 30000)))(__import__("random").Random(5))
--- check case | A fixed run of 60 events ends in the right state
(lambda s: ([s.on_ack(1000) if k % 10 not in (4, 5, 6, 7) else s.on_dupack() for k in range(60)], s.cwnd, s.ssthresh, s.dupacks, s.in_recovery)[1:])(RenoSender(1000, 8000))
=> (2500, 2000, 0, False)

+++ practice | Rounds of the sawtooth
--- task
Textbooks often model TCP in whole round trips, with the window counted in segments. Write `cwnd_rounds(ssthresh, rounds, losses)` that returns the list of the window used in each of the first `rounds` round trips.

- The window starts at 1, with the given `ssthresh`.
- In each round the sender uses the current window. Then the window for the next round is set:
  - if `losses` (a dict from round number, counting from 0, to an event) says `"timeout"` for this round: `ssthresh = max(window // 2, 2)` and the window becomes 1;
  - if it says `"3dup"`: `ssthresh = max(window // 2, 2)` and the window becomes `ssthresh`;
  - otherwise, below `ssthresh` the window doubles but never past `ssthresh` (it becomes `min(2 * window, ssthresh)`), and at or above `ssthresh` it grows by 1.

For example, `cwnd_rounds(8, 7, {})` is `[1, 2, 4, 8, 9, 10, 11]`.
--- starter
def cwnd_rounds(ssthresh, rounds, losses):
    return [1] * rounds
--- solution
def cwnd_rounds(ssthresh, rounds, losses):
    window = 1
    used = []
    for r in range(rounds):
        used.append(window)
        event = losses.get(r)
        if event == "timeout":
            ssthresh = max(window // 2, 2)
            window = 1
        elif event == "3dup":
            ssthresh = max(window // 2, 2)
            window = ssthresh
        elif window < ssthresh:
            window = min(2 * window, ssthresh)
        else:
            window += 1
    return used
--- hint
Record the window for the round first, then work out the next one. Both loss events set the same new `ssthresh`; they differ only in where the window restarts.
--- hint
Growth is `min(2 * window, ssthresh)` below the threshold and `window + 1` at or above it.
--- check case | The example: no losses
cwnd_rounds(8, 7, {})
=> [1, 2, 4, 8, 9, 10, 11]
--- check case | Slow start stops exactly at a threshold that is not a power of two
cwnd_rounds(6, 6, {})
=> [1, 2, 4, 6, 7, 8]
--- check case | Triple duplicate ACKs: halve and continue linearly
cwnd_rounds(16, 12, {6: "3dup"})
=> [1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 17, 18, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13]
--- check case | A timeout: back to 1, slow start up to the new threshold
cwnd_rounds(16, 12, {6: "timeout"})
=> [1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 17, 18, 1, 2, 4, 8, 9]
--- check case | Losses at a tiny window: the threshold floor of 2
cwnd_rounds(64, 6, {0: "timeout", 1: "3dup", 3: "3dup"})
=> [1, 1, 2, 3, 2, 3]
--- check case | Zero rounds
cwnd_rounds(10, 0, {0: "timeout"})
=> []

+++ practice | How many round trips
--- task
A sender starts a transfer of `segments` segments with no losses. Using the round-trip model (the window starts at 1; below `ssthresh` it becomes `min(2 * window, ssthresh)` each round, and at or above it grows by 1), but also obeying flow control: in each round the sender sends `min(window, rwnd, segments still to send)` segments.

Write `rounds_needed(segments, ssthresh, rwnd)` that returns the number of round trips needed to send all the segments. Sending 0 segments takes 0 rounds. Raise `ValueError` if `rwnd` is less than 1.

For example, `rounds_needed(20, 8, 100)` is 5: the rounds send 1, 2, 4, 8 and then the last 5 segments.
--- starter
def rounds_needed(segments, ssthresh, rwnd):
    return segments
--- solution
def rounds_needed(segments, ssthresh, rwnd):
    if rwnd < 1:
        raise ValueError("the receiver window must be at least 1")
    window = 1
    left = segments
    rounds = 0
    while left > 0:
        left -= min(window, rwnd, left)
        rounds += 1
        if window < ssthresh:
            window = min(2 * window, ssthresh)
        else:
            window += 1
    return rounds
--- hint
Simulate round by round: subtract what this round sends from what is left, count the round, then grow the window by the usual rule.
--- hint
The window keeps growing even while `rwnd` caps what is sent; only the amount sent is capped.
--- check case | The example from the task
rounds_needed(20, 8, 100)
=> 5
--- check case | Exactly filling the slow-start rounds
rounds_needed(15, 64, 100)
=> 4
--- check case | Flow control caps the rate
rounds_needed(20, 64, 3)
=> 8
--- check case | Congestion avoidance after a low threshold
rounds_needed(30, 2, 1000)
=> 8
--- check case | Nothing to send, one segment, and a bad window
(rounds_needed(0, 8, 8), rounds_needed(1, 8, 8), raises(ValueError, lambda: rounds_needed(5, 8, 0)))
=> (0, 1, True)
--- check test | A big transfer finishes in far fewer rounds than segments
rounds_needed(10 ** 6, 10 ** 9, 10 ** 9) == 20 and rounds_needed(10 ** 6, 1024, 10 ** 9) < 2000

+++ practice | Many flows through one bottleneck
--- task
Simulate AIMD flows sharing one bottleneck link, in ticks. Write `share(windows, rtts, capacity, ticks)`.

- `windows` is the list of the flows' starting windows, in segments, and `rtts` the list of their round-trip times, in ticks. Do not change the list you are given.
- Flow `i` adds 1 to its window at the end of every tick `t` (counting from 0) where `(t + 1) % rtts[i] == 0`: one segment per round trip.
- After the increases of a tick, if the total of all windows is more than `capacity`, the queue overflows and **every** flow halves its window, rounding down, but never below 1. Count each such tick as one loss event.

Return a tuple `(final, losses)`: the list of final windows and the number of loss events.

For example, `share([1, 1], [1, 1], 10, 6)` is `([4, 4], 1)`. Both windows climb to 2, 3, 4, 5 and then 6, when the total of 12 is over 10, so both halve to 3; in the sixth tick they grow to 4.
--- starter
def share(windows, rtts, capacity, ticks):
    return (windows, 0)
--- solution
def share(windows, rtts, capacity, ticks):
    current = list(windows)
    losses = 0
    for t in range(ticks):
        for i, rtt in enumerate(rtts):
            if (t + 1) % rtt == 0:
                current[i] += 1
        if sum(current) > capacity:
            current = [max(1, w // 2) for w in current]
            losses += 1
    return (current, losses)
--- hint
Copy the starting list first. Each tick has two steps: every flow whose round trip ends at this tick adds 1, then one check of the total against the capacity.
--- hint
Halving is `max(1, w // 2)` for every flow at once, in the same tick.
--- check case | The example from the task
share([1, 1], [1, 1], 10, 6)
=> ([4, 4], 1)
--- check case | Very unequal flows end up nearly equal
share([90, 2], [1, 1], 100, 300)
=> ([37, 37], 12)
--- check case | The shorter round trip gets the bigger share
share([1, 1], [1, 4], 40, 200)
=> ([31, 8], 10)
--- check case | A link too small for three flows to grow: a loss every tick
share([1, 1, 1], [1, 1, 1], 5, 10)
=> ([1, 1, 1], 10)
--- check case | No flows, and zero ticks
(share([], [], 10, 5), share([7, 3], [1, 1], 5, 0))
=> (([], 0), ([7, 3], 0))
--- check test | The given list is not changed
(lambda w: share(w, [1, 2], 20, 50) is not None and w == [5, 6])([5, 6])
--- check test | The link is never left overfull at the end of a tick
(lambda R: all((lambda rtts, cap: all(sum(share([1] * len(rtts), rtts, cap, t)[0]) <= cap for t in range(60)))([R.randint(1, 5) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 4))], R.randint(4, 40)) for _ in range(40)))(__import__("random").Random(3))

+++ practice | Spotting fast retransmits
--- task
Write `fast_retransmits(acks)`. `acks` is the list of acknowledgement numbers a sender receives, in order, as 32-bit sequence numbers. Return the list of sequence numbers the sender fast-retransmits.

- The first ACK just sets the highest ACK seen.
- An ACK that comes **after** the highest seen (in serial arithmetic: `(ack - highest) mod 2³²` is from 1 to 2³¹ − 1) is new: it becomes the highest, and the duplicate count goes back to 0.
- An ACK equal to the highest is a duplicate: add 1 to the count. When the count reaches exactly 3, retransmit the segment starting at that number (append it to the result). Further duplicates of the same number do not retransmit again.
- An ACK that comes **before** the highest is an old, reordered ACK: ignore it completely. It does not change the count.

For example, `fast_retransmits([100, 200, 200, 200, 200, 300])` is `[200]`.
--- starter
def fast_retransmits(acks):
    out = []
    for i in range(3, len(acks)):
        if acks[i] == acks[i - 1] == acks[i - 2] == acks[i - 3]:
            out.append(acks[i])
    return out
--- solution
MOD = 2 ** 32


def fast_retransmits(acks):
    out = []
    highest = None
    dups = 0
    for ack in acks:
        if highest is None:
            highest = ack
            continue
        d = (ack - highest) % MOD
        if d == 0:
            dups += 1
            if dups == 3:
                out.append(ack)
        elif d < 2 ** 31:
            highest = ack
            dups = 0
    return out
--- hint
Keep two things: the highest ACK so far and the duplicate count. For each ACK, the distance `(ack - highest) % 2**32` tells you which of the three cases you are in.
--- hint
Append only when the count becomes exactly 3, so that a long run of duplicates retransmits once. An old ACK falls through every case and changes nothing.
--- check case | The example from the task
fast_retransmits([100, 200, 200, 200, 200, 300])
=> [200]
--- check case | Only two duplicates: nothing
fast_retransmits([100, 200, 200, 200, 300])
=> []
--- check case | A long run retransmits once; a later run again
fast_retransmits([5, 9, 9, 9, 9, 9, 9, 9, 20, 20, 20, 20])
=> [9, 20]
--- check case | An old, reordered ACK does not reset the count
fast_retransmits([500, 700, 700, 600, 700, 700])
=> [700]
--- check case | Across the wrap
fast_retransmits([4294967000, 4294967290, 10, 10, 10, 10, 4294967290])
=> [10]
--- check case | Empty and single lists
(fast_retransmits([]), fast_retransmits([42]), fast_retransmits([42, 42, 42]))
=> ([], [], [])

+++ practice | Debug: the threshold that is always tiny
--- task
`TahoeSender` is the older TCP Tahoe: in slow start it adds one MSS per new ACK, in congestion avoidance it adds `max(1, mss * mss // cwnd)`, and on **any** loss (`on_loss`) it sets `ssthresh = max(cwnd // 2, 2 * mss)` and restarts from `cwnd = mss`.

After a loss at `cwnd` 16000 with `mss` 1000, `ssthresh` should become 8000, so slow start runs back up to 8000. Instead it is always 2000, whatever the window was, and the sender crawls in congestion avoidance from 2000. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
class TahoeSender:
    def __init__(self, mss, ssthresh):
        self.mss = mss
        self.cwnd = mss
        self.ssthresh = ssthresh

    def on_ack(self):
        if self.cwnd < self.ssthresh:
            self.cwnd += self.mss
        else:
            self.cwnd += max(1, self.mss * self.mss // self.cwnd)

    def on_loss(self):
        self.cwnd = self.mss
        self.ssthresh = max(self.cwnd // 2, 2 * self.mss)
--- solution
class TahoeSender:
    def __init__(self, mss, ssthresh):
        self.mss = mss
        self.cwnd = mss
        self.ssthresh = ssthresh

    def on_ack(self):
        if self.cwnd < self.ssthresh:
            self.cwnd += self.mss
        else:
            self.cwnd += max(1, self.mss * self.mss // self.cwnd)

    def on_loss(self):
        self.ssthresh = max(self.cwnd // 2, 2 * self.mss)
        self.cwnd = self.mss
--- hint
When `on_loss` computes the new threshold, what is `self.cwnd` at that moment?
--- hint
Compute the threshold from the window before the loss: swap the two lines.
--- check case | The example: a loss at 16000
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 16000), s.on_loss(), s.ssthresh, s.cwnd)[2:])(TahoeSender(1000, 64000))
=> (8000, 1000)
--- check case | Slow start runs back up to the new threshold
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 16000), s.on_loss(), [s.on_ack() for _ in range(8)], s.cwnd)[3])(TahoeSender(1000, 64000))
=> 8125
--- check case | The floor still applies to a small window
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 3000), s.on_loss(), s.ssthresh)[2])(TahoeSender(1000, 64000))
=> 2000
--- check case | Two losses in a row
(lambda s: (s.__setattr__("cwnd", 40000), s.on_loss(), s.ssthresh, [s.on_ack() for _ in range(5)], s.on_loss(), s.ssthresh)[2::3])(TahoeSender(1000, 64000))
=> (20000, 3000)

+++ practice | Will one flow fill the link?
--- task
A mission team wants to know if one TCP Reno flow can fill a ground-station link. Model the ideal sawtooth. The window, in segments, goes W/2, W/2 + 1, …, W, one round trip each. The round at window W ends with exactly one loss, and the next cycle starts again at W/2.

Write `sawtooth(w, mss, rtt_ms)` for an even `w` of at least 2. Return a tuple `(per_cycle, loss_rate, rate)`:

- `per_cycle`: the segments sent in one cycle, W/2 + (W/2 + 1) + … + W;
- `loss_rate`: 1 / `per_cycle`, as a `fractions.Fraction`;
- `rate`: the average throughput in bytes per second, as a `Fraction`: the bytes sent in a cycle (`per_cycle * mss`) divided by the cycle's length in seconds (the number of rounds times `rtt_ms / 1000`).

Raise `ValueError` if `w` is odd or less than 2. For example, `sawtooth(4, 1000, 100)` is `(9, Fraction(1, 9), Fraction(30000, 1))`: the rounds send 2, 3 and 4 segments in 0.3 seconds.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def sawtooth(w, mss, rtt_ms):
    return (w, Fraction(1, w), Fraction(w * mss))
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def sawtooth(w, mss, rtt_ms):
    if w < 2 or w % 2:
        raise ValueError("the window at loss must be even and at least 2")
    windows = range(w // 2, w + 1)
    per_cycle = sum(windows)
    seconds = Fraction(len(windows) * rtt_ms, 1000)
    return (per_cycle, Fraction(1, per_cycle), per_cycle * mss / seconds)
--- hint
The rounds of one cycle are `range(w // 2, w + 1)`. Their count is the number of round trips, and their sum is the number of segments.
--- hint
Keep everything exact: build the cycle length as `Fraction(rounds * rtt_ms, 1000)` and divide the bytes by it.
--- check case | The example from the task
sawtooth(4, 1000, 100)
=> (9, Fraction(1, 9), Fraction(30000, 1))
--- check case | The smallest window
sawtooth(2, 1460, 50)
=> (3, Fraction(1, 3), Fraction(43800, 1))
--- check case | A bigger window, with an RTT that does not divide evenly
sawtooth(10, 1000, 30)
=> (45, Fraction(1, 45), Fraction(250000, 1))
--- check test | Odd and tiny windows are rejected
raises(ValueError, lambda: sawtooth(7, 1000, 10)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: sawtooth(0, 1000, 10)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: sawtooth(-2, 1000, 10))
--- check test | For big windows it agrees with the Mathis formula within 3%
(lambda M: all(abs(float(sawtooth(w, 1460, 80)[2]) / (M.sqrt(1.5) * 1460 / (0.080 * M.sqrt(float(sawtooth(w, 1460, 80)[1])))) - 1) < 0.03 for w in (100, 500, 2000)))(__import__("math"))

+++ practice | Reno through a drop-tail router
--- task
Put a Reno sender on the event simulator of lessons 7 and 8, and watch it share a real bottleneck. Packets are numbered 0, 1, 2, … (no wrap-around), and the window is counted in packets. The starter holds the pieces; keep them:

- `Sim` and `Timer`, as in lesson 7 (`sim.now`, `sim.at(time, action)`, `timer.start(duration)`, `timer.stop()`);
- `Bottleneck(sim, service, buffer, delay)`: a router's output link that takes `service` time units to send one packet, holds at most `buffer` waiting packets (drop-tail: it drops a packet that arrives to a full buffer), and then takes `delay` to deliver it. `link.send(seq, deliver)` hands it a packet;
- `Receiver(sim, delay)`, which keeps out-of-order packets, delivers in order into its list `delivered`, and answers every packet with a cumulative ACK, the next packet number it expects, which reaches the sender's `on_ack(n)` after `delay`;
- `connect(sim, link, flow, receiver)`, which sets the flow's attributes `link` and `deliver` and tells the receiver about the flow.

Write the class `RenoFlow(sim, total, rto)` that sends packets 0 to `total - 1`. It starts with `cwnd = 1`, `ssthresh = 64`, an avoidance counter of 0, nothing acknowledged (`acked = 0`, the number of the first packet not yet acknowledged), `next = 0` (the next new packet to send), 0 duplicate ACKs, and not in recovery. It has the attributes `sent`, `fast_retransmits`, `timeouts` (counts starting at 0) and `done_at` (starting `None`), and one `Timer` for `rto` time units.

- **Sending** a packet `seq` means adding 1 to `sent` and calling `self.link.send(seq, self.deliver)`.
- **Filling the window**: while `next < total` and `next - acked < cwnd`: if `next == acked`, start the timer; send packet `next`; add 1 to `next`.
- `start()` fills the window.
- `on_ack(n)`: if the flow is done, ignore it.
  - If `n > acked` (a new ACK): if in recovery, set `cwnd = ssthresh` and leave recovery; otherwise, if `cwnd < ssthresh`, add 1 to `cwnd`; otherwise add 1 to the avoidance counter, and when it reaches `cwnd`, add 1 to `cwnd` and set the counter to 0. Then set `acked = n` and the duplicate count to 0. If `acked == total`, stop the timer, set `done_at = sim.now` and return. Otherwise restart the timer.
  - If `n == acked` (a duplicate): add 1 to the duplicate count. When it becomes 3 and the flow is not in recovery: set `ssthresh = max(cwnd // 2, 2)`, `cwnd = ssthresh`, the avoidance counter to 0, enter recovery, add 1 to `fast_retransmits`, send packet `acked` again, and restart the timer.
  - Finally, fill the window.
- **On timeout**: add 1 to `timeouts`, set `ssthresh = max(cwnd // 2, 2)`, `cwnd = 1`, the avoidance counter and the duplicate count to 0, leave recovery, set `next = acked` (go back and resend from the first unacknowledged packet), and fill the window.
--- starter
import heapq
from collections import deque


class Sim:
    def __init__(self):
        self.now = 0
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def run(self, until=10 ** 9):
        while self.queue and self.queue[0][0] <= until:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is not None:
                self.now = time
                action()


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Bottleneck:
    def __init__(self, sim, service, buffer, delay):
        self.sim = sim
        self.service = service
        self.buffer = buffer
        self.delay = delay
        self.waiting = deque()
        self.busy = False
        self.dropped = 0

    def send(self, packet, deliver):
        if not self.busy:
            self.busy = True
            self.sim.at(self.sim.now + self.service, lambda: self.done(packet, deliver))
        elif len(self.waiting) < self.buffer:
            self.waiting.append((packet, deliver))
        else:
            self.dropped += 1

    def done(self, packet, deliver):
        self.sim.at(self.sim.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(packet))
        if self.waiting:
            nxt, where = self.waiting.popleft()
            self.sim.at(self.sim.now + self.service, lambda: self.done(nxt, where))
        else:
            self.busy = False


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, delay):
        self.sim = sim
        self.delay = delay
        self.expected = 0
        self.held = set()
        self.delivered = []
        self.sender = None

    def on_packet(self, seq):
        if seq >= self.expected:
            self.held.add(seq)
        while self.expected in self.held:
            self.held.discard(self.expected)
            self.delivered.append(self.expected)
            self.expected += 1
        ack = self.expected
        self.sim.at(self.sim.now + self.delay, lambda: self.sender.on_ack(ack))


def connect(sim, link, flow, receiver):
    flow.link = link
    flow.deliver = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.sender = flow


class RenoFlow:
    def __init__(self, sim, total, rto):
        self.sim = sim
        self.total = total
        self.rto = rto
        self.link = None
        self.deliver = None
        self.sent = 0
        self.fast_retransmits = 0
        self.timeouts = 0
        self.done_at = None

    def start(self):
        pass

    def on_ack(self, n):
        pass
--- solution
import heapq
from collections import deque


class Sim:
    def __init__(self):
        self.now = 0
        self.queue = []
        self.count = 0

    def at(self, time, action):
        self.count += 1
        entry = [time, self.count, action]
        heapq.heappush(self.queue, entry)
        return entry

    def cancel(self, entry):
        entry[2] = None

    def run(self, until=10 ** 9):
        while self.queue and self.queue[0][0] <= until:
            time, _, action = heapq.heappop(self.queue)
            if action is not None:
                self.now = time
                action()


class Timer:
    def __init__(self, sim, callback):
        self.sim = sim
        self.callback = callback
        self.entry = None

    def start(self, duration):
        self.stop()
        self.entry = self.sim.at(self.sim.now + duration, self.fire)

    def stop(self):
        if self.entry is not None:
            self.sim.cancel(self.entry)
            self.entry = None

    def fire(self):
        self.entry = None
        self.callback()


class Bottleneck:
    def __init__(self, sim, service, buffer, delay):
        self.sim = sim
        self.service = service
        self.buffer = buffer
        self.delay = delay
        self.waiting = deque()
        self.busy = False
        self.dropped = 0

    def send(self, packet, deliver):
        if not self.busy:
            self.busy = True
            self.sim.at(self.sim.now + self.service, lambda: self.done(packet, deliver))
        elif len(self.waiting) < self.buffer:
            self.waiting.append((packet, deliver))
        else:
            self.dropped += 1

    def done(self, packet, deliver):
        self.sim.at(self.sim.now + self.delay, lambda: deliver(packet))
        if self.waiting:
            nxt, where = self.waiting.popleft()
            self.sim.at(self.sim.now + self.service, lambda: self.done(nxt, where))
        else:
            self.busy = False


class Receiver:
    def __init__(self, sim, delay):
        self.sim = sim
        self.delay = delay
        self.expected = 0
        self.held = set()
        self.delivered = []
        self.sender = None

    def on_packet(self, seq):
        if seq >= self.expected:
            self.held.add(seq)
        while self.expected in self.held:
            self.held.discard(self.expected)
            self.delivered.append(self.expected)
            self.expected += 1
        ack = self.expected
        self.sim.at(self.sim.now + self.delay, lambda: self.sender.on_ack(ack))


def connect(sim, link, flow, receiver):
    flow.link = link
    flow.deliver = receiver.on_packet
    receiver.sender = flow


class RenoFlow:
    def __init__(self, sim, total, rto):
        self.sim = sim
        self.total = total
        self.rto = rto
        self.link = None
        self.deliver = None
        self.cwnd = 1
        self.ssthresh = 64
        self.count = 0
        self.acked = 0
        self.next = 0
        self.dupacks = 0
        self.in_recovery = False
        self.sent = 0
        self.fast_retransmits = 0
        self.timeouts = 0
        self.max_cwnd = 1
        self.done_at = None
        self.timer = Timer(sim, self.on_timeout)

    def transmit(self, seq):
        self.sent += 1
        self.link.send(seq, self.deliver)

    def start(self):
        self.fill()

    def fill(self):
        while self.next < self.total and self.next - self.acked < self.cwnd:
            if self.next == self.acked:
                self.timer.start(self.rto)
            self.transmit(self.next)
            self.next += 1

    def on_ack(self, n):
        if self.done_at is not None:
            return
        if n > self.acked:
            if self.in_recovery:
                self.cwnd = self.ssthresh
                self.in_recovery = False
            elif self.cwnd < self.ssthresh:
                self.cwnd += 1
            else:
                self.count += 1
                if self.count >= self.cwnd:
                    self.cwnd += 1
                    self.count = 0
            self.max_cwnd = max(self.max_cwnd, self.cwnd)
            self.acked = n
            self.dupacks = 0
            if self.acked == self.total:
                self.timer.stop()
                self.done_at = self.sim.now
                return
            self.timer.start(self.rto)
        elif n == self.acked:
            self.dupacks += 1
            if self.dupacks == 3 and not self.in_recovery:
                self.ssthresh = max(self.cwnd // 2, 2)
                self.cwnd = self.ssthresh
                self.count = 0
                self.in_recovery = True
                self.fast_retransmits += 1
                self.transmit(self.acked)
                self.timer.start(self.rto)
        self.fill()

    def on_timeout(self):
        self.timeouts += 1
        self.ssthresh = max(self.cwnd // 2, 2)
        self.cwnd = 1
        self.count = 0
        self.dupacks = 0
        self.in_recovery = False
        self.next = self.acked
        self.fill()
--- hint
Keep the window test in one method, `fill`, and call it from `start`, at the end of `on_ack`, and at the end of the timeout. The timer only starts in `fill` when the window was empty, and restarts on every new ACK.
--- hint
A timeout rewinds `next` to `acked`, so the following `fill` resends everything from the first unacknowledged packet, as in Go-Back-N; the receiver throws nothing away, so the ACK jumps once the gap is filled.
--- check case | With a huge buffer nothing is lost: slow start, then avoidance
(lambda total, service, buffer, rto: (lambda sim: (lambda link, f, rcv: (connect(sim, link, f, rcv), f.start(), sim.run(), (f.done_at, f.sent, f.fast_retransmits, f.timeouts, rcv.delivered == list(range(total)), link.dropped))[3])(Bottleneck(sim, service, buffer, 100), RenoFlow(sim, total, rto), Receiver(sim, 100)))(Sim()))(200, 10, 1000, 1000)
=> (2940, 200, 0, 0, True, 0)
--- check test | A small buffer: losses, fast retransmits, and everything still arrives in order
(lambda r: r[0] is not None and r[1] > 2000 and r[2] >= 1 and r[4] and r[5] > 0)((lambda total, service, buffer, rto: (lambda sim: (lambda link, f, rcv: (connect(sim, link, f, rcv), f.start(), sim.run(), (f.done_at, f.sent, f.fast_retransmits, f.timeouts, rcv.delivered == list(range(total)), link.dropped))[3])(Bottleneck(sim, service, buffer, 100), RenoFlow(sim, total, rto), Receiver(sim, 100)))(Sim()))(2000, 10, 8, 1000))
--- check test | A buffer near the bandwidth-delay product finishes much sooner than a tiny one
(lambda small, big: small[4] and big[4] and big[0] < 0.75 * small[0])((lambda total, service, buffer, rto: (lambda sim: (lambda link, f, rcv: (connect(sim, link, f, rcv), f.start(), sim.run(), (f.done_at, f.sent, f.fast_retransmits, f.timeouts, rcv.delivered == list(range(total)), link.dropped))[3])(Bottleneck(sim, service, buffer, 100), RenoFlow(sim, total, rto), Receiver(sim, 100)))(Sim()))(2000, 10, 8, 1000), (lambda total, service, buffer, rto: (lambda sim: (lambda link, f, rcv: (connect(sim, link, f, rcv), f.start(), sim.run(), (f.done_at, f.sent, f.fast_retransmits, f.timeouts, rcv.delivered == list(range(total)), link.dropped))[3])(Bottleneck(sim, service, buffer, 100), RenoFlow(sim, total, rto), Receiver(sim, 100)))(Sim()))(2000, 10, 30, 1000))
--- check test | With no buffer at all, timeouts rescue the transfer
(lambda r: r[0] is not None and r[3] >= 1 and r[4])((lambda total, service, buffer, rto: (lambda sim: (lambda link, f, rcv: (connect(sim, link, f, rcv), f.start(), sim.run(), (f.done_at, f.sent, f.fast_retransmits, f.timeouts, rcv.delivered == list(range(total)), link.dropped))[3])(Bottleneck(sim, service, buffer, 100), RenoFlow(sim, total, rto), Receiver(sim, 100)))(Sim()))(300, 10, 0, 1000))
--- check test | Two flows share the link: both finish in order, close together, with the link kept busy
(lambda r: r[2] and r[3] and abs(r[0] - r[1]) < 0.15 * max(r[0], r[1]) and max(r[0], r[1]) < 1.25 * 60000 and r[4] >= 2)((lambda total, buffer, gap: (lambda sim: (lambda link, fa, fb, ra, rb: (connect(sim, link, fa, ra), connect(sim, link, fb, rb), sim.at(0, fa.start), sim.at(gap, fb.start), sim.run(), (fa.done_at, fb.done_at, ra.delivered == list(range(total)), rb.delivered == list(range(total)), fa.fast_retransmits + fb.fast_retransmits))[5])(Bottleneck(sim, 10, buffer, 100), RenoFlow(sim, total, 1000), RenoFlow(sim, total, 1000), Receiver(sim, 100), Receiver(sim, 100)))(Sim()))(3000, 20, 5000))

+++ question | The step that makes AIMD fair
--- ask
Two flows share a link. Both add 1 segment per round trip, and both halve on a loss. Which step of the fairness argument is right?
--- choice
The additive increase shrinks the gap between the flows, because the smaller flow grows faster.
--- choice correct
The additive increase leaves the gap x − y unchanged, and each halving turns it into (x − y)/2, so after k losses the gap is 2⁻ᵏ of what it was.
--- choice
The halving makes the flows equal in one step.
--- choice
Neither step changes the gap; the flows become equal because the link is shared.
--- why
Adding the same a to both leaves x − y alone; multiplying both by b multiplies the gap by b. Only the decreases shrink the gap, and they keep happening because the total cannot grow past the capacity forever. With additive decrease the gap would never shrink at all.

+++ question | Why wait for three
--- ask
Why does TCP wait for three duplicate ACKs before a fast retransmit, instead of retransmitting at the first one?
--- choice
Because the receiver only sends a duplicate ACK for every third segment.
--- choice correct
Because packets are sometimes reordered, which causes one or two duplicate ACKs even when nothing was lost; three in a row is strong evidence of a real loss.
--- choice
Because the first two duplicate ACKs might be forged by an attacker.
--- choice
Because cwnd must grow by three MSS first.
--- why
Every segment that arrives after a gap causes a duplicate ACK, whether the gap is a loss or a segment that is just late. A retransmission at the first duplicate would waste bandwidth and halve the window for nothing whenever two packets swapped places.

+++ question | Slow start in numbers
--- ask
A connection starts slow start with cwnd = 1 segment and a very high ssthresh, and loses nothing. Each round trip, every segment is ACKed. After how many round trips is cwnd 64 segments? Type the number.
--- answer
6
6 round trips
--- why
Slow start doubles cwnd every round trip: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64. That is six doublings, since 2⁶ = 64. Reaching a window of W takes about log₂ W round trips.

=== net-11 | DNS: names, delegation, resolution and caching
--- teach
The last two lessons were about moving bytes reliably. Now we move up to the application layer, starting with the service almost every connection needs before it can begin. People type names like `docs.python.org`, but IP packets need addresses like `151.101.0.223`. The **Domain Name System**, DNS, turns names into addresses. It answers billions of questions a day, and no single computer knows all the answers. This lesson shows how the work is split up, how a question finds the server that knows, and why caching makes it fast.

### Asking the way

Picture asking for a room in a huge university you have never visited. The front desk does not know every room, but it knows which building holds the physics department. The physics building's desk knows which floor has the labs. The lab floor's desk knows the room. Nobody knows everything; each one knows who to ask next. And if you are asked again tomorrow, you remember the answer and skip the walk.

### Names are paths in a tree

A domain name is a list of **labels** separated by dots, read from right to left. `docs.python.org` has three labels: `org`, then `python`, then `docs`. Together they form a path in a **tree**. At the top is the **root**, with no label. Below it are the **top-level domains** (TLDs) such as `com`, `org`, `int` and `uk`, then names under those, and so on.

Some rules:

- A label is 1 to 63 characters; a whole name is at most 255 bytes in the encoding used on the wire.
- Names are **case-insensitive**: `Docs.Python.ORG` is the same name.
- A name written with a final dot, like `docs.python.org.`, is **fully qualified**: the empty label after the dot is the root.

Every name has a chain of **ancestors**, one per suffix, from the root down:

```python
name = "ops.lunar.example"
labels = name.split(".")
for i in range(len(labels), -1, -1):
    print(repr(".".join(labels[i:])))
```

That prints `''` (the root), `'example'`, `'lunar.example'` and `'ops.lunar.example'`.

### Zones and delegation

The tree is split into **zones**. A zone is a piece of the tree run by one organisation, with its own servers. The root zone is run for the whole Internet; the `org` zone by the registry for `org`; the `python.org` zone by the Python Software Foundation.

A zone hands a part of its tree to someone else by **delegation**. The `org` zone does not hold the addresses of names inside `python.org`. It holds only a note: "for anything in `python.org`, ask these name servers". That note is a set of **NS records**.

When the name server itself has a name inside the zone it serves, say `ns1.python.org` for `python.org`, there is a chicken-and-egg problem: to find its address you would have to ask it. So the parent zone also keeps the server's address. Such an address is called a **[[glue record|glue]]**.

### Resource records

Everything DNS stores is a **resource record**: a name, a type, a **TTL** (time to live, in seconds), and the data. The common types:

| type | data | meaning |
|---|---|---|
| A | an IPv4 address | the name's address |
| AAAA | an IPv6 address | the name's IPv6 address |
| NS | a server name | this zone is served by that name server |
| CNAME | another name | this name is an alias; look up that one instead |
| MX | a priority and a host | where to deliver email for this domain |
| TXT | text | notes, and proofs of ownership |
| SOA | zone information | the zone's serial number, timers, and the TTL for "no such name" |

All the records with the same name and type form a **record set**. A name can have several A records, and a client may use any of them.

### Three kinds of DNS server

- An **authoritative server** holds the records of one or more zones and answers for them. It knows its zones and nothing else.
- A **recursive resolver** (your ISP's, or a public one like `1.1.1.1`) does the work of finding answers for clients, and caches what it learns.
- A **stub resolver** is the small piece of code in your operating system. It sends every question to a recursive resolver and waits for the final answer.

### Resolution, step by step

A stub asks its resolver "what is the A record of `docs.python.org`?". This is a **recursive query**: "find the answer for me". The resolver, starting with an empty cache, then makes **iterative queries**: each server gives the best answer it has, which is often only "ask them instead".

1. It asks a **root server** (whose addresses every resolver is shipped with). The root does not know the name, but it holds the delegation for `org`, so it replies with a **referral**: the NS records for `org`, with glue addresses.
2. It asks an `org` server, which refers it to the `python.org` servers.
3. It asks a `python.org` server, which is **authoritative** and answers with the A record.
4. It caches everything it learned and returns the answer to the stub.

A referral is a reply with an empty answer and NS records in its **authority** section, plus glue in its **additional** section. A sketch of the walk, with a table standing in for the servers:

```python
referrals = {"root": "int", "int": "esa.int", "esa.int": "answer"}
server = "root"
steps = 0
while server != "answer":
    steps += 1
    print("ask the", server, "servers")
    server = referrals[server]
print(steps, "queries")
```

If an answer is a **CNAME**, the name was an alias: the resolver starts again with the target name, and returns the target's records.

### Why resolution ends

Each referral sends the resolver to a zone that is a **strictly longer suffix** of the name it is looking up: from the root to `org` to `python.org`. A zone with more labels than the name cannot hold it. So a name with k labels can cause at most k referrals, one per ancestor, before an authoritative server answers or says the name does not exist.

That argument assumes servers only refer downward, and that CNAMEs do not loop. A server can be misconfigured, and two CNAMEs can point at each other, so real resolvers also cap the number of steps and CNAMEs they will follow.

### Caching and TTLs

Without caching, every lookup would start at the root, and the root servers would carry the whole Internet's questions. Instead a resolver keeps every record it learns, including the NS and glue records of referrals, until its **TTL** runs out. A record with a TTL of 300 received at time 1,000 may be used until time 1,300.

```python
cache = {}
now = 1000
cache[("mail.example", "A")] = (now + 300, ["10.2.2.2"])
for t in [1000, 1299, 1300]:
    expiry, values = cache[("mail.example", "A")]
    print(t, values if t < expiry else "expired")
```

The cache also makes later lookups start lower in the tree. Once the resolver has the NS records for `python.org`, a question about `www.python.org` goes straight to the `python.org` servers, skipping the root and `org`. That is why each step of resolution first looks for the **closest** zone it already knows servers for.

**Why caching is safe.** An authoritative server promises that a record it sends stays valid for its TTL. So a cached answer is at most TTL seconds out of date, and the zone's owner chooses that bound. A short TTL (60 seconds) lets an owner move a service quickly, at the price of more queries; a long one (a day) is cheap and slow to change. Resolvers also cache "no such name" answers for a while, called **negative caching**, using a TTL taken from the zone's SOA record.

### The message on the wire

DNS usually runs over UDP port 53 (lesson 6): one datagram for the question, one for the reply. A message is:

- a 12-byte **header**: a 16-bit ID that the reply must copy, flags (is this a reply, is it authoritative, was it cut short, is recursion wanted), and four counts;
- the **question**: a name, a type and a class;
- three lists of records: **answer**, **authority** and **additional**.

A name is written as a series of labels, each preceded by one byte giving its length, and ended by a zero byte for the root. One label:

```python
label = "lunar"
print(bytes([len(label)]) + label.encode("ascii"))    # b'\x05lunar'
```

To save space, a name can end with a **pointer** to a name written earlier in the message: two bytes whose top two bits are 1, holding the offset of the earlier name. This is **name compression**.

A reply too big for the datagram has its **TC** (truncated) flag set, and the resolver asks again over TCP.

### What it costs

- **A cold lookup** costs one round trip per level of the tree, typically three or four queries, often 50 to 200 ms in all.
- **A cached lookup** costs no queries at all, and most lookups are cached: popular names and the NS records of TLDs are almost always in the cache.
- **The root servers** see only a tiny share of all questions, because the NS records of TLDs have TTLs of two days. There are [[13 root server names|roots]], but each is hundreds of machines.
- **Memory:** a resolver's cache holds millions of record sets, each for at most its TTL, so memory is bounded by the rate of new names times the TTLs.

### Where it is used

- **Every connection** a browser makes starts with a DNS lookup, unless the answer is cached.
- **Email** finds its servers with MX records.
- **Content delivery networks** answer the same name with different addresses in different places, sending each user to a nearby server.
- **Service discovery** inside data centres and ground systems uses DNS names for machines that come and go.
- **Security** depends on DNS being right: an attacker who makes a resolver cache a false answer, called **[[cache poisoning|kaminsky]]**, sends everyone who asks to the attacker's server. [[DNSSEC|dnssec]] signs records to stop that, and [[DNS over HTTPS|doh]] hides the questions from eavesdroppers.

**Watch out:** the classic DNS bugs.

- Matching zones with plain string suffixes. `"notorbit.com".endswith("orbit.com")` is true, but `notorbit.com` is not inside `orbit.com`. Compare whole labels: a name is in a zone when it equals the zone or ends with a dot followed by the zone.
- Ignoring TTLs. A program that caches an address forever keeps connecting to an old server after the service moves.
- Forgetting CNAMEs. A resolver that only looks for A records misses every alias.
- Comparing names case-sensitively, or forgetting the trailing dot.

::: context glue Why glue is needed
If `python.org` is served by `ns1.python.org`, the `org` servers could just say "ask `ns1.python.org`". But finding the address of `ns1.python.org` means asking the `python.org` servers, which is the very thing you cannot yet do. Glue breaks the loop: the parent zone keeps the A record of a name server that lives inside the zone it serves, and sends it along with the referral. A name server outside the zone, say `ns.hosting.net` for `python.org`, needs no glue: it is found by an ordinary lookup of its own.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="140" y="8" width="80" height="26" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="180" y="25" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">org servers</text>
  <line x1="180" y1="34" x2="180" y2="60" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="40" y="60" width="280" height="44" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="180" y="78" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">referral: python.org NS ns1.python.org</text>
  <text x="180" y="95" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">glue: ns1.python.org A 192.0.2.53</text>
  <line x1="180" y1="104" x2="180" y2="120" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="120" y="120" width="120" height="26" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="180" y="137" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">ask 192.0.2.53</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context roots Thirteen names, hundreds of machines
The root servers are named `a.root-servers.net` to `m.root-servers.net`, run by 12 organisations. Thirteen names was the most that fitted in one 512-byte UDP reply in the 1980s. Today each name is served by anycast: the same address is announced from hundreds of places, and routing (lesson 5) delivers each query to the nearest copy. Together there are more than 1,500 root server machines on every continent.
:::

::: context kaminsky Poisoning a cache
A resolver accepts a reply if it arrives on the right port, carries the right 16-bit ID, and matches the question. An attacker who floods the resolver with forged replies needs to guess those. In 2008 Dan Kaminsky showed how to make the resolver ask again and again, about made-up names like `x123.bank.example`, with forged replies that also carried a false NS record for the whole `bank.example` zone. With only the ID to guess, a cache fell in seconds. The fix was to randomise the source port too, making the guess about 32 bits instead of 16.
:::

::: context dnssec Signing the answers
DNSSEC adds public-key signatures to DNS. Each zone signs its record sets, and the parent zone vouches for the child's key with a DS record, forming a chain of trust up to the root's key, which resolvers are shipped with. A forged answer then fails its signature check. It prevents poisoning but not eavesdropping: DNSSEC answers are signed, not encrypted. The idea of a chain of signatures up to a trusted root comes back in lesson 14.
:::

::: context doh Hiding the questions
Plain DNS travels unencrypted, so anyone on the path can see which names you look up, and some networks change the answers. DNS over TLS (port 853) and DNS over HTTPS put the same messages inside an encrypted connection to the resolver, using the TLS of lesson 14. The resolver itself still sees every question, so choosing one you trust matters.
:::
--- task
Write a caching, iterative resolver for a simulated set of DNS servers.

The starter already has three helpers; keep them.

- `in_zone(name, zone)` is `True` when `name` is `zone` or lies inside it (every name is in the root zone `""`).
- `ask(servers, ip, name, qtype)` asks the authoritative server at address `ip`. It returns a dict with lists `"answer"`, `"authority"` and `"additional"` of records `(name, type, value, ttl)`. The answer holds the records of type `qtype` for `name`, or else the name's CNAME. With no answer, a referral has NS records in `"authority"` and any glue A records in `"additional"`. With neither, all three lists are empty: the name or type does not exist.
- `sample_world()` builds the servers used in the checks. Its root server is at `"198.41.0.4"`.

Write a class `Resolver(servers, root_ip)` with an attribute `queries`, the number of times `ask` has been called, starting at 0, and a method `resolve(name, qtype, now)` that returns the **sorted** list of values of the answer, or `[]` if there is none. Use a cache from `(name, type)` to an expiry time and a sorted list of values. It works like this:

1. If `(name, qtype)` is cached and `now` is before its expiry, return its values.
2. Else, if `(name, "CNAME")` is cached and not expired, return the result of resolving the alias's target (the first value) with the same `qtype`. Following more than 8 CNAMEs in one `resolve` call raises `ValueError`.
3. Else pick a server. Go through the suffixes of `name`, from `name` itself up to the root `""`, and find the first zone with an unexpired cached `"NS"` record set. Take its server names in sorted order, and use the first address of the first one that has an unexpired cached `"A"` record set. If none of them has one, resolve the `"A"` record of the first server name (a `resolve` call of its own) and use its first address. If no zone has cached NS records, use `root_ip`.
4. Call `ask`, and cache every record it returned (in all three lists): group them by `(name, type)`, and store each group with expiry `now` + the smallest TTL in the group, replacing any older entry.
5. If the reply's answer and authority lists are both empty, return `[]`. Otherwise go back to step 1.

Every TTL is at least 1, and servers never refer you back up the tree. For example, on `sample_world()`, resolving `"www.orbit.com"` type `"A"` at time 0 returns `["10.0.1.1", "10.0.1.2"]` after 3 queries: the root, the `com` server and the `orbit.com` server.
--- starter
def in_zone(name, zone):
    return zone == "" or name == zone or name.endswith("." + zone)


def ask(servers, ip, name, qtype):
    records = servers[ip]
    answer = [r for r in records if r[0] == name and r[1] == qtype]
    if not answer:
        answer = [r for r in records if r[0] == name and r[1] == "CNAME"]
    if answer:
        return {"answer": answer, "authority": [], "additional": []}
    zones = [r[0] for r in records if r[1] == "NS" and in_zone(name, r[0])]
    if not zones:
        return {"answer": [], "authority": [], "additional": []}
    best = max(zones, key=len)
    ns = [r for r in records if r[1] == "NS" and r[0] == best]
    glue = [r for r in records if r[1] == "A" and r[0] in {n[2] for n in ns}]
    return {"answer": [], "authority": ns, "additional": glue}


def sample_world():
    return {
        "198.41.0.4": [
            ("com", "NS", "ns.com", 172800),
            ("ns.com", "A", "192.5.6.30", 172800),
            ("org", "NS", "ns.org", 172800),
            ("ns.org", "A", "199.19.54.1", 172800),
        ],
        "192.5.6.30": [
            ("orbit.com", "NS", "ns1.orbit.com", 3600),
            ("ns1.orbit.com", "A", "10.0.0.53", 3600),
            ("shop.com", "NS", "ns.hosting.org", 3600),
        ],
        "199.19.54.1": [
            ("hosting.org", "NS", "ns.hosting.org", 3600),
            ("ns.hosting.org", "A", "10.9.9.9", 3600),
        ],
        "10.0.0.53": [
            ("www.orbit.com", "A", "10.0.1.2", 200),
            ("www.orbit.com", "A", "10.0.1.1", 300),
            ("api.orbit.com", "CNAME", "www.orbit.com", 600),
            ("telemetry.orbit.com", "CNAME", "store.shop.com", 60),
            ("orbit.com", "MX", "mail.orbit.com", 3600),
        ],
        "10.9.9.9": [
            ("ns.hosting.org", "A", "10.9.9.9", 3600),
            ("store.shop.com", "A", "10.7.7.7", 120),
        ],
    }


class Resolver:
    def __init__(self, servers, root_ip):
        self.servers = servers
        self.root_ip = root_ip
        self.queries = 0

    def resolve(self, name, qtype, now):
        raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def in_zone(name, zone):
    return zone == "" or name == zone or name.endswith("." + zone)


def ask(servers, ip, name, qtype):
    records = servers[ip]
    answer = [r for r in records if r[0] == name and r[1] == qtype]
    if not answer:
        answer = [r for r in records if r[0] == name and r[1] == "CNAME"]
    if answer:
        return {"answer": answer, "authority": [], "additional": []}
    zones = [r[0] for r in records if r[1] == "NS" and in_zone(name, r[0])]
    if not zones:
        return {"answer": [], "authority": [], "additional": []}
    best = max(zones, key=len)
    ns = [r for r in records if r[1] == "NS" and r[0] == best]
    glue = [r for r in records if r[1] == "A" and r[0] in {n[2] for n in ns}]
    return {"answer": [], "authority": ns, "additional": glue}


def sample_world():
    return {
        "198.41.0.4": [
            ("com", "NS", "ns.com", 172800),
            ("ns.com", "A", "192.5.6.30", 172800),
            ("org", "NS", "ns.org", 172800),
            ("ns.org", "A", "199.19.54.1", 172800),
        ],
        "192.5.6.30": [
            ("orbit.com", "NS", "ns1.orbit.com", 3600),
            ("ns1.orbit.com", "A", "10.0.0.53", 3600),
            ("shop.com", "NS", "ns.hosting.org", 3600),
        ],
        "199.19.54.1": [
            ("hosting.org", "NS", "ns.hosting.org", 3600),
            ("ns.hosting.org", "A", "10.9.9.9", 3600),
        ],
        "10.0.0.53": [
            ("www.orbit.com", "A", "10.0.1.2", 200),
            ("www.orbit.com", "A", "10.0.1.1", 300),
            ("api.orbit.com", "CNAME", "www.orbit.com", 600),
            ("telemetry.orbit.com", "CNAME", "store.shop.com", 60),
            ("orbit.com", "MX", "mail.orbit.com", 3600),
        ],
        "10.9.9.9": [
            ("ns.hosting.org", "A", "10.9.9.9", 3600),
            ("store.shop.com", "A", "10.7.7.7", 120),
        ],
    }


class Resolver:
    def __init__(self, servers, root_ip):
        self.servers = servers
        self.root_ip = root_ip
        self.queries = 0
        self.cache = {}

    def cached(self, name, rtype, now):
        entry = self.cache.get((name, rtype))
        if entry is not None and now < entry[0]:
            return entry[1]
        return None

    def pick_server(self, name, now):
        labels = name.split(".") if name else []
        for i in range(len(labels) + 1):
            zone = ".".join(labels[i:])
            servers = self.cached(zone, "NS", now)
            if servers is None:
                continue
            for host in servers:
                addresses = self.cached(host, "A", now)
                if addresses:
                    return addresses[0]
            return self.resolve(servers[0], "A", now)[0]
        return self.root_ip

    def resolve(self, name, qtype, now, hops=0):
        while True:
            values = self.cached(name, qtype, now)
            if values is not None:
                return values
            alias = self.cached(name, "CNAME", now)
            if alias is not None:
                if hops == 8:
                    raise ValueError("too many CNAMEs")
                return self.resolve(alias[0], qtype, now, hops + 1)
            ip = self.pick_server(name, now)
            reply = ask(self.servers, ip, name, qtype)
            self.queries += 1
            groups = {}
            for r in reply["answer"] + reply["authority"] + reply["additional"]:
                groups.setdefault((r[0], r[1]), []).append(r)
            for key, records in groups.items():
                ttl = min(r[3] for r in records)
                self.cache[key] = (now + ttl, sorted(r[2] for r in records))
            if not reply["answer"] and not reply["authority"]:
                return []
--- hint
Write two small helpers first: one that returns a cached value list only if it has not expired (`now < expiry`), and one that picks the server to ask (step 3). The suffixes of `name` are `".".join(labels[i:])` for `i` from 0 up to the number of labels, and the last one is the root `""`.
--- hint
`resolve` is a `while True` loop over steps 1 to 5. Step 4 groups the reply's records in a dict keyed by `(name, type)`, then stores `(now + smallest ttl, sorted values)` for each group. Because referrals are cached, the next pass of the loop finds a closer zone by itself.
--- hint
For CNAMEs, pass a counter as an extra parameter with a default of 0, and add 1 on each alias you follow. When a zone's server names have no cached address, call `resolve(first_server_name, "A", now)` from the server-picking helper.
