+++ practice | Names on the wire
--- task
Write two functions for DNS names in wire format, without compression.

- `encode_name(name)` turns a name such as `"ops.lunar.example"` into bytes: each label as one length byte followed by its ASCII characters, then a zero byte for the root. A single trailing dot is allowed and ignored, and `""` or `"."` (the root itself) encodes as one zero byte. Raise `ValueError` for an empty label (as in `"a..b"`), a label longer than 63 characters, or an encoded result longer than 255 bytes.
- `decode_name(data)` reads a name from the start of `data` and returns a tuple `(name, length)`: the name with labels joined by dots (the root is `""`), and how many bytes it took. Raise `ValueError` if a length byte is above 63 or the data ends before the name does.

For example, `encode_name("ab.c")` is `b"\x02ab\x01c\x00"`, and `decode_name(b"\x02ab\x01c\x00rest")` is `("ab.c", 6)`.
--- starter
def encode_name(name):
    return name.encode("ascii")


def decode_name(data):
    return ("", 0)
--- solution
def encode_name(name):
    if name.endswith("."):
        name = name[:-1]
    out = b""
    if name:
        for label in name.split("."):
            if not 1 <= len(label) <= 63:
                raise ValueError("a label must be 1 to 63 characters")
            out += bytes([len(label)]) + label.encode("ascii")
    out += b"\x00"
    if len(out) > 255:
        raise ValueError("name too long")
    return out


def decode_name(data):
    labels = []
    pos = 0
    while True:
        if pos >= len(data):
            raise ValueError("name runs off the end")
        size = data[pos]
        if size == 0:
            return (".".join(labels), pos + 1)
        if size > 63:
            raise ValueError("bad label length")
        if pos + 1 + size > len(data):
            raise ValueError("label runs off the end")
        labels.append(data[pos + 1:pos + 1 + size].decode("ascii"))
        pos += 1 + size
--- hint
For encoding, strip one trailing dot, split on dots, and add each label with its length byte in front. Remember the root: `""` gives no labels at all, just the zero byte.
--- hint
For decoding, keep a position. Read a length byte; 0 means done (the length taken is the position plus one). Otherwise check the byte and that the label fits, then take that many bytes and move on.
--- check case | The example from the task
(encode_name("ab.c"), decode_name(b"\x02ab\x01c\x00rest"))
=> (b"\x02ab\x01c\x00", ("ab.c", 6))
--- check case | Trailing dot and the root
(encode_name("lunar.example."), encode_name(""), encode_name("."), decode_name(b"\x00"))
=> (b"\x05lunar\x07example\x00", b"\x00", b"\x00", ("", 1))
--- check test | Bad labels and names that are too long
raises(ValueError, lambda: encode_name("a..b")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: encode_name("a" * 64 + ".com")) and len(encode_name("a" * 63 + ".com")) == 69 and raises(ValueError, lambda: encode_name(".".join(["a" * 63] * 4))) and len(encode_name(".".join(["a" * 63] * 3 + ["a" * 61]))) == 255
--- check test | Truncated data and bad length bytes
raises(ValueError, lambda: decode_name(b"\x03ab")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: decode_name(b"\x02ab")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: decode_name(b"")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: decode_name(b"\x40" + b"a" * 64 + b"\x00"))
--- check test | decode undoes encode for random names
(lambda R: all((lambda n: decode_name(encode_name(n) + b"xyz") == (n, len(encode_name(n))))(".".join("".join(R.choice("abcdefghij0123456789-") for _ in range(R.randint(1, 20))) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 6)))) for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(11))

+++ practice | Building a query
--- task
Write `build_query(qid, name, qtype)` that returns the bytes of a DNS query message asking one question, with recursion desired.

- The 12-byte header is six 2-byte big-endian numbers: the ID `qid`, the flags `0x0100` (only the "recursion desired" bit), the question count 1, and three counts of 0.
- The question is the name in wire format (length-prefixed labels and a zero byte, a single trailing dot ignored, the root `""` as one zero byte), then the type code and the class 1 (Internet), each 2 bytes big-endian.
- `qtype` is one of the strings `"A"`, `"NS"`, `"CNAME"`, `"MX"`, `"TXT"` or `"AAAA"`, with codes 1, 2, 5, 15, 16 and 28.

Raise `ValueError` if `qid` is not from 0 to 65535, if `qtype` is not one of those, or if a label is empty or longer than 63 characters.

For example, `build_query(0x1234, "a.io", "A")` starts with the bytes `12 34 01 00 00 01 00 00 00 00 00 00`, then `01 61 02 69 6f 00`, then `00 01 00 01`.
--- starter
def build_query(qid, name, qtype):
    return b""
--- solution
TYPES = {"A": 1, "NS": 2, "CNAME": 5, "MX": 15, "TXT": 16, "AAAA": 28}


def build_query(qid, name, qtype):
    if not 0 <= qid <= 65535:
        raise ValueError("the ID is 16 bits")
    if qtype not in TYPES:
        raise ValueError("unknown type")
    header = b"".join(n.to_bytes(2, "big") for n in (qid, 0x0100, 1, 0, 0, 0))
    name = name[:-1] if name.endswith(".") else name
    question = b""
    for label in name.split(".") if name else []:
        if not 1 <= len(label) <= 63:
            raise ValueError("bad label")
        question += bytes([len(label)]) + label.encode("ascii")
    question += b"\x00" + TYPES[qtype].to_bytes(2, "big") + (1).to_bytes(2, "big")
    return header + question
--- hint
Build the header by turning each of the six numbers into 2 big-endian bytes with `to_bytes(2, "big")` and joining them.
--- hint
The question is the encoded name, then the type code and the class, 2 bytes each. A dict from type name to code makes the check for unknown types easy.
--- check case | The example from the task
build_query(0x1234, "a.io", "A").hex()
=> "123401000001000000000000016102696f0000010001"
--- check case | An MX query with a trailing dot
build_query(7, "orbit.com.", "MX").hex()
=> "000701000001000000000000056f7262697403636f6d00000f0001"
--- check case | The root, type NS
build_query(65535, "", "NS").hex()
=> "ffff010000010000000000000000020001"
--- check test | Bad IDs, types and labels
raises(ValueError, lambda: build_query(65536, "a.io", "A")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: build_query(-1, "a.io", "A")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: build_query(1, "a.io", "SRV")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: build_query(1, "a..io", "A")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: build_query(1, "x" * 64, "A"))
--- check test | The length is 12 + the name + 4
all(len(build_query(1, n, "AAAA")) == 12 + (len(n) + 2 if n else 1) + 4 for n in ["a", "ab.cd", "www.example.org", ""])

+++ practice | A cache that remembers "no"
--- task
Resolvers also cache negative answers. Write a class `DnsCache` with these methods; an entry stored at time `now` with a TTL of `ttl` is valid while the current time is less than `now + ttl`.

- `put(name, rtype, values, ttl, now)` stores a positive answer: the list `values`, sorted.
- `put_negative(name, rtype, soa_ttl, soa_minimum, now)` stores the answer "no such data". Its TTL is the smaller of the SOA record's own TTL and the SOA's minimum field, as RFC 2308 says.
- `get(name, rtype, now)` returns the sorted list for a valid positive entry, `[]` for a valid negative entry, and `None` if there is no valid entry. Names are case-insensitive: `"WWW.Orbit.com"` and `"www.orbit.com"` are the same key.
- `remaining(name, rtype, now)` returns how many seconds a valid entry has left (its expiry minus `now`), or 0 if there is no valid entry. A resolver passes this on as the TTL of the answers it gives from its cache.

A new `put` or `put_negative` for the same name and type replaces the old entry. The attributes `hits` and `misses` count the calls to `get` that found a valid entry (positive or negative) and those that did not.
--- starter
class DnsCache:
    def __init__(self):
        self.hits = 0
        self.misses = 0

    def put(self, name, rtype, values, ttl, now):
        pass

    def put_negative(self, name, rtype, soa_ttl, soa_minimum, now):
        pass

    def get(self, name, rtype, now):
        return None

    def remaining(self, name, rtype, now):
        return 0
--- solution
class DnsCache:
    def __init__(self):
        self.hits = 0
        self.misses = 0
        self.entries = {}

    def put(self, name, rtype, values, ttl, now):
        self.entries[(name.lower(), rtype)] = (now + ttl, sorted(values))

    def put_negative(self, name, rtype, soa_ttl, soa_minimum, now):
        self.entries[(name.lower(), rtype)] = (now + min(soa_ttl, soa_minimum), [])

    def valid(self, name, rtype, now):
        entry = self.entries.get((name.lower(), rtype))
        if entry is not None and now < entry[0]:
            return entry
        return None

    def get(self, name, rtype, now):
        entry = self.valid(name, rtype, now)
        if entry is None:
            self.misses += 1
            return None
        self.hits += 1
        return list(entry[1])

    def remaining(self, name, rtype, now):
        entry = self.valid(name, rtype, now)
        return entry[0] - now if entry else 0
--- hint
Store every entry as `(expiry, values)` under the key `(name.lower(), rtype)`. A negative entry is just an entry whose values are the empty list.
--- hint
One private helper that returns the entry only when `now < expiry` serves both `get` and `remaining`. Count a hit or a miss only in `get`.
--- check case | Positive entries, sorted, and their countdown
(lambda c: (c.put("www.orbit.com", "A", ["10.0.1.2", "10.0.1.1"], 300, 1000), c.get("www.orbit.com", "A", 1000), c.remaining("www.orbit.com", "A", 1100), c.get("www.orbit.com", "A", 1299), c.get("www.orbit.com", "A", 1300))[1:])(DnsCache())
=> (["10.0.1.1", "10.0.1.2"], 200, ["10.0.1.1", "10.0.1.2"], None)
--- check case | A negative entry uses the smaller of the two SOA numbers
(lambda c: (c.put_negative("nope.orbit.com", "A", 3600, 900, 0), c.get("nope.orbit.com", "A", 899), c.get("nope.orbit.com", "A", 900), c.put_negative("x.orbit.com", "A", 60, 900, 0), c.remaining("x.orbit.com", "A", 10))[1:])(DnsCache())
=> ([], None, None, 50)
--- check case | Names are case-insensitive; types are not shared
(lambda c: (c.put("Mail.Orbit.COM", "MX", ["m1"], 60, 0), c.get("mail.orbit.com", "MX", 1), c.get("MAIL.orbit.com", "A", 1))[1:])(DnsCache())
=> (["m1"], None)
--- check case | A new answer replaces a negative one
(lambda c: (c.put_negative("new.orbit.com", "A", 300, 300, 0), c.get("new.orbit.com", "A", 10), c.put("new.orbit.com", "A", ["10.1.1.1"], 60, 20), c.get("new.orbit.com", "A", 30))[1::2])(DnsCache())
=> ([], ["10.1.1.1"])
--- check case | Hits and misses, with a negative hit counted as a hit
(lambda c: (c.get("a", "A", 0), c.put("a", "A", ["1"], 5, 0), c.get("a", "A", 1), c.put_negative("b", "A", 5, 5, 0), c.get("b", "A", 1), c.get("a", "A", 5), c.hits, c.misses)[6:])(DnsCache())
=> (2, 2)
--- check test | Returned lists are copies: changing one does not change the cache
(lambda c: (c.put("a", "A", ["1"], 5, 0), c.get("a", "A", 1).append("evil"), c.get("a", "A", 1))[2] == ["1"])(DnsCache())

+++ practice | Compressed names
--- task
In a real DNS message, a name may end with a **pointer**: two bytes whose top two bits are both 1 (the first byte is 192 or more). The other 14 bits are an offset from the start of the message, where the rest of the name is written.

Write `read_name(msg, offset)` that reads the name starting at `offset` in the message `msg` (bytes) and returns `(name, next_offset)`: the name with labels joined by dots (the root is `""`), and the offset just after the name **where it started**, that is, after its zero byte, or after the first pointer it met (a pointer ends the name at that place, even if the name continues elsewhere).

Raise `ValueError` if:

- the data runs out before the name ends;
- a length byte is from 64 to 191 (those values are not labels or pointers);
- a pointer points at or beyond the end of the message;
- the pointers form a loop. Detect this by allowing at most 64 pointers in one name, or by remembering where you have been.

For example, in `b"\x05orbit\x03com\x00\x03www\xc0\x00"`, the name at offset 11 is `("www.orbit.com", 17)`.
--- starter
def read_name(msg, offset):
    labels = []
    while msg[offset] != 0:
        size = msg[offset]
        labels.append(msg[offset + 1:offset + 1 + size].decode())
        offset += 1 + size
    return (".".join(labels), offset + 1)
--- solution
def read_name(msg, offset):
    labels = []
    pos = offset
    end = None
    seen = set()
    while True:
        if pos >= len(msg):
            raise ValueError("name runs off the end")
        size = msg[pos]
        if size == 0:
            return (".".join(labels), end if end is not None else pos + 1)
        if size >= 192:
            if pos + 1 >= len(msg):
                raise ValueError("pointer cut short")
            target = ((size & 0x3F) << 8) | msg[pos + 1]
            if target >= len(msg):
                raise ValueError("pointer out of range")
            if target in seen:
                raise ValueError("pointer loop")
            seen.add(target)
            if end is None:
                end = pos + 2
            pos = target
            continue
        if size > 63:
            raise ValueError("bad length byte")
        if pos + 1 + size > len(msg):
            raise ValueError("label runs off the end")
        labels.append(msg[pos + 1:pos + 1 + size].decode("ascii"))
        pos += 1 + size
--- hint
Read length bytes in a loop. A byte of 192 or more starts a pointer: the target is `((byte & 0x3F) << 8) | next_byte`. Jump there and keep reading labels.
--- hint
The offset you return is fixed by the **first** pointer: remember `pos + 2` the first time you jump, and return it at the end instead of the position after the zero byte.
--- hint
Keep a set of pointer targets already followed. Following the same target twice means a loop.
--- check case | The example: a name that ends in a pointer
read_name(b"\x05orbit\x03com\x00\x03www\xc0\x00", 11)
=> ("www.orbit.com", 17)
--- check case | A name with no pointer, and the root
(read_name(b"\x05orbit\x03com\x00", 0), read_name(b"\x00", 0), read_name(b"xx\x00", 2))
=> (("orbit.com", 11), ("", 1), ("", 3))
--- check case | A pointer to a name that itself ends in a pointer
read_name(b"\x03com\x00\x05orbit\xc0\x00\x03api\xc0\x05", 13)
=> ("api.orbit.com", 19)
--- check case | A name that is only a pointer
read_name(b"\x03com\x00\xc0\x00", 5)
=> ("com", 7)
--- check test | Loops are caught
raises(ValueError, lambda: read_name(b"\xc0\x00", 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: read_name(b"\x01a\xc0\x04\x01b\xc0\x00", 0))
--- check test | Out-of-range pointers, bad length bytes and truncated data
raises(ValueError, lambda: read_name(b"\x01a\xc0\x09", 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: read_name(b"\x41" + b"a" * 65 + b"\x00", 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: read_name(b"\x05orb", 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: read_name(b"\x01a", 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: read_name(b"\x01a\xc0", 0))

+++ practice | Debug: the zone that swallowed its neighbour
--- task
`zone_for(name, zones)` should return the zone, from the list `zones`, that is responsible for `name`: the longest zone that `name` equals or lies inside, compared by whole labels and ignoring case. The root zone is `""`, and every name lies inside it. It returns `None` if no zone fits.

The code below sends queries for `notorbit.com` to the `orbit.com` servers, which know nothing about it. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def zone_for(name, zones):
    name = name.lower()
    best = None
    for zone in zones:
        z = zone.lower()
        if name.endswith(z):
            if best is None or len(z) > len(best):
                best = z
    return best
--- solution
def zone_for(name, zones):
    name = name.lower()
    best = None
    for zone in zones:
        z = zone.lower()
        if z == "" or name == z or name.endswith("." + z):
            if best is None or len(z) > len(best):
                best = z
    return best
--- hint
`"notorbit.com".endswith("orbit.com")` is `True`. What character must come right before the zone for the match to be on a label boundary?
--- hint
A name is inside a zone when the zone is the root, or the name equals the zone, or the name ends with a dot followed by the zone.
--- check case | The neighbour is not inside the zone
zone_for("notorbit.com", ["", "com", "orbit.com"])
=> "com"
--- check case | The zone itself and names inside it
(zone_for("orbit.com", ["", "com", "orbit.com"]), zone_for("a.b.orbit.com", ["", "com", "orbit.com"]))
=> ("orbit.com", "orbit.com")
--- check case | Case does not matter; the root catches the rest
(zone_for("WWW.Orbit.COM", ["", "orbit.com"]), zone_for("example.net", ["", "com"]), zone_for("example.net", ["com"]))
=> ("orbit.com", "", None)

+++ practice | Reading the addresses out of a reply
--- task
Write `parse_a_records(msg)` that takes a whole DNS reply message (bytes) and returns the list of its answers of type A, as tuples `(name, ttl, address)`, in the order they appear. The address is dotted text such as `"10.0.1.1"`.

The message is laid out like this:

- A 12-byte header: ID, flags, then four 2-byte big-endian counts: questions, answers, authority records, additional records.
- The questions: each is a name, then a 2-byte type and a 2-byte class.
- The answers: each is a name, then type (2 bytes), class (2), TTL (4), data length (2), and that many bytes of data. An A record has type 1 and 4 bytes of data, one per part of the address. Skip answers of other types (such as CNAME, type 5).

Names use compression: a name may end with (or be) a pointer, two bytes whose first byte is 192 or more, holding a 14-bit offset from the start of the message. Raise `ValueError` if the reply's flags do not have the top bit set (it is not a reply), or if an A record's data length is not 4.
--- starter
def parse_a_records(msg):
    return []
--- solution
def read_name(msg, pos):
    labels = []
    end = None
    jumps = 0
    while True:
        size = msg[pos]
        if size == 0:
            return ".".join(labels), (end if end is not None else pos + 1)
        if size >= 192:
            if end is None:
                end = pos + 2
            jumps += 1
            if jumps > 64:
                raise ValueError("pointer loop")
            pos = ((size & 0x3F) << 8) | msg[pos + 1]
            continue
        labels.append(msg[pos + 1:pos + 1 + size].decode("ascii"))
        pos += 1 + size


def parse_a_records(msg):
    if not msg[2] & 0x80:
        raise ValueError("not a reply")
    qdcount = int.from_bytes(msg[4:6], "big")
    ancount = int.from_bytes(msg[6:8], "big")
    pos = 12
    for _ in range(qdcount):
        _, pos = read_name(msg, pos)
        pos += 4
    found = []
    for _ in range(ancount):
        name, pos = read_name(msg, pos)
        rtype = int.from_bytes(msg[pos:pos + 2], "big")
        ttl = int.from_bytes(msg[pos + 4:pos + 8], "big")
        length = int.from_bytes(msg[pos + 8:pos + 10], "big")
        data = msg[pos + 10:pos + 10 + length]
        pos += 10 + length
        if rtype == 1:
            if length != 4:
                raise ValueError("an A record has 4 bytes")
            found.append((name, ttl, ".".join(str(b) for b in data)))
    return found
--- hint
Write a name reader first: it follows pointers, and returns the name and the offset just after the name where it started (after the first pointer, or after the zero byte). Then walk the message with one position variable.
--- hint
Skip each question: read its name, then add 4 for type and class. For each answer, read the name, then the 10 fixed bytes (type, class, TTL, data length), then skip the data, keeping it if the type is 1.
--- check case | One question, two A answers that point back at the question's name
parse_a_records(bytes.fromhex("abcd8180000100020000000003777777056f7262697403636f6d0000010001c00c000100010000012c00040a000101c00c00010001000000c800040a000102"))
=> [("www.orbit.com", 300, "10.0.1.1"), ("www.orbit.com", 200, "10.0.1.2")]
--- check case | A CNAME answer is skipped, and the A record's name points into it
parse_a_records(bytes.fromhex("00018180000100020000000003617069056f7262697403636f6d0000010001c00c0005000100000258000603777777c010c02b00010001000000780004c0a80001"))
=> [("www.orbit.com", 120, "192.168.0.1")]
--- check case | No answers at all
parse_a_records(bytes.fromhex("00028183000100000000000003626164036f72670000010001"))
=> []
--- check test | A query is not a reply, and a 5-byte A record is rejected
raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_a_records(bytes.fromhex("abcd01000001000000000000016102696f0000010001"))) and raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_a_records(bytes.fromhex("abcd8180000100010000000001610000010001c00c000100010000003c00050a00010101")))

+++ question | Why a cached answer is safe to use
--- ask
A resolver received `www.orbit.com A 10.0.1.1` with a TTL of 300 seconds at 12:00:00. At 12:04:00 the owner moves the service to a new address. What can a client of this resolver see, and why is that acceptable?
--- choice
The new address at once, because authoritative servers notify every resolver of changes.
--- choice correct
The old address until 12:05:00 at the latest, because the owner promised the record would stay valid for its TTL; an owner who plans a move lowers the TTL beforehand.
--- choice
The old address forever, until the resolver restarts.
--- choice
An error, because the cached record no longer matches the zone.
--- why
DNS has no notifications to resolvers; the TTL is the only contract. A cached answer is at most TTL seconds stale, and the zone owner chooses that bound. Before a planned move, owners lower the TTL to something like 60 seconds a day ahead, so that the switch spreads quickly.

+++ question | Who does the walking
--- ask
Your laptop asks its resolver for `docs.python.org`, and the resolver, with an empty cache, asks a root server, an `org` server and a `python.org` server. Which queries are recursive and which are iterative?
--- choice
All four are recursive: every server finds the full answer before replying.
--- choice correct
The laptop's query to the resolver is recursive (find the answer for me); the resolver's three queries are iterative (each server gives its best answer, often a referral).
--- choice
The laptop's query is iterative and the resolver's queries are recursive.
--- choice
The root query is recursive and the other two are iterative.
--- why
Root and TLD servers never do recursion for others: they answer from their own zone, which is usually a referral. That keeps them simple and stateless. The recursive resolver does the walk and caches the results, so the next client asking for a name in `python.org` costs one query or none.

+++ question | The step that ends resolution
--- ask
Why can resolving a name with k labels (and no CNAMEs) cause at most k referrals?
--- choice
Because each server answers after at most one referral by the rules of UDP.
--- choice
Because the TTLs add up to more than k seconds.
--- choice correct
Because each referral goes to a zone that is a strictly longer suffix of the name, and a name with k labels has only k non-root suffixes.
--- choice
Because the root servers know every name directly after k steps.
--- why
The zones visited go root, then a 1-label zone, then a 2-label zone, and so on, each an ancestor of the name. A zone longer than the name cannot contain it, so after at most k referrals the resolver is at the name's own zone, whose server must answer or say it does not exist. Real resolvers still cap the steps, because a misconfigured server could refer sideways.

=== net-12 | HTTP: requests, responses and keep-alive
--- teach
Last lesson a name became an address. With the address, a browser opens a TCP connection, and then it must say what it wants. The language it speaks is **HTTP**, the Hypertext Transfer Protocol. HTTP carries web pages, but also almost every API: weather services, payment systems, and the archives that serve spacecraft telemetry to scientists. This lesson is about HTTP/1.1 messages: what is in them, how a program finds where one ends and the next begins on a TCP byte stream, and how one connection carries many requests.

### Ordering at a counter

At a café counter you say one thing in a fixed shape: "A large coffee, with oat milk, to take away." The barista answers in a fixed shape too: "Here you go, that's three pounds", or "Sorry, we're out of oat milk." You both know where your sentence ends and where the answer starts. HTTP is exactly that: a **request** from the client, then a **response** from the server, each in an agreed shape.

### Resources and URLs

HTTP is about **resources**: things with names, such as a page, an image, or the latest reading of a sensor. A resource is named by a **URL**:

```text
http://archive.orbit.example:8080/telemetry/latest?sensor=temp
scheme  host                   port  path              query
```

The client uses the host (through DNS) and the port to open the connection. Then it sends only the path and the query, called the **request target**, and names the host again in a header.

### A request

A request is text in lines, each ended by the two characters **CR LF**, carriage return and line feed, written `\r\n`:

```text
GET /telemetry/latest?sensor=temp HTTP/1.1\r\n
Host: archive.orbit.example\r\n
Accept: application/json\r\n
\r\n
```

1. The **request line**: a **method** (what to do), the target, and the protocol version, separated by single spaces.
2. **Header lines**: `Name: value`, one per line. Header names are **case-insensitive**, so `host` and `HOST` are the same header. Spaces around the value do not count.
3. An **empty line**: CR LF on its own. It ends the headers.
4. A **body**, for requests that carry data, such as a form or an upload.

In Python, a request is built as bytes, since that is what a socket sends:

```python
line = "PUT /config/antenna HTTP/1.1"
print(repr((line + "\r\n").encode("ascii")))
```

### A response

A response has the same shape, with a **status line** instead of a request line:

```text
HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n
Content-Type: application/json\r\n
Content-Length: 18\r\n
\r\n
{"temp_c": -12.5}\n
```

The status line holds the version, a three-digit **status code** for programs, and a **reason phrase** for people.

### Methods, and which ones are safe to repeat

| method | meaning | safe | idempotent |
|---|---|---|---|
| GET | send me this resource | yes | yes |
| HEAD | like GET, but only the headers | yes | yes |
| POST | here is data for you to process | no | no |
| PUT | store this at this target, replacing what was there | no | yes |
| DELETE | remove this resource | no | yes |

- A **safe** method only reads: it changes nothing on the server.
- An **idempotent** method has the same effect whether it is done once or several times. Deleting a file twice leaves it deleted; putting the same contents twice leaves the same contents.

This matters for the unreliable networks of this course. When a connection breaks before the response arrives, a client cannot know whether the server acted. It may safely **retry** an idempotent request. Retrying a POST ("launch the rocket") might do it twice, which is the problem from lesson 6's command retries.

### Status codes

The first digit says what kind of answer it is:

| class | meaning | common codes |
|---|---|---|
| 1xx | informational | 100 Continue |
| 2xx | success | 200 OK, 201 Created, 204 No Content |
| 3xx | go elsewhere, or use your copy | 301 Moved Permanently, 304 Not Modified |
| 4xx | the client made a mistake | 400 Bad Request, 404 Not Found, 405 Method Not Allowed |
| 5xx | the server failed | 500 Internal Server Error, 503 Service Unavailable |

A program that meets a code it does not know treats it like the x00 of its class: an unknown 4xx is handled like 400.

### Important headers

- **Host** names the server the client wants. HTTP/1.1 requires it, because one IP address often serves [[many sites|vhost]].
- **Content-Type** says what the body is: `text/html`, `application/json`, `image/png`.
- **Content-Length** says how many **bytes** the body has.
- **Connection** controls whether the connection stays open after this message (below).
- **ETag** and **If-None-Match** let a client ask "only send it if it changed since the version I have". The server answers **304 Not Modified**, with no body, if it has not.

### Where does a message end?

TCP gives a stream of bytes (lesson 9). A server that has received some bytes must work out where one request stops, because the next one may follow right behind it. This is the framing problem of lesson 2 again.

1. **The header section** ends at the first empty line, that is, at the first `\r\n\r\n`. No header line may be empty, so the first empty line is always the end.
2. **The body** has its length set by the headers, in one of three ways:
   - **Content-Length: n**: the next n bytes are the body.
   - **Transfer-Encoding: chunked**: the body comes in pieces, each preceded by its size in hexadecimal on a line of its own, and ended by a piece of size 0. A server can then start sending before it knows the total size.
   - For a **response** with neither, the body is everything until the server closes the connection. A request has no such option: a request with neither header has no body.

Finding the end of the headers, on a buffer holding a little more than one message:

```python
buf = b"DELETE /old HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: x\r\n\r\nGET /next HTTP/1.1\r\n"
end = buf.find(b"\r\n\r\n")
print(end)                  # 29: the headers are buf[:29]
print(buf[end + 4:])        # the start of the next request
```

And splitting one header line into its name and value, with `partition`, which splits at the first colon only (a value may contain colons, as in times and URLs):

```python
name, colon, value = "Date: Tue, 29 Sep 2026 10:00:00 GMT".partition(":")
print(name.lower(), "=", value.strip())
```

**Why this framing is correct.** Suppose the bytes received so far start at the beginning of a well-formed message. The first `\r\n\r\n` is exactly the end of its headers, since no header line is empty. The headers then fix the body's length L, so the message is exactly the header section, the 4 bytes of the empty line, and L more bytes. The byte right after it is therefore the first byte of the next message. By induction on the number of messages, a parser that repeats this step splits any stream of well-formed messages exactly at their boundaries, however TCP cut the bytes into segments. If fewer than L body bytes have arrived, the parser must wait for more, not guess.

### Keep-alive: many requests, one connection

HTTP/1.0 opened a new TCP connection for every request and closed it after the response. Each connection costs a handshake round trip and starts again in slow start (lesson 10). A page with 50 images paid that 50 times.

HTTP/1.1 makes connections **persistent** by default, called **keep-alive**: after a response, the connection stays open for the next request. Either side can end it by sending the header `Connection: close`, and then closing after that message. HTTP/1.0 clients could ask for persistence with `Connection: keep-alive`.

A client may even send several requests without waiting for the responses, called **pipelining**. The server must answer them **in order**. So one slow response holds up all the ones behind it, like a slow customer at the front of a queue. This is **head-of-line blocking**, and it is why browsers rarely pipelined, and why [[HTTP/2 and HTTP/3|http2]] were designed.

### What it costs

- **Parsing** a message of n bytes takes O(n) time, if the parser never scans the same bytes twice. A server that reads in pieces and searches the whole buffer for `\r\n\r\n` again after each piece can take O(n²); it should remember where the last search stopped.
- **Headers are overhead.** A typical request carries 500 to 800 bytes of headers, even to fetch a 2-byte answer. HTTP/2 compresses them.
- **A new connection** costs one round trip for TCP (plus two for TLS 1.2 or one for TLS 1.3, lesson 14) before the request can even be sent. Keep-alive pays that once.
- **Memory:** a server must limit header size (commonly 8 to 16 KB) and body size, or one client can fill its memory.

### Where it is used

- **The web**, obviously: every page, image and script.
- **APIs**: [[REST|rest]] services use the methods on resources, with JSON bodies. Mission control systems expose telemetry archives and command queues this way.
- **Software updates, package managers and container registries** download over HTTP.
- **Health checks and monitoring**: load balancers send `GET /health` to servers every few seconds.

**Watch out:** the classic HTTP mistakes.

- Ending lines with `\n` instead of `\r\n`. Some servers accept it, many do not.
- Counting the body in **characters** instead of **bytes**. `"café"` is 4 characters but 5 bytes in UTF-8, and a Content-Length of 4 cuts off the last byte.
- Accepting a request with both `Content-Length` and `Transfer-Encoding`, or with two different lengths. Two servers in a chain may pick different ones, and then disagree about where the next request starts: that is **[[request smuggling|smuggling]]**.
- Comparing header names case-sensitively.

::: context vhost Many sites on one address
A single server, with one IP address, often hosts hundreds of websites. In HTTP/1.0 the request line held only the path, so the server could not tell which site `/index.html` was meant for. HTTP/1.1 made the Host header required, and servers pick the site by it; this is called name-based virtual hosting. HTTPS has the same problem one step earlier, before any HTTP is sent, and TLS solves it with the Server Name Indication field in its first message.
:::

::: context http2 HTTP/2 and HTTP/3
HTTP/2 (2015) keeps the methods, headers and status codes, but sends them as binary frames. Many requests share one TCP connection as separate numbered streams, with their frames interleaved, so one slow response no longer blocks the others at the HTTP level. But TCP still delivers bytes in order: one lost segment stalls every stream until it is resent. HTTP/3 (2022) runs over QUIC on UDP, where each stream has its own ordering, so a loss stalls only its own stream.
:::

::: context rest REST
REST, from Roy Fielding's 2000 thesis, is a style of designing APIs around HTTP itself: every thing is a resource with a URL, and the methods mean what they say. `GET /missions/42` reads mission 42, `PUT /missions/42` replaces it, `DELETE /missions/42` removes it, and `POST /missions` creates a new one. Because GET is safe and PUT and DELETE are idempotent, caches and retrying clients can work without knowing anything about the particular API.
:::

::: context smuggling Request smuggling
Many sites put a front-end proxy before their servers, and the two share one keep-alive connection for many users' requests. If an attacker sends a request that the proxy frames with Content-Length but the server frames with Transfer-Encoding, the server sees the leftover bytes as the start of a new request, prefixed to the next user's request. Attackers have used this to steal other users' cookies. The defence is strict parsing: reject any message whose length is ambiguous.
:::
--- task
Write `parse_requests(data)`, which splits the bytes a server has received on one connection into complete HTTP requests. Return a tuple `(requests, rest)`: a list of the complete requests, in order, and the bytes left over (the start of a request that has not fully arrived, or `b""`).

Each request is a dict with the keys `"method"`, `"target"`, `"version"`, `"headers"` and `"body"`:

- The header section ends at the first `\r\n\r\n`. Until it has arrived, the request is incomplete. Decode it with `"latin-1"`.
- The request line must be exactly three parts separated by single spaces. The method must be one or more ASCII capital letters, the target must not be empty, and the version must be `"HTTP/1.0"` or `"HTTP/1.1"`.
- Each header line is split at its first colon. The name must not be empty and must not contain a space or a tab; the value has spaces and tabs stripped from both ends. `"headers"` maps each name, **in lower case**, to its value; a header that appears more than once gets its values joined by `", "`, in order. A header line that starts with a space or a tab, or has no colon, is an error.
- An `HTTP/1.1` request must have a `host` header.
- A request with a `transfer-encoding` header is an error (this server does not support it).
- `content-length`, if present, gives the body's length in bytes. It must be ASCII digits only; if it appears more than once, every copy must be the same number. Without it, the body is `b""`. The request is incomplete until the whole body has arrived.

Raise `ValueError` for any error in a request whose header section is complete. For example, `parse_requests(b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: a\r\n\r\nPOST /x HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: a\r\nContent-Length: 5\r\n\r\nab")` returns one request (the GET) and, as the rest, the POST from its `P` up to the `ab` that has arrived so far.
--- starter
def parse_requests(data):
    return ([], data)
--- solution
def parse_requests(data):
    requests = []
    pos = 0
    while True:
        end = data.find(b"\r\n\r\n", pos)
        if end < 0:
            break
        lines = data[pos:end].decode("latin-1").split("\r\n")
        parts = lines[0].split(" ")
        if len(parts) != 3:
            raise ValueError("bad request line")
        method, target, version = parts
        if not (method.isascii() and method.isalpha() and method.isupper()):
            raise ValueError("bad method")
        if not target or version not in ("HTTP/1.0", "HTTP/1.1"):
            raise ValueError("bad target or version")
        headers = {}
        for line in lines[1:]:
            if line[:1] in (" ", "\t"):
                raise ValueError("folded header line")
            name, colon, value = line.partition(":")
            if not colon or not name or " " in name or "\t" in name:
                raise ValueError("bad header line")
            key = name.lower()
            value = value.strip(" \t")
            headers[key] = headers[key] + ", " + value if key in headers else value
        if version == "HTTP/1.1" and "host" not in headers:
            raise ValueError("HTTP/1.1 needs a Host header")
        if "transfer-encoding" in headers:
            raise ValueError("transfer encodings are not supported")
        length = 0
        if "content-length" in headers:
            copies = {v.strip(" \t") for v in headers["content-length"].split(",")}
            if len(copies) != 1:
                raise ValueError("conflicting lengths")
            text = copies.pop()
            if not (text.isascii() and text.isdigit()):
                raise ValueError("bad length")
            length = int(text)
        start = end + 4
        if len(data) - start < length:
            break
        requests.append({"method": method, "target": target, "version": version, "headers": headers, "body": data[start:start + length]})
        pos = start + length
    return (requests, data[pos:])
--- hint
Loop with a position `pos` where the next request starts. Find `b"\r\n\r\n"` from `pos`; if it is not there, stop. Otherwise decode the header section, split it into lines, and check the request line and each header line in turn.
--- hint
Build the headers dict with lower-case names, joining repeats with `", "`. Repeated Content-Length headers then look like `"5, 5"`: split on commas and check that every copy is the same.
--- hint
The body starts 4 bytes after the `\r\n\r\n`. If fewer than `length` bytes follow, stop and leave everything from `pos` on as the rest. Otherwise take the body, save the request, and move `pos` past it.
--- check case | One GET, nothing left over
parse_requests(b"GET /index.html HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: orbit.example\r\nAccept: */*\r\n\r\n")
=> ([{"method": "GET", "target": "/index.html", "version": "HTTP/1.1", "headers": {"host": "orbit.example", "accept": "*/*"}, "body": b""}], b"")
--- check case | The example from the task

=> b"\r\n\r\n\xff\x00"

parse_requests(b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHOST:   h  \r\nAccept: a\r\nX-Tag:\tone\r\naccept: b\r\n\r\n")[0][0]["headers"]
=> {"host": "h", "accept": "a, b", "x-tag": "one"}
--- check case | A value keeps its inner colons
parse_requests(b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h:8080\r\nDate: Tue, 29 Sep 2026 10:00:00 GMT\r\n\r\n")[0][0]["headers"]
=> {"host": "h:8080", "date": "Tue, 29 Sep 2026 10:00:00 GMT"}
--- check case | HTTP/1.0 needs no Host
parse_requests(b"GET /old HTTP/1.0\r\n\r\n")[0][0]["version"]
=> "HTTP/1.0"
--- check case | Nothing complete yet: everything is left over
(parse_requests(b""), parse_requests(b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n"), parse_requests(b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n\r"))
=> (([], b""), ([], b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n"), ([], b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n\r"))
