parse_requests(b"POST / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\nContent-Length: 2\r\nContent-Length: 2\r\n\r\nok")[0][0]["body"]
=> b"ok"
--- check test | Bad request lines
all(raises(ValueError, lambda d=d: parse_requests(d)) for d in [b"GET /  HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n\r\n", b"get / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n\r\n", b"GET HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n\r\n", b"GET / HTTP/2.0\r\nHost: h\r\n\r\n", b"G3T / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n\r\n", b"\r\n\r\n"])
--- check test | Bad header lines, a missing Host, and Transfer-Encoding
all(raises(ValueError, lambda d=d: parse_requests(d)) for d in [b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost h\r\n\r\n", b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n folded\r\n\r\n", b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost : h\r\n\r\n", b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\n: h\r\nHost: h\r\n\r\n", b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nAccept: x\r\n\r\n", b"POST / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\nTransfer-Encoding: chunked\r\n\r\n0\r\n\r\n"])
--- check test | Bad and conflicting lengths
all(raises(ValueError, lambda d=d: parse_requests(d)) for d in [b"POST / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\nContent-Length: -1\r\n\r\n", b"POST / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\nContent-Length: 3\r\nContent-Length: 4\r\n\r\nabcd", b"POST / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\nContent-Length: 1e3\r\n\r\n", b"POST / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\nContent-Length: \r\n\r\n"])
--- check test | Fed in random pieces, the same requests come out as from the whole stream
(lambda R, whole: all((lambda cuts: (lambda got: got == parse_requests(whole)[0])((lambda acc, buf: [acc.extend(r) or None for piece in [whole[a:b] for a, b in zip(cuts, cuts[1:])] for r, buf[0] in [parse_requests(buf[0] + piece)]] and acc)([], [b""])))([0] + sorted(R.sample(range(1, len(whole)), 8)) + [len(whole)]) for _ in range(100)))(__import__("random").Random(12), b"".join(b"POST /log/%d HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: gs\r\nContent-Length: %d\r\n\r\n" % (i, i * 3) + bytes([65 + i]) * (i * 3) for i in range(12)))

+++ practice | Building a response
--- task
Write `build_response(status, headers, body)` that returns the bytes of an HTTP/1.1 response.

- The status line is `HTTP/1.1 <code> <reason>` with these reasons: 200 `OK`, 201 `Created`, 204 `No Content`, 301 `Moved Permanently`, 304 `Not Modified`, 400 `Bad Request`, 404 `Not Found`, 405 `Method Not Allowed`, 500 `Internal Server Error`, 503 `Service Unavailable`. Raise `ValueError` for any other code.
- `headers` is a list of `(name, value)` pairs, written in order as `name: value` lines. Then add `Content-Length: <number of bytes in body>` as the last header, except for 204 and 304, which never have a body: for those, add no Content-Length, and raise `ValueError` if `body` is not empty.
- Every line ends with `\r\n`, and an empty line separates the headers from the body. `body` is `bytes`.

For example, `build_response(404, [], b"no")` is `b"HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found\r\nContent-Length: 2\r\n\r\nno"`.
--- starter
def build_response(status, headers, body):
    return b"HTTP/1.1 " + str(status).encode() + b"\r\n\r\n" + body
--- solution
REASONS = {
    200: "OK",
    201: "Created",
    204: "No Content",
    301: "Moved Permanently",
    304: "Not Modified",
    400: "Bad Request",
    404: "Not Found",
    405: "Method Not Allowed",
    500: "Internal Server Error",
    503: "Service Unavailable",
}


def build_response(status, headers, body):
    if status not in REASONS:
        raise ValueError("unknown status code")
    lines = [f"HTTP/1.1 {status} {REASONS[status]}"]
    lines += [f"{name}: {value}" for name, value in headers]
    if status in (204, 304):
        if body:
            raise ValueError("this status never has a body")
    else:
        lines.append(f"Content-Length: {len(body)}")
    return ("\r\n".join(lines) + "\r\n\r\n").encode("latin-1") + body
--- hint
Keep the reasons in a dict from code to phrase. Build a list of text lines: the status line, the given headers, and usually the Content-Length.
--- hint
Join the lines with `"\r\n"`, add `"\r\n\r\n"` to end the last header line and make the empty line, encode, and add the body bytes.
--- check case | The example from the task
build_response(404, [], b"no")
=> b"HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found\r\nContent-Length: 2\r\n\r\nno"
--- check case | Headers in order, Content-Length last
build_response(200, [("Content-Type", "application/json"), ("Cache-Control", "no-store")], b'{"ok":true}')
=> b'HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Type: application/json\r\nCache-Control: no-store\r\nContent-Length: 11\r\n\r\n{"ok":true}'
--- check case | 204 and 304 have no Content-Length and no body
(build_response(204, [], b""), build_response(304, [("ETag", '"v7"')], b""))
=> (b"HTTP/1.1 204 No Content\r\n\r\n", b'HTTP/1.1 304 Not Modified\r\nETag: "v7"\r\n\r\n')
--- check case | An empty body still gets a Content-Length
build_response(201, [("Location", "/missions/9")], b"")
=> b"HTTP/1.1 201 Created\r\nLocation: /missions/9\r\nContent-Length: 0\r\n\r\n"
--- check test | Unknown codes, and a body on 204 or 304
raises(ValueError, lambda: build_response(418, [], b"")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: build_response(204, [], b"x")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: build_response(304, [], b"x"))

+++ practice | Reading a response
--- task
Write `parse_response(data)` for a complete HTTP/1.1 response that a client has received, as bytes. Return a tuple `(status, reason, headers, body)`:

- The status line is `HTTP/1.x <code> <reason>`: split it at the first two spaces only, since a reason can contain spaces. `status` is the code as an `int`, and the reason may be empty.
- `headers` maps lower-case names to values stripped of spaces, as in the lesson (join repeats with `", "`).
- The body: if there is a `content-length` header, it is that many bytes after the empty line (raise `ValueError` if fewer have arrived; ignore any bytes after it). If there is none, the body is everything after the empty line, since such a response ends when the server closes the connection. Responses to status 204 and 304 always have an empty body.

Raise `ValueError` if there is no `\r\n\r\n`, or the status line does not start with `HTTP/1.`, or the code is not three digits.
--- starter
def parse_response(data):
    head, _, body = data.partition(b"\r\n\r\n")
    status = int(head.split(b" ")[1])
    return (status, "", {}, body)
--- solution
def parse_response(data):
    end = data.find(b"\r\n\r\n")
    if end < 0:
        raise ValueError("headers not complete")
    lines = data[:end].decode("latin-1").split("\r\n")
    parts = lines[0].split(" ", 2)
    if len(parts) < 2 or not parts[0].startswith("HTTP/1."):
        raise ValueError("bad status line")
    code = parts[1]
    if len(code) != 3 or not (code.isascii() and code.isdigit()):
        raise ValueError("bad status code")
    reason = parts[2] if len(parts) == 3 else ""
    headers = {}
    for line in lines[1:]:
        name, _, value = line.partition(":")
        key = name.strip().lower()
        value = value.strip()
        headers[key] = headers[key] + ", " + value if key in headers else value
    status = int(code)
    rest = data[end + 4:]
    if status in (204, 304):
        body = b""
    elif "content-length" in headers:
        length = int(headers["content-length"])
        if len(rest) < length:
            raise ValueError("body cut short")
        body = rest[:length]
    else:
        body = rest
    return (status, reason, headers, body)
--- hint
`line.split(" ", 2)` splits at the first two spaces only, giving at most three parts: the version, the code and the whole reason.
--- hint
Decide the body in this order: 204 and 304 have none; with a Content-Length, take exactly that many bytes; otherwise take everything after the headers.
--- check case | A normal response
parse_response(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Type: text/plain\r\nContent-Length: 5\r\n\r\nhello")
=> (200, "OK", {"content-type": "text/plain", "content-length": "5"}, b"hello")
--- check case | A reason with spaces, and bytes after the body ignored
parse_response(b"HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found\r\nContent-Length: 3\r\n\r\nno!HTTP/1.1 200 OK")[1::2]
=> ("Not Found", b"no!")
--- check case | No Content-Length: read to the end
parse_response(b"HTTP/1.0 200 OK\r\nServer: old\r\n\r\nall of this\r\nis body")[3]
=> b"all of this\r\nis body"
--- check case | 304 has no body, and an empty reason is allowed
(parse_response(b"HTTP/1.1 304 Not Modified\r\nETag: x\r\n\r\n")[3], parse_response(b"HTTP/1.1 500 \r\nContent-Length: 0\r\n\r\n")[:2])
=> (b"", (500, ""))
--- check test | Errors: incomplete, not HTTP, bad code, body cut short
raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_response(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_response(b"SPDY 200 OK\r\n\r\n")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_response(b"HTTP/1.1 20 OK\r\n\r\n")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_response(b"HTTP/1.1 2x0 OK\r\n\r\n")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_response(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: 9\r\n\r\nshort"))

+++ practice | Chunked bodies
--- task
When a server does not know a body's length in advance, it sends it with `Transfer-Encoding: chunked`. The body is a series of chunks, each written as its size in hexadecimal, `\r\n`, that many bytes, and `\r\n`. A chunk of size 0 ends the body; it is followed by `\r\n` (this lesson ignores trailer headers).

Write two functions.

- `chunk(body, size)` encodes `body` as chunks of `size` bytes (the last one may be shorter), in lower-case hex, followed by the final `0\r\n\r\n`. An empty body is just `b"0\r\n\r\n"`. Raise `ValueError` if `size` is less than 1.
- `unchunk(data)` decodes a chunked body from the start of `data` and returns `(body, rest)`, where `rest` is everything after the final `0\r\n\r\n`. Accept hex in either case, and ignore anything from a `;` to the end of a size line (a chunk extension). If the data is incomplete, return `None`. Raise `ValueError` if a size line is not hexadecimal or a chunk's data is not followed by `\r\n`.

For example, `chunk(b"telemetry", 4)` is `b"4\r\ntele\r\n4\r\nmetr\r\n1\r\ny\r\n0\r\n\r\n"`.
--- starter
def chunk(body, size):
    return body


def unchunk(data):
    return (data, b"")
--- solution
def chunk(body, size):
    if size < 1:
        raise ValueError("chunk size must be at least 1")
    out = b""
    for i in range(0, len(body), size):
        piece = body[i:i + size]
        out += b"%x\r\n" % len(piece) + piece + b"\r\n"
    return out + b"0\r\n\r\n"


def unchunk(data):
    body = b""
    pos = 0
    while True:
        eol = data.find(b"\r\n", pos)
        if eol < 0:
            return None
        text = data[pos:eol].split(b";")[0].strip()
        try:
            size = int(text, 16)
        except ValueError:
            raise ValueError("bad chunk size") from None
        if size < 0 or text.startswith(b"-"):
            raise ValueError("bad chunk size")
        start = eol + 2
        if size == 0:
            if len(data) < start + 2:
                return None
            if data[start:start + 2] != b"\r\n":
                raise ValueError("missing end of body")
            return (body, data[start + 2:])
        if len(data) < start + size + 2:
            return None
        if data[start + size:start + size + 2] != b"\r\n":
            raise ValueError("chunk not followed by CRLF")
        body += data[start:start + size]
        pos = start + size + 2
--- hint
`b"%x" % n` writes a number in lower-case hex as bytes. For decoding, `int(text, 16)` reads hex in either case and raises `ValueError` for anything else.
--- hint
Decode with a position: find the end of the size line, read the size (dropping any `;` extension), and check that the whole chunk and its `\r\n` have arrived before taking it. If anything has not arrived yet, return `None` rather than raising.
--- check case | The example from the task
chunk(b"telemetry", 4)
=> b"4\r\ntele\r\n4\r\nmetr\r\n1\r\ny\r\n0\r\n\r\n"
--- check case | Big chunks use hex, and an empty body
(chunk(bytes(26), 26)[:4], chunk(b"", 8))
=> (b"1a\r\n", b"0\r\n\r\n")
--- check case | unchunk returns the body and what follows
unchunk(b"4\r\ntele\r\n5\r\nmetry\r\n0\r\n\r\nGET / HTTP/1.1")
=> (b"telemetry", b"GET / HTTP/1.1")
--- check case | Upper-case hex and chunk extensions
unchunk(b"A;name=x\r\n0123456789\r\n0\r\n\r\n")
=> (b"0123456789", b"")
--- check case | Incomplete data gives None
(unchunk(b"4\r\ntel"), unchunk(b"4\r\ntele\r\n"), unchunk(b"4\r\ntele\r\n0\r\n"), unchunk(b""))
=> (None, None, None, None)
--- check test | Bad sizes, missing CRLF, and a bad chunk size argument
raises(ValueError, lambda: unchunk(b"zz\r\nab\r\n0\r\n\r\n")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: unchunk(b"2\r\nabX\r\n0\r\n\r\n")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: unchunk(b"-1\r\n\r\n")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: chunk(b"x", 0))
--- check test | unchunk undoes chunk for 200 random bodies and sizes
(lambda R: all((lambda b, n: unchunk(chunk(b, n) + b"tail") == (b, b"tail"))(bytes(R.randrange(256) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 300))), R.randint(1, 40)) for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(4))

+++ practice | Keep the connection or close it
--- task
After each message, both ends of an HTTP connection decide whether it stays open. Write `keep_alive(version, headers)`, where `version` is `"HTTP/1.0"` or `"HTTP/1.1"` and `headers` is a list of `(name, value)` pairs from one message, and return `True` if the connection stays open.

- Collect the **tokens** of every `Connection` header: header names are case-insensitive, a message may have several `Connection` headers, and each value is a comma-separated list of tokens, with spaces around them ignored, compared in lower case.
- If the token `close` is present, return `False`, whatever the version.
- Otherwise, HTTP/1.1 keeps the connection open, and HTTP/1.0 keeps it open only if the token `keep-alive` is present.

For example, `keep_alive("HTTP/1.1", [("Connection", "Upgrade, Close")])` is `False`.
--- starter
def keep_alive(version, headers):
    for name, value in headers:
        if name == "Connection":
            return value != "close"
    return True
--- solution
def keep_alive(version, headers):
    tokens = set()
    for name, value in headers:
        if name.lower() == "connection":
            tokens |= {t.strip().lower() for t in value.split(",")}
    if "close" in tokens:
        return False
    return version == "HTTP/1.1" or "keep-alive" in tokens
--- hint
Gather every token from every Connection header into one set first, lower-cased and stripped. Only then decide.
--- hint
`close` wins over everything. Without it, the answer depends on the version, and for HTTP/1.0 on `keep-alive`.
--- check case | The example from the task
keep_alive("HTTP/1.1", [("Connection", "Upgrade, Close")])
=> False
--- check case | The defaults of each version
(keep_alive("HTTP/1.1", []), keep_alive("HTTP/1.0", []), keep_alive("HTTP/1.1", [("Host", "h")]))
=> (True, False, True)
--- check case | HTTP/1.0 asking for keep-alive, in any case and spacing
(keep_alive("HTTP/1.0", [("connection", "  Keep-Alive ")]), keep_alive("HTTP/1.0", [("CONNECTION", "foo,keep-alive")]))
=> (True, True)
--- check case | Several Connection headers; close in any of them wins
(keep_alive("HTTP/1.0", [("Connection", "keep-alive"), ("Connection", "close")]), keep_alive("HTTP/1.1", [("Connection", "keep-alive"), ("connection", " CLOSE")]))
=> (False, False)
--- check case | A token only counts if it is a whole token
(keep_alive("HTTP/1.1", [("Connection", "closed")]), keep_alive("HTTP/1.0", [("Connection", "keep-alive-please")]), keep_alive("HTTP/1.1", [("X-Connection", "close")]))
=> (True, False, True)

+++ practice | Debug: the body that lost its last byte
--- task
`json_response(text)` should build an HTTP/1.1 `200 OK` response whose body is `text` encoded as UTF-8, with the headers `Content-Type: application/json; charset=utf-8` and `Content-Length`. Clients reading it stop after Content-Length bytes. Responses that contain names like `"Ångström"` or a `"°C"` arrive cut short, and the client then waits for bytes that never come. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def json_response(text):
    head = (
        "HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n"
        "Content-Type: application/json; charset=utf-8\r\n"
        f"Content-Length: {len(text)}\r\n"
        "\r\n"
    )
    return head.encode("ascii") + text.encode("utf-8")
--- solution
def json_response(text):
    body = text.encode("utf-8")
    head = (
        "HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n"
        "Content-Type: application/json; charset=utf-8\r\n"
        f"Content-Length: {len(body)}\r\n"
        "\r\n"
    )
    return head.encode("ascii") + body
--- hint
How many bytes does `"°C"` take in UTF-8? Compare `len("°C")` with `len("°C".encode("utf-8"))`.
--- hint
Content-Length counts bytes. Encode the body first and measure the encoded bytes.
--- check case | Plain ASCII still works
json_response('{"ok": true}')
=> b'HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Type: application/json; charset=utf-8\r\nContent-Length: 12\r\n\r\n{"ok": true}'
--- check case | A degree sign takes two bytes
json_response('{"t": "21°C"}').split(b"\r\n")[2]
=> b"Content-Length: 14"
--- check test | The length always equals the bytes after the headers
all((lambda r: int(r.split(b"\r\n")[2].split(b": ")[1]) == len(r.split(b"\r\n\r\n", 1)[1]))(json_response(t)) for t in ['{"name": "Ångström"}', "", "日本語", "🚀" * 5, "plain"])

+++ practice | A tiny file server
--- task
Write `respond(request, files)`, the decision part of a small static file server. `request` is a dict like those from the lesson's parser, with keys `"method"`, `"target"`, `"headers"` (lower-case names) and `"body"`. `files` maps a path such as `"/data/orbit.csv"` to a pair `(content_bytes, etag)`, where `etag` is a string like `'"v3"'`.

Return a tuple `(status, headers, body)`, where `headers` is a list of `(name, value)` pairs in the order given here:

1. The path is the target up to any `?` (the query is ignored).
2. If the method is not `GET` or `HEAD`: `(405, [("Allow", "GET, HEAD")], b"")`.
3. If the path is not in `files`: `(404, [], b"not found")`.
4. If the request has an `if-none-match` header, and one of its comma-separated values (spaces stripped) equals the file's etag, or is `*`: `(304, [("ETag", etag)], b"")`.
5. Otherwise `(200, [("ETag", etag)], content)` for `GET`. For `HEAD`, the same status and headers plus `("Content-Length", str(len(content)))` at the end, and an empty body, since HEAD must say how big the body would be without sending it.
--- starter
def respond(request, files):
    content, etag = files[request["target"]]
    return (200, [("ETag", etag)], content)
--- solution
def respond(request, files):
    path = request["target"].split("?", 1)[0]
    method = request["method"]
    if method not in ("GET", "HEAD"):
        return (405, [("Allow", "GET, HEAD")], b"")
    if path not in files:
        return (404, [], b"not found")
    content, etag = files[path]
    wanted = request["headers"].get("if-none-match")
    if wanted is not None:
        tags = [t.strip() for t in wanted.split(",")]
        if etag in tags or "*" in tags:
            return (304, [("ETag", etag)], b"")
    if method == "HEAD":
        return (200, [("ETag", etag), ("Content-Length", str(len(content)))], b"")
    return (200, [("ETag", etag)], content)
--- hint
Follow the steps in order and return as soon as one applies. `target.split("?", 1)[0]` drops the query.
--- hint
`If-None-Match` can hold several tags separated by commas. Strip each one before comparing, and remember that `*` matches any existing file.
--- check case | A plain GET, with a query that is ignored
respond({"method": "GET", "target": "/data/orbit.csv?day=3", "headers": {}, "body": b""}, {"/data/orbit.csv": (b"t,alt\n0,408\n", '"v3"')})
=> (200, [("ETag", '"v3"')], b"t,alt\n0,408\n")
--- check case | Missing file, and a method that is not allowed
(respond({"method": "GET", "target": "/nope", "headers": {}, "body": b""}, {}), respond({"method": "POST", "target": "/nope", "headers": {}, "body": b"x"}, {}))
=> ((404, [], b"not found"), (405, [("Allow", "GET, HEAD")], b""))
--- check case | The client already has this version
respond({"method": "GET", "target": "/a", "headers": {"if-none-match": '"v1", "v2"'}, "body": b""}, {"/a": (b"data", '"v2"')})
=> (304, [("ETag", '"v2"')], b"")
--- check case | The client has an old version
respond({"method": "GET", "target": "/a", "headers": {"if-none-match": '"v1"'}, "body": b""}, {"/a": (b"data", '"v2"')})
=> (200, [("ETag", '"v2"')], b"data")
--- check case | A star matches any file that exists, but not a missing one
(respond({"method": "GET", "target": "/a", "headers": {"if-none-match": "*"}, "body": b""}, {"/a": (b"x", '"q"')})[0], respond({"method": "GET", "target": "/b", "headers": {"if-none-match": "*"}, "body": b""}, {"/a": (b"x", '"q"')})[0])
=> (304, 404)
--- check case | HEAD gives the length without the body
respond({"method": "HEAD", "target": "/big", "headers": {}, "body": b""}, {"/big": (bytes(5000), '"z"')})
=> (200, [("ETag", '"z"'), ("Content-Length", "5000")], b"")

+++ question | Why Host is required
--- ask
HTTP/1.1 made the `Host` header required. What problem does it solve?
--- choice
It lets routers find the server, since the IP header has no destination.
--- choice correct
One IP address often serves many websites; the request line has only the path, so the server needs Host to know which site's `/index.html` is meant.
--- choice
It tells the server which DNS resolver the client used.
--- choice
It replaces the TCP port number.
--- why
DNS maps many names to the same address, and by the time the request arrives the name is gone: only the address and port reached the server. Host carries the name the user typed. TLS has the same need earlier, and sends the name in its ClientHello.

+++ question | Which request may be retried
--- ask
A client sends a request, and the connection breaks before any response arrives. The client cannot tell whether the server acted. For which request is it always safe to send it again?
--- choice
`POST /commands` with the body `fire thruster 2 for 3 s`.
--- choice correct
`PUT /config/antenna` with the body `mode=high-gain`.
--- choice
`POST /payments` with the body `amount=100`.
--- choice
None: no HTTP request can ever be repeated safely.
--- why
PUT is idempotent: storing the same thing twice leaves the same state as storing it once. POST is not, so repeating a POST can fire the thruster twice or pay twice. APIs that need safe retries of POST give each request a unique ID and remember the ones they have done, which is exactly the at-most-once idea from lesson 6.

+++ question | Head-of-line blocking
--- ask
A client pipelines three requests on one HTTP/1.1 connection: a large report that takes the server 2 seconds to build, then two small icons that take 1 ms each. When can the client have the icons?
--- choice
After about 1 ms, because the server sends each response as soon as it is ready.
--- choice correct
Only after the report has been sent, because HTTP/1.1 responses must come back in the order of the requests.
--- choice
Never: pipelined requests after a slow one are dropped.
--- choice
After about 1 ms, because TCP reorders the responses.
--- why
HTTP/1.1 responses carry no request number, so the only way to match them to requests is their order. The slow first response blocks everything behind it: head-of-line blocking. HTTP/2 numbers its streams so responses can interleave, and HTTP/3 removes the blocking caused by TCP's own ordering as well.

=== net-13 | A client and a server: sockets, byte streams and serving many clients
--- teach
Last lesson you took HTTP messages apart and put them together. This lesson builds the two programs at the ends of the wire: a **server** that waits for connections and answers requests, and a **client** that connects and asks. You will see Python's real socket API, learn the three facts about byte streams that trip up everyone the first time, and build an HTTP server that serves several clients at once, over an in-memory network that behaves like TCP.

### A restaurant

Picture a restaurant. A host stands at the door. When guests arrive, the host seats them at a table and gives them their own waiter, then goes straight back to the door for the next guests. Each waiter takes orders from one table, brings the food, and takes more orders until the guests leave.

A TCP server works the same way. One **listening socket** waits at a port, the door. Each time a client connects, it produces a **new socket** for that one connection, the waiter, and goes back to waiting. Each connection socket carries one conversation until one side closes it.

### The real socket API: a server

On a real computer, a Python server that answers one connection at a time looks like this. It needs a real network, so it is shown but does not run here.

```python fragment
import socket

srv = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM)     # IPv4, byte stream: TCP
srv.setsockopt(socket.SOL_SOCKET, socket.SO_REUSEADDR, 1)   # allow a quick restart
srv.bind(("127.0.0.1", 8080))                               # this computer only, port 8080
srv.listen()                                                # become a listening socket
while True:
    conn, addr = srv.accept()        # wait for a client; conn is the new socket
    with conn:
        data = conn.recv(4096)       # up to 4096 bytes: maybe less!
        conn.sendall(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: 2\r\n\r\nhi")
```

- `SOCK_STREAM` asks for a byte stream, which is TCP. Last time `SOCK_DGRAM` asked for UDP.
- `bind` picks the address and port. `127.0.0.1` is **localhost**: this computer talking to itself, never leaving the machine.
- `listen` turns the socket into a listening socket. From now on the operating system completes three-way handshakes on its own, and keeps finished connections in a queue, the **[[backlog|backlog]]**.
- `accept` takes the next finished connection from that queue, or waits for one. It returns a brand-new socket for it, and the client's address.
- `SO_REUSEADDR` lets a restarted server bind its port again at once, while old connections are still in [[TIME-WAIT|reuse]] from lesson 9.

### The real socket API: a client

```python fragment
import socket

conn = socket.create_connection(("127.0.0.1", 8080))   # socket() and connect(): the handshake
conn.sendall(b"GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: localhost\r\nConnection: close\r\n\r\n")
reply = b""
while True:
    piece = conn.recv(4096)
    if not piece:                 # b"": the server closed its side
        break
    reply += piece
conn.close()
```

`connect` performs the three-way handshake, so it takes one round trip. The operating system picks an ephemeral port for the client, as in lesson 6.

### Three facts about byte streams

These three are behind most network bugs.

1. **`recv(n)` returns whatever has arrived, from 1 up to n bytes.** TCP carries a stream, not messages (lesson 9). A 300-byte request may come as 300 bytes, as 1 + 299, or as 100 pieces of 3. So a program keeps a **buffer**: it adds each piece to it, and takes out a message only once the whole message is there, using the framing rules of lesson 12.
2. **`recv` returning `b""` means the other side has closed its sending direction.** That is the FIN of lesson 9, called **end of file** (EOF). There will never be more data. A loop that ignores this and keeps calling `recv` spins forever.
3. **`send(data)` may send only part of `data`.** It returns how many bytes it took, when the operating system's buffer is nearly full. `sendall` loops until everything is taken. Use `sendall`, or loop yourself.

Here is fact 1 with a list of pieces standing in for the network, and a buffer that finds complete lines:

```python
pieces = [b"TEMP 2", b"1.5\nPRES", b"S 101", b"3\n"]
buf = b""
for piece in pieces:
    buf += piece
    while b"\n" in buf:
        line, buf = buf.split(b"\n", 1)
        print("complete:", line)
print("left over:", buf)
```

### Serving many clients at once

The server above handles one client at a time. While it waits in `recv` for a slow client, every other client waits too. Real servers use one of these:

- **A thread per connection.** Each connection gets its own thread, which can wait in `recv` without holding up the others:

```python fragment
import threading

def serve(conn):
    with conn:
        ...                                   # recv, answer, repeat

while True:
    conn, addr = srv.accept()
    threading.Thread(target=serve, args=(conn,)).start()
```

- **Non-blocking sockets and an event loop.** After `conn.setblocking(False)`, a `recv` with no data does not wait: it raises **`BlockingIOError`**, meaning "nothing yet, try later". One thread keeps many connections, and asks the operating system which ones are ready (the `selectors` module, or `asyncio`, which builds on it). This is how [[servers with thousands of clients|c10k]] work.

### Sockets in ORBIT

Real sockets cannot run in ORBIT, so this lesson's starter contains an in-memory network that behaves like TCP, in the non-blocking style:

| real API | ORBIT's simulation |
|---|---|
| `socket`, `bind`, `listen` | `listener = net.listen(ip, port)` |
| `srv.accept()` | `listener.accept()` returns `(conn, addr)`, or raises `BlockingIOError` if nobody is waiting |
| `socket.create_connection(addr)` | `conn = net.connect(my_ip, addr)`; `ConnectionRefusedError` if nobody listens there |
| `conn.recv(n)` | up to `n` bytes, and never more than the network's `chunk`; `b""` at EOF; `BlockingIOError` if nothing has arrived yet |
| `conn.sendall(data)` | delivers `data` to the other side's buffer |
| `conn.shutdown(socket.SHUT_WR)` | `conn.shutdown_write()`: send EOF, but keep receiving |
| `conn.close()` | `conn.close()` |

`StreamNetwork(chunk)` sets the largest piece one `recv` returns, so a check can cut every message into 1-byte pieces and make sure your buffering is right.

### Generators as threads

The server and client must take turns, and a generator is a function that can pause, as in the Operating Systems course. Each task is a generator. When it would have to wait, because `recv` or `accept` raised `BlockingIOError`, it runs `yield`: "I cannot go on yet; let someone else run". The starter's `run(*tasks)` is a **round-robin scheduler**: it calls `next` on each task in turn, until all have finished.

Two more rules of `run`:

- A task can **start a new task** by yielding a generator object instead of nothing: `yield handle(conn, routes)`. That is how a server gives each connection its own "thread".
- When a task finishes, its `return` value becomes the `value` of the `StopIteration` that `next` raised, and `run` returns the values of the tasks it was given, in order.

A tiny scheduler of the same kind, on its own:

```python
from collections import deque

def counter(name, n):
    for i in range(n):
        print(name, i)
        yield
    return name + " done"

tasks = deque([counter("a", 2), counter("b", 3)])
while tasks:
    task = tasks.popleft()
    try:
        next(task)
        tasks.append(task)
    except StopIteration as stop:
        print(stop.value)
```

The lines come out interleaved: a 0, b 0, a 1, b 1, then "a done", b 2, "b done".

### Why the connection handler is correct

A connection handler keeps a buffer and loops: take every complete request out of the buffer and answer it, then read more. The invariant is: **the buffer holds exactly the bytes received and not yet used by an answered request**, starting at the first byte of the next request.

- At the start the buffer is empty, and nothing has been received.
- Each pass hands the buffer to the parser from lesson 12. Its framing argument says it removes exactly the complete requests at the front, and leaves the rest untouched. Those requests are answered in order, and what is left starts at the first byte of the next request.
- Adding a piece from `recv` to the end keeps the invariant, because TCP delivers the bytes in order.

So every request is answered exactly once, in the order it was sent, however the stream was cut into pieces. The handler stops when a request asks to close the connection, or at EOF. A handler that forgot EOF would call `recv` forever.

One way two correct programs can still hang: each waits for the other. A client that sends `Content-Length: 10` with only 8 bytes of body waits for a response, while the server waits for the last 2 bytes. That is a **protocol deadlock**, and real servers break it with timeouts.

### What it costs

- **Per connection**, a server keeps a socket, a buffer and a task. An event loop keeps only those; a thread per connection also keeps a stack of its own, often reserved at megabytes, and the operating system must switch between threads.
- **Per request**, parsing and answering are O(n) in the bytes, if the buffer is not rescanned from the start each time.
- **Per new connection**, the client pays one round trip for the handshake before its first request, which keep-alive (lesson 12) pays only once.
- **Round-robin scheduling** gives every task a turn, so one slow client costs the others only the turns it takes, not the time it spends waiting.

### Where it is used

- **Every web server.** nginx runs a small number of event loops; Apache classically ran a process or thread per connection. Python's own [[http.server|httpserver]] module has both styles.
- **Python's asyncio** is this lesson's scheduler, grown up: `await` plays the part of `yield`, and the event loop asks the operating system which sockets are ready.
- **Ground systems** run servers that many operators' consoles connect to at once, each one receiving the telemetry it subscribed to.

**Watch out:** the classic socket bugs.

- Assuming one `recv` is one message. Test with tiny pieces, which is what `StreamNetwork(1)` is for.
- Ignoring `b""`. At EOF, `recv` returns `b""` immediately, forever, so a loop that does not stop spins at full speed.
- Using `send` and ignoring its return value. Use `sendall`.
- Never closing sockets. Each open socket uses a file descriptor, and a server that leaks them eventually cannot accept anything.

::: context backlog The accept queue
The operating system finishes handshakes even while the server is busy, and parks the new connections in a queue until the program calls `accept`. `listen(n)` suggests how long that queue may be. When it is full, new SYNs are dropped and the clients retry after a timeout, so a server that accepts too slowly makes new clients wait seconds. A SYN flood fills a related queue of half-open connections on purpose; SYN cookies let the server avoid storing anything until the third handshake message arrives.
:::

::: context reuse Why a restart says "address already in use"
When a server closes its connections, the server's side is often the one that sent the first FIN, so those connections sit in TIME-WAIT for up to a few minutes. A new server trying to bind the same port is then refused, even though nothing is listening. `SO_REUSEADDR` tells the operating system that binding is fine as long as no socket is actually listening there. Almost every server sets it before `bind`.
:::

::: context c10k Ten thousand clients
In 1999 Dan Kegel wrote "The C10K problem", asking how one server could handle ten thousand clients at once, when a thread per connection needed too much memory and switching. The answer was non-blocking sockets and event notification: `epoll` on Linux and `kqueue` on BSD tell a program which of thousands of sockets are ready, in time proportional to the ready ones. Servers such as nginx, and Python's asyncio, are built on them. Today's servers handle millions of connections this way.
:::

::: context httpserver Python's own web server
The standard library includes `http.server`. Running `python -m http.server 8000` serves the files in the current folder at `http://localhost:8000`. Its `HTTPServer` handles one connection at a time, and `ThreadingHTTPServer` starts a thread per connection. It is meant for testing and teaching, not for the open Internet: it does no security checks beyond the basics, which is why real services put nginx or a proper framework in front.
:::
--- task
The starter holds the in-memory network (`StreamNetwork`), the scheduler `run`, last lesson's `parse_requests`, a dict `REASONS` of reason phrases, and `sample_routes()`, which the checks use. Keep them. Write three generator functions.

`routes` maps a path to a dict from method to a function. That function takes the request body and returns `(status, content_type, body)`.

**1. `handle(conn, routes)`** serves one connection. Keep a buffer of received bytes, and repeat:

- Pass the buffer to `parse_requests`. If it raises `ValueError`, send a 400 response with content type `text/plain` and body `b"bad request"`, marked as closing, then close the connection and return.
- Answer every complete request, in order. The path is the target up to any `?`. An unknown path gets 404, `text/plain`, `b"not found"`. A known path with no function for the method gets 405, `text/plain`, `b"method not allowed"`. Otherwise call the function with the request's body.
- A response is the line `HTTP/1.1 <status> <reason>`, then the headers `Content-Type`, `Content-Length` and, only if the connection is closing, `Connection: close`, each line ended by `\r\n`; then an empty line and the body.
- The connection closes after a request whose `connection` header has the token `close`, or an `HTTP/1.0` request without the token `keep-alive`. Send that request's response, close, and return.
- Then read more with `recv(4096)`. On `BlockingIOError`, `yield` and go round again. On `b""` (EOF), close the connection and return.

`handle` returns the number of requests it answered.

**2. `http_server(listener, routes, connections)`** accepts `connections` connections, one after another. When `accept` raises `BlockingIOError`, `yield`. For each connection, start a handler task by yielding `handle(conn, routes)`. After the last one, close the listener and return the number accepted.

**3. `http_client(net, ip, server, requests)`** connects from `ip` to `server`, an `(ip, port)` pair. `requests` is a list of `(method, path, body)`. For each one, send the request line `<method> <path> HTTP/1.1`, a `Host` header with the server's IP, a `Content-Length` header only if the body is not empty, and `Connection: close` on the last request only; then the empty line and the body. Then read until the whole response has arrived (the headers, then `Content-Length` bytes of body, or no body without that header), yielding on `BlockingIOError` and raising `ConnectionError` if EOF comes first. Record `(status, body)`, with the status as an `int`. If the response has a `Connection: close` header, send nothing more. At the end, close the socket and return the list of `(status, body)` pairs.

For example, with `net = StreamNetwork(3)` and `listener = net.listen("10.0.0.1", 80)`, the call `run(http_server(listener, sample_routes(), 1), http_client(net, "10.0.0.2", ("10.0.0.1", 80), [("GET", "/hello", b"")]))` returns `[1, [(200, b"hello, orbit")]]`.
--- starter
from collections import deque


class StreamNetwork:
    def __init__(self, chunk=1024):
        self.chunk = chunk
        self.listeners = {}
        self.next_port = 49152

    def listen(self, ip, port):
        if (ip, port) in self.listeners:
            raise OSError("address already in use")
        sock = ListeningSocket(self, (ip, port))
        self.listeners[(ip, port)] = sock
        return sock

    def connect(self, ip, addr):
        listener = self.listeners.get(tuple(addr))
        if listener is None:
            raise ConnectionRefusedError("nobody is listening there")
        local = (ip, self.next_port)
        self.next_port += 1
        client = StreamSocket(self, local, tuple(addr))
        server = StreamSocket(self, tuple(addr), local)
        client.peer, server.peer = server, client
        listener.pending.append((server, local))
        return client


class ListeningSocket:
    def __init__(self, net, addr):
        self.net = net
        self.addr = addr
        self.pending = deque()

    def accept(self):
        if not self.pending:
            raise BlockingIOError("no connection is waiting")
        return self.pending.popleft()

    def close(self):
        self.net.listeners.pop(self.addr, None)


class StreamSocket:
    def __init__(self, net, local, remote):
        self.net = net
        self.local = local
        self.remote = remote
        self.peer = None
        self.inbox = bytearray()
        self.eof = False
        self.can_send = True
        self.closed = False

    def sendall(self, data):
        if not self.can_send:
            raise OSError("this socket can no longer send")
        if self.peer.closed:
            raise BrokenPipeError("the other side has closed")
        self.peer.inbox += data

    def recv(self, n):
        if self.closed:
            raise OSError("this socket is closed")
        if self.inbox:
            size = min(n, self.net.chunk, len(self.inbox))
            data = bytes(self.inbox[:size])
            del self.inbox[:size]
            return data
        if self.eof:
            return b""
        raise BlockingIOError("no data yet")

    def shutdown_write(self):
        if self.can_send:
            self.can_send = False
            self.peer.eof = True

    def close(self):
        self.shutdown_write()
        self.closed = True


def run(*tasks, limit=100000):
    results = [None] * len(tasks)
    queue = deque(enumerate(tasks))
    steps = 0
    while queue:
        steps += 1
        if steps > limit:
            raise RuntimeError("the tasks never finished")
        i, task = queue.popleft()
        try:
            new = next(task)
        except StopIteration as stop:
            if i is not None:
                results[i] = stop.value
            continue
        if new is not None:
            queue.append((None, new))
        queue.append((i, task))
    return results


def parse_requests(data):
    requests = []
    pos = 0
    while True:
        end = data.find(b"\r\n\r\n", pos)
        if end < 0:
            break
        lines = data[pos:end].decode("latin-1").split("\r\n")
        parts = lines[0].split(" ")
        if len(parts) != 3:
            raise ValueError("bad request line")
        method, target, version = parts
        if not (method.isascii() and method.isalpha() and method.isupper()):
            raise ValueError("bad method")
        if not target or version not in ("HTTP/1.0", "HTTP/1.1"):
            raise ValueError("bad target or version")
        headers = {}
        for line in lines[1:]:
            if line[:1] in (" ", "\t"):
                raise ValueError("folded header line")
            name, colon, value = line.partition(":")
            if not colon or not name or " " in name or "\t" in name:
                raise ValueError("bad header line")
            key = name.lower()
            value = value.strip(" \t")
            headers[key] = headers[key] + ", " + value if key in headers else value
        if version == "HTTP/1.1" and "host" not in headers:
            raise ValueError("HTTP/1.1 needs a Host header")
        if "transfer-encoding" in headers:
            raise ValueError("transfer encodings are not supported")
        length = 0
        if "content-length" in headers:
            copies = {v.strip(" \t") for v in headers["content-length"].split(",")}
            if len(copies) != 1:
                raise ValueError("conflicting lengths")
            text = copies.pop()
            if not (text.isascii() and text.isdigit()):
                raise ValueError("bad length")
            length = int(text)
        start = end + 4
        if len(data) - start < length:
            break
        requests.append({"method": method, "target": target, "version": version, "headers": headers, "body": data[start:start + length]})
        pos = start + length
    return (requests, data[pos:])


REASONS = {200: "OK", 201: "Created", 400: "Bad Request", 404: "Not Found", 405: "Method Not Allowed"}


def sample_routes():
    log = []

    def add_entry(body):
        log.append(body)
        return (201, "text/plain", str(len(log)).encode())

    return {
        "/hello": {"GET": lambda body: (200, "text/plain", b"hello, orbit")},
        "/echo": {"POST": lambda body: (200, "application/octet-stream", body)},
        "/log": {"POST": add_entry, "GET": lambda body: (200, "text/plain", b"\n".join(log))},
    }


def handle(conn, routes):
    return 0
    yield


def http_server(listener, routes, connections):
    return 0
    yield


def http_client(net, ip, server, requests):
    return []
    yield
--- solution
from collections import deque


class StreamNetwork:
    def __init__(self, chunk=1024):
        self.chunk = chunk
        self.listeners = {}
        self.next_port = 49152

    def listen(self, ip, port):
        if (ip, port) in self.listeners:
            raise OSError("address already in use")
        sock = ListeningSocket(self, (ip, port))
        self.listeners[(ip, port)] = sock
        return sock

    def connect(self, ip, addr):
        listener = self.listeners.get(tuple(addr))
        if listener is None:
            raise ConnectionRefusedError("nobody is listening there")
        local = (ip, self.next_port)
        self.next_port += 1
        client = StreamSocket(self, local, tuple(addr))
        server = StreamSocket(self, tuple(addr), local)
        client.peer, server.peer = server, client
        listener.pending.append((server, local))
        return client


class ListeningSocket:
    def __init__(self, net, addr):
        self.net = net
        self.addr = addr
        self.pending = deque()

    def accept(self):
        if not self.pending:
            raise BlockingIOError("no connection is waiting")
        return self.pending.popleft()

    def close(self):
        self.net.listeners.pop(self.addr, None)


class StreamSocket:
    def __init__(self, net, local, remote):
        self.net = net
        self.local = local
        self.remote = remote
        self.peer = None
        self.inbox = bytearray()
        self.eof = False
        self.can_send = True
        self.closed = False

    def sendall(self, data):
        if not self.can_send:
            raise OSError("this socket can no longer send")
        if self.peer.closed:
            raise BrokenPipeError("the other side has closed")
        self.peer.inbox += data

    def recv(self, n):
        if self.closed:
            raise OSError("this socket is closed")
        if self.inbox:
            size = min(n, self.net.chunk, len(self.inbox))
            data = bytes(self.inbox[:size])
            del self.inbox[:size]
            return data
        if self.eof:
            return b""
        raise BlockingIOError("no data yet")

    def shutdown_write(self):
        if self.can_send:
            self.can_send = False
            self.peer.eof = True

    def close(self):
        self.shutdown_write()
        self.closed = True


def run(*tasks, limit=100000):
    results = [None] * len(tasks)
    queue = deque(enumerate(tasks))
    steps = 0
    while queue:
        steps += 1
        if steps > limit:
            raise RuntimeError("the tasks never finished")
        i, task = queue.popleft()
        try:
            new = next(task)
        except StopIteration as stop:
            if i is not None:
                results[i] = stop.value
            continue
        if new is not None:
            queue.append((None, new))
        queue.append((i, task))
    return results


def parse_requests(data):
    requests = []
    pos = 0
    while True:
        end = data.find(b"\r\n\r\n", pos)
        if end < 0:
            break
        lines = data[pos:end].decode("latin-1").split("\r\n")
        parts = lines[0].split(" ")
        if len(parts) != 3:
            raise ValueError("bad request line")
        method, target, version = parts
        if not (method.isascii() and method.isalpha() and method.isupper()):
            raise ValueError("bad method")
        if not target or version not in ("HTTP/1.0", "HTTP/1.1"):
            raise ValueError("bad target or version")
        headers = {}
        for line in lines[1:]:
            if line[:1] in (" ", "\t"):
                raise ValueError("folded header line")
            name, colon, value = line.partition(":")
            if not colon or not name or " " in name or "\t" in name:
                raise ValueError("bad header line")
            key = name.lower()
            value = value.strip(" \t")
            headers[key] = headers[key] + ", " + value if key in headers else value
        if version == "HTTP/1.1" and "host" not in headers:
            raise ValueError("HTTP/1.1 needs a Host header")
        if "transfer-encoding" in headers:
            raise ValueError("transfer encodings are not supported")
        length = 0
        if "content-length" in headers:
            copies = {v.strip(" \t") for v in headers["content-length"].split(",")}
            if len(copies) != 1:
                raise ValueError("conflicting lengths")
            text = copies.pop()
            if not (text.isascii() and text.isdigit()):
                raise ValueError("bad length")
            length = int(text)
        start = end + 4
        if len(data) - start < length:
            break
        requests.append({"method": method, "target": target, "version": version, "headers": headers, "body": data[start:start + length]})
        pos = start + length
    return (requests, data[pos:])


REASONS = {200: "OK", 201: "Created", 400: "Bad Request", 404: "Not Found", 405: "Method Not Allowed"}


def sample_routes():
    log = []

    def add_entry(body):
        log.append(body)
        return (201, "text/plain", str(len(log)).encode())

    return {
        "/hello": {"GET": lambda body: (200, "text/plain", b"hello, orbit")},
        "/echo": {"POST": lambda body: (200, "application/octet-stream", body)},
        "/log": {"POST": add_entry, "GET": lambda body: (200, "text/plain", b"\n".join(log))},
    }


def response_bytes(status, content_type, body, close):
    lines = [f"HTTP/1.1 {status} {REASONS[status]}", f"Content-Type: {content_type}", f"Content-Length: {len(body)}"]
    if close:
        lines.append("Connection: close")
    return ("\r\n".join(lines) + "\r\n\r\n").encode("latin-1") + body


def wants_close(request):
    tokens = {t.strip().lower() for t in request["headers"].get("connection", "").split(",")}
    return "close" in tokens or (request["version"] == "HTTP/1.0" and "keep-alive" not in tokens)


def handle(conn, routes):
    buf = b""
    served = 0
    while True:
        try:
            requests, buf = parse_requests(buf)
        except ValueError:
            conn.sendall(response_bytes(400, "text/plain", b"bad request", True))
            conn.close()
            return served
        for request in requests:
            path = request["target"].split("?", 1)[0]
            methods = routes.get(path)
            if methods is None:
                status, kind, body = 404, "text/plain", b"not found"
            elif request["method"] not in methods:
                status, kind, body = 405, "text/plain", b"method not allowed"
            else:
                status, kind, body = methods[request["method"]](request["body"])
            close = wants_close(request)
            conn.sendall(response_bytes(status, kind, body, close))
            served += 1
            if close:
                conn.close()
                return served
        try:
            data = conn.recv(4096)
        except BlockingIOError:
            yield
            continue
        if not data:
            conn.close()
            return served
        buf += data


def http_server(listener, routes, connections):
    accepted = 0
    while accepted < connections:
        try:
            conn, _ = listener.accept()
        except BlockingIOError:
            yield
            continue
        accepted += 1
        yield handle(conn, routes)
    listener.close()
    return accepted


def http_client(net, ip, server, requests):
    conn = net.connect(ip, server)
    results = []
    buf = b""
    for k, (method, path, body) in enumerate(requests):
        lines = [f"{method} {path} HTTP/1.1", f"Host: {server[0]}"]
        if body:
            lines.append(f"Content-Length: {len(body)}")
        if k == len(requests) - 1:
            lines.append("Connection: close")
        conn.sendall(("\r\n".join(lines) + "\r\n\r\n").encode("latin-1") + body)
        while True:
            end = buf.find(b"\r\n\r\n")
            if end >= 0:
                head = buf[:end].decode("latin-1").split("\r\n")
                headers = {}
                for line in head[1:]:
                    name, _, value = line.partition(":")
                    headers[name.strip().lower()] = value.strip()
                length = int(headers.get("content-length", "0"))
                if len(buf) >= end + 4 + length:
                    results.append((int(head[0].split(" ")[1]), buf[end + 4:end + 4 + length]))
                    buf = buf[end + 4 + length:]
                    break
            try:
                data = conn.recv(4096)
            except BlockingIOError:
                yield
                continue
            if not data:
                raise ConnectionError("the server closed the connection early")
            buf += data
        if headers.get("connection", "").lower() == "close":
            break
    conn.close()
    return results
--- hint
In `handle`, the loop body has three parts: parse (catching `ValueError`), answer every request in the list the parser returned, then `recv` one piece. `yield` only in the `except BlockingIOError` branch. A small helper that builds a response from `(status, content_type, body, closing)` keeps it short.
--- hint
`http_server` is a loop around `listener.accept()`, with `yield` when it raises. After accepting, `yield handle(conn, routes)`: yielding a generator object asks `run` to start it as a new task.
--- hint
In `http_client`, after sending each request, loop: look for `b"\r\n\r\n"` in the buffer; if found, read the headers and the Content-Length, and if the whole body is there too, record the response, cut it off the front of the buffer, and stop looping. Otherwise `recv` another piece.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda net: (lambda lst: run(http_server(lst, sample_routes(), 1), http_client(net, "10.0.0.2", ("10.0.0.1", 80), [("GET", "/hello", b"")])))(net.listen("10.0.0.1", 80)))(StreamNetwork(3))
=> [1, [(200, b"hello, orbit")]]
--- check case | Six requests on one kept-alive connection, with shared state, 404 and 405
(lambda net: (lambda lst: run(http_server(lst, sample_routes(), 1), http_client(net, "10.0.0.2", ("10.0.0.1", 80), [("GET", "/hello", b""), ("POST", "/log", b"first"), ("POST", "/log", b"second"), ("GET", "/log", b""), ("GET", "/nope", b""), ("DELETE", "/hello", b"")])))(net.listen("10.0.0.1", 80)))(StreamNetwork(3))
=> [1, [(200, b"hello, orbit"), (201, b"1"), (201, b"2"), (200, b"first\nsecond"), (404, b"not found"), (405, b"method not allowed")]]
--- check case | Every byte arrives on its own
(lambda net: (lambda lst: run(http_server(lst, sample_routes(), 1), http_client(net, "c", ("s", 8080), [("POST", "/echo", bytes(range(256)) + b"end"), ("GET", "/hello?lang=en", b"")])))(net.listen("s", 8080)))(StreamNetwork(1))

=> (1, 0)
--- check case | At EOF the handler closes and returns how many requests it answered
(lambda net: (lambda lst, c: (lambda s: (c.sendall(b"GET /hello HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n\r\nGET /hello HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\n\r\n"), c.shutdown_write(), run(handle(s, sample_routes())), s.closed)[2:])(lst.accept()[0]))(net.listen("h", 80), net.connect("c", ("h", 80))))(StreamNetwork(6))
=> ([2], True)
--- check case | The client stops sending after a response that closes the connection
(lambda net: (lambda lst: run(http_server(lst, sample_routes(), 1), http_client(net, "c", ("h", 80), [("get", "/hello", b""), ("GET", "/hello", b"")])))(net.listen("h", 80)))(StreamNetwork(3))
