+++ practice | Reading exactly n bytes
--- task
Because `recv` may return fewer bytes than asked for, protocols with fixed-size fields need a helper that keeps reading. The starter has `PieceSocket(data, chunk)`, a socket whose `recv(n)` returns at most `min(n, chunk)` bytes of `data`, then `b""` forever once `data` runs out. Its attribute `calls` counts the calls to `recv`. Keep it.

Write two functions.

- `recv_exactly(sock, n)` returns exactly `n` bytes. Each call to `recv` asks for the number of bytes still needed. For `n = 0`, return `b""` without calling `recv`. If `recv` returns `b""` before `n` bytes have arrived, raise `ConnectionError`.
- `read_record(sock)` reads one record: a 2-byte big-endian length, then that many bytes, and returns the bytes. If the stream is already at its end before the first byte of the length, return `None` (a clean end). If it ends anywhere inside a record, raise `ConnectionError`.

For example, with `sock = PieceSocket(b"\x00\x03abc\x00\x01z", 2)`, `read_record(sock)` is `b"abc"`, the next call is `b"z"`, and the one after that is `None`.
--- starter
class PieceSocket:
    def __init__(self, data, chunk):
        self.data = data
        self.chunk = chunk
        self.calls = 0

    def recv(self, n):
        self.calls += 1
        piece = self.data[:min(n, self.chunk)]
        self.data = self.data[len(piece):]
        return piece


def recv_exactly(sock, n):
    return sock.recv(n)


def read_record(sock):
    size = int.from_bytes(sock.recv(2), "big")
    return sock.recv(size)
--- solution
class PieceSocket:
    def __init__(self, data, chunk):
        self.data = data
        self.chunk = chunk
        self.calls = 0

    def recv(self, n):
        self.calls += 1
        piece = self.data[:min(n, self.chunk)]
        self.data = self.data[len(piece):]
        return piece


def recv_exactly(sock, n):
    buf = b""
    while len(buf) < n:
        piece = sock.recv(n - len(buf))
        if not piece:
            raise ConnectionError("the stream ended early")
        buf += piece
    return buf


def read_record(sock):
    first = sock.recv(1)
    if not first:
        return None
    size = int.from_bytes(first + recv_exactly(sock, 1), "big")
    return recv_exactly(sock, size)
--- hint
Loop while you have fewer than `n` bytes, asking `recv` for `n - len(buf)` each time. An empty piece means the stream ended: raise.
--- hint
To tell a clean end from a cut-off record, read the first byte of the length on its own. Empty means clean end; otherwise read the second byte and the body with `recv_exactly`.
--- check case | recv_exactly gathers pieces, asking only for what is missing
(lambda s: (recv_exactly(s, 7), s.calls, s.data))(PieceSocket(b"telemetry", 3))
=> (b"telemet", 3, b"ry")
--- check case | Zero bytes need no recv at all
(lambda s: (recv_exactly(s, 0), s.calls))(PieceSocket(b"abc", 1))
=> (b"", 0)
--- check test | The stream ending early raises
raises(ConnectionError, lambda: recv_exactly(PieceSocket(b"abc", 2), 4)) and raises(ConnectionError, lambda: recv_exactly(PieceSocket(b"", 2), 1))
--- check case | The example: two records, then a clean end
(lambda s: (read_record(s), read_record(s), read_record(s)))(PieceSocket(b"\x00\x03abc\x00\x01z", 2))
=> (b"abc", b"z", None)
--- check case | A 300-byte record read one byte at a time, and an empty record
(lambda s: (len(read_record(s)), read_record(s), read_record(s)))(PieceSocket(b"\x01\x2c" + bytes(300) + b"\x00\x00", 1))
=> (300, b"", None)
--- check test | A record cut off in its length or its body raises
raises(ConnectionError, lambda: read_record(PieceSocket(b"\x00", 5))) and raises(ConnectionError, lambda: read_record(PieceSocket(b"\x00\x05abc", 5)))

+++ practice | Sending all of it
--- task
A socket's `send(data)` may take only part of `data`, and returns how many bytes it took. The starter has `SlowSocket(limits)`, whose i-th call to `send` takes at most `limits[i % len(limits)]` bytes (the list repeats). What it took is added to its attribute `sent`, and `calls` counts the calls. Keep it.

Write `send_all(sock, data)` that calls `send` until every byte of `data` has been taken, each time passing only the bytes not yet taken, and returns the number of calls it made. For empty `data`, make no calls and return 0. If `send` takes 0 bytes while bytes remain, the connection is broken: raise `ConnectionError`.

For example, with `SlowSocket([3])`, `send_all(sock, b"abcdefgh")` returns 3, and `sock.sent` is `b"abcdefgh"`.
--- starter
class SlowSocket:
    def __init__(self, limits):
        self.limits = limits
        self.sent = b""
        self.calls = 0

    def send(self, data):
        limit = self.limits[self.calls % len(self.limits)]
        self.calls += 1
        taken = data[:limit]
        self.sent += taken
        return len(taken)


def send_all(sock, data):
    sock.send(data)
    return 1
--- solution
class SlowSocket:
    def __init__(self, limits):
        self.limits = limits
        self.sent = b""
        self.calls = 0

    def send(self, data):
        limit = self.limits[self.calls % len(self.limits)]
        self.calls += 1
        taken = data[:limit]
        self.sent += taken
        return len(taken)


def send_all(sock, data):
    calls = 0
    pos = 0
    while pos < len(data):
        taken = sock.send(data[pos:])
        calls += 1
        if taken == 0:
            raise ConnectionError("the connection took nothing")
        pos += taken
    return calls
--- hint
Keep a position: how many bytes have been taken so far. Each call sends `data[pos:]` and moves the position on by what `send` returned.
--- hint
Stop when the position reaches `len(data)`. A return of 0 with bytes still to go means the connection is broken.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda s: (send_all(s, b"abcdefgh"), s.sent))(SlowSocket([3]))
=> (3, b"abcdefgh")
--- check case | Changing limits
(lambda s: (send_all(s, bytes(range(20))), s.sent == bytes(range(20))))(SlowSocket([1, 5, 2]))
=> (8, True)
--- check case | A socket that takes everything at once, and nothing to send
(lambda s: (send_all(s, b"x" * 1000), send_all(s, b""), s.calls))(SlowSocket([4096]))
=> (1, 0, 1)
--- check test | A socket that stops taking bytes raises, after sending what it could
(lambda s: raises(ConnectionError, lambda: send_all(s, b"abcdef")) and s.sent == b"ab")(SlowSocket([2, 0]))

+++ practice | Messages on a stream
--- task
Many protocols built on TCP put a length in front of each message, like lesson 1's headers. Write a function and a class.

- `frame(message)` returns a 4-byte big-endian length followed by `message`.
- `MessageReader(max_size)` collects bytes from a stream. `feed(data)` adds the bytes `data` and returns the list of every message now complete, in order (often empty). If a length prefix says more than `max_size` bytes, raise `ValueError` as soon as the 4 bytes of that length have arrived, without waiting for the body: a reader must not let one bad length make it wait for, and store, gigabytes. `pending()` returns how many bytes are buffered but not yet part of a complete message.

For example, `r = MessageReader(100)`, then `r.feed(frame(b"hi")[:3])` is `[]`, and `r.feed(frame(b"hi")[3:] + frame(b"yo"))` is `[b"hi", b"yo"]`.
--- starter
def frame(message):
    return message


class MessageReader:
    def __init__(self, max_size):
        self.max_size = max_size

    def feed(self, data):
        return [data]

    def pending(self):
        return 0
--- solution
def frame(message):
    return len(message).to_bytes(4, "big") + message


class MessageReader:
    def __init__(self, max_size):
        self.max_size = max_size
        self.buf = b""

    def feed(self, data):
        self.buf += data
        out = []
        while len(self.buf) >= 4:
            size = int.from_bytes(self.buf[:4], "big")
            if size > self.max_size:
                raise ValueError("message too big")
            if len(self.buf) < 4 + size:
                break
            out.append(self.buf[4:4 + size])
            self.buf = self.buf[4 + size:]
        return out

    def pending(self):
        return len(self.buf)
--- hint
Keep one buffer. After adding the new bytes, loop: if at least 4 bytes are there, read the length; check it against `max_size` at once; if the whole message is there, cut it off and keep going.
--- hint
Stop the loop when fewer than 4 bytes remain, or when the body has not fully arrived. Whatever is left stays in the buffer for the next `feed`.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda r: (r.feed(frame(b"hi")[:3]), r.feed(frame(b"hi")[3:] + frame(b"yo")), r.pending()))(MessageReader(100))
=> ([], [b"hi", b"yo"], 0)
--- check case | frame itself, and an empty message
(frame(b"abc"), frame(b""))
=> (b"\x00\x00\x00\x03abc", b"\x00\x00\x00\x00")
--- check case | Byte by byte, with an empty message in the middle
(lambda r, data: [m for b in data for m in r.feed(bytes([b]))])(MessageReader(10), frame(b"one") + frame(b"") + frame(b"three"))
=> [b"one", b"", b"three"]
--- check case | pending counts the partial message
(lambda r: (r.feed(frame(b"abcdef")[:7] ), r.pending()))(MessageReader(10))
=> ([], 7)
--- check test | A length over the limit raises before the body arrives
(lambda r: r.feed(frame(b"ok")) == [b"ok"] and raises(ValueError, lambda: r.feed((11).to_bytes(4, "big"))))(MessageReader(10)) and MessageReader(10).feed(frame(b"x" * 10)) == [b"x" * 10]
--- check test | 100 random cuttings of 20 messages give back the messages
(lambda R: all((lambda msgs: (lambda data: (lambda cuts: (lambda r: [m for a, b in zip(cuts, cuts[1:]) for m in r.feed(data[a:b])] == msgs)(MessageReader(1000)))([0] + sorted(R.sample(range(1, len(data)), 15)) + [len(data)]))(b"".join(frame(m) for m in msgs)))([bytes(R.randrange(256) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 40))) for _ in range(20)]) for _ in range(100)))(__import__("random").Random(13))

+++ practice | When is a response complete?
--- task
A client reading a response must decide when it is complete, and the rules have edge cases. Write `split_response(buf, method, eof)`. `buf` is the bytes received so far, `method` is the request's method, and `eof` says whether the server has closed the connection. Return `(status, body, rest)` if a whole response is at the front of `buf`, where `rest` is whatever follows it, or `None` if more bytes are needed.

- The headers end at the first `\r\n\r\n`. The status is the second word of the first line, as an `int`. Header names are case-insensitive.
- There is **no body** if the method was `HEAD` (even if Content-Length says otherwise: it gives the size a GET would get), or if the status is 1xx, 204 or 304.
- Otherwise, with a `Content-Length` header, the body is exactly that many bytes.
- Otherwise the body is everything until the server closes: complete only when `eof` is true, and then the body is the rest of `buf` and `rest` is `b""`.

If `eof` is true and the response is still incomplete, raise `ConnectionError`.
--- starter
def split_response(buf, method, eof):
    head, _, body = buf.partition(b"\r\n\r\n")
    status = int(head.split(b" ")[1])
    return (status, body, b"")
--- solution
def split_response(buf, method, eof):
    end = buf.find(b"\r\n\r\n")
    if end < 0:
        if eof:
            raise ConnectionError("closed in the middle of the headers")
        return None
    lines = buf[:end].decode("latin-1").split("\r\n")
    status = int(lines[0].split(" ")[1])
    headers = {}
    for line in lines[1:]:
        name, _, value = line.partition(":")
        headers[name.strip().lower()] = value.strip()
    start = end + 4
    if method == "HEAD" or 100 <= status < 200 or status in (204, 304):
        return (status, b"", buf[start:])
    if "content-length" in headers:
        length = int(headers["content-length"])
        if len(buf) - start >= length:
            return (status, buf[start:start + length], buf[start + length:])
    elif eof:
        return (status, buf[start:], b"")
    if eof:
        raise ConnectionError("closed in the middle of the body")
    return None
--- hint
Decide in this order: headers complete? Then the no-body cases (HEAD, 1xx, 204, 304). Then Content-Length. Then read-until-close.
--- hint
Every "not complete yet" path ends the same way: raise if `eof`, else return `None`.
--- check case | A normal response followed by the next one
split_response(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: 2\r\n\r\nokHTTP/1.1 404", "GET", False)
=> (200, b"ok", b"HTTP/1.1 404")
--- check case | HEAD has no body, whatever Content-Length says
split_response(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\ncontent-length: 5000\r\n\r\nHTTP/1.1 200 OK", "HEAD", False)
=> (200, b"", b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK")
--- check case | 204, 304 and 100 have no body
(split_response(b"HTTP/1.1 204 No Content\r\n\r\nX", "DELETE", False), split_response(b"HTTP/1.1 304 Not Modified\r\nContent-Length: 9\r\n\r\n", "GET", False), split_response(b"HTTP/1.1 100 Continue\r\n\r\nHTTP", "POST", False))
=> ((204, b"", b"X"), (304, b"", b""), (100, b"", b"HTTP"))
--- check case | Waiting for more
(split_response(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: 5\r\n\r\nabc", "GET", False), split_response(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n", "GET", False), split_response(b"HTTP/1.0 200 OK\r\n\r\npartial", "GET", False))
=> (None, None, None)
--- check case | Read until close, once the server has closed
split_response(b"HTTP/1.0 200 OK\r\nServer: old\r\n\r\nall of it", "GET", True)
=> (200, b"all of it", b"")
--- check case | Content-Length 0
split_response(b"HTTP/1.1 201 Created\r\nContent-Length: 0\r\n\r\n", "POST", True)
=> (201, b"", b"")
--- check test | Closed too early
raises(ConnectionError, lambda: split_response(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: 5\r\n\r\nabc", "GET", True)) and raises(ConnectionError, lambda: split_response(b"HTTP/1.1 200", "GET", True))

+++ practice | Debug: the echo that lost its tail
--- task
`serve_once(conn)` should read one HTTP request with a body from a blocking connection, however the bytes are cut into pieces, and answer with `HTTP/1.1 200 OK`, a Content-Length, and the request's body echoed back. It returns the body. The starter's `Conn(data, chunk)` stands in for the socket: `recv(n)` returns at most `chunk` bytes, and `sendall` collects the reply in the attribute `out`. Keep it.

With big pieces it works. With small pieces the echoed body is often missing up to its last 4 bytes, and sometimes the reply says `Content-Length: 0`. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
class Conn:
    def __init__(self, data, chunk):
        self.data = data
        self.chunk = chunk
        self.out = b""

    def recv(self, n):
        piece = self.data[:min(n, self.chunk)]
        self.data = self.data[len(piece):]
        return piece

    def sendall(self, data):
        self.out += data


def serve_once(conn):
    data = b""
    while b"\r\n\r\n" not in data:
        piece = conn.recv(1024)
        if not piece:
            raise ConnectionError("closed early")
        data += piece
    end = data.index(b"\r\n\r\n")
    length = 0
    for line in data[:end].decode("latin-1").split("\r\n")[1:]:
        name, _, value = line.partition(":")
        if name.strip().lower() == "content-length":
            length = int(value)
    while len(data) < end + length:
        piece = conn.recv(1024)
        if not piece:
            raise ConnectionError("closed early")
        data += piece
    body = data[end + 4:end + 4 + length]
    conn.sendall(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: %d\r\n\r\n" % len(body) + body)
    return body
--- solution
class Conn:
    def __init__(self, data, chunk):
        self.data = data
        self.chunk = chunk
        self.out = b""

    def recv(self, n):
        piece = self.data[:min(n, self.chunk)]
        self.data = self.data[len(piece):]
        return piece

    def sendall(self, data):
        self.out += data


def serve_once(conn):
    data = b""
    while b"\r\n\r\n" not in data:
        piece = conn.recv(1024)
        if not piece:
            raise ConnectionError("closed early")
        data += piece
    end = data.index(b"\r\n\r\n")
    length = 0
    for line in data[:end].decode("latin-1").split("\r\n")[1:]:
        name, _, value = line.partition(":")
        if name.strip().lower() == "content-length":
            length = int(value)
    while len(data) < end + 4 + length:
        piece = conn.recv(1024)
        if not piece:
            raise ConnectionError("closed early")
        data += piece
    body = data[end + 4:end + 4 + length]
    conn.sendall(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: %d\r\n\r\n" % len(body) + body)
    return body
--- hint
`end` is where `\r\n\r\n` starts. Where does the body start, and so how many bytes must `data` hold before the whole body is there?
--- hint
The second loop must wait for `end + 4 + length` bytes: the headers, the 4 bytes of the empty line, and the body.
--- check case | Big pieces
(lambda c: (serve_once(c), c.out))(Conn(b"POST /echo HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\nContent-Length: 5\r\n\r\nhello", 4096))
=> (b"hello", b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: 5\r\n\r\nhello")
--- check case | One byte at a time
(lambda c: (serve_once(c), c.out[-8:]))(Conn(b"POST /echo HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: h\r\nContent-Length: 8\r\n\r\nABCDEFGH", 1))
=> (b"ABCDEFGH", b"ABCDEFGH")
--- check case | The headers and the empty line arrive, the body comes later
serve_once(Conn(b"PUT /x HTTP/1.1\r\nContent-Length: 3\r\n\r\nxyz", 38))
=> b"xyz"
--- check test | The whole body always comes back, for every piece size
all(serve_once(Conn(b"POST / HTTP/1.1\r\nContent-Length: 10\r\n\r\n0123456789", k)) == b"0123456789" for k in range(1, 60))

+++ practice | A telemetry relay
--- task
Ground stations often run a **relay**: consoles connect, subscribe to the topics they care about, and receive every message published on those topics. Build one over the lesson's in-memory network. The starter holds `StreamNetwork` and `run` from the lesson; keep them.

Write `relay(listener, clients)`, a generator task. It accepts `clients` connections (yielding when `accept` raises `BlockingIOError`), starts one handler task for each by yielding a generator, and then returns the number accepted. All the handlers share one table of subscriptions.

Each handler reads lines ending in `"\n"` from its connection (keep a buffer: a line may arrive in pieces, and several lines may arrive at once), yielding when `recv` raises `BlockingIOError`, and acts on each complete line:

- `SUB <topic>`: subscribe this connection to the topic. Subscribing twice to the same topic changes nothing.
- `PUB <topic> <message>`: send `<topic> <message>\n` to every connection subscribed to the topic, in the order they subscribed. The message is everything after the topic and its space, and may contain spaces.
- `QUIT`: remove this connection from every topic, close it, and finish.
- Anything else: send `ERR\n` back to this connection.

At EOF (`recv` returns `b""`), do the same as `QUIT`.
--- starter
from collections import deque


class StreamNetwork:
    def __init__(self, chunk=1024):
        self.chunk = chunk
        self.listeners = {}
        self.next_port = 49152

    def listen(self, ip, port):
        if (ip, port) in self.listeners:
            raise OSError("address already in use")
        sock = ListeningSocket(self, (ip, port))
        self.listeners[(ip, port)] = sock
        return sock

    def connect(self, ip, addr):
        listener = self.listeners.get(tuple(addr))
        if listener is None:
            raise ConnectionRefusedError("nobody is listening there")
        local = (ip, self.next_port)
        self.next_port += 1
        client = StreamSocket(self, local, tuple(addr))
        server = StreamSocket(self, tuple(addr), local)
        client.peer, server.peer = server, client
        listener.pending.append((server, local))
        return client


class ListeningSocket:
    def __init__(self, net, addr):
        self.net = net
        self.addr = addr
        self.pending = deque()

    def accept(self):
        if not self.pending:
            raise BlockingIOError("no connection is waiting")
        return self.pending.popleft()

    def close(self):
        self.net.listeners.pop(self.addr, None)


class StreamSocket:
    def __init__(self, net, local, remote):
        self.net = net
        self.local = local
        self.remote = remote
        self.peer = None
        self.inbox = bytearray()
        self.eof = False
        self.can_send = True
        self.closed = False

    def sendall(self, data):
        if not self.can_send:
            raise OSError("this socket can no longer send")
        if self.peer.closed:
            raise BrokenPipeError("the other side has closed")
        self.peer.inbox += data

    def recv(self, n):
        if self.closed:
            raise OSError("this socket is closed")
        if self.inbox:
            size = min(n, self.net.chunk, len(self.inbox))
            data = bytes(self.inbox[:size])
            del self.inbox[:size]
            return data
        if self.eof:
            return b""
        raise BlockingIOError("no data yet")

    def shutdown_write(self):
        if self.can_send:
            self.can_send = False
            self.peer.eof = True

    def close(self):
        self.shutdown_write()
        self.closed = True


def run(*tasks, limit=100000):
    results = [None] * len(tasks)
    queue = deque(enumerate(tasks))
    steps = 0
    while queue:
        steps += 1
        if steps > limit:
            raise RuntimeError("the tasks never finished")
        i, task = queue.popleft()
        try:
            new = next(task)
        except StopIteration as stop:
            if i is not None:
                results[i] = stop.value
            continue
        if new is not None:
            queue.append((None, new))
        queue.append((i, task))
    return results


def relay(listener, clients):
    return 0
    yield
--- solution
from collections import deque


class StreamNetwork:
    def __init__(self, chunk=1024):
        self.chunk = chunk
        self.listeners = {}
        self.next_port = 49152

    def listen(self, ip, port):
        if (ip, port) in self.listeners:
            raise OSError("address already in use")
        sock = ListeningSocket(self, (ip, port))
        self.listeners[(ip, port)] = sock
        return sock

    def connect(self, ip, addr):
        listener = self.listeners.get(tuple(addr))
        if listener is None:
            raise ConnectionRefusedError("nobody is listening there")
        local = (ip, self.next_port)
        self.next_port += 1
        client = StreamSocket(self, local, tuple(addr))
        server = StreamSocket(self, tuple(addr), local)
        client.peer, server.peer = server, client
        listener.pending.append((server, local))
        return client


class ListeningSocket:
    def __init__(self, net, addr):
        self.net = net
        self.addr = addr
        self.pending = deque()

    def accept(self):
        if not self.pending:
            raise BlockingIOError("no connection is waiting")
        return self.pending.popleft()

    def close(self):
        self.net.listeners.pop(self.addr, None)


class StreamSocket:
    def __init__(self, net, local, remote):
        self.net = net
        self.local = local
        self.remote = remote
        self.peer = None
        self.inbox = bytearray()
        self.eof = False
        self.can_send = True
        self.closed = False

    def sendall(self, data):
        if not self.can_send:
            raise OSError("this socket can no longer send")
        if self.peer.closed:
            raise BrokenPipeError("the other side has closed")
        self.peer.inbox += data

    def recv(self, n):
        if self.closed:
            raise OSError("this socket is closed")
        if self.inbox:
            size = min(n, self.net.chunk, len(self.inbox))
            data = bytes(self.inbox[:size])
            del self.inbox[:size]
            return data
        if self.eof:
            return b""
        raise BlockingIOError("no data yet")

    def shutdown_write(self):
        if self.can_send:
            self.can_send = False
            self.peer.eof = True

    def close(self):
        self.shutdown_write()
        self.closed = True


def run(*tasks, limit=100000):
    results = [None] * len(tasks)
    queue = deque(enumerate(tasks))
    steps = 0
    while queue:
        steps += 1
        if steps > limit:
            raise RuntimeError("the tasks never finished")
        i, task = queue.popleft()
        try:
            new = next(task)
        except StopIteration as stop:
            if i is not None:
                results[i] = stop.value
            continue
        if new is not None:
            queue.append((None, new))
        queue.append((i, task))
    return results


def relay_client(conn, topics):
    buf = b""
    while True:
        while b"\n" in buf:
            line, buf = buf.split(b"\n", 1)
            words = line.decode("utf-8").split(" ", 2)
            if words[0] == "SUB" and len(words) == 2:
                subscribers = topics.setdefault(words[1], [])
                if conn not in subscribers:
                    subscribers.append(conn)
            elif words[0] == "PUB" and len(words) == 3:
                for other in topics.get(words[1], []):
                    other.sendall(f"{words[1]} {words[2]}\n".encode("utf-8"))
            elif words == ["QUIT"]:
                buf = None
                break
            else:
                conn.sendall(b"ERR\n")
        if buf is None:
            break
        try:
            data = conn.recv(4096)
        except BlockingIOError:
            yield
            continue
        if not data:
            break
        buf += data
    for subscribers in topics.values():
        if conn in subscribers:
            subscribers.remove(conn)
    conn.close()


def relay(listener, clients):
    topics = {}
    accepted = 0
    while accepted < clients:
        try:
            conn, _ = listener.accept()
        except BlockingIOError:
            yield
            continue
        accepted += 1
        yield relay_client(conn, topics)
    return accepted
--- hint
Keep the subscriptions in a dict from topic to a list of connections, created once in `relay` and passed to every handler. A handler is a generator of its own, like the lesson's `handle`.
--- hint
Split a line into at most three parts with `split(" ", 2)`: the command, the topic, and the whole message. When a handler finishes, for QUIT or EOF, remove its connection from every list before closing it.
--- check case | Two subscribers, one publisher, and an unknown command
(lambda net: (lambda lst, a, b, c: (a.sendall(b"SUB temp\n"), b.sendall(b"SUB temp\nSUB power\nSUB temp\n"), run(relay(lst, 3), (f() for f in [lambda: None] * 40 + [lambda: c.sendall(b"PUB temp 21.5 C\nPUB power 88 W\nhello\n")] + [lambda: None] * 40 + [lambda: a.sendall(b"QUIT\n"), lambda: b.shutdown_write(), lambda: c.sendall(b"QUIT\n")])), [b"".join(iter(lambda s=s: s.recv(100), b"")) for s in (a, b, c)])[2:])(net.listen("gs", 7000), net.connect("a", ("gs", 7000)), net.connect("b", ("gs", 7000)), net.connect("c", ("gs", 7000))))(StreamNetwork(2))
=> ([3, None], [b"temp 21.5 C\n", b"temp 21.5 C\npower 88 W\n", b"ERR\n"])
--- check case | A console that quits gets nothing more, and nothing breaks
(lambda net: (lambda lst, a, b, c: (a.sendall(b"SUB t\n"), b.sendall(b"SUB t\n"), run(relay(lst, 3), (f() for f in [lambda: None] * 40 + [lambda: c.sendall(b"PUB t one\n")] + [lambda: None] * 40 + [lambda: a.sendall(b"QUIT\n")] + [lambda: None] * 40 + [lambda: c.sendall(b"PUB t two\n")] + [lambda: None] * 40 + [lambda: b.close(), lambda: c.close()])), [b"".join(iter(lambda s=s: s.recv(100), b"")) for s in (a,)], b.inbox)[3:])(net.listen("gs", 1), net.connect("a", ("gs", 1)), net.connect("b", ("gs", 1)), net.connect("c", ("gs", 1))))(StreamNetwork(3))
=> ([b"t one\n"], bytearray(b"t one\nt two\n"))
--- check case | A publisher subscribed to its own topic hears itself; lines split across pieces
(lambda net: (lambda lst, a: (a.sendall(b"SUB ops\nPUB ops go for launch\nQUIT\n"), run(relay(lst, 1)), b"".join(iter(lambda: a.recv(100), b"")))[1:])(net.listen("gs", 2), net.connect("a", ("gs", 2))))(StreamNetwork(1))
=> ([1], b"ops go for launch\n")

+++ question | Where the rest of the request went
--- ask
A client sends a 1,000-byte request with one `sendall`. On the server, the first `conn.recv(4096)` returns only 536 bytes. What happened?
--- choice
The other 464 bytes were lost, and TCP will report an error.
--- choice correct
Nothing is wrong: TCP is a byte stream and `recv` returns whatever has arrived so far; the server must keep reading until its framing rules say the request is complete.
--- choice
The client's `sendall` sent only part of the data, so the client has a bug.
--- choice
`recv(4096)` always returns at most 536 bytes, the minimum MSS.
--- why
A single send can be split into several segments on the way, and `recv` returns as soon as some bytes are ready. The rest arrives in later `recv` calls. That is why every server keeps a buffer and parses only complete messages.

+++ question | What recv gives at the end
--- ask
The other side has closed its sending direction, and you have already read every byte it sent. What does `conn.recv(4096)` return now? Type the Python value.
--- answer
b""
b''
empty bytes
an empty bytes object
--- why
An empty bytes object means end of file: the peer sent its FIN and nothing more will ever come. It is returned at once, and again on every later call, so a loop that does not check for it spins forever.

+++ question | Why yield instead of trying again
--- ask
In this lesson's server, a handler whose `recv` raises `BlockingIOError` runs `yield`. Why not just call `recv` again in a loop until data arrives?
--- choice
Because calling `recv` twice in a row loses data.
--- choice correct
Because only one task runs at a time; a task that loops on `recv` never gives the others a turn, so the client that would send the data never runs, and the program hangs.
--- choice
Because `yield` makes the data arrive faster.
--- choice
Because `BlockingIOError` closes the socket, and `yield` reopens it.
--- why
The scheduler is cooperative: a task keeps the processor until it yields. Waiting by looping (busy waiting) starves every other task, including the one that would produce the data. Real event loops work the same way: a coroutine awaits, which returns control to the loop until the socket is ready.

=== net-14 | TLS: keys, certificates and the handshake
--- teach
Last lesson's server and client sent HTTP as plain bytes. Every router, Wi-Fi access point and cable company between them could read those bytes, and change them. Your password, your messages, the command you send to a spacecraft: all open to whoever sits on the path. **TLS**, Transport Layer Security, fixes that. It sits between TCP and the application, and HTTP over TLS is **HTTPS**. This lesson treats TLS as a protocol: what it promises, the building blocks it uses, and why each message of its handshake is there. The Security course goes deeper into the cryptography itself.

### A sealed, signed letter

Picture sending a letter through a post office you do not trust. A clerk might read it, or steam it open and change a word. You want three things. Nobody but your friend can read it. Any change is noticed. And when a reply comes, you know it really is from your friend, not from the clerk pretending. TLS gives exactly these three to a connection.

### What TLS promises

- **Confidentiality:** only the two ends can read the data.
- **Integrity:** any change, deletion, reordering or replay of the data is detected, and the connection is closed.
- **Authentication:** the client knows it is talking to the server it named, say `gs.orbit.example`, and not an impostor. (Servers can also check clients, but usually they do not.)

The attacker TLS is designed against is strong. It can read every packet, drop any, change any, send packets of its own, and replay old ones. It sits **on the path**: a hostile Wi-Fi network, a compromised router, or a DNS answer poisoned as in lesson 11 that sends you to the attacker's machine. It cannot break the underlying mathematics, and it does not have the server's private key.

### Why HTTPS is needed

Without TLS:

- a password typed on café Wi-Fi crosses the air in the clear;
- ISPs have injected adverts and tracking code into pages on the way;
- a poisoned DNS cache sends you to a fake bank, and nothing tells you.

Encryption alone does not solve the last one. If you encrypt to whoever answered, and the attacker answered, you have a secure channel to the attacker. That is why TLS spends most of its handshake on **authentication**.

### Building block 1: hashes

A **cryptographic hash** such as SHA-256 turns any input into a fixed 32-byte **digest**. It is fast to compute, and nobody knows how to find two different inputs with the same digest (a **collision**), or an input with a given digest. So a digest works as a short fingerprint of a long message.

```python
import hashlib
print(hashlib.sha256(b"burn 3.2 s").hexdigest()[:16])
print(hashlib.sha256(b"burn 3.3 s").hexdigest()[:16])   # one character changed: a different digest
```

### Building block 2: MACs

A **message authentication code** (MAC) is a tag computed from a message and a **secret key**. Only someone with the key can compute the right tag, so a correct tag proves the message came from a key holder and was not changed. **HMAC** builds a MAC from a hash:

```python
import hmac, hashlib
key = b"shared secret"
tag = hmac.new(key, b"mode=safe", hashlib.sha256).digest()
check = hmac.new(key, b"mode=safe", hashlib.sha256).digest()
print(hmac.compare_digest(tag, check))    # True: same key, same message
```

`hmac.compare_digest` compares in a time that does not depend on where the first difference is, so an attacker cannot learn a correct tag byte by byte from how long the comparison took.

### Building block 3: symmetric encryption

With a shared secret key, fast **symmetric ciphers** such as AES or ChaCha20 encrypt the data. TLS uses **AEAD** modes (authenticated encryption with associated data), which encrypt and compute a MAC-like tag in one step. Each record's **sequence number** goes into the encryption, so a record that is replayed, dropped or moved is detected.

That leaves the hard question: how do two machines that have never met agree on a secret key, while the attacker reads every byte?

### Building block 4: Diffie–Hellman key exchange

Both sides agree, in public, on a large prime p and a number g. Then:

1. The client picks a secret number a and sends A = gᵃ mod p.
2. The server picks a secret number b and sends B = gᵇ mod p.
3. The client computes Bᵃ mod p and the server computes Aᵇ mod p. Both equal gᵃᵇ mod p, the **shared secret**.

It works because (gᵇ)ᵃ = gᵇᵃ = (gᵃ)ᵇ, even modulo p, as you saw with modular exponentiation. With tiny numbers:

```python
p, g = 23, 5
a, b = 6, 15                   # the two secrets
A, B = pow(g, a, p), pow(g, b, p)
print(A, B)                    # 8 19: these are sent in public
print(pow(B, a, p), pow(A, b, p))   # 2 2: the same shared secret
```

The eavesdropper sees p, g, A and B. To get the secret it would need a from A, the **[[discrete logarithm|dlog]]** problem, which nobody can solve quickly for well-chosen groups of 2,048 bits, or for elliptic curves of 256 bits. TLS 1.3 uses a fresh a and b for every connection, which gives **[[forward secrecy|pfs]]**.

### Diffie–Hellman alone is not enough

An attacker in the middle, Mallory, can run **two** exchanges. She answers the client with her own number M = gᵐ mod p, pretending to be the server, and opens her own exchange with the server, pretending to be the client. She now shares one key with each side, decrypts everything from one, reads or changes it, and encrypts it again for the other. Neither side notices, because nothing in the numbers says who sent them.

The fix is to make the server **sign** its part of the exchange with a key that is known to belong to the server's name.

### Building block 5: signatures

You built RSA in Discrete Mathematics. A **signature** is RSA run the other way: the signer computes s = hᵈ mod n from the hash h of the message, using the private exponent d, and anyone can check that sᵉ mod n = h using the public key (n, e). Only the holder of d can make a signature that checks, and any change to the message changes h.

### Building block 6: certificates

How does the client know that a public key belongs to `gs.orbit.example`, and not to Mallory? A **certificate** is a small document that says "the public key (n, e) belongs to `gs.orbit.example`", signed by a **certificate authority** (CA). Your operating system and browser ship with the public keys of about 150 trusted **root CAs**, the **[[trust store|trust]]**.

To check a certificate, the client:

1. verifies the CA's signature on it with a public key it trusts (in practice through a short **chain**: root CA, intermediate CA, server);
2. checks that the name in it matches the name it wanted to reach;
3. checks the dates, since certificates expire.

This is the same idea as DNSSEC's chain of trust from lesson 11: each link vouches for the next, up to a key you already trust.

### The TLS 1.3 handshake

```text
client                                              server
ClientHello: random, key share A, server name  --->
                         <---  ServerHello: random, key share B
                               (from here on, encrypted with handshake keys)
                         <---  Certificate: the server's certificate chain
                         <---  CertificateVerify: signature over the transcript so far
                         <---  Finished: MAC over the transcript so far
Finished: MAC over the transcript  --->
application data, both ways, encrypted with application keys
```

- The **transcript** is every handshake message so far, in order. Its hash goes into every signature, MAC and key.
- Both sides compute the Diffie–Hellman secret right after ServerHello, and derive keys from it and the transcript hash with a **key derivation function** (TLS uses HKDF, built from HMAC).
- **CertificateVerify** is the server's signature, with the private key of its certificate, over the hash of the transcript up to and including the Certificate. That transcript contains **both key shares**.
- Each **Finished** is a MAC, with a key derived from the shared secret, over the hash of the transcript so far.

It costs one round trip: the client can send its request right after its Finished.

### Why the handshake is secure

Follow the attacker's options, one step at a time.

1. **Mallory swaps in her own key share.** The server's CertificateVerify signs the real transcript, with the real server's share. The client's transcript has Mallory's share, so its hash differs and the signature does not check. Mallory cannot sign the changed transcript herself without the server's private key.
2. **Mallory sends her own certificate.** Either it is for another name (the name check fails), or it is not signed by a trusted CA (the signature check fails). CAs check that whoever asks for a certificate for a name really controls that name.
3. **Mallory changes the ClientHello**, say to remove the strongest ciphers and force a weaker one, a **downgrade**. The server signs and MACs the transcript with the changed hello; the client's transcript has the original. The signature fails.
4. **Mallory replays a whole server flight from an old session.** The client's random is new, so the transcript differs, and the old signature does not match it.
5. **Mallory, passively, records everything.** She sees A and B but cannot compute the shared secret (the discrete logarithm problem), so she cannot compute any key.

So when the client accepts the server's Finished, it knows three things: the other end holds the private key for a certificate a trusted CA issued for this name; both sides saw exactly the same handshake messages; and only the two of them know the shared secret, and so the keys. The client's Finished then proves to the server that the client saw the same transcript.

### What it costs

- **Round trips:** TCP's handshake (1 round trip), then TLS 1.3's (1 more; TLS 1.2 needed 2). So the first byte of the response arrives after about 3 round trips. Resumed sessions can send data in the first flight, called **0-RTT**, but that data can be **replayed** by an attacker, so it must only be used for idempotent requests (lesson 12).
- **Computation:** one Diffie–Hellman exchange and one signature per handshake, about a millisecond on a server. The symmetric encryption afterwards runs at gigabytes per second with hardware support, so HTTPS costs almost nothing once the connection is open.
- **Bytes:** a certificate chain is typically 3 to 5 KB, sent in every full handshake.

### Where it is used

- **HTTPS**: almost all web traffic today. [[Let's Encrypt|le]] made certificates free and automatic.
- **Email** between servers, **DNS over TLS**, and VPNs.
- **QUIC** (HTTP/3) builds TLS 1.3 into its own handshake, so transport and security setup share one round trip.
- **Space links:** CCSDS defined the Space Data Link Security protocol, which applies the same ideas (authenticated encryption, sequence numbers against replay) to telecommand and telemetry frames, since an unauthenticated command link is an open door.

**Watch out:** the classic TLS mistakes.

- **Turning verification off.** `requests.get(url, verify=False)` "fixes" a certificate error by accepting any certificate, including Mallory's. It removes the only thing stopping a man in the middle.
- **Checking the chain but not the name.** Every website can get a valid certificate for its own name. A client that does not check the name accepts `evil.example`'s certificate for `bank.example`.
- **Comparing MACs with `==`.** Use `hmac.compare_digest`.
- **Inventing your own protocol.** Each message of TLS is there because some attack defeated an earlier design. Use a real library.

::: context dlog Why the discrete logarithm is hard
Computing gᵃ mod p is fast, with repeated squaring, even for 2,048-bit numbers. Going back, finding a from gᵃ mod p, has no known fast method: the best general algorithms take time that grows faster than any polynomial in the number of digits. This one-way gap is what Diffie–Hellman rests on. Elliptic-curve Diffie–Hellman (X25519 in TLS 1.3) does the same over a different group, where 256-bit keys are as strong as 3,072-bit ones. A large quantum computer would break both, which is why post-quantum key exchange is now being added to TLS.
:::

::: context pfs Forward secrecy
In old TLS, the client often encrypted the session key with the server's long-term RSA key. Anyone who recorded the traffic and later stole that key, even years later, could decrypt every recorded session. With a fresh Diffie–Hellman exchange per connection, the long-term key only signs; the session secret comes from numbers that are thrown away after the handshake. Stealing the server's key later reveals nothing about past sessions. TLS 1.3 requires this.
:::

::: context trust Trusting certificate authorities
A CA's power is enormous: any trusted CA can issue a certificate for any name. In 2011 the Dutch CA DigiNotar was hacked, and false certificates for Google were used to spy on Iranian users; browsers removed DigiNotar from their trust stores and the company went bankrupt. Since then, Certificate Transparency requires CAs to publish every certificate they issue in public, append-only logs, so a false certificate for your name can be spotted.
:::

::: context le Free certificates
Let's Encrypt, a non-profit CA launched in 2015, issues certificates automatically and for free. A server proves it controls a name by answering a challenge: putting a given file on the web server at that name, or a given TXT record in its DNS. Certificates last 90 days and are renewed by a script. Before it, many sites skipped HTTPS because certificates cost money and effort; today most web pages load over HTTPS.
:::
--- task
The starter already has every cryptographic piece; keep all of it:

- the Diffie–Hellman group `P` and `G` (far too small for real use, but the steps are real);
- `encode(kind, *fields)`, which builds a message from a 2-byte kind such as `b"CH"` and fields (numbers, text or bytes), and `decode(msg)`, which returns `(kind, fields)` with every field as `bytes`;
- `th(*messages)`, the hash of a transcript; `kdf(secret, label, transcript_hash)`, which derives a key; `mac(key, data)`;
- `rsa_sign(key, data)` and `rsa_verify(n, e, data, signature)`;
- certificates, which are tuples `(subject, n, e, issuer, signature)`, where the signature is the issuer's over `encode(b"TB", subject, n, e, issuer)`;
- the keys and certificates the checks use: `CA_KEY`, `CA` (the trusted CA as `(name, n, e)`), `SERVER_KEY`, `SERVER_CERT` (for `"gs.orbit.example"`) and `MALLORY_KEY`.

Write two classes for a small handshake in the style of TLS 1.3. A field comes back from `decode` as bytes: turn it into a number with `int.from_bytes(field, "big")`, and into text with `field.decode()`. Compare MACs with `hmac.compare_digest`.

**`TlsServer(cert, key, b, rand)`** holds its certificate, its private key, its secret number `b` and its random bytes `rand`. Its attribute `key_out` starts as `None`.

- `respond(hello)`: `hello` must decode to kind `b"CH"` with two fields, the client's random and its share; otherwise raise `ValueError`. The shared secret is the client's share to the power `b`, mod `P`. Build and return the list `[sh, ct, cv, sf]`:
  - `sh = encode(b"SH", rand, pow(G, b, P))`
  - `ct = encode(b"CT", *cert)`
  - `cv = encode(b"CV", rsa_sign(key, b"server verify" + th(hello, sh, ct)))`
  - `sf = encode(b"SF", mac(kdf(secret, b"s fin", th(hello, sh)), th(hello, sh, ct, cv)))`
- `finish(finished)`: `finished` must be kind `b"CF"` with one field equal to `mac(kdf(secret, b"c fin", th(hello, sh)), th(hello, sh, ct, cv, sf))`. If it is, set `key_out = kdf(secret, b"app", th(hello, sh, ct, cv, sf))`; otherwise raise `ValueError`.

**`TlsClient(hostname, ca, a, rand)`**: `ca` is the trusted CA as `(name, n, e)`, `a` the client's secret number. Its attribute `key_out` starts as `None`.

- `hello()` returns, and remembers, `encode(b"CH", rand, pow(G, a, P))`.
- `receive(flight)` checks the server's four messages, in this order, and raises `ValueError` at the first failure:
  1. there are four messages, of kinds `b"SH"`, `b"CT"`, `b"CV"` and `b"SF"`;
  2. the certificate's issuer is the CA's name, and its signature verifies with the CA's `n` and `e` over `encode(b"TB", subject, n, e, issuer)`, using the four fields exactly as received;
  3. the certificate's subject is `hostname`;
  4. the CertificateVerify signature verifies with the certificate's own `n` and `e` over `b"server verify" + th(hello, sh, ct)`;
  5. the server Finished holds the MAC the server should have computed, as above, using the secret: the server's share to the power `a`, mod `P`.

  Then it sets `key_out = kdf(secret, b"app", th(hello, sh, ct, cv, sf))` and returns its own Finished, `encode(b"CF", mac(kdf(secret, b"c fin", th(hello, sh)), th(hello, sh, ct, cv, sf)))`.

With both written, `server.finish(client.receive(server.respond(client.hello())))` completes a handshake, and then `client.key_out == server.key_out`.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac

P = 2 ** 127 - 1
G = 3


def to_bytes(x):
    if isinstance(x, int):
        return x.to_bytes(max(1, (x.bit_length() + 7) // 8), "big")
    if isinstance(x, str):
        return x.encode("utf-8")
    return bytes(x)


def encode(kind, *fields):
    out = kind
    for f in fields:
        b = to_bytes(f)
        out += len(b).to_bytes(2, "big") + b
    return out


def decode(msg):
    kind, pos, fields = msg[:2], 2, []
    while pos < len(msg):
        size = int.from_bytes(msg[pos:pos + 2], "big")
        fields.append(msg[pos + 2:pos + 2 + size])
        pos += 2 + size
    return kind, fields


def th(*messages):
    h = hashlib.sha256()
    for m in messages:
        h.update(len(m).to_bytes(4, "big") + m)
    return h.digest()


def kdf(secret, label, transcript_hash):
    return hmac.new(to_bytes(secret), label + transcript_hash, hashlib.sha256).digest()


def mac(key, data):
    return hmac.new(key, data, hashlib.sha256).digest()


def make_key(p, q, e=65537):
    return {"n": p * q, "e": e, "d": pow(e, -1, (p - 1) * (q - 1))}


def rsa_sign(key, data):
    h = int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(data).digest(), "big") % key["n"]
    return pow(h, key["d"], key["n"])


def rsa_verify(n, e, data, sig):
    h = int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(data).digest(), "big") % n
    return pow(sig, e, n) == h


def make_cert(subject, key, issuer, issuer_key):
    body = encode(b"TB", subject, key["n"], key["e"], issuer)
    return (subject, key["n"], key["e"], issuer, rsa_sign(issuer_key, body))


CA_KEY = make_key(2 ** 89 - 1, 2 ** 107 - 1)
SERVER_KEY = make_key(2 ** 61 - 1, 2 ** 127 - 1)
MALLORY_KEY = make_key(2 ** 31 - 1, 2 ** 521 - 1)
CA = ("Orbit Root CA", CA_KEY["n"], CA_KEY["e"])
SERVER_CERT = make_cert("gs.orbit.example", SERVER_KEY, "Orbit Root CA", CA_KEY)


class TlsServer:
    def __init__(self, cert, key, b, rand):
        self.key_out = None

    def respond(self, hello):
        return []

    def finish(self, finished):
        pass


class TlsClient:
    def __init__(self, hostname, ca, a, rand):
        self.key_out = None

    def hello(self):
        return b""

    def receive(self, flight):
        return b""
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac

P = 2 ** 127 - 1
G = 3


def to_bytes(x):
    if isinstance(x, int):
        return x.to_bytes(max(1, (x.bit_length() + 7) // 8), "big")
    if isinstance(x, str):
        return x.encode("utf-8")
    return bytes(x)


def encode(kind, *fields):
    out = kind
    for f in fields:
        b = to_bytes(f)
        out += len(b).to_bytes(2, "big") + b
    return out


def decode(msg):
    kind, pos, fields = msg[:2], 2, []
    while pos < len(msg):
        size = int.from_bytes(msg[pos:pos + 2], "big")
        fields.append(msg[pos + 2:pos + 2 + size])
        pos += 2 + size
    return kind, fields


def th(*messages):
    h = hashlib.sha256()
    for m in messages:
        h.update(len(m).to_bytes(4, "big") + m)
    return h.digest()


def kdf(secret, label, transcript_hash):
    return hmac.new(to_bytes(secret), label + transcript_hash, hashlib.sha256).digest()


def mac(key, data):
    return hmac.new(key, data, hashlib.sha256).digest()


def make_key(p, q, e=65537):
    return {"n": p * q, "e": e, "d": pow(e, -1, (p - 1) * (q - 1))}


def rsa_sign(key, data):
    h = int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(data).digest(), "big") % key["n"]
    return pow(h, key["d"], key["n"])


def rsa_verify(n, e, data, sig):
    h = int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(data).digest(), "big") % n
    return pow(sig, e, n) == h


def make_cert(subject, key, issuer, issuer_key):
    body = encode(b"TB", subject, key["n"], key["e"], issuer)
    return (subject, key["n"], key["e"], issuer, rsa_sign(issuer_key, body))


CA_KEY = make_key(2 ** 89 - 1, 2 ** 107 - 1)
SERVER_KEY = make_key(2 ** 61 - 1, 2 ** 127 - 1)
MALLORY_KEY = make_key(2 ** 31 - 1, 2 ** 521 - 1)
CA = ("Orbit Root CA", CA_KEY["n"], CA_KEY["e"])
SERVER_CERT = make_cert("gs.orbit.example", SERVER_KEY, "Orbit Root CA", CA_KEY)


class TlsServer:
    def __init__(self, cert, key, b, rand):
        self.cert = cert
        self.key = key
        self.b = b
        self.rand = rand
        self.key_out = None

    def respond(self, hello):
        kind, fields = decode(hello)
        if kind != b"CH" or len(fields) != 2:
            raise ValueError("expected a ClientHello")
        sh = encode(b"SH", self.rand, pow(G, self.b, P))
        secret = pow(int.from_bytes(fields[1], "big"), self.b, P)
        base = th(hello, sh)
        ct = encode(b"CT", *self.cert)
        cv = encode(b"CV", rsa_sign(self.key, b"server verify" + th(hello, sh, ct)))
        sf = encode(b"SF", mac(kdf(secret, b"s fin", base), th(hello, sh, ct, cv)))
        full = th(hello, sh, ct, cv, sf)
        self.expected = mac(kdf(secret, b"c fin", base), full)
        self.pending = kdf(secret, b"app", full)
        return [sh, ct, cv, sf]

    def finish(self, finished):
        kind, fields = decode(finished)
        if kind != b"CF" or len(fields) != 1 or not hmac.compare_digest(fields[0], self.expected):
            raise ValueError("bad client Finished")
        self.key_out = self.pending


class TlsClient:
    def __init__(self, hostname, ca, a, rand):
        self.hostname = hostname
        self.ca = ca
        self.a = a
        self.rand = rand
        self.key_out = None

    def hello(self):
        self.ch = encode(b"CH", self.rand, pow(G, self.a, P))
        return self.ch

    def receive(self, flight):
        if len(flight) != 4:
            raise ValueError("expected four messages")
        sh, ct, cv, sf = flight
        kinds = [decode(m)[0] for m in flight]
        if kinds != [b"SH", b"CT", b"CV", b"SF"]:
            raise ValueError("unexpected message")
        secret = pow(int.from_bytes(decode(sh)[1][1], "big"), self.a, P)
        subject, n, e, issuer, signature = decode(ct)[1]
        body = encode(b"TB", subject, n, e, issuer)
        ca_name, ca_n, ca_e = self.ca
        if issuer.decode() != ca_name or not rsa_verify(ca_n, ca_e, body, int.from_bytes(signature, "big")):
            raise ValueError("certificate not signed by the trusted CA")
        if subject.decode() != self.hostname:
            raise ValueError("certificate is for another name")
        n, e = int.from_bytes(n, "big"), int.from_bytes(e, "big")
        if not rsa_verify(n, e, b"server verify" + th(self.ch, sh, ct), int.from_bytes(decode(cv)[1][0], "big")):
            raise ValueError("bad CertificateVerify")
        base = th(self.ch, sh)
        if not hmac.compare_digest(decode(sf)[1][0], mac(kdf(secret, b"s fin", base), th(self.ch, sh, ct, cv))):
            raise ValueError("bad server Finished")
        full = th(self.ch, sh, ct, cv, sf)
        self.key_out = kdf(secret, b"app", full)
        return encode(b"CF", mac(kdf(secret, b"c fin", base), full))
--- hint
Write the server first; the client then checks exactly what the server built. In both classes, keep the messages you sent and received (the hello, `sh`, `ct`, `cv`, `sf`) because every later step hashes the transcript of them so far.
--- hint
In `receive`, unpack the certificate with `subject, n, e, issuer, signature = decode(ct)[1]`. The body the CA signed is `encode(b"TB", subject, n, e, issuer)` with those same byte fields. Only after the certificate checks out may you trust `n` and `e` to check the CertificateVerify.
--- hint
The shared secret on the client is `pow(int.from_bytes(server_share, "big"), a, P)`. Recompute the server's Finished with it and compare; then derive `key_out` and build the client Finished from the full transcript `th(hello, sh, ct, cv, sf)`.
--- check test | An honest handshake: both sides end with the same 32-byte key
(lambda c, s: s.finish(c.receive(s.respond(c.hello()))) is None and c.key_out == s.key_out and len(c.key_out) == 32)(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, 12345678901234567890, b"client-random-01"), TlsServer(SERVER_CERT, SERVER_KEY, 98765432109876543210, b"server-random-01"))
--- check case | The exact key, so every step follows the specification
(lambda c, s: (s.finish(c.receive(s.respond(c.hello()))), c.key_out.hex()[:16]))(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, 12345678901234567890, b"client-random-01"), TlsServer(SERVER_CERT, SERVER_KEY, 98765432109876543210, b"server-random-01"))
=> (None, "94d2d77f7e0923af")
--- check case | The server's flight has the right kinds, and its hello the right share
(lambda f: ([m[:2] for m in f], decode(f[0])[1][1] == to_bytes(pow(G, 777, P))))(TlsServer(SERVER_CERT, SERVER_KEY, 777, b"srv").respond(encode(b"CH", b"cli", pow(G, 5, P))))
=> ([b"SH", b"CT", b"CV", b"SF"], True)
--- check test | Fresh secrets give a fresh key
(lambda k1, k2: k1 != k2)(*[(lambda c, s: (s.finish(c.receive(s.respond(c.hello()))), c.key_out)[1])(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, a, b"r"), TlsServer(SERVER_CERT, SERVER_KEY, 99, b"s")) for a in (1001, 1002)])
--- check test | Mallory answers with her own share and the real certificate: the signature fails
(lambda c: raises(ValueError, lambda: c.receive(TlsServer(SERVER_CERT, MALLORY_KEY, 777, b"mallory").respond(c.hello()))) and c.key_out is None)(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, 111, b"r1"))
--- check test | Mallory swaps her share into the real server's flight: the signature fails
(lambda c: (lambda f: raises(ValueError, lambda: c.receive([encode(b"SH", decode(f[0])[1][0], pow(G, 4242, P))] + f[1:])))(TlsServer(SERVER_CERT, SERVER_KEY, 5, b"rs").respond(c.hello())))(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, 111, b"r1"))
--- check test | A CA-signed certificate for another name is refused
(lambda c: raises(ValueError, lambda: c.receive(TlsServer(make_cert("evil.example", MALLORY_KEY, "Orbit Root CA", CA_KEY), MALLORY_KEY, 777, b"m").respond(c.hello()))))(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, 111, b"r1"))
--- check test | A certificate with the right name but not signed by the CA is refused
(lambda c: raises(ValueError, lambda: c.receive(TlsServer(make_cert("gs.orbit.example", MALLORY_KEY, "Orbit Root CA", MALLORY_KEY), MALLORY_KEY, 777, b"m").respond(c.hello()))))(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, 111, b"r1"))
--- check test | A changed ClientHello (a downgrade) is caught
(lambda c: (lambda ch: raises(ValueError, lambda: c.receive(TlsServer(SERVER_CERT, SERVER_KEY, 5, b"rs").respond(encode(b"CH", b"weaker", decode(ch)[1][1]))))) (c.hello()))(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, 111, b"r1"))
--- check test | A flight replayed from an old session is refused
(lambda old: (lambda c: (c.hello(), raises(ValueError, lambda: c.receive(old)))[1])(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, 111, b"fresh-random")))((lambda c0: TlsServer(SERVER_CERT, SERVER_KEY, 5, b"rs").respond(c0.hello()))(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, 111, b"old-random")))
--- check test | A changed server Finished is caught, and so is a flight with the wrong messages
(lambda c: (lambda f: raises(ValueError, lambda: c.receive(f[:3] + [encode(b"SF", bytes(32))])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: c.receive(f[:3])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: c.receive([f[1], f[0], f[2], f[3]])))(TlsServer(SERVER_CERT, SERVER_KEY, 5, b"rs").respond(c.hello())))(TlsClient("gs.orbit.example", CA, 111, b"r1"))
--- check test | The server refuses a bad client Finished and anything but a ClientHello
