+++ practice | Diffie–Hellman and the man in the middle
--- task
Write three functions for Diffie–Hellman over the numbers mod a prime `p`, with base `g`.

- `dh_public(g, p, secret)` returns the number to send: `g` to the power `secret`, mod `p`.
- `dh_shared(p, their_public, secret)` returns the shared secret.
- `mitm(g, p, a, b, m)` simulates Mallory in the middle of an exchange between a client with secret `a` and a server with secret `b`. Mallory uses the secret `m` towards both of them: the client receives Mallory's public number instead of the server's, and the server receives Mallory's instead of the client's. Return a tuple `(client_key, server_key, mallory_client_key, mallory_server_key)`: the secret the client computes, the one the server computes, and the ones Mallory computes with each of them.

For example, `mitm(5, 23, 6, 15, 13)` is `(18, 7, 18, 7)`: the client and server hold different keys, and Mallory holds both.
--- starter
def dh_public(g, p, secret):
    return g * secret % p


def dh_shared(p, their_public, secret):
    return their_public * secret % p


def mitm(g, p, a, b, m):
    return (0, 0, 0, 0)
--- solution
def dh_public(g, p, secret):
    return pow(g, secret, p)


def dh_shared(p, their_public, secret):
    return pow(their_public, secret, p)


def mitm(g, p, a, b, m):
    fake = dh_public(g, p, m)
    client_key = dh_shared(p, fake, a)
    server_key = dh_shared(p, fake, b)
    mallory_client = dh_shared(p, dh_public(g, p, a), m)
    mallory_server = dh_shared(p, dh_public(g, p, b), m)
    return (client_key, server_key, mallory_client, mallory_server)
--- hint
`pow(x, e, p)` computes x to the power e, mod p, quickly, even for huge numbers.
--- hint
In the attack, both honest sides compute with Mallory's public number `g^m mod p`, and Mallory computes with each side's real public number.
--- check case | Public numbers and a shared secret with tiny numbers
(dh_public(5, 23, 6), dh_public(5, 23, 15), dh_shared(23, 19, 6), dh_shared(23, 8, 15))
=> (8, 19, 2, 2)
--- check test | Both sides always agree, for 200 random secrets in a real-sized group
(lambda R, p: all((lambda a, b: dh_shared(p, dh_public(3, p, b), a) == dh_shared(p, dh_public(3, p, a), b))(R.randrange(2, p - 1), R.randrange(2, p - 1)) for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(14), 2 ** 127 - 1)
--- check case | The example: Mallory holds both keys
mitm(5, 23, 6, 15, 13)
=> (18, 7, 18, 7)
--- check test | In a big group, the two honest keys differ and Mallory matches each one
(lambda r: r[0] == r[2] and r[1] == r[3] and r[0] != r[1])(mitm(3, 2 ** 127 - 1, 123456789, 987654321, 555555555))

+++ practice | HKDF, the key derivation function of TLS
--- task
TLS 1.3 derives all its keys with **HKDF** (RFC 5869), built from HMAC-SHA-256. Write it with the `hmac` and `hashlib` modules.

- `hkdf_extract(salt, ikm)` returns `HMAC(key=salt, message=ikm)`, a 32-byte "pseudo-random key". An empty salt means 32 zero bytes.
- `hkdf_expand(prk, info, length)` returns the first `length` bytes of T(1) + T(2) + …, where T(0) is empty and T(i) = `HMAC(key=prk, message=T(i-1) + info + bytes([i]))`. Raise `ValueError` if `length` is more than 255 · 32.
- `hkdf(salt, ikm, info, length)` does both.

The checks include the first test case of RFC 5869, so a correct answer matches the standard exactly.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


def hkdf_extract(salt, ikm):
    return hashlib.sha256(salt + ikm).digest()


def hkdf_expand(prk, info, length):
    return prk[:length]


def hkdf(salt, ikm, info, length):
    return hkdf_expand(hkdf_extract(salt, ikm), info, length)
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def hkdf_extract(salt, ikm):
    if not salt:
        salt = bytes(32)
    return hmac.new(salt, ikm, hashlib.sha256).digest()


def hkdf_expand(prk, info, length):
    if length > 255 * 32:
        raise ValueError("HKDF can produce at most 8160 bytes")
    out = b""
    block = b""
    i = 1
    while len(out) < length:
        block = hmac.new(prk, block + info + bytes([i]), hashlib.sha256).digest()
        out += block
        i += 1
    return out[:length]


def hkdf(salt, ikm, info, length):
    return hkdf_expand(hkdf_extract(salt, ikm), info, length)
--- hint
Extract is one HMAC call with the salt as the key. Expand chains blocks: each block is an HMAC of the previous block, the info and a one-byte counter starting at 1.
--- hint
Keep going until you have at least `length` bytes, then cut to exactly `length`. The counter is `bytes([i])`, so it cannot pass 255: that is where the length limit comes from.
--- check case | RFC 5869 test case 1: the pseudo-random key
hkdf_extract(bytes(range(13)), bytes([0x0b] * 22)).hex()
=> "077709362c2e32df0ddc3f0dc47bba6390b6c73bb50f9c3122ec844ad7c2b3e5"
--- check case | RFC 5869 test case 1: 42 bytes of output
hkdf(bytes(range(13)), bytes([0x0b] * 22), bytes(range(0xf0, 0xfa)), 42).hex()
=> "3cb25f25faacd57a90434f64d0362f2a2d2d0a90cf1a5a4c5db02d56ecc4c5bf34007208d5b887185865"
--- check test | An empty salt is 32 zero bytes, and lengths of 0, 32 and 33 work
hkdf_extract(b"", b"k") == hkdf_extract(bytes(32), b"k") and hkdf_expand(b"p" * 32, b"", 0) == b"" and len(hkdf_expand(b"p" * 32, b"x", 32)) == 32 and hkdf_expand(b"p" * 32, b"x", 33)[:32] == hkdf_expand(b"p" * 32, b"x", 32)
--- check test | The largest length works and one more is refused
len(hkdf_expand(b"p" * 32, b"i", 8160)) == 8160 and raises(ValueError, lambda: hkdf_expand(b"p" * 32, b"i", 8161))

+++ practice | Checking a certificate chain
--- task
A server usually sends a **chain**: its own certificate, then the intermediate CA's certificate that signed it, and so on. Write `check_chain(chain, roots, hostname, now)` that returns `"ok"` or the first problem found.

Each certificate is a tuple `(subject, issuer, n, e, not_before, not_after, is_ca, signature)`. The signature is the issuer's RSA signature over `body(cert)`, a helper in the starter, and `verify(n, e, data, sig)` checks it. `roots` maps the name of each trusted root CA to its public key `(n, e)`. `chain[0]` is the server's certificate; each next one should be the issuer of the one before.

Check in this order, and return the first failure:

1. `"empty"` if the chain is empty.
2. For each certificate, in order from `chain[0]`: `"expired"` if `now` is before `not_before` or after `not_after`.
3. For each certificate except the last, in order: `"bad issuer"` if its issuer is not the next certificate's subject; `"not a CA"` if the next certificate's `is_ca` is false; `"bad signature"` if its signature does not verify with the next certificate's `n` and `e`.
4. For the last certificate: `"untrusted"` if its issuer is not in `roots`; `"bad signature"` if its signature does not verify with that root's key.
5. `"wrong name"` if `chain[0]`'s subject is not `hostname`, ignoring case.

Otherwise return `"ok"`. The starter's `make_key` and `issue` helpers are used by the checks; keep them.
--- starter
import hashlib


def body(cert):
    return repr(cert[:7]).encode()


def verify(n, e, data, sig):
    return pow(sig, e, n) == int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(data).digest(), "big") % n


def make_key(p, q, e=65537):
    return {"n": p * q, "e": e, "d": pow(e, -1, (p - 1) * (q - 1))}


def issue(subject, key, issuer, issuer_key, not_before, not_after, is_ca):
    cert = (subject, issuer, key["n"], key["e"], not_before, not_after, is_ca)
    h = int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(body(cert + (0,))).digest(), "big") % issuer_key["n"]
    return cert + (pow(h, issuer_key["d"], issuer_key["n"]),)


def check_chain(chain, roots, hostname, now):
    return "ok"
--- solution
import hashlib


def body(cert):
    return repr(cert[:7]).encode()


def verify(n, e, data, sig):
    return pow(sig, e, n) == int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(data).digest(), "big") % n


def make_key(p, q, e=65537):
    return {"n": p * q, "e": e, "d": pow(e, -1, (p - 1) * (q - 1))}


def issue(subject, key, issuer, issuer_key, not_before, not_after, is_ca):
    cert = (subject, issuer, key["n"], key["e"], not_before, not_after, is_ca)
    h = int.from_bytes(hashlib.sha256(body(cert + (0,))).digest(), "big") % issuer_key["n"]
    return cert + (pow(h, issuer_key["d"], issuer_key["n"]),)


def check_chain(chain, roots, hostname, now):
    if not chain:
        return "empty"
    for cert in chain:
        if now < cert[4] or now > cert[5]:
            return "expired"
    for cert, parent in zip(chain, chain[1:]):
        if cert[1] != parent[0]:
            return "bad issuer"
        if not parent[6]:
            return "not a CA"
        if not verify(parent[2], parent[3], body(cert), cert[7]):
            return "bad signature"
    last = chain[-1]
    if last[1] not in roots:
        return "untrusted"
    n, e = roots[last[1]]
    if not verify(n, e, body(last), last[7]):
        return "bad signature"
    if chain[0][0].lower() != hostname.lower():
        return "wrong name"
    return "ok"
--- hint
Go through the rules in the order given, returning as soon as one fails. `zip(chain, chain[1:])` gives each certificate paired with the one after it, its claimed issuer.
--- hint
The fields by position: 0 subject, 1 issuer, 2 n, 3 e, 4 not_before, 5 not_after, 6 is_ca, 7 signature. The last certificate is checked against `roots[its issuer]`.
--- check case | A good chain: server, intermediate, root
(lambda R, I, S: (lambda root, inter, leaf: check_chain([leaf, inter], {"Root X": (R["n"], R["e"])}, "gs.orbit.example", 150))(None, issue("Inter Y", I, "Root X", R, 0, 1000, True), issue("gs.orbit.example", S, "Inter Y", I, 100, 200, False)))(make_key(2 ** 89 - 1, 2 ** 107 - 1), make_key(2 ** 61 - 1, 2 ** 89 - 1), make_key(2 ** 31 - 1, 2 ** 61 - 1))
=> "ok"
--- check case | Expired, and not yet valid
(lambda R, S: (check_chain([issue("a.example", S, "Root X", R, 100, 200, False)], {"Root X": (R["n"], R["e"])}, "a.example", 201), check_chain([issue("a.example", S, "Root X", R, 100, 200, False)], {"Root X": (R["n"], R["e"])}, "a.example", 99), check_chain([issue("a.example", S, "Root X", R, 100, 200, False)], {"Root X": (R["n"], R["e"])}, "A.Example", 200)))(make_key(2 ** 89 - 1, 2 ** 107 - 1), make_key(2 ** 31 - 1, 2 ** 61 - 1))
=> ("expired", "expired", "ok")
--- check case | A chain whose middle certificate is not a CA
(lambda R, I, S: check_chain([issue("gs.orbit.example", S, "Inter Y", I, 0, 9, False), issue("Inter Y", I, "Root X", R, 0, 9, False)], {"Root X": (R["n"], R["e"])}, "gs.orbit.example", 5))(make_key(2 ** 89 - 1, 2 ** 107 - 1), make_key(2 ** 61 - 1, 2 ** 89 - 1), make_key(2 ** 31 - 1, 2 ** 61 - 1))
=> "not a CA"
--- check case | Issuer names that do not link up, and an unknown root
(lambda R, I, S: (check_chain([issue("gs.orbit.example", S, "Inter Z", I, 0, 9, False), issue("Inter Y", I, "Root X", R, 0, 9, True)], {"Root X": (R["n"], R["e"])}, "gs.orbit.example", 5), check_chain([issue("gs.orbit.example", S, "Root Q", R, 0, 9, False)], {"Root X": (R["n"], R["e"])}, "gs.orbit.example", 5)))(make_key(2 ** 89 - 1, 2 ** 107 - 1), make_key(2 ** 61 - 1, 2 ** 89 - 1), make_key(2 ** 31 - 1, 2 ** 61 - 1))
=> ("bad issuer", "untrusted")
--- check case | A forged signature in the chain, and one at the root
(lambda R, I, S, M: (check_chain([issue("gs.orbit.example", S, "Inter Y", M, 0, 9, False), issue("Inter Y", I, "Root X", R, 0, 9, True)], {"Root X": (R["n"], R["e"])}, "gs.orbit.example", 5), check_chain([issue("gs.orbit.example", S, "Root X", M, 0, 9, False)], {"Root X": (R["n"], R["e"])}, "gs.orbit.example", 5)))(make_key(2 ** 89 - 1, 2 ** 107 - 1), make_key(2 ** 61 - 1, 2 ** 89 - 1), make_key(2 ** 31 - 1, 2 ** 61 - 1), make_key(2 ** 31 - 1, 2 ** 107 - 1))
=> ("bad signature", "bad signature")
--- check case | A valid chain for another name, and an empty chain
(lambda R, S: (check_chain([issue("evil.example", S, "Root X", R, 0, 9, False)], {"Root X": (R["n"], R["e"])}, "gs.orbit.example", 5), check_chain([], {}, "x", 0)))(make_key(2 ** 89 - 1, 2 ** 107 - 1), make_key(2 ** 31 - 1, 2 ** 61 - 1))
=> ("wrong name", "empty")

+++ practice | Does the name match the certificate?
--- task
A certificate may name a host exactly, like `gs.orbit.example`, or with a **wildcard** in its first label, like `*.orbit.example`. Write `name_matches(pattern, host)` following the rules browsers use (RFC 6125):

- Compare without regard to case, and ignore one trailing dot on either name.
- Without a `*`, the names must be equal.
- A `*` is allowed only as the **whole first label** of the pattern, and the pattern must have at least three labels (`*.orbit.example` is allowed, `*.example` is not). Any other use of `*` matches nothing.
- A wildcard matches exactly **one** label, which must not be empty: `*.orbit.example` matches `gs.orbit.example`, but not `orbit.example` and not `a.b.orbit.example`.

Return `True` or `False`. Empty names match nothing.
--- starter
def name_matches(pattern, host):
    if pattern.startswith("*."):
        return host.endswith(pattern[1:])
    return pattern == host
--- solution
def name_matches(pattern, host):
    pattern = pattern.lower()
    host = host.lower()
    if pattern.endswith("."):
        pattern = pattern[:-1]
    if host.endswith("."):
        host = host[:-1]
    if not pattern or not host:
        return False
    p_labels = pattern.split(".")
    h_labels = host.split(".")
    if "*" not in pattern:
        return p_labels == h_labels
    if p_labels[0] != "*" or "*" in pattern[1:] or len(p_labels) < 3:
        return False
    return len(h_labels) == len(p_labels) and h_labels[0] != "" and h_labels[1:] == p_labels[1:]
--- hint
Normalise first: lower case, one trailing dot removed. Then split both names into labels and compare label lists, not strings.
--- hint
A valid wildcard pattern has `"*"` as its first label and nowhere else, and at least three labels. Then the host must have the same number of labels, a non-empty first label, and the same labels after it.
--- check case | Exact names, with case and a trailing dot
(name_matches("gs.orbit.example", "GS.Orbit.Example."), name_matches("gs.orbit.example", "gs2.orbit.example"))
=> (True, False)
--- check case | A wildcard covers exactly one label
(name_matches("*.orbit.example", "gs.orbit.example"), name_matches("*.orbit.example", "orbit.example"), name_matches("*.orbit.example", "a.b.orbit.example"))
=> (True, False, False)
--- check case | Not a suffix match: the label boundary matters
(name_matches("*.orbit.example", "gs.notorbit.example"), name_matches("*.orbit.example", "gsorbit.example"))
=> (False, False)
--- check case | Wildcards that are not allowed match nothing
(name_matches("*.example", "orbit.example"), name_matches("g*.orbit.example", "gs.orbit.example"), name_matches("gs.*.example", "gs.orbit.example"), name_matches("*.*.example", "a.b.example"))
=> (False, False, False, False)
--- check case | Empty names and empty labels
(name_matches("", ""), name_matches("*.orbit.example", ".orbit.example"), name_matches("gs.orbit.example", ""))
=> (False, False, False)

+++ practice | Debug: the record that could be played twice
--- task
After the handshake, TLS protects each **record** of data. In this simplified version, `seal(key, seq, data)` returns `data` followed by a 32-byte tag, `HMAC(key, seq as 8 big-endian bytes + data)`. The receiver, `Opener(key)`, keeps the sequence number it expects next, starting at 0; `open(record)` checks the tag for that number, returns the data, and adds one to the number. A wrong tag raises `ValueError`.

Because the sequence number is part of every tag, a record that is replayed, dropped or moved to another place fails its check. But the receiver below accepts a replayed record: an attacker who resends yesterday's `"unlock hatch"` gets it executed twice. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


def seal(key, seq, data):
    tag = hmac.new(key, seq.to_bytes(8, "big") + data, hashlib.sha256).digest()
    return data + tag


class Opener:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.seq = 0

    def open(self, record):
        data, tag = record[:-32], record[-32:]
        expected = hmac.new(self.key, data, hashlib.sha256).digest()
        if not hmac.compare_digest(tag, expected) and not hmac.compare_digest(tag, seal(self.key, self.seq, data)[-32:]):
            raise ValueError("bad record")
        self.seq += 1
        return data
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


def seal(key, seq, data):
    tag = hmac.new(key, seq.to_bytes(8, "big") + data, hashlib.sha256).digest()
    return data + tag


class Opener:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.seq = 0

    def open(self, record):
        data, tag = record[:-32], record[-32:]
        if len(record) < 32 or not hmac.compare_digest(tag, seal(self.key, self.seq, data)[-32:]):
            raise ValueError("bad record")
        self.seq += 1
        return data
--- hint
The condition accepts a record when **either** of two tags matches. Which of the two leaves the sequence number out?
--- hint
Accept a record only if its tag matches the tag for the expected sequence number. Remove the other test (and reject records shorter than a tag).
--- check test | Records in order are accepted
(lambda o: o.open(seal(b"k", 0, b"a")) == b"a" and o.open(seal(b"k", 1, b"b")) == b"b" and o.seq == 2)(Opener(b"k"))
--- check test | A replayed record is refused
(lambda o, r: o.open(r) == b"unlock hatch" and raises(ValueError, lambda: o.open(r)))(Opener(b"k"), seal(b"k", 0, b"unlock hatch"))
--- check test | Swapped or skipped records are refused, and so is a changed one
(lambda o: raises(ValueError, lambda: o.open(seal(b"k", 1, b"second"))))(Opener(b"k")) and (lambda o: raises(ValueError, lambda: o.open(b"X" + seal(b"k", 0, b"data")[1:])))(Opener(b"k")) and (lambda o: raises(ValueError, lambda: o.open(seal(b"other key", 0, b"data"))))(Opener(b"k"))
--- check test | A record made without a sequence number is refused
(lambda o: raises(ValueError, lambda: o.open(b"cmd" + hmac.new(b"k", b"cmd", hashlib.sha256).digest())))(Opener(b"k"))

+++ practice | A protected channel over a byte stream
--- task
Put the record layer on a TCP byte stream. Both sides share a 32-byte `key`. Write a class `SecureChannel(key)` with two independent halves, one for sending and one for receiving, each with its own sequence number starting at 0.

**Sending**, `protect(data, max_record)`, splits `data` into pieces of at most `max_record` bytes (an empty `data` sends nothing) and returns the bytes of their records, one after another. For the piece with sequence number `seq`:

- the **keystream** is `HMAC(key, b"ks" + seq as 8 big-endian bytes + counter as 4 big-endian bytes)` for counter 0, 1, 2, …, joined, cut to the piece's length;
- the **ciphertext** is the piece XOR the keystream, byte by byte;
- the **tag** is `HMAC(key, b"tag" + seq as 8 bytes + ciphertext)`;
- the **record** is the ciphertext's length as 2 big-endian bytes, then the ciphertext, then the 32-byte tag.

**Receiving**, `feed(data)`, adds bytes from the stream to a buffer and returns the plaintext of every record now complete, joined into one `bytes` value. It checks each tag with the expected sequence number (comparing with `hmac.compare_digest`) and raises `ValueError` on a bad one; after an error, the channel is broken and every later `feed` raises `ValueError` too.

For example, two `SecureChannel` objects with the same key form a working channel: `b.feed(a.protect(b"burn 3.2 s", 4))` is `b"burn 3.2 s"`.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


class SecureChannel:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key

    def protect(self, data, max_record):
        return data

    def feed(self, data):
        return data
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


class SecureChannel:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.send_seq = 0
        self.recv_seq = 0
        self.buf = b""
        self.broken = False

    def keystream(self, seq, length):
        out = b""
        counter = 0
        while len(out) < length:
            block = b"ks" + seq.to_bytes(8, "big") + counter.to_bytes(4, "big")
            out += hmac.new(self.key, block, hashlib.sha256).digest()
            counter += 1
        return out[:length]

    def tag(self, seq, ciphertext):
        return hmac.new(self.key, b"tag" + seq.to_bytes(8, "big") + ciphertext, hashlib.sha256).digest()

    def protect(self, data, max_record):
        out = b""
        for i in range(0, len(data), max_record):
            piece = data[i:i + max_record]
            cipher = bytes(x ^ y for x, y in zip(piece, self.keystream(self.send_seq, len(piece))))
            out += len(cipher).to_bytes(2, "big") + cipher + self.tag(self.send_seq, cipher)
            self.send_seq += 1
        return out

    def feed(self, data):
        if self.broken:
            raise ValueError("the channel is broken")
        self.buf += data
        plain = b""
        while len(self.buf) >= 2:
            size = int.from_bytes(self.buf[:2], "big")
            if len(self.buf) < 2 + size + 32:
                break
            cipher = self.buf[2:2 + size]
            tag = self.buf[2 + size:2 + size + 32]
            if not hmac.compare_digest(tag, self.tag(self.recv_seq, cipher)):
                self.broken = True
                raise ValueError("bad record")
            plain += bytes(x ^ y for x, y in zip(cipher, self.keystream(self.recv_seq, size)))
            self.recv_seq += 1
            self.buf = self.buf[2 + size + 32:]
        return plain
--- hint
Write two helpers first: one that builds the keystream for a sequence number and a length, and one that computes the tag. XOR two byte strings with `bytes(x ^ y for x, y in zip(a, b))`; doing it again with the same keystream decrypts.
--- hint
`feed` is the length-prefix reader from lesson 13's practice, with a fixed 32-byte tag after each body: wait for `2 + size + 32` bytes, check the tag for the expected sequence number before decrypting, then move on.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda a, b: b.feed(a.protect(b"burn 3.2 s", 4)))(SecureChannel(bytes(32)), SecureChannel(bytes(32)))
=> b"burn 3.2 s"
--- check case | The exact record, so the format follows the specification
SecureChannel(b"k" * 32).protect(b"hi", 10).hex()
=> "0002" + bytes(x ^ y for x, y in zip(b"hi", hmac.new(b"k" * 32, b"ks" + bytes(12), hashlib.sha256).digest())).hex() + hmac.new(b"k" * 32, b"tag" + bytes(8) + bytes(x ^ y for x, y in zip(b"hi", hmac.new(b"k" * 32, b"ks" + bytes(12), hashlib.sha256).digest())), hashlib.sha256).digest().hex()
--- check test | The ciphertext does not contain the plaintext
(lambda r: b"telemetry frame" not in r and len(r) == 2 + 15 + 32)(SecureChannel(bytes(32)).protect(b"telemetry frame", 100))
--- check test | Long records use several keystream blocks; any cutting of the stream works
(lambda R: (lambda a, b, msg: (lambda stream: (lambda cuts: b"".join(b.feed(stream[x:y]) for x, y in zip(cuts, cuts[1:])) == msg)([0] + sorted(R.sample(range(1, len(stream)), 30)) + [len(stream)]))(a.protect(msg, 100)))(SecureChannel(b"x" * 32), SecureChannel(b"x" * 32), bytes(R.randrange(256) for _ in range(1000))))(__import__("random").Random(15))
--- check test | Sequence numbers carry on across calls, and an empty message sends nothing
(lambda a, b: a.protect(b"", 5) == b"" and b.feed(a.protect(b"one", 5)) == b"one" and b.feed(a.protect(b"two", 5)) == b"two")(SecureChannel(b"s" * 32), SecureChannel(b"s" * 32))
--- check test | A replayed record breaks the channel, and it stays broken
(lambda a, b: (lambda r: b.feed(r) == b"open" and raises(ValueError, lambda: b.feed(r)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: b.feed(a.protect(b"x", 5))))(a.protect(b"open", 10)))(SecureChannel(b"s" * 32), SecureChannel(b"s" * 32))
--- check test | A flipped bit or a wrong key is caught
(lambda a, b: (lambda r: raises(ValueError, lambda: b.feed(r[:3] + bytes([r[3] ^ 1]) + r[4:])))(a.protect(b"data", 10)))(SecureChannel(b"s" * 32), SecureChannel(b"s" * 32)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: SecureChannel(b"t" * 32).feed(SecureChannel(b"s" * 32).protect(b"data", 10)))

+++ question | What stops the man in the middle
--- ask
Mallory sits between a client and `gs.orbit.example`. She replaces the server's key share with her own, and forwards the real certificate. Which check makes the client notice?
--- choice
The client's Finished, because Mallory cannot compute the MAC.
--- choice correct
The CertificateVerify: the real server signed a transcript containing its own share, the client's transcript contains Mallory's, and Mallory cannot sign the changed transcript without the server's private key.
--- choice
The certificate's signature, because the CA's signature covers the key shares.
--- choice
Nothing: Diffie–Hellman is secure against eavesdroppers, so it is secure against Mallory too.
--- why
The certificate binds the name to the server's public key, and CertificateVerify binds that key to this handshake's transcript, including both shares. Mallory can compute Finished MACs for her own half of the exchange, so Finished alone would not stop her. And Diffie–Hellman on its own resists only a passive eavesdropper, not someone who can change messages.

+++ question | Forward secrecy
--- ask
An attacker records all the TLS 1.3 traffic of a ground station for a year, then steals the server's private key. What can the attacker decrypt?
--- choice
Every recorded session, since the private key protects them all.
--- choice
Only the sessions of the last day, since keys are rotated daily.
--- choice correct
None of the recorded sessions: their keys came from ephemeral Diffie–Hellman secrets that were thrown away; the stolen key can only be used to impersonate the server from now on.
--- choice
Only the certificates, which were encrypted with the private key.
--- why
In TLS 1.3 the long-term key only signs the handshake. The session keys come from a fresh a and b per connection, which never leave memory and are deleted after the handshake. With the private key the attacker can pretend to be the server to new clients, until the certificate is revoked, but cannot go back in time.

+++ question | Skipping the name check
--- ask
A client verifies the whole certificate chain up to a trusted root, and all the signatures, but forgets to compare the certificate's name with the host it wanted. What can an attacker do?
--- choice
Nothing, because every certificate in the chain is valid.
--- choice correct
Present a perfectly valid certificate for a domain the attacker owns, such as `evil.example`, and be accepted as `bank.example`.
--- choice
Only read the traffic, not change it.
--- choice
Only attack clients whose clocks are wrong.
--- why
A CA will happily issue a certificate for `evil.example` to whoever owns it. The chain proves the key belongs to some name; only the name check proves it belongs to the name you asked for. This bug has been found in real libraries and apps many times.

=== net-15 | Latency and bandwidth: delays, the bandwidth-delay product and queues
--- teach
Every lesson so far has said "then the packet arrives", and TCP, congestion control and TLS all counted round trips. This last lesson puts numbers on that time. Where does it go? Why can a fast link still give a slow download? Why does a network feel fine at 70% load and terrible at 95%? And how should you measure it? These questions decide how real systems are designed, from data centres to links with spacecraft.

### A motorway with a toll booth

Picture lorries driving from one city to another. The **speed limit** decides how long the trip takes once a lorry is on its way. The **number of lanes** decides how many lorries per minute the road carries. At a **toll booth** each lorry takes a while to pay, and when lorries arrive faster than the booth serves them, a **queue** forms. A long convoy also takes time just to pass the booth, lorry after lorry. Networks have all four of these.

### The four delays

A packet of L bits crossing one link of rate R bits per second, and length d metres, meets:

1. **Transmission delay**, L / R: the time to push all its bits onto the wire, one after another. 12,000 bits at 100 Mbit/s take 120 microseconds.
2. **Propagation delay**, d / s: the time for a bit to travel the length of the link. Signals travel at about s = 200,000 km/s in fibre and copper, two thirds of the speed of light, and at the full speed of light, about 300,000 km/s, through vacuum. 1,000 km of fibre take 5 milliseconds.
3. **Queueing delay**: the time spent waiting in a router's queue behind other packets. It can be zero or huge, and it changes from moment to moment.
4. **Processing delay**: the time a router takes to look at the header and pick the output. Usually microseconds.

The first two are easy to mix up. Transmission depends on the packet's **size** and the link's **rate**; propagation depends on the link's **length**. Neither depends on the other.

```python
bits = 1500 * 8
rate = 10_000_000              # 10 Mbit/s
length_m = 2_000_000           # 2,000 km of fibre
speed = 200_000_000            # metres per second
print("transmission", bits / rate * 1000, "ms")          # 1.2 ms
print("propagation", length_m / speed * 1000, "ms")      # 10.0 ms
```

### Store-and-forward, and pipelining

A router receives a whole packet before it forwards any of it, called **store-and-forward**. So one packet crossing N links of rate R pays the transmission delay on **every** link: N · L/R, plus the propagation delays.

Now send a message as P packets over those N links. Packet 1 arrives after N · L/R (ignoring propagation). But the links work at the same time: while packet 1 crosses link 2, packet 2 crosses link 1. Packet k leaves the first link at time k · L/R, and then needs N − 1 more link times, so the last packet arrives at

(P + N − 1) · L/R, plus the propagation delays.

**Why:** by induction on the link number j, packet k finishes crossing link j at time (k + j − 1) · L/R. It holds for j = 1, since the first link sends packets back to back. For link j + 1, packet k can start once it has finished link j, at time (k + j − 1) · L/R, and once packet k − 1 has finished link j + 1, at time (k − 1 + j) · L/R by the same claim for packet k − 1. Both are the same moment, so it finishes at (k + j) · L/R. Putting k = P and j = N gives the formula.

Cutting a message into packets therefore saves time over many hops: one 10-packet message over 5 links takes 14 packet times, not 50. Smaller packets pipeline better, but each carries a header, so there is a best size (a practice problem finds it).

### Latency and bandwidth are different things

**Latency** is how long one bit takes to get there. **Bandwidth** is how many bits per second get through. A lorry full of hard disks driving for a day has enormous bandwidth and terrible latency.

For a transfer of S bits that fits in the window, a first estimate is

time ≈ one round trip + S / R.

For a small web request, S / R is tiny and the round trips are everything: a 10 KB page on a 1 Gbit/s link with a 100 ms round trip spends 0.08 ms transmitting and hundreds of milliseconds on round trips (DNS, TCP, TLS, the request). Buying more bandwidth does not help at all. For a 10 GB file the opposite is true. So always ask which of the two a task is **bound** by.

### The bandwidth-delay product

How many bits are "in the pipe" at once, sent but not yet acknowledged, when a sender keeps a link of rate R busy and the round trip is RTT? Exactly

**bandwidth-delay product** = R × RTT.

A 100 Mbit/s path with an 80 ms round trip holds 8,000,000 bits, which is 1,000,000 bytes, in flight.

Lessons 8 to 10 all had a window W of unacknowledged data. Here is the rule that ties them together:

throughput = min(R, W / RTT).

**Why:** the sender may have at most W bytes unacknowledged. The ACK for a byte comes back one RTT after the byte was sent, so in any stretch of one RTT the sender can send at most W bytes: throughput ≤ W / RTT. It also cannot beat the link: throughput ≤ R. When W ≥ R × RTT, ACKs return before the window runs out, and the link never has to wait: throughput = R. So a sender needs a window of at least one bandwidth-delay product to fill a path. That is why TCP needed window scaling (lesson 9), and why BBR keeps one BDP in flight (lesson 10).

### Queues

A router's output link serves packets one at a time, first in, first out (**FIFO**). Packet i arrives at time aᵢ and needs sᵢ = Lᵢ / R seconds to transmit. It starts when it has arrived **and** the packet before it has left:

startᵢ = max(aᵢ, finishᵢ₋₁), and finishᵢ = startᵢ + sᵢ.

Its queueing delay is startᵢ − aᵢ. The recurrence works whatever the arrival pattern, and each packet costs a constant amount of work, so a whole trace takes O(n) time.

```python
arrivals = [0, 1, 2, 10]       # microseconds
service = 3                    # every packet takes 3 microseconds to send
finish = 0
for a in arrivals:
    start = max(a, finish)
    finish = start + service
    print("arrive", a, "wait", start - a, "leave", finish)
```

The third packet waits 4 microseconds behind the first two; the fourth finds the link idle.

A router has room for only so many waiting packets, its **buffer**. A packet that arrives to a full buffer is dropped, **drop-tail**, and that drop is the loss signal of lesson 10.

### Utilisation and why delay explodes

The **utilisation** ρ is the fraction of time the link is busy: the arrival rate λ (packets per second) divided by the service rate μ (packets per second the link can send). If ρ > 1, the queue grows without limit.

Below 1, how long the queue gets depends on how **bursty** the arrivals are. Perfectly regular arrivals, one every 1/λ seconds, never queue at all. Random arrivals do: for the classic model of random (Poisson) arrivals and random service times, the **[[M/M/1 queue|mm1]]** from probability theory, the average time a packet spends in the system is

T = 1 / (μ − λ) = (1/μ) / (1 − ρ).

At ρ = 0.5, packets spend twice their service time; at 0.9, ten times; at 0.99, a hundred times. Delay does not grow steadily with load: it **explodes** as ρ approaches 1. That is why network operators upgrade links long before they are "full".

### Little's law

One law holds for **any** stable queue, whatever the arrival pattern or service order:

**L = λ · W**

where L is the average number of packets in the system, λ the average arrival rate, and W the average time each spends inside.

**Why:** draw N(t), the number inside at time t, over a long stretch of length T. The area under it can be counted two ways. Along time, it is the average height times T, that is L · T. Packet by packet, each one adds its own time inside, so the area is the sum of those times: (number of packets) · W = λ · T · W. The two counts are the same area, so L · T = λ · T · W, and L = λ · W. (Packets still inside at the ends of the stretch make a small error that shrinks as T grows.)

It is used everywhere: a server handling 500 requests per second, each taking 20 ms on average, has on average 500 × 0.02 = 10 requests in progress, so 10 threads keep up.

### Bufferbloat

Big buffers mean fewer drops, which sounds good. But a full buffer is pure delay: 1 MB of buffer in front of a 10 Mbit/s link holds 0.8 seconds of packets. TCP's loss-based congestion control keeps the buffer full, because it only slows down on a drop, so every packet waits the whole 0.8 s, and a video call through that router is unusable. This is **[[bufferbloat|bufferbloat]]**. The classic rule of thumb sizes a buffer at about one bandwidth-delay product, enough to keep the link busy while TCP recovers from halving, and no more.

### Measuring

- **ping** sends small packets and times the replies, giving the **round-trip time**. The **minimum** over many pings is close to propagation plus transmission, with no queueing: the path's floor.
- Report **percentiles**, not just the average. The median (p50) is the typical case; p99 is the time that 99% of requests beat. The average hides the slow tail, and [[at scale the tail matters|tail]]: a page that needs 100 requests is slowed by whichever of them is slowest, so most page loads meet each server's p99.
- **Jitter** is how much the delay varies from packet to packet; voice and video need it small.
- **Throughput** is bytes delivered divided by the time taken, measured at the receiver, over a transfer long enough to leave slow start behind.

### What it costs

- **The simulation** in this lesson's task does O(1) work per packet with a queue of waiting packets, so O(n) for n packets, and O(B) memory for a buffer of B packets.
- **A millisecond of latency** costs real money: exchanges and trading firms pay for the straightest fibre routes, and even for microwave links, which beat fibre because radio in air travels at nearly the speed of light.
- **Distance cannot be bought off.** Light needs about 67 ms to go halfway round the Earth; the only fix is to move the server closer, which is what CDNs do.

### Where it is used

- **Capacity planning:** keeping utilisation well below 1 on every link, because of the queueing curve.
- **Data centres:** tiny round trips (tens of microseconds) and tiny buffers, with DCTCP keeping queues short.
- **Satellite links:** a [[geostationary satellite|geo]] 35,786 km up adds about half a second to every round trip, while low-orbit constellations near 550 km add only a few milliseconds.
- **Deep space:** the Deep Space Network plans every downlink around light time. A link to Mars has an enormous bandwidth-delay product: even at a few megabits per second, a 40-minute round trip holds gigabytes, so protocols there never wait for acknowledgements the way TCP does.

**Watch out:** the classic mistakes with numbers.

- **Bits and bytes.** Link rates are in bits per second, file sizes in bytes: divide by 8, or be 8 times wrong.
- **Mega means a million.** Network rates use powers of ten: 1 Mbit/s is 1,000,000 bits per second, not 1,048,576.
- **Averages hide the tail.** Look at p99 and the maximum, not just the mean.
- **Forgetting transmission time.** On a fast link it is tiny, but on a 9,600 bit/s radio link a 1,500-byte packet takes 1.25 seconds just to send.

::: context mm1 What M/M/1 means
The name is Kendall's notation for queues: the first M says arrivals are memoryless (a Poisson process, where the time to the next arrival does not depend on how long you have already waited), the second M says service times are memoryless too (exponentially distributed), and 1 is the number of servers. It is the simplest queue with a formula for everything, which is why it is the first model engineers reach for. Real traffic is burstier than Poisson, which makes queues worse, not better.

::: context bufferbloat Too much buffering
Jim Gettys named bufferbloat in 2010 after finding that his home connection added seconds of delay whenever someone uploaded a file. Memory had become cheap, so home routers and modems shipped with huge buffers, and TCP filled them. The fix is **active queue management**: algorithms such as CoDel and FQ-CoDel drop or mark packets when packets have been waiting too long, not when the buffer is full, so TCP slows down while the queue is still short. Most Linux systems now use FQ-CoDel by default.
:::

::: context tail Why the slowest request decides
Suppose a page needs answers from 100 servers, and each server is slow (beyond its p99) one time in a hundred. The page waits for the slowest answer, so it is slow unless all 100 are fast: that happens with probability 0.99 to the power 100, about 0.37. So 63% of page loads meet at least one slow server. Jeff Dean and Luiz Barroso described this in "The Tail at Scale" (2013); it is why large services send backup requests and measure p99.9.
:::

::: context geo Why geostationary links feel slow
A geostationary satellite circles the Earth once a day at 35,786 km above the equator, so it seems to stand still in the sky and a fixed dish can point at it. But a signal must go up and down again: about 72,000 km, 240 ms at the speed of light, for one way. A request and its answer need that twice, so a round trip is about 480 ms before any queueing. TCP's slow start and TLS's handshakes each pay it again, which is why satellite providers put proxies at both ends.
:::
--- task
Write `simulate_queue(packets, rate, buffer, prop)`, a FIFO router output queue with a drop-tail buffer, followed by a link.

- `packets` is a list of `(arrival, size)` pairs, sorted by arrival time: arrival in microseconds, size in bytes. Packets with the same arrival time arrive in list order.
- The link sends `rate` bytes per microsecond, so a packet of `size` bytes takes `size // rate` microseconds to transmit (every size in the checks is a multiple of `rate`).
- `buffer` is how many packets may **wait**, not counting the one being transmitted.
- `prop` is the propagation delay in microseconds, added after transmission ends.

A packet starts transmitting when it has arrived and the link has finished every earlier accepted packet: `start = max(arrival, finish of the previous accepted packet)`. When a packet arrives, count the accepted packets that are still waiting, which are those whose start time is **after** this arrival time (a packet starting exactly at this moment is no longer waiting). A packet that finds the link free starts at once and needs no place in the buffer. A packet that must wait is dropped if that count is already `buffer`.

Return a list with one entry per packet, in order: the time its last bit reaches the far end (`start + size // rate + prop`), or `None` if it was dropped.

For example, `simulate_queue([(0, 100), (0, 100), (0, 100), (5, 100)], 10, 1, 3)` is `[13, 23, None, None]`. The first packet is sent at once and reaches the far end at 0 + 10 + 3. The second takes the one waiting place and starts at 10. The third finds that place taken. So does the fourth: at time 5 the second packet is still waiting.
--- starter
def simulate_queue(packets, rate, buffer, prop):
    return [arrival + size // rate + prop for arrival, size in packets]
--- solution
from collections import deque


def simulate_queue(packets, rate, buffer, prop):
    waiting = deque()
    free_at = 0
    out = []
    for arrival, size in packets:
        while waiting and waiting[0] <= arrival:
            waiting.popleft()
        start = max(arrival, free_at)
        if start > arrival and len(waiting) >= buffer:
            out.append(None)
            continue
        free_at = start + size // rate
        out.append(free_at + prop)
        if start > arrival:
            waiting.append(start)
    return out
--- hint
Keep the time the link becomes free, and a queue (a `collections.deque`) of the start times of accepted packets that were still waiting when they were accepted. Start times only grow, so the packets that have begun by now are at the front.
--- hint
For each arrival: first remove from the front every start time that is at or before this arrival. Then compute when this packet would start. If it would have to wait and the queue already holds `buffer` entries, drop it. Otherwise record its finish, and add its start to the queue only if it has to wait.
--- check case | No packets, and one packet on an idle link
(simulate_queue([], 10, 5, 7), simulate_queue([(4, 1500)], 100, 5, 7))
=> ([], [26])
--- check case | A burst queues up behind the first packet
simulate_queue([(0, 50), (0, 50), (0, 50), (0, 50)], 5, 10, 0)
=> [10, 20, 30, 40]
--- check case | The example from the task
simulate_queue([(0, 100), (0, 100), (0, 100), (5, 100)], 10, 1, 3)
=> [13, 23, None, None]
?? At time 5 the second packet is still waiting (it starts at 10), so the one waiting place is taken.
--- check case | A place frees up at the exact moment a packet starts
simulate_queue([(0, 100), (0, 100), (10, 100)], 10, 1, 0)
=> [10, 20, 30]
?? At time 10 the second packet starts transmitting, so it is no longer waiting, and the third may wait in its place.
--- check case | An idle gap empties the queue
simulate_queue([(0, 40), (1, 40), (100, 40), (100, 40)], 4, 1, 2)
=> [12, 22, 112, 122]
--- check case | No buffer: only packets that find the link idle get through
simulate_queue([(0, 20), (1, 20), (2, 20), (10, 20), (15, 20)], 2, 0, 1)
=> [11, None, None, 21, None]
--- check case | Packets of different sizes
simulate_queue([(0, 300), (1, 30), (2, 3000), (400, 3)], 3, 5, 10)
=> [110, 120, 1120, 1121]
--- check test | With a big enough buffer, finishing times follow the recurrence start = max(arrival, previous finish)
(lambda R: all((lambda pk: simulate_queue(pk, 2, len(pk), 5) == [f + 5 for f in __import__("itertools").accumulate(pk, lambda fin, p: max(p[0], fin) + p[1] // 2, initial=0)][1:])(sorted((R.randint(0, 500), 2 * R.randint(1, 20)) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 60)))) for _ in range(100)))(__import__("random").Random(16))
--- check test | Drops never make later packets leave earlier, and every delivery is at least arrival + transmission + propagation
(lambda R: all((lambda pk, out: all(d is None or d >= a + s // 4 + 9 for (a, s), d in zip(pk, out)) and [d for d in out if d is not None] == sorted(d for d in out if d is not None))(pk, simulate_queue(pk, 4, 3, 9)) for pk in [sorted((R.randint(0, 300), 4 * R.randint(1, 30)) for _ in range(80)) for _ in range(60)]))(__import__("random").Random(17))
--- check case | 50,000 packets in bursts of 150, with a buffer of 100, are simulated quickly
(lambda out: (len(out), out.count(None), out[-1]))(simulate_queue([(i // 150 * 630, 8) for i in range(50000)], 2, 100, 10))
=> (50000, 16317, 210000)
?? Each burst of 150 arrives at once: one packet starts, 100 wait, and 49 are dropped. The simulation must not rescan every earlier packet on each arrival.

+++ practice | One packet, many hops
--- task
A packet of `size` bytes crosses `hops` identical store-and-forward links. Each link has a rate of `rate` bits per second and a length of `length` metres, and signals travel at `speed` metres per second. Each router also spends `processing` seconds on the packet before forwarding it (not the sender, and not the final receiver: there are `hops - 1` routers).

Write `one_packet(size, rate, length, speed, hops, processing)` that returns the total time from the first bit leaving the sender to the last bit arriving, in seconds, as an exact `fractions.Fraction`. Every argument is a whole number except `processing`, which is a `Fraction`. Raise `ValueError` if `hops` is less than 1.

For example, `one_packet(1500, 10_000_000, 2_000_000, 200_000_000, 1, Fraction(0))` is `Fraction(56, 5000)`: 1.2 ms to transmit and 10 ms to travel.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def one_packet(size, rate, length, speed, hops, processing):
    return Fraction(size * 8, rate) + Fraction(length, speed)
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def one_packet(size, rate, length, speed, hops, processing):
    if hops < 1:
        raise ValueError("at least one link")
    per_link = Fraction(size * 8, rate) + Fraction(length, speed)
    return hops * per_link + (hops - 1) * processing
--- hint
On each link the packet pays its whole transmission time (store-and-forward) plus the propagation time. Remember that the size is in bytes and the rate in bits.
--- hint
Multiply the per-link time by the number of links, and add the processing delay once for each router in between.
--- check case | The example from the task
one_packet(1500, 10_000_000, 2_000_000, 200_000_000, 1, Fraction(0))
=> Fraction(56, 5000)
--- check case | Three hops with processing at two routers
one_packet(1000, 8_000_000, 200_000, 200_000_000, 3, Fraction(1, 100000))
=> Fraction(3, 1000) + Fraction(3, 1000) + Fraction(2, 100000)
--- check case | Transmission dominates on a slow radio link
one_packet(1500, 9600, 100, 300_000_000, 1, Fraction(0))
=> Fraction(5, 4) + Fraction(1, 3000000)
--- check test | Zero hops are refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: one_packet(100, 1000, 1, 1, 0, Fraction(0)))

+++ practice | Is the window big enough?
--- task
Write two functions about the bandwidth-delay product.

- `window_needed(rate, rtt_ms)` returns the smallest whole number of **bytes** a sender must be allowed to have in flight to fill a path of `rate` bits per second with a round trip of `rtt_ms` milliseconds: the bandwidth-delay product in bytes, rounded up.
- `throughput(window, rate, rtt_ms)` returns the throughput, in bits per second, of a sender whose window is `window` bytes on that path: the smaller of the link rate and the window per round trip. Return it as an exact `fractions.Fraction`.

Raise `ValueError` in either function if `rtt_ms` is not positive. For example, `window_needed(100_000_000, 80)` is `1000000`, and `throughput(65535, 100_000_000, 80)` is `Fraction(6553500, 1)`, about 6.5 Mbit/s.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def window_needed(rate, rtt_ms):
    return rate * rtt_ms


def throughput(window, rate, rtt_ms):
    return Fraction(rate)
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def window_needed(rate, rtt_ms):
    if rtt_ms <= 0:
        raise ValueError("the round trip must be positive")
    return -(-rate * rtt_ms // 8000)


def throughput(window, rate, rtt_ms):
    if rtt_ms <= 0:
        raise ValueError("the round trip must be positive")
    return min(Fraction(rate), Fraction(window * 8 * 1000, rtt_ms))
--- hint
The bandwidth-delay product in bits is `rate * rtt_ms / 1000`; in bytes, divide by 8 more. Rounding a whole-number division up can be written `-(-a // b)`.
--- hint
The window allows `window * 8` bits per round trip, which lasts `rtt_ms / 1000` seconds. The answer is the smaller of that rate and the link rate.
--- check case | The example from the task
(window_needed(100_000_000, 80), throughput(65535, 100_000_000, 80))
=> (1000000, Fraction(6553500, 1))
--- check case | Rounding up
(window_needed(1_000_000, 1), window_needed(1, 1))
=> (125, 1)
--- check case | A window bigger than the BDP gives the full link rate
(throughput(2_000_000, 100_000_000, 80), throughput(1_000_000, 100_000_000, 80))
=> (Fraction(100000000, 1), Fraction(100000000, 1))
--- check case | A round trip that does not divide evenly
throughput(1000, 10_000_000, 3)
=> Fraction(8000000, 3)
--- check test | Bad round trips are refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: window_needed(10, 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: throughput(10, 10, -5))

+++ practice | Matching ping replies
--- task
A monitoring tool sends numbered probes over UDP and records the replies. Replies can be lost, duplicated and reordered (lesson 6). Write `match_pings(sent, received)`.

- `sent` is a list of `(seq, time_ms)` for each probe sent, one per sequence number.
- `received` is a list of `(seq, time_ms)` for each reply, in the order they arrived.

Return a tuple `(rtts, lost, duplicates)`:

- `rtts` is a dict from each sequence number that got a reply to its round-trip time, computed from the **first** reply with that number;
- `lost` is the sorted list of sequence numbers with no reply;
- `duplicates` counts replies whose sequence number had already been replied to.

Ignore replies whose sequence number was never sent (they are not counted anywhere). For example, `match_pings([(1, 0), (2, 10), (3, 20)], [(2, 45), (1, 50), (2, 60)])` is `({2: 35, 1: 50}, [3], 1)`.
--- starter
def match_pings(sent, received):
    rtts = {}
    for (seq, t0), (_, t1) in zip(sent, received):
        rtts[seq] = t1 - t0
    return (rtts, [], 0)
--- solution
def match_pings(sent, received):
    sent_at = dict(sent)
    rtts = {}
    duplicates = 0
    for seq, t in received:
        if seq not in sent_at:
            continue
        if seq in rtts:
            duplicates += 1
        else:
            rtts[seq] = t - sent_at[seq]
    lost = sorted(seq for seq in sent_at if seq not in rtts)
    return (rtts, lost, duplicates)
--- hint
Replies are not in the order of the probes, so never pair them by position. Build a dict from sequence number to sending time, and look each reply up by its number.
--- hint
The first reply for a number sets its RTT; any later one is a duplicate. The lost numbers are the sent ones that never got an RTT.
--- check case | The example from the task
match_pings([(1, 0), (2, 10), (3, 20)], [(2, 45), (1, 50), (2, 60)])
=> ({2: 35, 1: 50}, [3], 1)
--- check case | Nothing lost, nothing duplicated
match_pings([(0, 0), (1, 100)], [(0, 21), (1, 119)])
=> ({0: 21, 1: 19}, [], 0)
--- check case | Everything lost
match_pings([(5, 0), (9, 1), (7, 2)], [])
=> ({}, [5, 7, 9], 0)
--- check case | Replies to probes never sent are ignored
match_pings([(1, 0)], [(99, 5), (1, 8), (1, 9), (1, 10)])
=> ({1: 8}, [], 2)
--- check case | No probes at all
match_pings([], [(1, 3)])
=> ({}, [], 0)

+++ practice | Summarising latency
--- task
Write `summary(samples)`, where `samples` is a list of round-trip times in milliseconds (whole numbers), with `None` for a lost probe. Return a dict with these keys:

- `"sent"`: the number of samples; `"lost"`: how many are `None`;
- `"min"`, `"p50"`, `"p99"` and `"max"` of the received times, using the **nearest-rank** percentile: sort the received times, and the p-th percentile is the value at position ⌈p/100 · n⌉ counting from 1, where n is the number received;
- `"jitter"`: the average of the absolute differences between consecutive received times, in the order received, as a `fractions.Fraction` (lost probes are skipped, so the differences are between neighbours among the received ones).

When fewer than 1 time was received, the four statistics are `None`; when fewer than 2 were received, `"jitter"` is `None`.
--- starter
def summary(samples):
    got = [s for s in samples if s is not None]
    return {"sent": len(samples), "lost": 0, "min": min(got), "p50": got[len(got) // 2], "p99": max(got), "max": max(got), "jitter": None}
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def summary(samples):
    got = [s for s in samples if s is not None]
    out = {"sent": len(samples), "lost": len(samples) - len(got)}
    ordered = sorted(got)
    n = len(ordered)

    def rank(p):
        return ordered[-(-p * n // 100) - 1]

    if n:
        out.update({"min": ordered[0], "p50": rank(50), "p99": rank(99), "max": ordered[-1]})
    else:
        out.update({"min": None, "p50": None, "p99": None, "max": None})
    if n >= 2:
        out["jitter"] = Fraction(sum(abs(b - a) for a, b in zip(got, got[1:])), n - 1)
    else:
        out["jitter"] = None
    return out
--- hint
The nearest-rank position is ⌈p · n / 100⌉, which in whole numbers is `-(-p * n // 100)`, counting from 1; subtract 1 for a Python index into the sorted list.
--- hint
Jitter uses the received times in their original order, not sorted: pair each with the next using `zip(got, got[1:])`.
--- check case | Ten samples with one lost
summary([20, 22, None, 21, 35, 20, 23, 22, 21, 90])
=> {"sent": 10, "lost": 1, "min": 20, "p50": 22, "p99": 90, "max": 90, "jitter": Fraction(53, 4)}
--- check case | p99 of 100 samples is the 99th smallest, not the largest
(lambda s: (s["p50"], s["p99"], s["max"]))(summary(list(range(1, 101))))
=> (50, 99, 100)
--- check case | A single sample
summary([None, 17])
=> {"sent": 2, "lost": 1, "min": 17, "p50": 17, "p99": 17, "max": 17, "jitter": None}
--- check case | Everything lost, and nothing sent
(summary([None, None]), summary([]))
=> ({"sent": 2, "lost": 2, "min": None, "p50": None, "p99": None, "max": None, "jitter": None}, {"sent": 0, "lost": 0, "min": None, "p50": None, "p99": None, "max": None, "jitter": None})
--- check case | Jitter skips lost probes and uses arrival order
summary([10, None, 30, 10])["jitter"]
=> Fraction(20, 1)

+++ practice | Debug: the negative round trip
--- task
A spacecraft's radio stamps every packet with a 16-bit millisecond counter that wraps from 65535 back to 0 (about every 65.5 seconds). The ground echoes a probe back, and `rtt_ms(sent_stamp, echoed_stamp)` should return the round-trip time in milliseconds, assuming every real round trip is shorter than 65,536 ms. Usually it works, but a few times a minute it reports a negative round trip, which crashes the plotting tool. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def rtt_ms(sent_stamp, echoed_stamp):
    return echoed_stamp - sent_stamp
--- solution
def rtt_ms(sent_stamp, echoed_stamp):
    return (echoed_stamp - sent_stamp) % 65536
--- hint
What happens when the probe is sent at counter value 65530 and the echo comes back after the counter has wrapped to 20?
--- hint
The counter counts modulo 2¹⁶, like lesson 9's sequence numbers: take the difference modulo 65536.
--- check case | No wrap
rtt_ms(1000, 1480)
=> 480
--- check case | Across the wrap
rtt_ms(65530, 20)
=> 26
--- check case | Zero, and the longest measurable round trip
(rtt_ms(7, 7), rtt_ms(1, 0))
=> (0, 65535)

+++ practice | The best packet size
--- task
A ground station sends a file of `message` bytes to a spacecraft through `hops` store-and-forward links of the same rate. Each packet carries up to `payload` bytes of the file plus a `header` of fixed size. With P = ⌈message / payload⌉ packets, the last packet arrives after (P + hops − 1) packet times, where a packet time is (payload + header) bytes on one link (ignore propagation; the last packet may be shorter, but count it as full, as senders often pad it).

Write `best_payload(message, header, hops, choices)` that returns a tuple `(payload, cost)`: the payload size from the list `choices` that minimises the cost (P + hops − 1) · (payload + header), measured in bytes sent on one link, and that cost. On a tie, choose the smaller payload. Raise `ValueError` if `choices` is empty or `message` is less than 1.

For example, `best_payload(10000, 40, 5, [100, 500, 1000, 5000])` is `(500, 12960)`: 20 packets of 540 bytes take 24 packet times.
--- starter
def best_payload(message, header, hops, choices):
    return (max(choices), message + header)
--- solution
def best_payload(message, header, hops, choices):
    if not choices or message < 1:
        raise ValueError("need a message and at least one choice")
    best = None
    for payload in sorted(choices):
        packets = -(-message // payload)
        cost = (packets + hops - 1) * (payload + header)
        if best is None or cost < best[1]:
            best = (payload, cost)
    return best
--- hint
For each choice, the number of packets is the message size divided by the payload, rounded up; then apply the cost formula.
--- hint
Go through the choices from smallest to largest and keep one only if it is strictly better, so that ties keep the smaller payload.
--- check case | The example from the task
best_payload(10000, 40, 5, [100, 500, 1000, 5000])
=> (500, 12960)
--- check case | One hop: one big packet is best
best_payload(10000, 40, 1, [100, 500, 1000, 10000])
=> (10000, 10040)
--- check case | Many hops favour smaller packets
best_payload(100000, 20, 50, [200, 500, 1000, 2000, 4000])
=> (200, 120780)
--- check case | Ties go to the smaller payload, and the order of choices does not matter
best_payload(8, 0, 1, [8, 4, 2])
=> (2, 8)
--- check test | Bad inputs are refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: best_payload(100, 10, 2, [])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: best_payload(0, 10, 2, [5]))

+++ question | A bandwidth-delay product
--- ask
A path has a rate of 100 Mbit/s and a round-trip time of 80 ms. How many **bytes** must a sender have in flight to keep it full? Type the number.
--- answer
1000000
1,000,000
1 000 000
1 MB
1MB
10^6
--- why
100,000,000 bits per second × 0.08 s = 8,000,000 bits, which is 1,000,000 bytes. A sender whose window is smaller, such as the 65,535 bytes of TCP without window scaling, gets at most window / RTT, about 6.5 Mbit/s here, however fast the link is.

+++ question | Why delay explodes
--- ask
A link is 50% busy and packets wait a little. Traffic grows until the link is 95% busy. Assuming random arrivals, what happens to the average delay?
--- choice
It roughly doubles, since the load roughly doubled.
--- choice correct
It grows about tenfold: time in the system behaves like 1 / (1 − ρ), which is 2 at ρ = 0.5 and 20 at ρ = 0.95.
--- choice
It stays the same until the link is 100% busy, then packets are dropped.
--- choice
It falls, because a busy link sends packets back to back.
--- why
With random arrivals, bursts come faster than the link can clear them, and the closer ρ is to 1, the longer each burst takes to drain. That is the queueing curve behind "upgrade before it is full". Only perfectly regular arrivals avoid queueing below 100% load.

+++ question | Little's law
--- ask
A telemetry archive server receives 200 requests per second, and each request spends 50 ms inside the server on average. On average, how many requests are inside the server at once? Type the number.
--- answer
10
10 requests
--- why
Little's law: L = λ · W = 200 per second × 0.05 s = 10. It holds whatever the arrival pattern, which is why it is the first tool for sizing thread pools and connection limits.

=== net-gate | Computer Networks: mastery gate
--- teach
This gate covers the whole course, from bytes on a wire to HTTPS: framing and error detection, Ethernet, IP and routing, UDP and reliable transport, TCP and congestion control, DNS, HTTP, sockets, TLS, and latency. Most problems combine several lessons, the way real network code does. Get ready by redoing the practice problems of the lessons that felt hardest, especially the ones about byte formats, sequence numbers that wrap, and simulations; then take the gate in one sitting, with no hints.
--- gate
pass 7
questions 10
minutes 112


+++ problem | From the wire to a socket
--- task
Write `receive_frame(frame, my_mac, my_ips, sockets)`: everything a host does with one Ethernet frame, up to handing a UDP payload to a program. `frame` is the frame's bytes from the destination address to the FCS. Check in this order, and return the first failure as the given string:

1. `"bad fcs"`: the frame is shorter than 18 bytes, or its last 4 bytes are not `zlib.crc32` of everything before them, written little-endian.
2. `"not for me"`: the destination address (bytes 0 to 5) is neither `my_mac` (a string like `"02:00:00:00:00:0a"`, in any case) nor the broadcast address `ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff`.
3. `"not ipv4"`: the EtherType (bytes 12 and 13, big-endian) is not `0x0800`.
4. `"bad ip header"`: the IP packet (the bytes between the EtherType and the FCS) is shorter than 20 bytes, its version (upper 4 bits of byte 0) is not 4, its header length (lower 4 bits of byte 0, times 4) is less than 20, or its total length (bytes 2 and 3) is less than the header length or more than the bytes present. Bytes after the total length are Ethernet padding.
5. `"bad ip checksum"`: the Internet checksum of the IP header does not verify (the ones' complement sum of its 16-bit words, with carries folded back in, must be `0xFFFF`).
6. `"not udp"`: the protocol (byte 9) is not 17.
7. `"not my ip"`: the destination address (bytes 16 to 19), in dotted form, is not in the set `my_ips`.
8. `"bad udp length"`: the UDP datagram (from the end of the IP header to the IP total length) is shorter than 8 bytes, or its length field (bytes 4 and 5) differs from its real length.
9. `"bad udp checksum"`: the UDP checksum (bytes 6 and 7) is not 0 (which means "not computed") and the Internet checksum over the pseudo-header (source IP, destination IP, a zero byte, 17, and the UDP length as 2 bytes) followed by the whole datagram does not verify.
10. `"port unreachable"`: `sockets`, a dict from `(ip, port)` to a program name, has no entry for `(destination IP, destination port)`, and none for `("0.0.0.0", destination port)`. The exact address wins over the wildcard.

Otherwise return `(name, source_ip, source_port, payload)`, with the IP in dotted form.
--- starter
import zlib


def receive_frame(frame, my_mac, my_ips, sockets):
    return "not for me"
--- solution
import zlib


def checksum16(data):
    if len(data) % 2:
        data += b"\x00"
    total = 0
    for i in range(0, len(data), 2):
        total += (data[i] << 8) | data[i + 1]
    while total >> 16:
        total = (total & 0xFFFF) + (total >> 16)
    return total


def ip_text(b):
    return ".".join(str(x) for x in b)


def receive_frame(frame, my_mac, my_ips, sockets):
    if len(frame) < 18 or zlib.crc32(frame[:-4]).to_bytes(4, "little") != frame[-4:]:
        return "bad fcs"
    dst = ":".join(f"{b:02x}" for b in frame[:6])
    if dst not in (my_mac.lower(), "ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff"):
        return "not for me"
    if int.from_bytes(frame[12:14], "big") != 0x0800:
        return "not ipv4"
    packet = frame[14:-4]
    if len(packet) < 20 or packet[0] >> 4 != 4:
        return "bad ip header"
    ihl = (packet[0] & 0x0F) * 4
    total = int.from_bytes(packet[2:4], "big")
    if ihl < 20 or total < ihl or total > len(packet):
        return "bad ip header"
    if checksum16(packet[:ihl]) != 0xFFFF:
        return "bad ip checksum"
    if packet[9] != 17:
        return "not udp"
    src_ip, dst_ip = packet[12:16], packet[16:20]
    if ip_text(dst_ip) not in my_ips:
        return "not my ip"
    udp = packet[ihl:total]
    if len(udp) < 8 or int.from_bytes(udp[4:6], "big") != len(udp):
        return "bad udp length"
    if udp[6:8] != b"\x00\x00":
        pseudo = src_ip + dst_ip + bytes([0, 17]) + len(udp).to_bytes(2, "big")
        if checksum16(pseudo + udp) != 0xFFFF:
            return "bad udp checksum"
    src_port = int.from_bytes(udp[0:2], "big")
    dst_port = int.from_bytes(udp[2:4], "big")
    name = sockets.get((ip_text(dst_ip), dst_port), sockets.get(("0.0.0.0", dst_port)))
    if name is None:
        return "port unreachable"
    return (name, ip_text(src_ip), src_port, udp[8:])

+++ problem | Finding frames in a noisy downlink
--- task
A spacecraft downlink is a stream of bytes with frames scattered in it, some damaged, with noise between them. Each frame is:

- the 4-byte attached sync marker `1A CF FC 1D`;
- a 2-byte big-endian length L of the payload;
- the L payload bytes;
- a 2-byte big-endian CRC-16 of the length field and the payload (the 2 + L bytes after the marker).

The CRC-16 is CCITT-FALSE: a 16-bit register starting at `0xFFFF`; for each byte, XOR it into the top 8 bits of the register, then do 8 steps, each shifting left by one, keeping 16 bits, and XORing in `0x1021` when the bit that fell off the top was 1. No reflection and no final XOR. (Its check value: the CRC of `b"123456789"` is `0x29B1`.)

Write `decode_stream(stream, max_len)` that returns `(payloads, bad)`: the payloads of the good frames, in order, and the number of bad candidates. Search for the marker from the start. At each marker found:

- if the length is more than `max_len`, it is a bad candidate: count it and search again from one byte after this marker's first byte;
- if the stream ends before the frame would end (including when the length field itself is cut off), skip it without counting, and search again from one byte after the marker's first byte;
- if the CRC is wrong, count it as bad and search again from one byte after the marker's first byte;
- if the CRC is right, keep the payload and search again right after the frame.

The marker's bytes can also appear inside payloads, or by chance in noise, which is why a bad candidate only moves the search on by one byte.
--- starter
def decode_stream(stream, max_len):
    return ([], 0)
--- solution
ASM = bytes.fromhex("1acffc1d")


def crc16(data):
    reg = 0xFFFF
    for byte in data:
        reg ^= byte << 8
        for _ in range(8):
            if reg & 0x8000:
                reg = ((reg << 1) ^ 0x1021) & 0xFFFF
            else:
                reg = (reg << 1) & 0xFFFF
    return reg


def decode_stream(stream, max_len):
    payloads = []
    bad = 0
    pos = 0
    while True:
        i = stream.find(ASM, pos)
        if i < 0:
            return (payloads, bad)
        if i + 6 > len(stream):
            pos = i + 1
            continue
        size = int.from_bytes(stream[i + 4:i + 6], "big")
        end = i + 6 + size + 2
        if size > max_len:
            bad += 1
            pos = i + 1
            continue
        if end > len(stream):
            pos = i + 1
            continue
        if crc16(stream[i + 4:end - 2]) == int.from_bytes(stream[end - 2:end], "big"):
            payloads.append(stream[i + 6:end - 2])
            pos = end
        else:
            bad += 1
            pos = i + 1
--- check case | Good frames, an empty payload, a damaged frame, a marker inside a payload, and a cut-off frame at the end

=> ([b"TEMP=21", b"", b"\x1a\xcf\xfc\x1dinside"], 1)
--- check case | A false marker with an impossible length hides nothing

=> ([b"ok"], 1)
--- check case | A false marker whose length runs past the end is skipped, not counted

=> ([b"hidden"], 0)
--- check case | No marker at all, and an empty stream

=> (([], 0), ([], 0))
--- check case | A stream that ends inside the length field

=> ([], 0)
--- check test | 300 frames with noise between them all come back
(lambda R: (lambda frames: (lambda stream: decode_stream(stream, 300) == (frames, 0))(b"".join(bytes(R.randrange(256) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 5))).replace(b"\x1a", b"\x00") + bytes.fromhex("1acffc1d") + len(f).to_bytes(2, "big") + f + __import__("binascii").crc_hqx(len(f).to_bytes(2, "big") + f, 0xFFFF).to_bytes(2, "big") for f in frames)))([bytes(R.randrange(256) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 40))) for _ in range(300)]))(__import__("random").Random(21))

+++ problem | Spreading flows over equal-cost paths
--- task
Routers often have several shortest paths to a destination and use all of them, called equal-cost multipath (ECMP). To keep each TCP connection's packets in order, every packet of one flow must take the same path, so the router picks the path by hashing the flow's addresses and ports.

Write two functions.

- `ecmp_routes(graph, me)`: `graph[u][v]` is the positive cost of the link from router `u` to router `v`. Return a dict from every router reachable from `me` (not `me` itself) to `(cost, first_hops)`: the cost of a shortest path, and the **sorted** list of every neighbour of `me` that begins some shortest path. Use Dijkstra's algorithm.
- `assign_flows(graph, me, flows)`: each flow is `(src_ip, src_port, dst_ip, dst_port, router)`, where `router` is where its packets must go. Return one entry per flow: `None` if the router is `me` or unreachable; otherwise the first hop at position `zlib.crc32(key) % n` of that router's sorted list of first hops, where `n` is the list's length and `key` is the text `f"{src_ip}:{src_port}>{dst_ip}:{dst_port}"` encoded as bytes.

For example, in a network where `A` reaches `F` for cost 4 both through `B` and through `C`, `ecmp_routes(graph, "A")["F"]` is `(4, ["B", "C"])`.
--- starter
import heapq
import zlib


def ecmp_routes(graph, me):
    return {}


def assign_flows(graph, me, flows):
    return [None] * len(flows)
--- solution
import heapq
import zlib


def ecmp_routes(graph, me):
    dist = {me: 0}
    hops = {me: set()}
    heap = [(0, me)]
    done = set()
    while heap:
        d, u = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if u in done:
            continue
        done.add(u)
        for v, cost in graph.get(u, {}).items():
            nd = d + cost
            first = {v} if u == me else hops[u]
            if v not in dist or nd < dist[v]:
                dist[v] = nd
                hops[v] = set(first)
                heapq.heappush(heap, (nd, v))
            elif nd == dist[v]:
                hops[v] |= first
    return {v: (dist[v], sorted(hops[v])) for v in dist if v != me}


def assign_flows(graph, me, flows):
    routes = ecmp_routes(graph, me)
    out = []
    for src_ip, src_port, dst_ip, dst_port, router in flows:
        if router not in routes:
            out.append(None)
            continue
        choices = routes[router][1]
        key = f"{src_ip}:{src_port}>{dst_ip}:{dst_port}".encode()
        out.append(choices[zlib.crc32(key) % len(choices)])
    return out
--- check case | Two equal paths to most routers; the direct link to D is not the cheapest

=> {"B": (1, ["B"]), "C": (1, ["C"]), "D": (4, ["B", "C"]), "E": (3, ["B", "C"]), "F": (4, ["B", "C"])}

=> (4, ["B", "C", "D"])
--- check case | Four paths through a double diamond still have only two first hops

--- check case | Flows are spread by their hash; unreachable and local destinations get None

--- check test | On a large grid, costs match plain Dijkstra and every first hop really starts a shortest path
(lambda n: (lambda g: (lambda r: len(r) == n * n - 1 and r[(n - 1, n - 1)][0] == 2 * (n - 1) and r[(n - 1, n - 1)][1] == [(0, 1), (1, 0)] and r[(0, 5)] == (5, [(0, 1)]))(ecmp_routes(g, (0, 0))))({(i, j): {(a, b): 1 for a, b in [(i + 1, j), (i - 1, j), (i, j + 1), (i, j - 1)] if 0 <= a < n and 0 <= b < n} for i in range(n) for j in range(n)}))(60)

+++ problem | Traceroute
--- task
The `traceroute` tool finds the routers on a path by sending probes with TTL 1, 2, 3, and so on. Each router that receives a packet not addressed to one of its own networks decrements its TTL; the router where the TTL reaches 0 drops it and reports back. Simulate the tool.

`tables` maps each router's name to its forwarding table: a list of `(prefix, target)` pairs, where `prefix` is CIDR text such as `"192.168.0.0/16"` and `target` is either the name of the next router or `"local"` (the destination network is attached to this router). A router chooses the entry with the **longest** prefix that matches the destination.

Write `traceroute(tables, start, dst, max_ttl)`. For each TTL from 1 to `max_ttl`, send a probe into router `start` with that TTL and follow it:

- at each router, look up `dst`. With no matching entry, the trace ends with the line `"<router> !N"` (network unreachable). With target `"local"`, the trace ends with `"<router> reached"`.
- otherwise decrement the TTL. If it is now 0, the router reports: add the line `"<router>"`, and go on with the next TTL. If not, forward the probe to the target router.

Return the list of lines. A routing loop shows up as routers that repeat until `max_ttl` runs out.
--- starter
def traceroute(tables, start, dst, max_ttl):
    return [start]
--- solution
def to_int(ip):
    a, b, c, d = (int(x) for x in ip.split("."))
    return (a << 24) | (b << 16) | (c << 8) | d


def lookup(table, ip):
    addr = to_int(ip)
    best = None
    for prefix, target in table:
        net, length = prefix.split("/")
        length = int(length)
        mask = ((1 << 32) - 1) ^ ((1 << (32 - length)) - 1)
        if addr & mask == to_int(net) & mask and (best is None or length > best[0]):
            best = (length, target)
    return None if best is None else best[1]


def traceroute(tables, start, dst, max_ttl):
    lines = []
    for ttl in range(1, max_ttl + 1):
        router = start
        left = ttl
        while True:
            target = lookup(tables[router], dst)
            if target is None:
                lines.append(router + " !N")
                return lines
            if target == "local":
                lines.append(router + " reached")
                return lines
            left -= 1
            if left == 0:
                lines.append(router)
                break
            router = target
    return lines

+++ problem | Following a TCP stream in a capture
--- task
A packet capture holds the segments of one TCP connection, both directions mixed, in the order they were captured, with retransmissions, overlaps and reordering. Rebuild what each side sent, as a tool like Wireshark does.

Write `follow_stream(segments)`. Each segment is `(side, seq, flags, payload)`: `side` is `"client"` or `"server"`, `seq` its 32-bit sequence number, `flags` a string of flag letters (`"S"` SYN, `"A"` ACK, `"F"` FIN, `"P"` PSH, in any combination), and `payload` its bytes.

- A side's first SYN gives its ISN: its first data byte is numbered ISN + 1, modulo 2³². Later SYNs from that side are ignored, and so is every segment from a side before its first SYN. A SYN carries no data.
- Byte `i` of a payload has number `seq + i`, and its **offset** in the stream is `(number - (ISN + 1)) mod 2³²`. Ignore bytes whose offset is 2³¹ or more (they come before the start). When several segments carry a byte with the same offset, the **first** one captured wins.
- A FIN's offset is that of the byte just after its payload.

Return `((client_bytes, client_closed), (server_bytes, server_closed))`. A side's bytes are the unbroken run of bytes from offset 0 up to the first missing offset. A side is closed when it sent a FIN and its bytes reach the FIN's offset. A side with no SYN gives `(b"", False)`.
--- starter
def follow_stream(segments):
    client = b"".join(p for side, seq, flags, p in segments if side == "client")
    server = b"".join(p for side, seq, flags, p in segments if side == "server")
    return ((client, False), (server, False))
--- solution
MOD = 2 ** 32


def follow_stream(segments):
    state = {}
    for side, seq, flags, payload in segments:
        if "S" in flags:
            if side not in state:
                state[side] = {"start": (seq + 1) % MOD, "bytes": {}, "fin": None}
            continue
        if side not in state:
            continue
        s = state[side]
        for i, b in enumerate(payload):
            offset = (seq + i - s["start"]) % MOD
            if offset < 2 ** 31 and offset not in s["bytes"]:
                s["bytes"][offset] = b
        if "F" in flags:
            offset = (seq + len(payload) - s["start"]) % MOD
            if offset < 2 ** 31:
                s["fin"] = offset
    result = []
    for side in ("client", "server"):
        s = state.get(side)
        if s is None:
            result.append((b"", False))
            continue
        out = bytearray()
        while len(out) in s["bytes"]:
            out.append(s["bytes"][len(out)])
        result.append((bytes(out), s["fin"] is not None and len(out) >= s["fin"]))
    return tuple(result)
