+++ problem | How much may the sender send?
--- task
A TCP sender may have `min(cwnd, rwnd)` bytes in flight: the congestion window and the receiver's advertised window. Track both through a series of events. Write `send_windows(isn, mss, ssthresh, rwnd, events)` that returns the list of `min(cwnd, rwnd)` after each event.

At the start, the highest ACK number received is `isn + 1` (modulo 2³²), `cwnd = mss`, the duplicate count is 0, the sender is not in recovery, and `ssthresh` and `rwnd` are as given. Each event is `("timeout",)` or `("ack", number, window)`.

For an ACK, let `d = (number - highest) mod 2³²`:

- **New ACK**, when `0 < d < 2³¹`: it acknowledges `d` bytes. If in recovery, set `cwnd = ssthresh` and leave recovery; otherwise, if `cwnd < ssthresh`, add `min(d, mss)`; otherwise add `max(1, mss * mss // cwnd)`. Then set the duplicate count to 0, `highest = number`, and `rwnd = window`.
- **Duplicate ACK**, when `d == 0` and `window == rwnd`: add 1 to the duplicate count. If in recovery, add `mss` to `cwnd`. Otherwise, if the count is now 3, set `ssthresh = max(cwnd // 2, 2 * mss)`, then `cwnd = ssthresh + 3 * mss`, and enter recovery.
- **Window update**, when `d == 0` but `window != rwnd`: set `rwnd = window` and change nothing else (it is not a duplicate, and the count stays as it is).
- **Old ACK**, any other `d`: ignore it completely.

For a timeout: `ssthresh = max(cwnd // 2, 2 * mss)`, then `cwnd = mss`, the duplicate count is 0, and the sender leaves recovery.
--- starter
def send_windows(isn, mss, ssthresh, rwnd, events):
    return [min(mss, rwnd) for _ in events]
--- solution
MOD = 2 ** 32


def send_windows(isn, mss, ssthresh, rwnd, events):
    highest = (isn + 1) % MOD
    cwnd = mss
    dupacks = 0
    recovery = False
    out = []
    for event in events:
        if event[0] == "timeout":
            ssthresh = max(cwnd // 2, 2 * mss)
            cwnd = mss
            dupacks = 0
            recovery = False
        else:
            _, number, window = event
            d = (number - highest) % MOD
            if 0 < d < 2 ** 31:
                if recovery:
                    cwnd = ssthresh
                    recovery = False
                elif cwnd < ssthresh:
                    cwnd += min(d, mss)
                else:
                    cwnd += max(1, mss * mss // cwnd)
                dupacks = 0
                highest = number
                rwnd = window
            elif d == 0 and window == rwnd:
                dupacks += 1
                if recovery:
                    cwnd += mss
                elif dupacks == 3:
                    ssthresh = max(cwnd // 2, 2 * mss)
                    cwnd = ssthresh + 3 * mss
                    recovery = True
            elif d == 0:
                rwnd = window
        out.append(min(cwnd, rwnd))
    return out
--- check case | Slow start, a window update between duplicates, fast recovery, an old ACK, a small receiver window, and a timeout

=> [2000, 3000, 4000, 4000, 4000, 4000, 5000, 6000, 7000, 7000, 2000, 1000, 2000]

=> [2000, 2000, 3000]
--- check case | The receiver's window is the limit

=> [1500, 1500, 0, 4000]
--- check case | Congestion avoidance grows by about one MSS per window

=> [2000, 2500, 2900, 3244, 3552, 3833, 4093]
--- check case | No events
send_windows(5, 1460, 10000, 65535, [])
=> []

+++ problem | The answer inside a DNS reply
--- task
A resolver has sent a query with ID `qid` and received the reply `msg` (bytes). Write `resolve_reply(msg, qid)` that returns the addresses the reply gives for the question's name, following CNAMEs inside the message, and how long they may be cached.

The message: a 12-byte header (ID; flags, where the top bit of byte 2 marks a reply and the low 4 bits of byte 3 are the response code; then the question, answer, authority and additional counts, 2 bytes each, big-endian); the questions (a name, then 2 bytes of type and 2 of class); then the answers (a name, then type, class, TTL of 4 bytes, data length, and the data). Names are length-prefixed labels ending in a zero byte, and may end in (or be) a compression pointer: two bytes whose first is 192 or more, holding a 14-bit offset into the message. Compare names without regard to case.

- Raise `ValueError` if the ID is not `qid` or the message is not a reply.
- Return `None` if the response code is not 0 (for example 3, "no such name").
- Among the answers, an A record (type 1) holds a 4-byte address, and a CNAME record (type 5) holds a name (which may use pointers). Ignore other types.
- Start from the first question's name. If it has A records, the answer is theirs. If instead it has a CNAME, move to the CNAME's target and repeat. Following more than 8 CNAMEs raises `ValueError`. If a name has neither, return `None`.
- Return `(addresses, ttl)`: the sorted list of dotted addresses, and the smallest TTL among the A records returned and every CNAME followed on the way, because the answer is only valid while every link of the chain is.
--- starter
def resolve_reply(msg, qid):
    return None
--- solution
def read_name(msg, pos):
    labels = []
    end = None
    jumps = 0
    while True:
        size = msg[pos]
        if size == 0:
            return ".".join(labels).lower(), (end if end is not None else pos + 1)
        if size >= 192:
            if end is None:
                end = pos + 2
            jumps += 1
            if jumps > 32:
                raise ValueError("pointer loop")
            pos = ((size & 0x3F) << 8) | msg[pos + 1]
            continue
        labels.append(msg[pos + 1:pos + 1 + size].decode("ascii"))
        pos += 1 + size


def resolve_reply(msg, qid):
    if int.from_bytes(msg[0:2], "big") != qid or not msg[2] & 0x80:
        raise ValueError("not the reply to this query")
    if msg[3] & 0x0F:
        return None
    qdcount = int.from_bytes(msg[4:6], "big")
    ancount = int.from_bytes(msg[6:8], "big")
    pos = 12
    qname = None
    for _ in range(qdcount):
        name, pos = read_name(msg, pos)
        qname = qname or name
        pos += 4
    records = []
    for _ in range(ancount):
        name, pos = read_name(msg, pos)
        rtype = int.from_bytes(msg[pos:pos + 2], "big")
        ttl = int.from_bytes(msg[pos + 4:pos + 8], "big")
        length = int.from_bytes(msg[pos + 8:pos + 10], "big")
        start = pos + 10
        if rtype == 1:
            records.append((name, "A", ".".join(str(b) for b in msg[start:start + 4]), ttl))
        elif rtype == 5:
            records.append((name, "CNAME", read_name(msg, start)[0], ttl))
        pos = start + length
    name = qname
    ttl = None
    for _ in range(9):
        addresses = [r for r in records if r[0] == name and r[1] == "A"]
        if addresses:
            low = min(r[3] for r in addresses)
            ttl = low if ttl is None else min(ttl, low)
            return (sorted(r[2] for r in addresses), ttl)
        alias = [r for r in records if r[0] == name and r[1] == "CNAME"]
        if not alias:
            return None
        ttl = alias[0][3] if ttl is None else min(ttl, alias[0][3])
        name = alias[0][2]
    raise ValueError("too many CNAMEs")

=> (["198.51.100.7", "198.51.100.8"], 60)
--- check case | A plain A record
resolve_reply(bytes.fromhex("000981800001000100000000026773056f72626974076578616d706c650000010001c00c0001000100000e1000040a000001"), 9)
=> (["10.0.0.1"], 3600)
--- check case | No such name, and a CNAME whose target is not in the reply

=> (None, None)
--- check test | A wrong ID, a query instead of a reply, and a CNAME loop
raises(ValueError, lambda: resolve_reply(bytes.fromhex("000981800001000100000000026773056f72626974076578616d706c650000010001c00c0001000100000e1000040a000001"), 10)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: resolve_reply(bytes.fromhex("0009" + "0100" + "000981800001000100000000026773056f72626974076578616d706c650000010001c00c0001000100000e1000040a000001"[8:]), 9)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: resolve_reply(bytes.fromhex("0001818000010002000000000161076578616d706c650000010001c00c000500010000000a00040162c00ec027000500010000000a0002c00c"), 1))

+++ problem | Untangling pipelined responses
--- task
A client pipelined several requests on one HTTP/1.1 connection, then read everything the server sent until the server closed the connection. Write `split_responses(data, methods)` that splits `data` (bytes) into one `(status, body)` pair per request. `methods` lists the requests' methods, in order.

For each request, read a response:

- The headers end at the first `\r\n\r\n`. The status is the second word of the first line, as an `int`. Header names are case-insensitive.
- A response with a 1xx status is only informational (such as `100 Continue`): it has no body, and the real response to the same request follows it. Skip it.
- There is no body if the request was `HEAD`, or the status is 204 or 304.
- Otherwise, with `Transfer-Encoding: chunked` (the value in any case), the body is chunked: a size line in hexadecimal (anything from a `;` on is an extension, to be ignored), `\r\n`, that many bytes, `\r\n`, repeated, until a size of 0, which is followed by `\r\n`.
- Otherwise, with `Content-Length`, the body is that many bytes.
- Otherwise the body is everything up to the end of `data`.

Raise `ValueError` if `data` ends inside a response, or if bytes are left over after the last request's response.
--- starter
def split_responses(data, methods):
    return [(200, b"") for _ in methods]
--- solution
def split_responses(data, methods):
    results = []
    pos = 0
    for method in methods:
        while True:
            end = data.find(b"\r\n\r\n", pos)
            if end < 0:
                raise ValueError("response cut off in its headers")
            lines = data[pos:end].decode("latin-1").split("\r\n")
            status = int(lines[0].split(" ")[1])
            headers = {}
            for line in lines[1:]:
                name, _, value = line.partition(":")
                headers[name.strip().lower()] = value.strip()
            pos = end + 4
            if 100 <= status < 200:
                continue
            break
        if method == "HEAD" or status in (204, 304):
            body = b""
        elif headers.get("transfer-encoding", "").lower() == "chunked":
            body = b""
            while True:
                eol = data.find(b"\r\n", pos)
                if eol < 0:
                    raise ValueError("chunk size cut off")
                size = int(data[pos:eol].split(b";")[0], 16)
                pos = eol + 2
                if size == 0:
                    if data[pos:pos + 2] != b"\r\n":
                        raise ValueError("bad end of chunks")
                    pos += 2
                    break
                if pos + size + 2 > len(data):
                    raise ValueError("chunk cut off")
                body += data[pos:pos + size]
                pos += size + 2
        elif "content-length" in headers:
            length = int(headers["content-length"])
            if pos + length > len(data):
                raise ValueError("body cut off")
            body = data[pos:pos + length]
            pos += length
        else:
            body = data[pos:]
            pos = len(data)
        results.append((status, body))
    if pos != len(data):
        raise ValueError("bytes left over")
    return results
--- check case | Content-Length, 100 Continue then chunked, HEAD, 304, and read-until-close
split_responses((b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: 5\r\n\r\nhello" b"HTTP/1.1 100 Continue\r\n\r\nHTTP/1.1 201 Created\r\nTransfer-Encoding: chunked\r\n\r\n4\r\nmiss\r\n3;x=1\r\nion\r\n0\r\n\r\n" b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: 999\r\n\r\n" b"HTTP/1.1 304 Not Modified\r\nETag: \"v2\"\r\n\r\n" b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nConnection: close\r\n\r\nthe rest\r\nof it"), ["GET", "POST", "HEAD", "GET", "GET"])
=> [(200, b"hello"), (201, b"mission"), (200, b""), (304, b""), (200, b"the rest\r\nof it")]
--- check case | A chunk may contain CRLFs, and header names and values can be in any case
split_responses(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\ntransfer-ENCODING: Chunked\r\n\r\n5\r\na\r\nb\n\r\n0\r\n\r\nHTTP/1.1 204 No Content\r\n\r\n", ["GET", "DELETE"])
=> [(200, b"a\r\nb\n"), (204, b"")]
--- check case | No requests and no data
split_responses(b"", [])
=> []
--- check test | Cut off inside a body, or bytes left over
raises(ValueError, lambda: split_responses(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: 9\r\n\r\nshort", ["GET"])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: split_responses(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Length: 2\r\n\r\nokEXTRA", ["GET"])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: split_responses(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nTransfer-Encoding: chunked\r\n\r\n5\r\nab", ["GET"])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: split_responses(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n", ["GET"]))

+++ problem | Records over a datagram link
--- task
TLS assumes TCP delivers records in order. Over UDP (DTLS, and the protocols that protect spacecraft links), records can be lost and reordered, so each record carries its own sequence number, and the receiver keeps a **replay window** instead of expecting one exact number.

A record is 8 bytes of sequence number (big-endian), the data, and a 32-byte tag: `HMAC-SHA256(key, the 8 sequence bytes + data)`. Write a class `ReplayGuard(key, size)`:

- `accept(record)` returns the data if the record is accepted, or `None`. First, a record shorter than 40 bytes, or with a wrong tag (compare with `hmac.compare_digest`), is **forged**: count it and reject it, changing nothing else. Then, with `highest` the largest sequence number accepted so far (none at the start):
  - a sequence number above `highest` is accepted, and becomes the new `highest`;
  - one at or below `highest - size` is **too old**: count it and reject it;
  - otherwise it is inside the window: if it was already accepted it is **replayed** (count it and reject it); if not, accept it.
- The attributes `forged`, `too_old` and `replayed` hold the three counts, starting at 0.

Only the numbers inside the window need remembering; forget older ones, so that memory stays bounded by `size`.
--- starter
import hashlib
import hmac


class ReplayGuard:
    def __init__(self, key, size):
        self.forged = 0
        self.too_old = 0
        self.replayed = 0

    def accept(self, record):
        return record[8:-32]
--- solution
import hashlib
import hmac


class ReplayGuard:
    def __init__(self, key, size):
        self.key = key
        self.size = size
        self.highest = -1
        self.seen = set()
        self.forged = 0
        self.replayed = 0
        self.too_old = 0

    def accept(self, record):
        if len(record) < 40:
            self.forged += 1
            return None
        head, data, tag = record[:8], record[8:-32], record[-32:]
        if not hmac.compare_digest(tag, hmac.new(self.key, head + data, hashlib.sha256).digest()):
            self.forged += 1
            return None
        seq = int.from_bytes(head, "big")
        if seq > self.highest:
            self.highest = seq
            self.seen = {s for s in self.seen if s > seq - self.size}
            self.seen.add(seq)
            return data
        if seq <= self.highest - self.size:
            self.too_old += 1
            return None
        if seq in self.seen:
            self.replayed += 1
            return None
        self.seen.add(seq)
        return data
--- check case | Reordering inside the window is fine; a replay and records that are too old are not
(lambda g, rec: ([g.accept(rec(b"k", s, b"d%d" % s)) for s in [0, 2, 1, 2, 7, 3, 4, 6, 5, 3, 100]], g.replayed, g.too_old, g.forged))(ReplayGuard(b"k", 4), (lambda k, s, d: s.to_bytes(8, "big") + d + __import__("hmac").new(k, s.to_bytes(8, "big") + d, "sha256").digest()))
=> ([b"d0", b"d2", b"d1", None, b"d7", None, b"d4", b"d6", b"d5", None, b"d100"], 1, 2, 0)
--- check case | Forged records change nothing, even with a high sequence number
(lambda g, rec: (g.accept(rec(b"wrong", 50, b"x")), g.accept(rec(b"k", 1, b"a")), g.accept(b"short"), g.accept(rec(b"k", 1, b"a")[:-1] + b"\x00"), g.accept(rec(b"k", 0, b"b")), g.forged, g.too_old))(ReplayGuard(b"k", 8), (lambda k, s, d: s.to_bytes(8, "big") + d + __import__("hmac").new(k, s.to_bytes(8, "big") + d, "sha256").digest()))
=> (None, b"a", None, None, b"b", 3, 0)
--- check case | The edge of the window
(lambda g, rec: (g.accept(rec(b"k", 10, b"")), g.accept(rec(b"k", 7, b"")), g.accept(rec(b"k", 6, b"")), g.too_old))(ReplayGuard(b"k", 4), (lambda k, s, d: s.to_bytes(8, "big") + d + __import__("hmac").new(k, s.to_bytes(8, "big") + d, "sha256").digest()))
=> (b"", b"", None, 1)
--- check test | Memory stays bounded while 4,992 records arrive shuffled within the window
(lambda R, g, rec: (lambda seqs: sum(g.accept(rec(b"k", s, b"")) is not None for s in seqs) == 4992 and all(len(v) <= 128 for v in vars(g).values() if isinstance(v, (set, dict, list, bytearray))))([s + d for s in range(0, 4992, 32) for d in R.sample(range(32), 32)]))(__import__("random").Random(23), ReplayGuard(b"k", 64), (lambda k, s, d: s.to_bytes(8, "big") + d + __import__("hmac").new(k, s.to_bytes(8, "big") + d, "sha256").digest()))

+++ problem | How long a page takes
--- task
Estimate how long it takes to fetch one resource over HTTPS, from typing the address to the last byte, with this model, and write `page_load_ms(rtt_ms, rate, size, mss, iw, dns_rtts, tls)`. Times are in milliseconds, `rate` in bits per second, `size` and `mss` in bytes.

- **Setup:** `dns_rtts` round trips for DNS (0 if the answer is cached), one round trip for TCP's handshake, and one more for TLS 1.3 if `tls` is true.
- **The response** takes `size` bytes, that is ⌈size / mss⌉ segments, sent in rounds of one round trip each (the first round includes sending the request). The first round sends `iw` segments, and each round after that twice as many as the one before, but never more than `cap`, the bandwidth-delay product in whole segments: `rate * rtt_ms // (8 * 1000 * mss)`, and at least 1. The last round sends whatever is left.
- **Transmission:** add, once, the time to put all `size` bytes on the link: `size * 8 / rate` seconds.

Return the total as an exact `fractions.Fraction`. Raise `ValueError` if `size` is negative or `mss` or `iw` is less than 1. For example, a 100,000-byte response with `rtt_ms` 100, `rate` 100,000,000, `mss` 1460, `iw` 10, one DNS round trip and TLS takes 608 ms: 3 setup round trips, 3 rounds (10, 20 and 40 segments cover 69), and 8 ms of transmission.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def page_load_ms(rtt_ms, rate, size, mss, iw, dns_rtts, tls):
    return Fraction(size * 8 * 1000, rate)
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def page_load_ms(rtt_ms, rate, size, mss, iw, dns_rtts, tls):
    if size < 0 or mss < 1 or iw < 1:
        raise ValueError("bad parameters")
    cap = max(1, (rate * rtt_ms) // (8 * 1000 * mss))
    segments = -(-size // mss)
    window = min(iw, cap)
    rounds = 0
    while segments > 0:
        segments -= window
        rounds += 1
        window = min(2 * window, cap)
    setup = dns_rtts + 1 + (1 if tls else 0)
    return (setup + rounds) * Fraction(rtt_ms) + Fraction(size * 8 * 1000, rate)
--- check case | The example from the task
page_load_ms(100, 100_000_000, 100_000, 1460, 10, 1, True)
=> Fraction(608, 1)
--- check case | A big download reaches the cap of the bandwidth-delay product
page_load_ms(100, 100_000_000, 10_000_000, 1460, 10, 0, True)
=> Fraction(2400, 1)
--- check case | A slow link: the cap is small, and there is no TLS
page_load_ms(40, 1_000_000, 50_000, 1000, 10, 0, False)
=> Fraction(840, 1)
--- check case | An empty response still pays for the setup
page_load_ms(50, 10_000_000, 0, 1460, 10, 2, True)
=> Fraction(200, 1)
--- check case | A cap of one segment per round
page_load_ms(10, 1_000_000, 3000, 1000, 10, 0, False)
=> Fraction(64, 1)
--- check case | A cap below the initial window, and a time that is not a whole number
page_load_ms(10, 7_000_000, 3000, 1000, 10, 0, False)
=> Fraction(164, 7)
--- check test | Bad parameters are refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: page_load_ms(10, 10, -1, 1000, 10, 0, False)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: page_load_ms(10, 10, 5, 0, 10, 0, False)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: page_load_ms(10, 10, 5, 1000, 0, 0, False))

+++ question | Bytes in a frame
--- ask
A program sends a 1,000-byte message with UDP over IPv4 over Ethernet, with no IP options. How many bytes long is the Ethernet frame, counting from the destination address to the end of the FCS? Type the number.
--- answer
1046
1,046
1046 bytes
--- why
Each layer adds its own header: 8 bytes of UDP, 20 of IPv4, and 14 of Ethernet header plus a 4-byte FCS trailer. 1,000 + 8 + 20 + 14 + 4 = 1,046. The frame is over the 64-byte minimum, so no padding is added.

+++ question | What a CRC always catches
--- ask
A link uses a 16-bit CRC whose generator polynomial has (x + 1) as a factor. Which errors is it guaranteed to detect?
--- choice
Every error of any size, since a 16-bit CRC has 65,536 possible values.
--- choice correct
Every error with an odd number of flipped bits, and every burst of 16 or fewer consecutive bits.
--- choice
Only single-bit errors; everything else is left to chance.
--- choice
Every error, as long as the frame is shorter than 16 bytes.
--- why
An error is missed only when its error polynomial is a multiple of the generator. The factor (x + 1) divides no polynomial with an odd number of terms, and a burst of length at most 16 cannot be a multiple of a degree-16 generator with a constant term. Other errors slip through with probability about 2⁻¹⁶: very small, but not zero.

+++ question | Flooding the unknown
--- ask
A learning switch receives a frame for a MAC address that is not in its table. Why does it send the frame out of every other port, instead of dropping it?
--- choice
Because dropping frames is forbidden by the Ethernet standard.
--- choice correct
Because the destination may be on any port; flooding delivers the frame anyway, and the destination's reply teaches the switch where it is, so later frames go out of one port only.
--- choice
Because flooding is how the switch checks the frame's CRC.
--- choice
Because the switch assumes the frame is a broadcast.
--- why
Switches learn only from source addresses. A frame for an unknown destination is flooded once; when the destination answers, its source address fills in the table. Dropping would break every first conversation, since nobody would ever be learned.

+++ question | Counting a prefix
--- ask
How many IPv4 addresses does the prefix `10.20.0.0/14` contain? Type the number.
--- answer
262144
262,144
2^18
2**18
--- why
A /14 fixes the first 14 of the 32 bits, leaving 18 bits free: 2¹⁸ = 262,144 addresses, from 10.20.0.0 to 10.23.255.255.

+++ question | What a distance-vector router knows
--- ask
In distance-vector routing, what does one router know about the network?
--- choice
The whole map: every router and every link with its cost.
--- choice correct
Only its own links, and the distance to each destination that each neighbour last reported; it never sees the map beyond its neighbours.
--- choice
Nothing but its neighbours' names; routes are computed by a central server.
--- choice
Every path to every destination, so that it can pick one when a link fails.
--- why
That is why distance vector is simple and cheap, and also why it can count to infinity: a router cannot tell whether a neighbour's route to X goes back through itself. Link-state routers flood the whole map instead and each runs Dijkstra on it.

+++ question | A window for selective repeat
--- ask
A selective-repeat protocol uses 3-bit sequence numbers. What is the largest window it can use safely? Type the number.
--- answer
4
--- why
With M = 2³ = 8 numbers, selective repeat needs a window of at most M / 2 = 4: the receiver's window and the range of numbers that may still be retransmitted must not overlap, or an old packet looks like a new one. Go-Back-N, whose receiver accepts only one number, can use M − 1 = 7.

+++ question | Stop-and-wait on a slow link
--- ask
Stop-and-wait sends 1,000-byte frames on a 1 Mbit/s link with a round-trip time of 72 ms. What fraction of the time is the link sending? Type the answer as a decimal.
--- answer
0.1
.1
0.10
10%
1/10
--- why
One frame takes 8,000 bits / 1,000,000 bits per second = 8 ms to send, then the sender waits a round trip of 72 ms for the ACK. Utilisation = 8 / (8 + 72) = 0.1.

+++ question | A closed window and a lost update
--- ask
A TCP receiver advertises a window of 0. Later its program reads data and it sends an ACK that opens the window again, but that ACK is lost. What stops both sides from waiting forever?
--- choice
The receiver resends the window update every second until data arrives.
--- choice correct
The sender's persist timer: while the window is 0 it keeps sending small window probes, and the receiver's reply to a probe carries the current window.
--- choice
The sender's congestion window, which grows by one MSS per round trip even with a zero window.
--- choice
Nothing: the connection is reset after the retransmission timeout.
--- why
ACKs that carry no data are never retransmitted, because nothing acknowledges an ACK. So the side that wants to send is the one that must ask: the sender probes a zero window for as long as it stays zero.

+++ question | Climbing back after a loss
--- ask
A TCP Reno flow in congestion avoidance has a window of 100 segments when a single loss is repaired by fast retransmit and fast recovery. With no more losses, about how many round trips does it take to get back to 100 segments? Type the number.
--- answer
50
50 round trips
--- why
Fast recovery ends with cwnd = ssthresh = 50 segments. Congestion avoidance then adds one segment per round trip, so it needs 50 round trips to climb from 50 back to 100. On fast long paths this recovery time is what CUBIC and BBR were designed to shorten.

+++ question | Glue
--- ask
The `com` servers delegate `orbit.com` to the name server `ns1.orbit.com`. Why must the `com` zone also hold the address of `ns1.orbit.com`?
--- choice
To make the reply bigger, so that it cannot be forged.
--- choice correct
Because finding the address of `ns1.orbit.com` would otherwise mean asking the `orbit.com` servers, which is exactly what the resolver cannot do yet.
--- choice
Because name servers are not allowed to have A records in their own zone.
--- choice
Because the root servers do not cache anything.
--- why
That address is a glue record, sent along with the referral. It is only needed when the name server's name lies inside the zone being delegated; a server named in another zone is found by an ordinary lookup.

+++ question | Counting requests in a stream
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
buf = b""
count = 0
for piece in [b"GET / HT", b"TP/1.1\r\n\r\nGET", b" /x HTTP/1.1\r\n\r\n"]:
    buf += piece
    while b"\r\n\r\n" in buf:
        _, buf = buf.split(b"\r\n\r\n", 1)
        count += 1
print(count, len(buf))
```
--- answer
2 0
--- why
The first piece holds no complete request. After the second, the buffer holds one complete request and the start of the next (`GET`), so the count becomes 1 and `GET` stays in the buffer. The third piece completes the second request, the count becomes 2, and nothing is left: `2 0`.

+++ question | The risk of 0-RTT
--- ask
TLS 1.3 lets a returning client send its first request in the very first flight, with no round trip (0-RTT). What is the danger, and what follows from it?
--- choice
The data is sent unencrypted, so it must not contain passwords.
--- choice correct
An attacker who records that first flight can replay it to the server, so 0-RTT data should only carry requests that are safe to repeat, like a GET.
--- choice
The server cannot check its certificate, so the client may be talking to an impostor.
--- choice
It disables forward secrecy for the whole connection.
--- why
The early data is encrypted with keys from an earlier session, before the server has contributed any fresh random value, so nothing in it proves it is new. A replayed "transfer 100" would run twice; a replayed GET does no harm. That is lesson 12's idempotence again.
